In the quiet turning of the earth, there are days when the nameless do the naming, and the forgotten speak the truth that kings later forget. History often treats these lives as cold stones, cast against the bosom of the reader to glance off without a mark. Yet, within the musty pages of a Latin chronicle, buried beneath four centuries of silence, lies the story of two souls who lived and died unheard. Fate, it seems, is rarely kind to the obscure; but perhaps, if we can breathe life into their dry records, we may grant them a moment of grace in our hearts.

It was the mid-fifteenth century. Louis XI ruled France with a calculating eye; Edward IV held the English throne by right of conquest rather than pure claim; and in the Low Countries, Philip "the Good," Duke of Burgundy, reigned supreme over Holland. He had seized power from his cousin Jacqueline, breaking her spirit to secure his own, and now his rule was unchallenged.

Our tale begins in the modest town of Tergou, where Elias and Catherine lived. Elias was a merchant of cloth, silk, and brown holland, but above all, he traded in curried leather—a durable material prized by the common folk for its ability to withstand the sharp blades of a century where violence was as casual as carving meat at dinner. Though the couple possessed a comfortable estate, their peace was shadowed by the weight of nine children. In their youth, the arrival of each child had been a cause for celebration, viewed as divine playthings brought for the family's amusement. But as the parents aged and the olive branches of their household grew thick, the joy mingled with dread. They belonged to a prudent people, where recklessness was rare, yet the sheer size of their family became a fortress of worry. When the massive loaf was served, seeming to vanish before the tour of the table was complete, Elias and Catherine would exchange a silent glance. "Who will find bread for them all when we are gone?" Elias would ask.

To the younger children, food was as natural as the rising sun; to the elders, it was a looming crisis. Their anxiety manifested in mortifying ways. They refused to let any of their blood fade into obscurity among the burghers. Through strict self-denial, they managed to clothe and feed their growing mouths, saving a small hoard that brought them a secret pleasure unknown to the miser who saves only for himself.

One day, the second eldest, Richart, aged nineteen, approached his mother. With a composure that often masked the turmoil of the Dutch temperament, he begged permission to leave for Amsterdam to apprentice with a merchant. "Merchants are wealthy," he argued, "and I am good with numbers." Catherine threw up her hands in dismay. "Leave Tergou? Quit your poor father now he is no longer young?" Richart's resolve was granite. "If I can leave the folk of Tergou, I can surely leave the stones," he declared, his voice steady despite the tear in his eye. "There will be one mouth less for you to feed."

The departure broke Catherine's heart; she watched her first young bird attempt to fly from the nest. Richart left with a long, sad face, and that evening, Elias shouted at the remaining children to "sit wider" at the table, turning away in silence. The pattern repeated two years later with Jacob, who also departed for Amsterdam. By the time the third son, Giles, was considered for apprenticeship, the family coffers were drained. Elias, adhering to the creed that "a penny saved is a penny gained," had spent their entire reserve on Richart and Jacob. Now, they began to gather like ants, denying themselves the humblest luxuries to build a new fund.

Three years of frugality allowed them to establish their fourth son as a tailor and their eldest daughter as a robermaker. Yet, the coffers emptied again. Two children remained unable to work: Giles, a dwarf of malice and half-stupidity, and Little Catherine, a frail girl who moved on crutches yet smiled through her pain. Two others were unwilling: Sybrandt, lost in play, and Cornelis, the eldest, who waited passively for "dead men's shoes." Dispirited by their infirmities and the endless cycle of worry, the parents would often lament, "What will become of these when we are gone?" But the domestic horizon would suddenly clear, and they would add cheerfully, "But thanks to St. Bavon, there's Gerard."

Gerard was a son apart, destined for the Church. He possessed a frivolous nature, wrapped up in reading and penmanship, often neglecting meals to tend to his candles and books. The monks of a neighboring convent encouraged his talents, teaching him calligraphy until he inadvertently taught them in return. However, Gerard harbored a secret ambition: illumination. He wished to paint the borders and miniatures of sacred texts, but he lacked the means. The monks noted his inability to color, suggesting he lacked the gold, blue, and red necessary to make the words truly sing.

His fortunes changed with the arrival of Margaret Van Eyck, a fellow enthusiast and the surviving sister of the famous painters. She bought a house near Tergou and, upon seeing Gerard's work, invited him to her home. Initially, she dampened his spirit by comparing his sketches to her own masterpieces, but she soon revealed the true barrier: he could not afford the expensive pigments. Moved by his plight, she gifted him brushes, gold leaf, vermilion, and ultramarine, along with fine vellum. This act of sympathy sparked a deep affection between the old painter and the young scribe, a bond characteristic of an age where art and life were inseparable.

Encouraged by Margaret and the monks, Gerard's spirits soared. He became a source of joy at the dinner table, his wit drawing laughter from antiquity. As a token of gratitude, he created exquisite copies of the monastery's manuscripts. Meanwhile, Prince Philip "the Good" announced a grand competition, offering prizes for the finest works in jewelry, painting, and illumination. The burgomasters were ordered to assist poorer competitors, sending their entries to Rotterdam.

Gerard's heart leaped. He told his family timidly of his intention to compete. The reaction was immediate and harsh. The dwarf, Giles, erupted in a bassoon-like laugh, mocking the audacity of the plan. "Two gold crowns!" Catherine screamed, horrified by the cost of such "vanity." She refused to spend a month's worth of food on a dream, leaving the room in a fury. Little Catherine pleaded, but Gerard sighed, resigned to giving up or asking Margaret.

However, the housewife returned moments later, her face softening. She placed two gold pieces in his hand, claiming they were not needed for the venture. Gerard, wishing to spare her purse, proposed using vellum from his own copy of Terence, reducing the cost to a single crown. His mother, ever pragmatic, agreed to keep the crown in her pocket, warning him that houses are never built for less than the builder counts on.

On the eve of his departure, Margaret gave Gerard a letter addressed to Princess Marie in Rotterdam. Dressed in his finest holiday suit—silver-grey cloth, buckskin hose, and a hat secured by a purple ribbon from Little Catherine—Gerard set out. Near Rotterdam, he encountered an old man and a young woman, both exhausted and hungry. Recognizing their plight, Gerard stopped to gather sticks, light a fire, and cook bread and water from his flask. The young woman, initially reserved, softened as she watched his energy.

Just as Gerard prepared to offer them shelter, a figure on a mule approached. It was Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the burgomaster of Tergou. He was a man of wealth, dressed in ermine and velvet, yet his face was furrowed with a strange pain. He recognized the faded old man and the bright girl by the roadside fire. The sight of them, reduced to begging by the wayside, struck a chord of deep remorse in the miser's heart. Twenty years prior, Ghysbrecht had committed a heartless act of roguery that had haunted him ever since. Seeing the youth, enterprise, and knowledge of Gerard, and the dignity of the Van Swieten family in their poverty, triggered a sudden, painful realization. He reined in his mule, his hand trembling in his purse, unable to offer the charity he felt compelled to give.

"Peter, Margaret," he called out, his voice strained. "What mummery is this?"

"My father was exhausted," Margaret explained quickly, shielding her father.

Ghysbrecht reached for his purse, but his hand fumbled, afraid to draw out a coin that might stick to his finger. Just then, Gerard arrived, holding two straws, ready to help cook. Upon recognizing the burgomaster, Gerard blushed, and Ghysbrecht withdrew his hand, bitterness replacing his hesitation. "Oh!" he muttered, "I am not wanted," and rode on, casting a look of suspicion at Margaret and hostility at Gerard.

Gerard, misinterpreting the glance, laughed. "By St. Bavon, I think the old miser grudges us our soup." Margaret smiled, relieved, unaware that the man she had helped was carrying a scorpion of remorse in his heart. Ghysbrecht plodded on, richer than the beggars beside him, yet poorer in spirit, burdened by the knowledge that the wealth he hoarded belonged to the very people he had passed by, and the sins of his past had finally caught up with him.

"The broth is piping hot," Gerard announced, his voice cutting through the quiet of the room.

"But how shall we bring it to our lips?" the elder man sighed, his shoulders slumping in despair.

"Father, the youth has provided straws," Margaret replied, a mischievous glint dancing in her eyes.

"Ay, ay!" the old scholar nodded, though his tone remained skeptical. "Yet my bones are stiff, and kneeling over such a fierce fire with mere reeds seems impossible. St. John the Baptist, but the lad is dexterous!"

As he voiced his complaint, Gerard moved with practiced ease. He loosened the knot at his chest, removed his cap, and placed a stone in each corner of it. Wrapping his hand in the tail of his jerkin, he snatched the flask from the heat, wedged it securely between the stones, and lifted the cap to the elder's nose with a cheerful grin. Trembling slightly, the old man inserted the reed and drew. Instantly, his pale, drawn features seemed to ignite, glowing with renewed vigor. Upon taking a long, deep breath, he exclaimed, "Hippocrates and Galen! It is *soupe au vin*—the restorer of restoratives. Blessed be the nation that devised it, the woman who crafted it, and the young man who delivers it to the fainting. Have a taste, my girl, while I recount to our youthful host the history and virtues of this sovereign elixir. This remedy was unknown to the ancients; it appears in neither their medical treatises nor their popular narratives of surgery and healing. Hector, in the *Iliad*, if my memory serves me correctly—

(Margaret whispered, "Alas! he is off.")

—was invited by a lady of the poem to drink a draught of wine. Yet he declined, citing his imminent entry into battle and the need to preserve his strength. Had the *soupe au vin* been known in Troy, surely Hector would have added to his hexameter, 'But a *soupe au vin*, madam, I shall taste, and with gratitude.' Not only would this have been a mark of common civility—a virtue every perfect commander possesses—but failing to do so would have branded him shallow and imprudent, unfit to lead armies. For men entering battle require sustenance and every possible advantage, a truth proven by the fact that foolish generals, sending hungry soldiers against well-fed foes, have been defeated by inferior forces throughout the ages. The Romans lost a great battle in northern Italy to Hannibal solely due to this neglect. Now, this divine nectar grants strength to the limbs and ardor to the spirit in an instant. Taken by Hector at the critical moment, aided by Phoebus, Venus, and the blessed saints, it would likely have secured a Greek victory. Note how faint and weary I was a moment ago; well, I sip this celestial cordial, and now behold me brave as Achilles and strong as an eagle."

"Oh, father, now? An eagle, alas!" Margaret cried softly.

"Girl, I defy thee and all the world," the old man declared proudly. "Ready, I say, like a foaming charger, to devour the distance between here and Rotterdam, and strong enough to combat life's ills, even poverty and old age, which philosophers call the greatest evil. Negatur; unless a man's life has been ill-spent—which, by the way, it generally has. Now, to the moderns!"

"Father! Dear father!"

"Fear me not, girl; I shall be brief, unreasonably and unseasonably brief. The *soupe au vin* is absent from modern science; but this is merely further proof that for the last few centuries, physicians have been fools, relying on chicken broth and decoctions of gold, attributing supreme qualities to meat with the least juice and metal with the least chemical properties. Mountebanks! Dunces! Homicides! Since no light can be gathered from these sources, let us turn to the chroniclers. First, we find Duguesclin, a French knight, preparing to engage the English—who then ruled half of France and struck hard by sea and land. He drank not one, but three *soupes au vin* in honor of the Blessed Trinity. Having done so, he charged the islanders; and, as might have been predicted, slew a multitude and drove the rest into the sea. But he was merely the first of a long line of holy, hard-hitting warriors who, through this divine restorative, have been sustained, fortified, corroborated, and consoled."

"Dear father, pray join that venerable company before the soup grows cold," Margaret pleaded, holding the hat imploringly in both hands until he inserted the straw once more.

This spared them the "modern instances" and gave Gerard the chance to tell Margaret how proud his mother would be that her soup had benefited a man of learning.

"Ay! but," Margaret countered, "it would ill suit her to see her son give all and take none himself. Why did you bring but two straws?"

"Fair mistress, I hoped you would allow me to kiss your straw, seeing there were only two," Gerard replied.

Margaret smiled and blushed. "Never beg that you may command," she said. "The straw is not mine; it is yours: you cut it in that field."

"I cut it, and thus it became mine; but afterward, your lips touched it, and thus it became yours."

"Did it? Then I will lend it to you. There—now it is yours again; your lips have touched it."

"No, it belongs to us both now. Let us divide it."

"By all means; you have a knife."

"No, I will not cut it—that would be unlucky. I shall bite it. There, I shall keep my half; you will burn yours once you return home, I suspect."

"You know me not. I waste nothing. It is odds I shall fashion it into a hairpin or something similar."

This answer, rather than provoking Gerard, dashed his spirits, and he fell silent. With the bread and soup consumed, the old scholar prepared to resume his journey. A small difficulty arose: Gerard, the adroit one, could not retie his ribbon as Catherine had tied it. Margaret, after watching his futile efforts with a coy smile, offered assistance. For at her age, girls delight in alternating between sauciness and gentleness, and she saw she had momentarily disconcerted him. Then a fair head, crowned with a stately mass of auburn hair, glossy and glowing through the silver threads, bowed sweetly toward him. As it ravished his gaze, two white, supple hands played delicately upon the stubborn ribbon, molding it with soft, airy touches. A heavenly thrill ran through the innocent young man, and vague glimpses of a new world of feeling and sentiment opened before him. Margaret unwittingly prolonged these exquisite sensations; it is not natural for her sex to hasten anything pertaining to the sacred toilet. Indeed, when the taper fingers finally subdued the ends of the knot, her mind was not quite at ease until, by a maneuver peculiar to the female hand, she made her palm convex and applied it with gentle pressure to the center of the knot—a sweet, little coaxing hand-kiss, as much as to say, "Now be a good knot, and stay so." The palm-kiss was bestowed upon the ribbon, but the wearer's heart leaped to meet it.

"There, that is how it was," Margaret said, drawing back to survey her work with a keen eye. Looking up for simple approval of her skill, she received a gaze of such ardent adoration that she quickly lowered her eyes, flushing all over. An indescribable tremor seized her, and she retreated with downcast lashes and tell-tale cheeks, taking her father's arm on the opposite side. Gerard, blushing at having startled her with his gaze, took the other arm; and so the two youths walked downcast and conscious, propping the "eagle" along in silence.

They entered Rotterdam through the Schiedamze Poort. Unacquainted with the town, Peter directed Gerard toward the Hooch Straet, where the Stadthouse stood. He himself was accompanying Margaret to his cousin in the Ooster-Waagen Straet, so almost upon entering the gate, their paths diverged. They bid each other a friendly farewell, and Gerard plunged into the great city. A profound sense of solitude fell upon him, yet the streets teemed with people. He lamented too late that, out of delicacy, he had not asked his former companions who they were and where they lived.

"Beshrew my shamefacedness!" he muttered. "But their words and breeding were above their means, and something whispered that they would not wish to be known. I shall never see her again. Oh, weary world, I hate you and your ways. To think I must meet beauty, goodness, and learning—three pearls of price—and never see them more!"

Lost in this sad reverie, letting his body drift wherever it would, he lost his way. But soon meeting a crowd moving in one direction, he mingled with them, reasoning they must be heading for the Stadthouse. Soon the noisy throng containing the moody Gerard emerged, not onto the Stadthouse grounds, but onto a large meadow beside the Maas. Then the attraction was revealed. Games of all sorts were underway: wrestling, the game of palm, the quintain, legerdemain, archery, and tumbling—an art in which, I blush to admit, women as well as men performed, to the great delight of the spectators. There was also a trained bear standing on his head, marching upright, and bowing with prodigious gravity to his master; a hare beating a drum; and a cock strutting disdainfully on little stilts. These sights made Gerard laugh occasionally, but the gay scene could not truly enliven him, for his heart was not in tune with it. Hearing a young man tell his companion that the Duke had been in the meadow but had gone to the Stadthouse to entertain the burgomasters, aldermen, prize competitors, and their friends, Gerard suddenly remembered his hunger and his desire to sup with a prince. He left the riverbank, and this time he found the Hooch Straet, which swiftly led him to the Stadthouse.

However, upon arrival, he was refused entry at one door, then another, until he reached the great gate of the courtyard. Guarded by soldiers and supervised by a pompous major-domo glittering in an embroidered collar and gold chain of office, holding a white staff with a gold knob, the gate was a fortress. A crowd of people attempted to soften this official rock, coming up in turn like ripples and retreating as such. It cost Gerard a struggle to approach, and when he was within four heads of the gate, he saw something that made his heart race: there was Peter, with Margaret on his arm, soliciting humbly for entrance.

"My cousin the alderman is not at home; they say he is here," Peter said.

"What is that to me, old man?" the guard retorted.

"If you will not let us pass to him, at least take this leaf from my tablet to my cousin. See, I have written his name; he will come out to us."

"For what do you take me? I carry no messages; I keep the gate."

He then bawled in a stentorian voice, inexorably: "No strangers enter here, but the competitors and their companies."

"Come, old man," a voice cried from the crowd, "you have gotten your answer; make way."

Margaret turned half-round, imploringly: "Good people, we are come from afar, and my father is old; and my cousin has a new servant who knows us not and would not let us sit in our cousin's house."

At this, the crowd laughed hoarsely. Margaret shrank as if struck. At that moment, a hand grasped hers—a magic grasp; it felt like heart meeting heart, or magnet meeting steel. She turned quickly, and it was Gerard. Such a little cry of joy and appeal escaped her bosom, and she began to whimper prettily.

They had hustled her and frightened her for one thing; and her cousin's thoughtfulness—or lack thereof—in not even telling his servant they were coming, was cruel. The servant's caution, however wise and faithful to his master, was bitterly mortifying to her father and herself. And to her, so mortified, anxious, and jostled, came suddenly this kind hand and face. "Hinc illae lacrimae."

"All is well now," remarked a coarse humorist. "She hath gotten her sweetheart."

"Haw! haw! haw!" went the crowd.

She dropped Gerard's hand directly and turned round, eyes flashing through her tears: "I have no sweetheart, you rude men. But I am friendless in your boorish town, and this is a friend; and one who knows, what you know not, how to treat the aged and the weak."

The crowd fell dead silent. They had only been thoughtless, and now felt the rebuke, though severe, was just. The silence enabled Gerard to treat with the porter.

"I am a competitor, sir."

"What is your name?" the man eyed him suspiciously.

"Gerard, the son of Elias."

The janitor inspected a slip of parchment he held: "Gerard Eliassoen can enter."

"With my company, these two?"

"Nay; those are not your company; they came before you."

"What matter? They are my friends, and without them I go not in."

"Stay without, then."

"That will I not."

"That we shall see."

"We will, and speedily." And with this, Gerard raised a voice of astounding volume and power, routing the air so that the whole street rang: "Ho! PHILIP, EARL OF HOLLAND!"

"Are you mad?" cried the porter.

"Hush, hush!"

"Hush! Murder! The Dukes are there. I'm dead," cried the janitor, quaking.

A grave, white-haired seneschal approached their table, his demeanor courteous as he inquired if Gerard Eliassoen was among their party. Upon receiving confirmation, he announced that the Princess Marie wished to confer with him and would conduct him to her presence. Instantly, every face within hearing turned sharply toward the man destined to meet royalty, etched with a mixture of curiosity and envy.

Gerard rose to obey.

"I wager we shall not see you again," Margaret said calmly, though a faint flush colored her cheeks.

"That you will," the seneschal replied, leaning close to whisper, "This is my good princess; but you are my queen." Turning to the group, he added aloud, "Wait for me, I pray you; I will presently return."

"Ay, ay!" Peter exclaimed, waking from his reverie and speaking in unison with the others.

Once Gerard was gone, the pair, whose attire was so plainly homely compared to the man the Princess had summoned, became the center of neighboring gazes. Observing this shift in attention, William Johnson stepped forward, feigning surprise to claim his relation.

"And to think that there was I at your backs, and you saw me not," he remarked.

"Nay, cousin Johnson, I saw you long since," Margaret replied coldly.

"You saw me, and spoke not to me?"

"Cousin, it was for you to welcome us to Rotterdam, as it is for us to welcome you at Sevenbergen. Your servant denied us a seat in your house."

"The idiot!" Johnson muttered.

"And I had a mind to see whether it was 'like maid like master,' for there is sooth in bywords." William Johnson blushed a deep purple. He sensed Margaret's keenness and suspected her motives. Under the circumstances, he chose the wisest course: trusting to deeds rather than words, he insisted they accompany him home immediately, promising to show them whether they were truly welcome to Rotterdam.

"Who doubts it, cousin? Who doubts it?" the scholar interjected.

Margaret thanked him graciously but demurred to leave at once, claiming she wished to hear the minstrels again. After a quarter-hour, Johnson renewed his proposal, noting that many guests had departed. Then her true reason surfaced.

"It were ill manners to our friend; and he will lose us. He knows not where we lodge in Rotterdam, and the city is vast, and we have parted company once already."

"Oh!" Johnson responded. "We will provide for that. My young man—ahem! I mean my secretary—shall sit here and wait, and bring him on to my house. He shall lodge with me and with no other."

"Cousin, we shall be too burdensome."

"Nay, nay; you shall see whether you are welcome or not, you and your friends, and your friends' friends, if need be; and I shall hear what the Princess would with him."

Margaret felt a thrill of joy that Gerard might be lodged under the same roof as her, yet a slight misgiving followed. "But if your young man should be thoughtless, and go play, and Gerard miss him?"

"He go play? Leave that spot where I put him, and bid him stay? Ho! Stand forth, Hans Cloterman."

A figure clad in black serge and dark violet hose arose, taking two steps to stand before them without moving a muscle. He was a solemn, precise young man, the very statue of gravity and starched propriety. At his appearance, Margaret, overwhelmed by happiness, could hardly maintain her composure. Yet she whispered to Johnson, "I would put my hand in the fire for him. We are at your command, cousin, as soon as you have given him his orders."

Hans was instructed to sit at the table and wait for Gerard, conducting him to Ooster-Waagen Straet. He replied not in words, but by calmly taking the indicated seat. Margaret, Peter, and William Johnson then departed together.

"And, indeed, it is time you were abed, father, after all your travel," Margaret said, a thought that had occupied her mind all along.

Hans Cloterman sat waiting for Gerard, solemn and businesslike. Minutes flew by, yet excited no impatience in that perfect young man. Johnson did him no more than justice when he laughed to scorn the idea of his secretary leaving his post or neglecting his duty in pursuit of sport or youthful hilarity.

As Gerard remained long in coming, the patient Hans, with his employer's eye no longer upon him, improved the time by quaffing goblets of Corsican wine at solemn, silent, and accurately measured intervals. The wine was strong, and so was Cloterman's resolve. Gerard had been gone nearly an hour ere the model secretary imbibed the notion that Creation expected him to toast the health of all good fellows and the Duke of Burgundy present. With this view, he filled a bumper nine times, then rose gingerly but solemnly and slowly. Having reached his full height, he instantly rolled onto the grass, goblet in hand, spilling the cold liquor on more than one ankle—whose owners frisked—but not disturbing a muscle in his own long face. In the total eclipse of reason, his gravity, primness, and infallibility remained intact.

The seneschal led Gerard through several passages to the door of the pavilion, where young noblemen, embroidered and feathered, sat sentinel guarding the heir-apparent, playing cards by the red light of torches held by their servants. A whisper from the seneschal caused one of them to rise reluctantly, stare at Gerard with haughty surprise, and enter the pavilion. He presently returned, beckoning the pair, and led them through a passage or two into an ante-chamber. There sat three more young gentlemen, feathered, furred, and embroidered like pieces of fancy work, deeply engrossed in that instructive and edifying branch of learning known as dice.

"You can't see the Princess—it is too late," one declared.

Another followed suit: "She passed this way but now with her nurse. She is gone to bed, doll and all. Deuce—ace again!"

Gerard prepared to retire. The seneschal, with an incredulous smile, replied, "The young man is here by the Countess's orders; be so good as conduct him to her ladies."

On this, a superb Adonis rose with an injured look and led Gerard into a room where eleven ladies sat or lolloped, chattering like magpies. Two, more industrious than the rest, played cat's-cradle with fingers as nimble as their tongues. At the sight of a stranger, all tongues stopped like one piece of complicated machinery, and all eyes turned on Gerard, as if the same string that checked the tongues had turned the eyes. Gerard was ill at ease before, but this battery of eyes discountenanced him further, and his gaze dropped to the ground. Then, finding, like hares running by ponds and frogs scuttling into water, that there was a creature they could frighten, the cowards giggled and enjoyed their prowess. A duenna then said severely, "Mesdames!" and they were all abashed at once, as though a modesty string had been pulled. This same duenna took Gerard and marched before him in solemn silence. The young man's heart sank, and he had half a mind to turn and run out of the place.

"What must princes be," he thought, "when their courtiers are so freezing? Doubtless they take their breeding from him they serve." These reflections were interrupted by the duenna suddenly introducing him into a room where three ladies sat working and a pretty little girl tuned a lute. The ladies were richly but not showily dressed. The duenna approached the one hemming a kerchief and spoke a few words in a low tone. This lady then turned toward Gerard with a smile and beckoned him to come near. She did not rise, but she laid aside her work, and her manner of turning toward him, slight as the movement was, was full of grace, ease, and courtesy. She began a conversation at once.

"Margaret Van Eyck is an old friend of mine, sir, and I am right glad to have a letter from her hand, and thankful to you, sir, for bringing it to me safely. Marie, my love, this is the gentleman who brought you that pretty miniature."

"Sir, I thank you a thousand times," the young lady said.

"I am glad you feel her debtor, sweetheart, for our friend would have us to do him a little service in return."

"I will do anything on earth for him," the young lady replied with ardor.

"Anything on earth is nothing in the world," the Countess of Charolois said quietly.

"Well, then, I will—What would you have me to do, sir?"

Gerard had just discovered the nature of the high society in which he stood. "My sovereign demoiselle," he said gently, a little tremulously, "where there have been no pains, there needs no reward."

"But we must obey mamma. All the world must obey."

"That is true. Then, our demoiselle, reward me, if you will, by letting me hear the stave you were going to sing and I did interrupt it."

"What! You love music, sir?"

"I adore it."

The little princess looked inquiringly at her mother and received a smile of assent. She then took her lute and sang a romaunt of the day. Although but twelve years old, she was a well-taught and painstaking musician. Her little claw swept the chords with courage and precision, striking out the notes of the arpeggio clear, distinct, and bright, like twinkling stars; but the main charm was her voice. It was not mighty, but it was round, clear, full, and ringing like a bell. She sang with a certain modest eloquence, though knowing none of the tricks of feeling. She was too young to be theatrical, or even sentimental, so nothing was forced—all gushed. Her little mouth seemed the mouth of Nature. The ditty, too, was as pure as its utterance. There were none of those false divisions, those whining slurs now sold so dearly by Italian songsters, though every jackal in India delivers them gratis to his customers all night, and sometimes gets shot for them, and always deserves it. So there were no cadences and fiorituri, the trite, turgid, and feeble expletives of song, the skim-milk with which mindless musicians and mindless writers quench fire, wash out color, and drown melody and meaning dead.

While the pure and tender strain flowed from the pure young throat, Gerard's eyes filled. The Countess watched him with interest, for it was usual to applaud the Princess loudly, but not with cheek and eye. So when the voice ceased and the glasses left off ringing, she asked demurely, "Was he content?"

Gerard gave a little start; the spoken voice broke the charm and brought him back to earth.

"Oh, madam!" he cried, "surely it is thus that cherubs and seraphs sing, and charm the saints in heaven."

"I am somewhat of your opinion, my young friend," the Countess said, with emotion, and she bent a look of love and gentle pride upon her girl—a heavenly look, such as, they say, is given to the eye of the short-lived resting on the short-lived.

The Countess resumed: "My old friend requests me to be serviceable to you. It is the first favor she has done us the honor of asking, and the request is sacred. You are in holy orders, sir?"

Gerard bowed.

"I fear you are not a priest; you look too young."

"Oh no, madam; I am not even a sub-deacon. I am only a lector; but next month I shall be an exorcist, and before long an acolyte."

"Well, Monsieur Gerard, with your accomplishments you can soon pass through the inferior orders. And let me beg you to do so. For the day after you have said your first mass, I shall have the pleasure of appointing you to a benefice."

"Oh, madam!"

"And, Marie, remember I make this promise in your name as well as my own."

"Fear not, mamma: I will not forget. But if he will take my advice, what he will be is Bishop of Liege. The Bishop of Liege is a beautiful bishop. What! Do you not remember him, mamma, that day we were at Liege? He was braver than grandpapa himself. He had on a crown, a high one, and it was cut in the middle, and it was full of oh! such beautiful jewels; and his gown stiff with gold; and his mantle, too; and it had a broad border, all pictures; but, above all, his gloves; you have no such gloves, mamma. They were embroidered and covered with jewels, and scented with such lovely scent; I smelt them all the time he was giving me his blessing on my head with them. Dear old man! I dare say he will die soon, most old people do, and then, sir, you can be bishop, you know, and wear—"

"Gently, Marie, gently: bishoprics are for old gentlemen; and this is a young gentleman."

"Mamma! He is not so very young."

"Not compared with you, Marie, eh?"

"He is a good birth, dear mamma; and I am sure he is good enough for a bishop."

"Alas! mademoiselle, you are mistaken."

"I know not that, Monsieur Gerard; but I am a little puzzled to know on what grounds mademoiselle there pronounces your character so boldly."

"Alas, mamma," said the Princess, "you have not looked at his face, then;" and she raised her eyebrows at her mother's simplicity.

"I beg your pardon," said the Countess. "I have. Well, sir, if I cannot go quite so fast as my daughter, attribute it to my age, not to a want of interest in your welfare. A benefice will do to begin your career with; and I must take care it is not too far from—what call you the place?"

"Tergou, mad

It was nearly four in the afternoon when Eli returned to his shop, finding the place empty save for the hum of the street outside. His eldest and youngest sons had ventured forth, leaving the household in a state of suspended animation. Catherine and her frail, crippled daughter, Little Lily, had already departed down the lane, their hearts racing with the hope of spotting Gerard returning from the city. In the quiet sitting-room, only Giles remained, a figure of grotesque disproportion against the backdrop of a room that spoke volumes of Dutch ingenuity.

The people of Holland were renowned for their inventions—printing, oil-painting, banking, and above all, cleanliness. While the English gentry of the era still trod upon floors of stale rushes, a foul receptacle of bones, discarded food, and vermin, the hosier's home in Tergou gleamed with immaculate tile. These glazed surfaces were so pristine one might have eaten directly upon them. A single large window dominated the wall, its central stone cross standing in bold relief, a focal point for the family's daily devotions. The panes were small, diamond-shaped, and sealed with lead, a rustic charm that persisted in the countryside to this day.

The furniture was sparse and rough, save for a towering oak armchair. Its back rose at a right angle, so high that a seated person's head hovered two feet below the top, carved with intricate detail. Beside it stood a copper basin filled with holy water and a small besom for sprinkling, alongside a massive oak table. Perched upon the table's edge was a dwarf named Sampsonet, his teeth gripping the wood, his eyes blazing, his claws raised like those of a predatory bird. Nature, it seemed, had crafted Giles with the same care she bestowed upon the dwarf's head and torso, but had left the rest to chance. The result was a human wedge, an inverted cone where the upper body outweighed the lower by a factor of three. As Horace might have quipped, the amphora began well, yet the wheel turned until the jar spilled.

This peculiar imbalance, however, granted Giles a supernatural agility. His brawny arms, unburdened by heavy legs, allowed him to scale vertical poles with squirrel-like ease or hang from branches by a single hand. He possessed a peculiar obsession with the dinner table, often gripping it with both hands before seizing it with his teeth to swing in a frenzy of delight.

"Stop, Giles!" cried Little Kate, interrupting his acrobatics. She stood frozen, her heart aligned with the furniture rather than the gymnast. "Mother is coming! You are denting the table!"

"Go tell her, little tale-bearer," Giles snarled, retaking his perch. "You are the one who brings mischief."

"Am I?" Kate replied calmly, her tone dry. "That is news to me."

"The biggest troublemaker in Tergou," Giles grumbled, fastening his grip tighter.

"Oh, indeed!" she said drily.

With this unexpected barb launched, and Giles seemingly unscathed, Kate sat down quietly and began to weep. Her mother entered moments later, and Giles dove beneath the table, glaring from the shadows.

"What is the matter now?" the woman asked sharply. Turning her experienced gaze from Kate to the hidden figure, she noted his posture and a look of sheepishness, hinting at a cuffing of ears.

"Nay, mother," the girl said softly. "It was but a foolish word Giles spoke. I did not notice it at another time; I was weary and worried for Gerard."

"Let no one be in care for me," a faint voice called from the doorway.

Gerard stumbled in, pale, dusty, and utterly spent. Amidst cries of delight and rising anxiety, he collapsed into the nearest chair, his strength finally giving way. The long journey from Rotterdam, searching for Margaret, had drained him completely. Yet, the elastic vitality of youth soon surged back, and he became the center of an eager circle.

First, they demanded to hear of the prizes. Gerard recounted how he had been permitted to view the competitors' works in a vast hall before the judges' decision.

"Oh, mother! Oh, Kate!" he exclaimed, his eyes shining. "When I saw the goldsmiths' creations, I felt as though I would fall to the floor. I thought no smith on earth possessed so much gold, silver, and jewels, nor such mastery of design. Truly, all arts are divine."

To please the women, he described the reliquaries, chalices, crosses, and ecclesiastical wonders, followed by goblets, chains, brooches, and clocks, causing their mouths to water.

"But, Kate," he continued, his voice softening, "when I came to the illuminated manuscripts from Ghent and Bruges, my heart sank. My own work seemed dirt by comparison. For the first minute, I nearly wept; but I prayed for a better spirit, and soon I was able to admire them, thanking God for those lovely works and the skilled craftsmen who created them. The colored pages were so beautiful I forgot the black and white entirely."

"Then came the writing prizes," Gerard said, leaning forward. "Whose name do you think was called first?"

"Yours," Kate answered instantly.

The others laughed her to scorn.

"You may well laugh," Gerard said, a flush rising on his cheeks. "Yet, Gerard Eliassoen of Tergou was the name the herald shouted. I stood stunned; they pushed me forward. Everything swam before my eyes. I found myself kneeling on a cushion at the feet of the Duke. He spoke to me, but I was so fluttered I could not answer. Then he reached to his side—not to draw a glaive and sever my dull head—but to give me a gold medal. And here it is."

A yell erupted, followed by a scramble.

"And then he gave me fifteen great bright golden angels," Gerard declared, holding them up. "I had seen one before, but never handled one. Here they are."

"Oh, Gerard! Oh, Gerard!" the family cried out.

"There is one for you, our eldest; one for you, Sybrandt; and one for you, Little Mischief," Gerard distributed, his voice warm. "And two for thee, Little Lily, because God has afflicted thee. One for myself, to buy colors and vellum; and nine for her who nursed us all and risked the two crowns upon poor Gerard's hand."

The gold revealed their true natures. Cornelis and Sybrandt clutched their coins with glares of greed and envy directed at Kate, who held two pieces. Giles seized his medal, rolling it across the floor and gamboling after it. Kate set down her crutches, sat, and extended her small arms to Gerard in a gesture of heavenly love and tenderness. The mother, initially numbed by the shower of gold falling on her apron, suddenly cried out, "Leave kissing him, Kate; he is my son, not yours. Ah, Gerard! My boy! I have not loved you as you deserved."

Gerard threw himself on his knees beside her, and she flung her arms around him, weeping for joy and pride upon his neck.

"Good lad! Good lad!" cried the hosier, moved to emotion. "I must go and tell the neighbors. Lend me the medal, Gerard; I will show it to my good friend Peter Buyskens. He is always regaling me with tales of how his son Jorian won a tin mug for shooting at the butts."

"Ay, do, my man," Gerard encouraged. "Show Peter Buyskens one of the angels. Tell him there are fourteen more where that came from. Mind you bring it back!"

"Wait a moment, father," Gerard said, flushing with the joy he had caused. "There is better news behind."

"Better than this?"

Gerard then recounted his audience with the Countess, and the house rang with renewed joy.

"Now, God bless the good lady, and bless Dame Van Eyck!" the mother exclaimed. "A benefice? Our son! My cares are at an end. Eli, my good friend and master, now we two can die happy whenever our time comes. This dear boy will take our place, and none of these loved ones will lack a home or a friend."

From that hour, Gerard was regarded as the pillar of the family. He was a son apart, elevated above the rest. He was always in the right, and nothing was too good for him. Cornelis and Sybrandt grew increasingly jealous, longing for the day Gerard would depart for his benefice, freeing the family's resources for themselves. With these thoughts, Gerard cooperated, though the wound love had inflicted upon him throbbed with a duller, heavier pain. His success and the adoration of his parents made him view himself with greater height, resenting Margaret's ingratitude and discourtesy with growing spirit.

Yet, she held the power to cool his ardor toward women. Now, for every reason, he wished to be ordained a priest as soon as he could pass the intermediate orders. He knew the Vulgate better than most clergy and studied the rubrics and dogmas of the Church with his monastic friends. When the bishop first visited the area, Gerard applied to be admitted as an "exorcist," the third step in holy orders. The bishop questioned him briefly and ordained him immediately. Gerard knelt, offered a short prayer, and received a small manuscript filled with exorcisms.

"Take this, Gerard," the bishop said solemnly. "Have power to lay hands on the possessed, whether baptized or catechumens!"

He took the book reverently and returned home, invested by the Church with the power to cast out demons.

On his return from the church, he was met by Little Kate on her crutches.

"Oh, Gerard! Who, do you think, has sent to our house seeking you? The burgomaster himself."

"Ghysbrecht Van Swieten! What would he with me?"

"Nay, Gerard, I know not. But he seems urgent to see you. You are to go to his house on the instant."

"Well, he is the burgomaster; I will go. But it does not please me. Kate, I have seen him cast such a look upon me as no friend casts. No matter; such looks forewarn the wise. To be sure, he knows."

"Knows what, Gerard?"

"Nothing."

"Nothing?"

"Kate, I'll go."

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was a man of subtle design, one who often cast a wide net to catch a fish he intended to spear with precision. Addressing the novice, he feigned a concern for historical preservation. "The town records," he remarked, his tone dripping with feigned reverence, "are penned in a crabbed hand, the ink long since turned to rust by the passage of time." With a flourish, he extended an invitation to Gerard: the honor of transcribing these ancient documents into fair copy.

Gerard, ever practical, immediately inquired of the compensation.

Van Swieten named a sum so meager it barely covered the cost of pens, ink, and parchment.

"But, Burgomaster," Gerard pressed, his voice steady despite the slight chill in the air, "what of my labor? This is a year's work."

"Your labor?" Van Swieten scoffed, leaning back with a dismissive wave. "Do you call the marking of parchment labor? Little sweat goes to that, I trow."

"It is labor, and skilled labor to boot," Gerard countered, his eyes narrowing. "In every craft, the hand that guides the pen is paid more generously than the brute strength that breaks stone, regardless of the sweat shed. Furthermore, there is the matter of my time."

"Your time?" Van Swieten laughed, a sharp, brittle sound. "Why, what is time to a boy of twenty-two?" He fixed his gaze upon Gerard, searching for the tremor of guilt he expected to find. "Let us say, rather, that you have grown idle. That your heart has wandered. Your body remains among these chanting monks, but your spirit is elsewhere—with Peter Brandt and his red-haired paramour."

"I know no Peter Brandt," Gerard denied, the words tumbling out before he could check them.

This denial only hardened Van Swieten's resolve. The suspicion that the exorcist was playing a dangerous game now took root in the burgomaster's mind.

"You lie!" Van Swieten roared, slamming his hand onto the desk. "Did I not find you at her elbow on the road to Rotterdam?"

"Ah!" Gerard gasped, caught off guard.

"Ah! And you were seen at Sevenbergen but the other day."

"Was I?"

"Ah, and at Peter's house."

"At Sevenbergen?"

"Aye, at Sevenbergen."

In the parlance of the modern world, this was a bold gamble—a statement of fact offered merely to provoke a reaction that would reveal the truth. Van Swieten waited, his eyes gleaming with anticipation.

The result was unexpected. Instead of the calm denial he anticipated, Gerard sprang to his feet, seized by a strange, electric agitation.

"Burgomaster," he stammered, his voice trembling with a mixture of fear and defiance, "I have not set foot in Sevenbergen for three years. I know not the names of those you claim to have seen me with, nor where they dwell. But since my time is precious, even if yours is not, I bid you good day." With that, he darted from the room, his eyes sparkling with a feverish intensity.

Van Swieten rose in a fit of towering rage, only to sink back into his chair as the fury drained away, leaving behind a cold, calculating dread.

"He fears me not," he muttered, his voice low. "He knows something, if not everything."

He signaled frantically to his trusted servant, nearly dragging the man to the window.

"Do you see that man?" Van Swieten commanded, pointing a shaking finger toward the retreating figure. "Haste! Follow him! But let him not perceive you. He is young, yet old in cunning. Keep him in your sight all day. Tell me where he goes and what he does."

Night had fallen by the time the servant returned, breathless and weary.

"Well? Well?" Van Swieten cried, leaning forward eagerly.

"Master," the servant panted, "the young man went from you straight to Sevenbergen."

Van Swieten let out a groan that seemed to echo through the silent chamber.

"To the house of Peter the Magician."

"Look into your own heart and write!" cried Herr Cant, and the cuckoos of the earth seemed to echo his command. Delve into the deepest currents of the Rhine or the thickest waters of the Thames, and you shall find the truth painted upon the bottom. Cast a bucket into a well of self-deception, and whatever rises must be immortal verity, must it not? Yet, no son of Adam ever truly reads his own heart unless he has first studied the hearts of others for years, acquiring a sagacity that allows him merely to decipher his own soul rather than read it fluently. Halfway to Sevenbergen, Gerard cast his gaze inward and asked his heart why he journeyed there. The reply came without hesitation: "We go out of curiosity to know why she spurned us, to prove to her that our hearts remain unbroken, and that we are content with our future honors and benefice, needing neither her nor any ally of her fickle sex."

Soon, Gerard located Peter Brandt's cottage. A girl sat in the doorway, busily working with her needle, while a stalwart figure leaned against a long bow, conversing with her. At the sight of him, Gerard felt a sudden, inexplicable pang. The man, however, proved to be past fifty, an old soldier whose shooting skills Gerard remembered with admiration. A moment later, the youth stood before them. Margaret looked up, dropped her work, and let out a faint cry, her face flushing white and then red in rapid succession. Yet, these signs of emotion vanished swiftly, replaced by a chill indifference that would have been absent had she not betrayed such agitation.

"What! Is it you, Master Gerard? What on earth brings you here?" she asked.

"I was passing by and saw you; so I thought I would pay my respects and inquire after your father."

"My father is well. He will be here shortly."

"Then I may as well wait for his arrival."

"As you wish. Good Martin, go into the village and tell my father a friend has come."

"And not of yours?"

"My father's friends are mine."

"That is doubtful. It was not like a friend to promise to wait, only to vanish the moment my back was turned. Cruel Margaret, you little know how I searched the town for you; how, without you, nothing was pleasant to me."

"These are idle words. If you had truly desired my father's company or mine, you would have returned. I had a bed prepared for you at my cousin's, and he would have made much of you; perhaps I might have done so too. I was in the humor that day. You will not catch me in the same mind again, neither you nor any young man, I warrant."

"Margaret, I returned the moment the Countess released me, but you were not there."

"Nay, you did not, or you would have seen Hans Cloterman at our table; we left him to escort you."

"I saw no one there, only a drunken man who had just fallen."

"At our table? How was he dressed?"

"I paid little heed; he wore sad-colored garb."

At this, Margaret's face gradually warmed. Presently, assuming an air of incredulity and severity, she posed many shrewd questions, to which Gerard answered most loyally. Finally, the clouds cleared, and they deduced the source of the misunderstanding. Then came a revulsion of tenderness, all the more powerful because they had wronged one another, followed by mutual confessions. Neither had been happy since their parting; neither ever would have been so, but for this fortunate reunion.

Gerard found a manuscript of the Vulgate lying open on the table and pounced upon it like a hawk. Manuscripts were his delight, but before he could reach it, two white hands quickly covered the page, and a red face appeared above them.

"Nay, take away your hands, Margaret, that I may see where you are reading. I will read there too at home, so that my soul may meet yours in the sacred page. Will you not? Nay, then I must kiss them away." He kissed them so often that, overcome with shame, they were forced to withdraw. Lo! the sacred book lay open to the verse: "An apple of gold in a setting of silver."

"There, now," she said. "I had been hunting for it for so long, and found it but even now—and to be caught!" With a touch of inconsistency, she pointed it out to Gerard with her white finger.

"Ay," he replied, "but today it is all hidden beneath that great cap."

"It is a comely cap, I am told by some."

"Perhaps; but what it hides is beautiful."

"It is not; it is hideous."

"Well, it was beautiful at Rotterdam."

"Ay, everything was beautiful that day," she sighed softly.

Then Peter entered, welcoming Gerard cordially and inviting him to supper. Margaret disappeared, and Gerard enjoyed a learned conversation with Peter. Soon, Margaret reappeared, her hair arranged in a silver net, casting a glance that was half-arch, half-coy. She glided about them, spreading supper, and beamed with gaiety and happiness. In the cool evening, Gerard coaxed her out; she objected but came. He urged her onto the road to Tergou; she declined but came. They strolled up and down, hand in hand. When he must depart, they pledged each other never to quarrel or misunderstand one another again, sealing the promise with a long, loving kiss. Gerard went home on wings.

From that day forward, Gerard spent most of his evenings with Margaret. Their attachment deepened on both sides until the hours they shared were the hours they truly lived; the rest of time they merely endured. At the outset of this profound bond, all went smoothly. Obstacles existed, yet they seemed distant and small through the eyes of hope, youth, and love. The feelings and passions of so many others that this attachment might thwart gave no warning smoke to reveal their volcanic nature. The course of true love ran smoothly and placidly until it had drawn these two young hearts into its current forever.

And then—

The morning sun broke over Tergou with a brilliance that seemed to defy the season, casting a velvet glow upon the cobblestones where horses' hooves clattered in rhythmic haste. The air buzzed with the murmur of a city holding its breath, windows and balconies crowded with faces straining to catch a glimpse of the spectacle unfolding below. The French Ambassador rode through the streets, a figure of diplomatic grandeur, yet his procession was dominated by the eccentricities of the Duke of Burgundy's retinue.

Leading the way was the Duke's tumbler, a man of such sedate and grave majesty that his more noble companions appeared by comparison to be mere frivolous children. Yet, whenever the weight of the crowd's awe became too heavy, the tumbler would perform antics so ignoble and comical that even the stoic Ambassador could not suppress a laugh. He would roll from his horse with a graceless tumble, only to scramble back up by the tail, playing his part with a provoking display of buffoonery. Bringing up the rear of this motley parade was a creature that drew far more attention: the Duke's leopard. Mounted on a Flemish steed of monstrous size and power, a huntsman carried a long box strapped to his loins, upon which a bright leopard crouched, chained securely. The townspeople pressed close, mesmerized by her glossy hide and spotted coat, until the huntsman's voice cut through the air like a whip.

"Beware!" he roared. "In Antwerp, a man dared but throw a handful of dust at her, and the Duke made dust of him!"

"Gramercy!" someone whispered.

"I speak truth," the huntsman continued, his voice trembling with theatrical menace. "The good Duke imprisoned him in a cell beneath the earth, and the rats stripped the flesh from his bones in a single night. He served him right for molesting the beast."

A ripple of fear swept through the Tergovians; they shrank back, terrified to even tickle the sovereign's leopard. But the tension broke with the arrival of the final member of the procession: the Duke's giant. Standing seven feet four inches tall, this Hungarian brute possessed a treble, fluty voice of negligible power. He was vain, utterly unaware of his vocal defect, and now, catching sight of Giles perched atop a balcony, he stopped to mock him.

"Hallo! Brother!" the giant squeaked, his voice a shrill whistle. "I nearly passed without seeing thee."

"You are plain enough to see," Giles bellowed back, his bass tones shaking the air.

"Come on my shoulder, brother," the giant squealed again, extending a shoulder like a mutton fist to help him down.

"If I do, I'll cuff your ears," roared the dwarf.

The giant, sensing the homunculus's irascibility and encouraged by the crowd's laughter, played upon him. He failed to realize that the people laughed not at his wit, but at the absurd incongruity of the two voices—the gigantic body emitting a feeble fife-like sound, and the tiny dwarf belching out thunder.

The singular duet ended as abruptly as it began. Giles, losing all patience and self-command, dropped onto the giant's neck, seizing his hair with one hand and punching his head with the other. The giant's first impulse was to laugh, but the sudden weight and rapidity of the blows corrected that inclination instantly.

"He! he! Ah! ha! hallo! oh! oh! Holy saints! Here! Help! Or I must throttle the imp! I can't! I'll split your skull against the—"

He made a wild run backward toward the balcony. Giles saw his danger, grabbed the stone railing with both hands, and whipped over it just as the giant's head struck it with a stunning crack. The crowd roared with laughter and exultation at the little champion's address. The indignant giant, enraged, seized two of the laughers, knocked them together like dumbbells, and shook them flat. Catherine shrieked, throwing her apron over Giles, before the giant strode wrathfully away after the party.

Though no one present foresaw the consequences, the immediate result was agreeable. The Tergovians turned proud of Giles, listening with newfound affability to his pleas for parchment. He conducted a regular trade with his brother Gerard in this article, begging it gratis while Gerard supplied the coppers.

That afternoon, Catherine and her daughter Kate sat chatting about their favorite theme: Gerard, his goodness, his benefice, and the brightened prospects of the family. Their good fortune had arrived in the very shape they desired. Beyond the advantage of a benefice the Countess Charolois would not disdain, there lay the feminine delight of having a priest, a holy man, within their own household.

"He will marry Cornelis and Sybrandt," Catherine declared. "For they can wed, good housewives, now, if they will. Gerard will take care of you and Giles when we are gone."

"Yes, mother, and we can confess to him instead of to a stranger," Kate said.

"Ay, girl! And he can give the sacred oil to your father and me, and close our eyes when our time comes."

"Oh, mother! Not for many, many years, I do pray Heaven. Pray speak not of that; it always makes me sad. I hope to go before you, mother dear. No; let us be gay today. I am out of pain, mother, quite out of all pain; it does seem so strange. I feel so bright and happy that—mother, can you keep a secret?"

"Nobody better, child. Why, you know I can."

"Then I will show you something so beautiful. You never saw the like, I trow. Only Gerard must never know; for sure he means to surprise us with it; he covers it up so, and sometimes he carries it away altogether."

Kate took her crutches and moved slowly away, leaving her mother in an exalted state of curiosity. Soon she returned with something wrapped in a cloth. Uncovering it, she revealed a lovely picture of the Virgin, adorned with all her insignia, wearing her tiara over a wealth of beautiful hair flowing loose over her shoulders. Catherine was struck with awe.

"It is herself," she cried. "It is the Queen of Heaven. I never saw one like her to my mind before."

"And her eyes, mother: lifted to the sky, as if they belonged there, and not to a mortal creature. And her beautiful hair of burning gold."

"And to think I have a son that can make the saints live again upon a piece of wood!"

"The reason is, he is a young saint himself, mother. He is too good for this world; he is here to portray the blessed, and then to go away and be with them forever."

Before they had finished admiring the image, a strange voice was heard at the door. Driven by instinct, the women hastily hid the picture in the cloth, though there was no real need. The next moment, a man entered, casting furtive eyes around the room: Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the burgomaster, who had not visited the house in ten years.

The two women stared at him, exchanging a glance of contemptuous incredulity.

"The burgomaster!" they said in a tone so expressive that Ghysbrecht felt compelled to answer.

"Yes! I own the last time I came here was not on a friendly errand. Men love their own interest—Eli's and mine were contrary. Well, let this visit atone for the last. Today I come on your business and none of mine."

Catherine and her daughter exchanged a swift glance of disbelief. They knew the man better than he thought.

"It is about your son Gerard."

"Ay! Ay! You want him to work for the town all for nothing. He told us."

"I come on no such errand. It is to let you know he has fallen into bad hands."

"Now Heaven and the saints forbid! Man, torture not a mother! Speak out, and quickly: speak ere you have time to coin falsehood: we know thee."

Ghysbrecht turned pale at this affront, spite mingling with his other motives. "Thus it is, then," he said, grinding his teeth and speaking very fast. "Your son Gerard is more likely to be a father of a family than a priest: he is forever with Margaret, Peter Brandt's red-haired girl, and loves her like a cow her calf."

Mother and daughter both burst out laughing. Ghysbrecht stared at them, bewildered.

"What! You knew it?"

"Carry this tale to those who know not my son, Gerard. Women are nought to him."

"Other women, mayhap. But this one is the apple of his eye to him, or will be, if you part them not, and soon. Come, dame, make me not waste time and friendly counsel: my servant has seen them together a score of times, handing and reading babies in one another's eyes like—you know, dame—you have been young, too."

"Girl, I am ill at ease. Yea, I have been young, and know how blind and foolish the young are. My heart! He has turned me sick in a moment. Kate, if it should be true?"

"Nay, nay!" cried Kate eagerly. "Gerard might love a young woman: all young men do: I can't find what they see in them to love so; but if he did, he would let us know; he would not deceive us. You wicked man! No, dear mother, look not so! Gerard is too good to love a creature of earth. His love is for our Lady and the saints. Ah! I will show you the picture there: if his heart was earthly, could he paint the Queen of Heaven like that—look! Look!"

She held the picture out triumphantly. More radiant and beautiful in this moment of enthusiasm than any dead picture ever was or would be, she overwhelmed the burgomaster with her eloquence and her feminine proof of Gerard's purity. His eyes and mouth opened wide, remaining fixed in that state as he turned, face and all as if on a pivot, from the picture to the women, and from the women to the picture.

"Why, it is herself," he gasped.

"Isn't it!" cried Kate, her hostility softening. "You admire it? I forgive you for frightening us."

"Am I in a mad-house?" said Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, thoroughly puzzled. "You show me a picture of the girl; and you say he painted it; and that is a proof he cannot love her. Why, they all paint their sweethearts, painters do."

"A picture of the girl?" exclaimed Kate, shocked. "Fie! This is no girl; this is our blessed Lady."

"No, no; it is Margaret Brandt."

"Oh blind! It is the Queen of Heaven."

"No; only of Sevenbergen village."

"Profane man! Behold her crown!"

"Silly child! Look at her red hair! Would the Virgin be seen in red hair? She who had the pick of all the colors ten thousand years before the world began."

At that moment, an anxious face insinuated itself around the edge of the open door: their neighbor Peter Buyskens.

"What is to do?" he asked in a cautious whisper. "We can hear you all across the street. What on earth is to do?"

"Oh, neighbor! What is to do? Why, here is the burgomaster blackening our Gerard."

"Stop!" cried Van Swieten. "Peter Buyskens is come in the nick of time. He knows father and daughter both. They cast their glamour on him."

"What! Is she a witch too?"

"Else the egg takes not after the bird. Why is her father called the magician? I tell you they bewitched this very Peter here; they cast unholy spells on him, and cured him of the colic: now, Peter, look and tell me who is that? And you be silent, women, for a moment, if you can; who is it, Peter?"

"Well, to be sure!" said Peter, in reply; and his eye seemed fascinated by the picture.

"Who is it?" repeated Ghysbrecht impetuously.

Peter Buyskens smiled. "Why, you know as well as I do; but what have they put a crown on her for? I never saw her in a crown, for my part."

"Man alive! Can't you open your great jaws, and just speak a wench's name plain out to oblige three people?"

"I'd do a great deal more to oblige one of you than that, burgomaster. If it isn't as natural as life!"

"Curse the man! He won't, he won't—curse him!"

"Why, what have I done now?"

"Oh, sir!" said little Kate. "For pity's sake tell us; are these the features of a living woman, of—of—Margaret Brandt?"

"A mirror is not truer, my little maid."

"But is it she, sir, for very certain?"

"Why, who else should it be?"

"Now, why couldn't you say so at once?" snarled Ghysbrecht.

"I did say so, as plain as I could speak," snapped Peter; and they growled over this small bone of contention so zealously that they did not see Catherine and her daughter had thrown their aprons over their heads, rocking to and fro in deep distress.

The next moment Elias came in from the shop, standing aghast. Catherine, though her face was covered, knew his footsteps.

"That is my poor man," she sobbed. "Tell him, good Peter Buyskens, for I have not the courage."

Elias turned pale. The presence of the burgomaster in his house, after so many years of coolness, coupled with his wife's and daughter's distress, made him fear some heavy misfortune.

"Richart! Jacob!" he gasped.

"No, no!" said the burgomaster. "It is nearer home, and nobody is dead or dying, old friend."

"God bless you, burgomaster! Ah! Something

While the burgomaster of Tergou laid his traps for Gerard, a quieter, yet no less desperate storm brewed within the walls of Sevenbergen. Margaret found herself in a predicament born of a housewife's deepest shame: hunger. She stood before Martin Wittenhaagen, the veteran soldier, her eyes wet with unshed tears.

"Martin," she whispered, her voice trembling, "there is nothing left in the larder. Gerard returns tonight, and he is so careless; he forgets to eat at home until his labor is done, and then he comes to me, poor soul, nearly fainting. How can I offer food to the one who loves me so dearly when my cup is empty?"

Martin scratched his beard, his brow furrowed in concern. "What remedy do I have?"

"It is Thursday," she insisted, "your appointed day for the hunt. I had counted on your return today."

"Nay," the soldier replied, shaking his head. "I dare not draw my bow when the Duke or his retinue are out in the chase. Read this." He produced a parchment sealed with the grand insignia of Philip, Duke of Burgundy. It was a stipend and license granting Martin, one of the Duke's archers, a yearly allowance of four merks for past service and a wound sustained at the Duke's side. The license permitted him to shoot three arrows weekly—specifically on Thursdays—in the Duke's forests of Holland, provided the game was limited to a seven-year-old buck or a doe with a fawn. However, the proviso was absolute: if the Duke or his friends were hunting, Martin was forbidden from disturbing the woods, lest he forfeit his salary, his life, and pay a fine of a penny.

Margaret sighed, the weight of the law pressing down on her.

"Cheer up, mistress," Martin said gently, though his tone was grave. "For your sake, I will risk my carcass. I have done worse for women of lesser worth. It is not a great danger; I shall merely step to the edge of the forest. Odds are they will drive a hare or a fawn within range."

"If I permit you to go," Margaret pleaded, "promise me you will not stray far and remain unseen. Far better that Gerard goes supperless than that harm should come to you, faithful Martin."

With the promise extracted, Martin took his bow and three arrows, slipping cautiously into the woods, barely a furlong from the house. The faint cry of horns echoed in the distance, signaling the Duke's party; all the game was now on the move.

"Come," Martin thought, "I shall soon fill the pot, and none the wiser." He positioned himself behind a thick oak overlooking an open glade, drawing his weapon—a formidable instrument of English yew, six feet two inches tall and thick as a man's thigh. Martin, broad-chested with arms of iron and cord, had drawn this bow since infancy. He could pull a three-foot arrow to its head, and when released, the arrow flew so fast the eye could scarcely track it, the string twanging with a musical snap. This bow had claimed many a stout soldier in the wars of the Hoecks and Cabbel-jaws, where battles were fought with few combatants but many deaths, for they faced their enemies clearly rather than through the haze of modern smoke.

A hare cantered into view, then sat alert, its ears forming a perfect 'V'. Martin leveled his massive weapon. The arrow flew, the string sang, but in his haste to secure the kill, he missed by an inch. The shaft struck the earth beside the startled hare, passing beneath her belly like a flash before vanishing into the grass. The hare leaped three feet vertically and bolted at full speed.

"Bungler!" Martin muttered. A true proof he was not an habitual bungler, for he would have blamed the hare if he were. He fitted another arrow just as a wood-pigeon settled on the very branch above him. "Aha!" he thought. "Small, but dainty." This time he took care, drawing smoothly and releasing with precision. The arrow appeared to pierce the bird cleanly, sending feathers skyward like dust. Yet, the bird, its breast torn rather than pierced, fluttered weakly away. By sheer effort, it rose above the canopy, flew fifty yards, and fell dead, hidden by the thick foliage.

" Luck is against me," Martin said despondently. He fitted a third arrow and scanned the glade. Suddenly, he heard a commotion behind him and turned just in time to see a noble buck crossing the open space—too late to shoot. He dashed his bow to the ground in frustration.

At that precise moment, a long-spotted animal glided swiftly across the path, its belly skimming the earth. Martin snatched up his bow instantly. He recognized the Duke's leopard.

"The hunters will not be far behind," he realized. "I must vanish. Gerard must go supperless tonight."

He plunged into the woods, following the trail of the buck and the leopard, the only path back to safety. Soon, he heard an unnatural sound ahead: leaves rustling violently, the ground trampled. Hurrying forward, he discovered the leopard atop the buck, tearing at its flesh with teeth and claws. The deer ran in a frantic circle, convulsing as blood poured from its hide.

Martin formed a desperate resolve. He needed the venison for Margaret. Drawing his arrow to the head, he buried it deep in the deer. Despite the predator on its back, the buck bounded high into the air and collapsed, dead. The leopard continued to tear at the carcass as if nothing had happened.

Martin hoped the beast would gorge itself and then leave the meat. Minutes passed. He walked resolutely forward and placed his hand on the buck's leg. The leopard gave a frightful growl, pausing its feeding. She sensed his presence, sulky and wary. What was to be done? Martin recalled an old tale: wild creatures could not withstand the steady gaze of a human. Standing erect, he fixed his eyes upon the beast. The leopard returned a savage glare, never breaking contact.

Continuing to stare down the animal, the leopard, brutally ignorant of natural history, lunged at his head with a terrifying yell, her eyes flaming, jaws distended. Martin had but a split second to catch her by the throat before her teeth crushed his face. One claw seized his shoulder, ripping the fabric; the other, aimed at his cheek, would have been fatal. Martin was old-fashioned, wearing no hat, only a scapulary of rough leather over his jerkin. He had thrown it over his head like a hood, and the brute's claw snagged in the loose material.

He struggled to keep her teeth from his face, gripping her throat with iron force while she tore at his shoulder. It felt like blunt reaping-hooks grinding against bone. The pain was excruciating, yet instead of cowering, it ignited his blood. He gnashed his teeth in rage almost as fierce as hers, squeezing her neck with unyielding strength. Their eyes flared in a silent duel—the man's fury matching the brute's. Realizing she was being throttled, she made a wild attempt to break free, dragging his hood across his face, blinding him, and tearing her claw from his shoulder, taking flesh and bone with it. Still, he held her.

Presently, her long tail, raised high in the air, dropped. "Aha!" Martin cried joyfully, gripping her like death. Next, her body lost its elasticity. He held a choked, powerless thing, maintaining his grip until all motion ceased. Only then did he dash her to the earth. Panting, he removed his hood. The leopard lay mute at his feet, tongue protruding, paws bloody. For the first time, terror seized Martin.

"I am a dead man," he whispered. "I have slain the Duke's leopard."

Hastily, he gathered handfuls of leaves and threw them over the carcass, then shouldered the heavy deer and staggered away, leaving a trail of mingled blood—his own and the deer's. He burst into Peter's house a horror-stricken figure, bleeding and stained, and flung the deer's carcass onto the floor.

"There," he gasped, refusing questions. "Broil me a steak on it; I am faint."

Margaret did not see his wounds; she assumed the blood belonged solely to the deer. She busied herself at the fire, while the stout soldier stanching and binding his own wound apart. Soon, Martin, Gerard, and Margaret sat supping royally on the broiled venison.

They were merry indeed. Gerard, with wonderful thoughtfulness, had brought a flask of Schiedam, and under its influence, Martin revived, recounting the tale of the hunt. They laughed over the exploit, the tension momentarily forgotten.

Then, their mirth was shattered. Margaret's gaze became fixed, fascinated, her cheeks paling with sudden fear. She gasped, unable to speak, pointing a trembling finger toward the window. Their eyes followed hers. There, in the twilight, crouched a dark form with eyes like glowing worms.

It was the leopard.

As they stood petrified, hypnotized by the green fire of its eyes, a single, deep bay echoed from the woods. Martin trembled at the sound.

"They have lost her," he realized, dread filling his heart. "They have laid muzzled bloodhounds on her scent. They will find her here, and the venison. Good-bye, friends. Martin Wittenhaagen ends here."

Gerard seized his bow and pressed it into the soldier's hands.

"Be a man!" he cried. "Shoot her and fling her into the wood before they arrive. Who will know?"

More voices of hounds broke out, growing louder, closer.

"Curse her!" Martin roared. "I spared her once; now she must die, or I, or both of us more likely." He reared his bow, drawing the arrow to the head.

"Nay! Nay!" cried Margaret, seizing the arrow. It snapped in half; the pieces fell on either side of the bow. Simultaneously, the air filled with the tongues of the hounds; they were hot upon the scent.

"What have you done, wench?" Martin screamed, despair clawing at his throat. "You have put the halter round my neck!"

"No!" Margaret cried. "I have saved you! Stand back from the window, both! Your knife, quick!"

She seized his long-pointed knife, almost tearing it from his girdle, and darted from the room. The house was now surrounded by baying dogs and shouting men.

The glowworm eyes moved not.

Margaret severed a massive chunk of venison and hurled it through the window, where it landed before the emerald eyes of the fire. The beast lunged forward with a savage snarl, tearing and crunching the meat. Suddenly, a hound bayed so loudly and close that the sound echoed through the very rafters, causing the three animals at the window to recoil in terror. The leopard, fearing her supper was stolen, glided swiftly and silently away toward the woods. Before she had vanished, a chaotic throng of horses, men, and dogs thundered past the window, pursuing her with a full cry. Martin and his companions exhaled, relieved; the leopard was too swift to be caught within a league of their house. They clasped hands in relief. Seizing the moment, Margaret let out a small sob, and Gerard tenderly kissed the tears from her cheeks.

They returned to the table, and Gerard raised a toast to woman's wit, declaring it stronger than man's physical force. Margaret agreed, adding that this strength was reserved solely for moments when those she loved were in peril. That night, Gerard stayed with her longer than usual, returning home feeling prouder than ever and as happy as a prince. A short distance from home, beneath the shadow of ancient trees, he encountered two figures blocking his path. It was his father and mother.

"Why are you out so late?" Gerard asked, a chill settling over him. "What could be the cause?"

His father stopped him with a grim silence. "Why ask? You know why we are here."

"Oh, Gerard!" his mother interjected, her voice thick with reproach yet underlined with affection.

Gerard's heart hammered against his ribs; he fell silent. Seeing his son's confusion, his father softened slightly. "Do not hang your head. You are not the first young fool caught by a red cheek and a pair of blue eyes."

"Nay, nay!" Catherine added, "It was witchcraft. Peter the Magician is well known for such tricks."

"Come, Sir Priest," the father resumed, "you know you must not meddle with women folk. But give us your promise to go no more to Sevenbergen, and here all ends. We will not be hard on you for one fault."

"I cannot promise that, father."

"Not promise it, you young hypocrite!"

"Nay, father, do not miscall me. I lacked the courage to tell you what I knew would vex you. I am right grateful to that good friend, whoever he be, who let you know. It is a load off my mind. Yes, father, I love Margaret. And call me not a priest, for a priest I will never be. I would die sooner."

"That we shall see, young man. Come, gainsay me no more; you will learn what it is to disrespect a father."

Gerard held his peace, and the three walked home in a gloomy silence, broken only by deep sighs from Catherine.

From that hour, the little house at Tergou was no longer a sanctuary of peace.
