The next day, Gerard was summoned before the entire family, and every voice rose against him, save for little Kate's and the dwarf's, who instinctively sided with him. Cornelis and Sybrandt were bitterer than their father. Gerard was dismayed to find so many enemies and looked wistfully at his little sister. Her eyes brimmed with tears as harsh words were showered upon one who had been the universal pet just yesterday. Yet she offered no encouragement, turning her head away to say, "Dear, dear Gerard, pray to Heaven to cure you of this folly!"

"What, are you against me too?" Gerard asked sadly. He rose with a heavy sigh and left the house, walking toward Sevenbergen.

The beginning of a quarrel between parties bound by affection but opposed in interest is often innocent; both may be right at the start. It is only as the dissension endures, widening and deepening through human fallibility and irritability, that both sides inevitably become wrong. The combatants were unequally matched: Elias was angry, Cornelis and Sybrandt spiteful. But Gerard, possessing a larger and more cultivated mind, saw both sides where they saw only one. He suffered from fits of irresolution, feeling not wrath but profound unhappiness. He was lonely in this struggle, unable to open his heart to anyone. Margaret was a high-spirited girl; he dared not tell her of his torment at home, fearing she might side with his relations by resigning him, even at the cost of her own happiness. Though Margaret Van Eyck had been a great comfort to him on another occasion, he dared not make her his confidant now. Her history was well known: in her youth, she had refused many offers of marriage for the sake of her art, duties that a wife's and mother's life would render fatal. Thus, she remained single, painting with her brothers. How could he tell her that he had declined the benefice she had secured for him, doing so for the sake of something she had once despised and sacrificed lightly?

Gerard seemed poised to succumb. But the other side had a terrible ally in Catherine the elder. This good-hearted but uneducated woman could not act quietly or firmly like her daughter, nor could she execute a plan. She irritated Gerard at times, which paradoxically helped him, for anger sustains courage; at others, she turned round and launched attacks on her own forces. On one occasion, when they were all at home, Cornelis declared, "Our Gerard wed Margaret Brandt? Why, it is hunger marrying thirst."

"And what will it be when you marry?" Catherine cried. "Gerard can paint, Gerard can write, but what can you do to keep a woman, ye lazy loon? Nought but wait for your father's shoes. Oh, we can see why you and Sybrandt would not have the poor boy to marry. You are afraid he will come to us for a share of our substance. And say that he does, and say that we give it him, it isn't yourn we part from, and mayhap never will be."

On such occasions, Gerard smiled slyly, picked up his heart, and temporary confusion fell on Catherine's unfortunate allies. But finally, after more than six months of irritation, the climax arrived. The father announced to the son, before the whole family, that he had ordered the burgomaster to imprison him in the Stadthouse rather than let him marry Margaret. Gerard turned pale with anger but held his peace through a great effort. His father continued, "And a priest you shall be before the year is out, nilly-willy."

"Is it so?" Gerard cried. "Then, hear me, all. By God and St. Bavon I swear I will never be a priest while Margaret lives. Since force is to decide it, and not love and duty, try force, father; but force shall not serve you. For the day I see the burgomaster come for me, I leave Tergou forever, and Holland too, and my father's house, where it seems I have been valued all these years not for myself, but for what is to be got out of me."

He flung himself out of the room, white with anger and desperation.

"There!" cried Catherine. "That comes of driving young folks too hard. But men are crueller than tigers, even to their own flesh and blood. Now, Heaven forbid he should ever leave us, married or single."

As Gerard emerged from the house, cheeks pale and heart panting, he met Reicht Heynes, who bore a message: Margaret Van Eyck desired to see him. He found the old lady seated grim as a judge. She wasted no time on preliminaries, inquiring coldly why he had not visited her of late. Before he could answer, she said in a sarcastic tone, "I thought we had been friends, young sir."

At this, Gerard looked the picture of doubt and consternation.

"It is because you never told her you were in love," Reicht Heynes said, pitying his confusion.

"Silence, wench! Why should he tell us his affairs? We are not his friends; we have not deserved his confidence."

"Alas! my second mother," Gerard replied, "I did not dare to tell you my folly."

"What folly? Is it folly to love?"

"I am told so every day of my life."

"You need not have been afraid to tell my mistress; she is always kind to true lovers."

"Madam—Reicht, I was afraid because I was told..."

"Well, you were told—?"

"That in your youth you scorned love, preferring art."

"I did, boy; and what is the end of it? Behold me here a barren stock, while the women of my youth have a troop of children at their side and grandchildren at their knee. I gave up the sweet joys of wifehood and motherhood for what? For my dear brothers. They have gone and left me long ago. For my art. It has all but left me too. I have the knowledge still, but what avails that when the hand trembles? No, Gerard; I look on you as my son. You are good, you are handsome, you are a painter, though not like some I have known. I will not let you throw your youth away as I did mine: you shall marry this Margaret. I have inquired, and she is a good daughter. Reicht here is a gossip; she has told me all about it. But that need not hinder you from telling me."

Poor Gerard was overjoyed to be permitted to praise Margaret aloud to one who understood what he loved in her. Soon, there were two pairs of wet eyes over his story; and when the poor boy saw that, there were three.

Women are creatures brimful of courage. Theirs is not exactly the same quality as manly courage; that would never do, hang it all; we should have to give up trampling on them. No; it is a vicarious courage. They never take part in a bull-fight by any chance; but it is remarked that they sit at one unshaken by those tremors and apprehensions for the combatants to which the male spectator—a feeble-minded wretch—is subject. Nothing can exceed the resolution with which they have been known to send forth men to battle. As some witty dog says, "Les femmes sont très braves avec le peur d'autrui."

By this trait, Gerard now profited. Margaret and Reicht were agreed that a man should always take the bull by the horns. Gerard's only course was to marry Margaret Brandt off-hand; the old people would come to after a while, the deed once done. Whereas, the longer this misunderstanding continued on its present footing, the worse it would be for all parties, especially for Gerard.

"See how pale and thin they have made him amongst them."

"Indeed you are, Master Gerard," said Reicht. "It makes a body sad to see a young man so wasted and worn. Mistress, when I met him in the street today, I had liked to have burst out crying: he was so changed."

"And I'll be bound the others keep their colour; ah, Reicht? Such as it is."

"Oh, I see no odds in them."

"Of course not. We painters are no match for boors. We are glass, they are stone. We can't stand the worry, worry, worry of little minds; and it is not for the good of mankind we should be exposed to it. It is hard enough, Heaven knows, to design and paint a masterpiece, without having gnats and flies stinging us to death into the bargain."

Though exasperated by his father's threat of violence, Gerard listened to these friendly voices telling him the prudent course was rebellion. But though he listened, he was not convinced.

"I do not fear my father's violence," he said, "but I do fear his anger. When it came to the point, he would not imprison me. I would marry Margaret tomorrow if that was my only fear. No; he would disown me. I should take Margaret from her father and give her a poor husband, who would never thrive, weighed down by his parent's curse. Madam! I sometimes think if I could marry her secretly, and then take her away to some country where my craft is better paid than in this; and after a year or two, when the storm had blown over, you know, could come back with money in my purse, and say, 'My dear parents, we do not seek your substance, we but ask you to love us once more as you used, and as we have never ceased to love you'—but, alas! I shall be told these are the dreams of an inexperienced young man."

The old lady's eyes sparkled.

"It is no dream, but a piece of wonderful common-sense in a boy; it remains to be seen whether you have spirit to carry out your own thought. There is a country, Gerard, where certain fortune awaits you at this moment. Here the arts freeze, but there they flourish, as they never yet flourished in any age or land."

"It is Italy!" cried Gerard. "It is Italy!"

"Ay, Italy! Where painters are honored like princes, and scribes are paid three hundred crowns for copying a single manuscript. Know you not that His Holiness the Pope has written to every land for skilful scribes to copy the hundreds of precious manuscripts pouring into that favored land from Constantinople, whence learning and learned men are driven by the barbarian Turks?"

"Nay, I know not that; but it has been the dream and hope of my life to visit Italy, the queen of all the arts; oh, madam! But the journey, and we are all so poor."

"Find you the heart to go, I'll find the means. I know where to lay my hand on ten golden angels: they will take you to Rome: and the girl with you, if she loves you as she ought."

They sat till midnight over this theme. And after that day, Gerard recovered his spirits, seeming to carry a secret talisman against all the gibes and harsh words that flew about his ears at home.

Besides the money she procured him for the journey, Margaret Van Eyck gave him money's worth. She said, "I will tell you secrets

The banns of marriage were proclaimed three times, as the custom demanded, yet with a peculiar haste born of desperation. The young couple, Margaret and Gerard, managed to persuade the newly arrived curé to conduct all three readings within a single twenty-four-hour period. They were announced on Monday at matins and vespers, and to their immense relief, no one from Tergou appeared in the church to object. The following morning, they stood before the altar, hearts pounding with anxiety, only to be met with horror when a stranger rose to forbid the union. He claimed the parties were underage and lacked parental consent.

Outside the church doors, Margaret and Gerard trembled in a consultation of despair. Before they could formulate a plan, the man who had obstructed them approached, feigning regret. He explained that while his inclination was to bless their happiness, his livelihood depended on forbidding such banns. "The young people give me a crown, and I undo my work handsomely," he offered, suggesting Gerard tell the curé he had been misinformed.

"A crown!" Gerard exclaimed eagerly, offering a golden angel instead. The man accepted the bribe with equal eagerness, returning to the curé to claim he had made a ridiculous mistake. The priest, satisfied, agreed to marry the couple the next day at ten. The professional impediment to bliss departed with Gerard's angel, only to succumb to his own folly. He proceeded to a local hostelry, where a green was dedicated to archery and common sports. Drunk on his success, he boasted of his exploit to a frequent patron named Sybrandt. Sybrandt, a ne'er-do-well, ran home to tell his father, who was away in Rotterdam purchasing cloth. Sybrandt signaled his elder brother, and the two conspirators—Gerard's black brothers—convened.

Idleness was a vitiating force for these two brothers, whose minds were consumed by the inheritance they believed was theirs by right. Their parents' parsimony, originally a virtue born of industry and love, had been twisted in their selfish hearts into avarice, a fertile source of crime. They decided not to inform their mother, whose sentiments were uncertain, but to seek the burgomaster first. They were cunning enough to sense his aversion to the match, though they could not fathom the reason.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten saw through them immediately but maintained a mask of magisterial dignity. Hearing their story, he declared, "Since the father of the family is not here, his duty falleth on me, who am the father of the town. I know your father's mind; leave all to me; and, above all, tell not a woman a word of this, least of all the women that are in your own house: for chattering tongues mar wisest counsels." He dismissed them with a touch of supercilious shame.

Upon returning home, they found Gerard seated at their mother's knee. She caressed his hair, promising to take his part with his father and thwart his love no more. This sudden change of heart stemmed from a moment of female irritation; she had previously cut Margaret's picture to pieces. Watching Gerard turn pale as death, holding the fragments with tear-blinded eyes, she had been terrified by her own violence. Her heart smote her bitterly, and though she dared not admit it, she resolved to make amends. Her bowels yearned over her son, and her feeble anger died a natural death. She was transferring her fatal alliance to Gerard when the two black sheep entered. Gerard, inexperienced in the intricacies of female emotion, mistook her kindness for forgiveness, kissing her repeatedly and sleeping happily, unaware that his fate was sealed.

The next morning at ten, Gerard and Margaret stood in the church at Sevenbergen. Gerard was radiant; Margaret blushed. Only Peter and Martin Wittenhaagen were present as witnesses; secrecy was paramount. Margaret had declined Italy, unable to leave her helpless, learned father. Instead, they planned to retire to Flanders for a few weeks until the storm at Tergou passed. The curé did not keep them waiting long. He stood at the altar, called them forward, and opened his book.

But ere he uttered a single word of the sacred rite, a harsh voice cried, "Forbear!" Constables of Tergou surged up the aisle and seized Gerard in the name of the law. Martin's long knife flashed out instantly.

"Forbear, man!" cried the priest. "What! draw your weapon in a church, and ye who interrupt this holy sacrament, what means this impiety?"

"There is no impiety, father," said the burgomaster's servant respectfully. "This young man would marry against his father's will, and his father has prayed our burgomaster to deal with him according to the law. Let him deny it if he can."

"Is this so, young man?"

Gerard hung his head.

"We take him to Rotterdam to abide the sentence of the Duke."

Margaret uttered a cry of despair. The lovers, so happy a moment before, fell to sobbing in each other's arms so piteously that the instruments of oppression drew back, ashamed. Yet, one of them, pretending to separate them, whispered to Margaret, "Rotterdam? it is a lie. We but take him to our Stadthouse."

They mounted Gerard on horseback, sending him on a dozen halts and sly detours back to Tergou. Just outside the town, a rude vehicle covered with canvas awaited. Gerard was placed inside and, by five in the evening, was secretly conveyed to the prison of the Stadthouse. He was taken up several flights of stairs and thrust into a small room lit only by a narrow window with a vertical iron bar. The sole furniture was a huge oak chest.

Imprisonment in that age was a highroad to death. Even in its mildest form, it implied cold, unbroken solitude, torture, starvation, and often poison. Gerard felt he was in the hands of an enemy. "Oh, the look that man gave me on the road to Rotterdam," he thought, kneeling to commend his soul to God. "There is more here than my father's wrath. I doubt I shall see no more the light of day."

Rising, he sprang at the iron bar of the window, clutching it to press his knees against the wall and look out. For a minute, he saw a sight only a captive could appreciate: Martin Wittenhaagen's back. Martin sat quietly fishing in the brook near the Stadthouse. Gerard whistled, and Martin instantly ceased fishing, turning to see the prisoner. He made a signal, took up his line and bow, and quickly departed. Gerard realized his friends were not idle, yet he preferred Martin had stayed; the sight of him was a comfort. Holding on, he watched the soldier's retiring form until he could no longer see, then fell back and wrenched the rusty iron bar, held only by rusty nails, away from the stone-work.

Just then, Ghysbrecht Van Swieten opened the door stealthily. The burgomaster's eye fell instantly on the displaced iron, then glanced at the window, but he said nothing. The window was a hundred feet from the ground. If Gerard fancied jumping, why should he balk it? The burgomaster brought a brown loaf and a pitcher of water, setting them on the chest in solemn silence. Gerard's first impulse was to brain him with the iron bar and flee down the stairs, but the burgomaster, sensing something wicked in his eye, coughed. Three stout fellows, armed, appeared at the door.

"My orders are to keep you thus until you shall bind yourself by an oath to leave Margaret Brandt, and return to the Church, to which you have belonged from your cradle."

"Death sooner."

"With all my heart." And the burgomaster retired.

Martin hastened to Sevenbergen, finding Margaret pale and agitated but full of resolution. She was finishing a letter to the Countess Charolois, appealing against the violence and treachery of Ghysbrecht.

"Courage!" cried Martin on entering. "I have found him. He is in the haunted tower, right at the top of it. Ay, I know the place: many a poor fellow has gone up there straight, and come down feet foremost."

He recounted how he had looked up and seen Gerard's face at a window like a slit in the wall.

"Oh, Martin! how did he look?"

"What mean you? He looked like Gerard Eliassoen."

"But was he pale?"

"A little."

"Looked he anxious? Looked he like one doomed?"

"Nay, nay; as bright as a pewter pot."

"You mock me. Stay! then that must have been at sight of you. He counts on us. Oh, what shall we do? Martin, good friend, take this at once to Rotterdam."

Martin held out his hand for the letter. Peter, who had sat silent, pondering keenly, spoke up. "Put not your trust in princes."

"Alas! what else have we to trust in?"

"Knowledge."

"Well-a-day, father! your learning will not serve us here."

"How know you that? Wit has been too strong for iron bars ere to-day."

"Ay, father; but nature is stronger than wit, and she is against us. Think of the height! No ladder in Holland might reach him."

"I need no ladder; what I need is a gold crown."

"Nay, I have money, for that matter. I have nine angels. Gerard gave them me to keep; but what do they avail? The burgomaster will not be bribed to let Gerard free."

"What do they avail? Give me but one crown, and the young man shall sup with us this night."

Peter spoke so eagerly and confidently that Margaret felt a fleeting hope, but she caught Martin's eye, which dwelt on Peter with benevolent contempt.

"It passes the powers of man's invention," she sighed deeply.

"Invention!" cried the old man. "A fig for invention. What need we invention at this time of day? Everything has been said that is to be said, and done that ever will be done. I shall tell you how a Florentine knight was shut up in a tower higher than Gerard's; yet did his faithful squire stand at the tower foot and get him out, with no other engine than that in your hand, Martin, and certain kickshaws I shall buy for a crown."

Martin looked at his bow, turning it in his hand as if interrogating it, but remained as incredulous as before. Peter then told his story of the faithful squire rescuing a knight from a high tower at Brescia. The maneuver, like most truly scientific things, was so simple that their wonder was they had taken for impossible what was not even difficult.

The letter never went to Rotterdam. They trusted to Peter's learning and their own dexterity.

It was nine o'clock on a clear moonlight night. Gerard, senior, was still away; the rest of his family had been abed. A figure stood by the dwarf's bed, white in the moonlight. With an unearthly noise, between a yell and a snarl, the gymnast rolled off his bed and under it by a single unbroken movement. A soft voice followed him.

"Why, Giles, are you afeard of me?"

Giles's head peeped cautiously up, revealing it was only his sister Kate. She put her finger to her lips. "Hush! lest the wicked Cornelis or the wicked Sybrandt hear us." Giles's claws seized the side of the bed, and he returned to his place by one undivided gymnastic.

Kate revealed that she had heard Cornelis and Sybrandt mention Gerard's name. Being in great anxiety at his absence, she had listened at their door and made a fearful discovery. Gerard was in prison, in the haunted tower of the Stadthouse, by their father's authority. But there must be treachery; how could their father order such a cruel act when he was in Rotterdam? She entreated Giles to accompany her to the foot of the haunted tower to comfort Gerard and let him know their father was absent and would surely release him upon his return.

"Dear Giles, I would go alone, but I am afeard of the spirits that men say do haunt the tower; but with you I shall not be afeard."

"Nor I with you," said Giles. "I don't believe there are any spirits in Tergou. I never saw one. This last was the likest one ever I saw; and it was but you, Kate, after all."

In less than half an hour, Giles and Kate opened the house door cautiously and issued forth. She made him carry a lantern, though the night was bright. "The lantern gives me more courage against the evil spirits," she said.

The first day of imprisonment is very trying, especially when the horror of captivity is compounded by the horror of utter solitude. In their own day, many persons committed suicide during the first twenty-four hours of solitary confinement. This was doubtless why their Jairi abstained so carefully from the impertinence of watching their little experiment upon the human soul at that particular stage.

As the sun declined, Gerard's heart sank; with the waning light, the embers of hope went out. He was faint with hunger, afraid to eat the food Ghysbrecht had brought him, for hunger alone cowards men. He sat upon the chest, arms and head drooping, a picture of despondency. Suddenly, something struck the wall beyond him sharply, then rattled on the floor at his feet. It was an arrow; he saw the white feather. A chill ran through him—they meant to assassinate him from the outside. He crouched. No more missiles came. Crawling on all fours, he took up the arrow; there was no head to it. He uttered a cry of hope: had a friendly hand shot it? He examined it and found a soft substance attached. His eccentricity proved useful; his tinder-box enabled him to strike a light. It revealed two things that made his heart bound with delight. Attached to the arrow was a skein of silk, and on the arrow itself were words written.

*Well beloved, make fast the silk to thy knife and lower to us: but hold thine end fast: then count an

The hatred burning in the burgomaster's gaze, sealed by the cold bars of his prison, had convinced Gerard that Ghysbrecht was his mortal foe. He moved silently around the tower's corner, expecting not a miracle, but the cruel trap of a villain plotting his doom in a cell from which escape would remain a secret.

As he stepped into the shadows, a soft yet firm hand slipped into his. Margaret stood beside him, sharing the peril.

Before they could speak, the haunted tower loomed ahead, and a sight froze them in place. Halfway up the stone wall, a creature with a head ablaze like a giant glowworm climbed steadily upward. Its body was dark, outlined only by the eerie radiance of its fiery crown, stretching nearly four feet in length. At the tower's base stood a figure in white, indistinguishable from a living woman. Gerard and Margaret trembled with awe.

"The rope!" Gerard gasped. "It is ascending the rope!"

As they watched, the glowing entity vanished into the prison window, its light flooding the cell and turning the glass a deep crimson. The white figure remained motionless below.

Those who survive the initial shock of the supernatural often succumb to a terrifying impulse: a wild urge to rush toward the horror, just as a bird might fly into a snake. The great tragedian Macready once portrayed this perfectly in *Macbeth*, when Banquo's ghost appeared; he threw himself at the shadow with his head averted. That same magnetic pull seized Margaret. She released Gerard's hand, standing bewildered for a heartbeat before letting out a wild cry and darting toward the specter. Gerard, unaware of this natural instinct, believed the devil was dragging her to ruin. He fell to his knees.

"Exorcizo vos! In the name of the Blessed Mary, I exorcise you!" he shrieked, his voice cracking with terror.

To his immense relief, the specter emitted a feeble cry of fear. Discovering that even hell possessed weaknesses was encouraging. Gerard redoubled his prayers, and soon the ghastly shape knelt at Margaret's feet, begging piteously for mercy.

Soon, Kate and Giles arrived at the tower. Their surprise was palpable upon finding a new rope dangling from the prisoner's window to the ground.

"I see how it is," the dwarf declared, accepting facts as they came. "Our Gerard has descended this rope. He is free. I shall ascend and verify."

"No, Giles, no!" the superior intelligence cried, blinded by prejudice. "Do you not see this is glamour? This rope is a line cast by the evil one to lure you to destruction. He knows the weaknesses of every heart; he sees how fond you are of climbing. Where would Gerard procure such a rope? How could he fasten it in the sky? It defies nature. Holy saints protect us tonight, for hell is abroad."

"Stuff!" the dwarf retorted. "The way to hell is down, and this rope leads up. I have never had the luck to climb such a long rope. It may be years before I encounter one so prepared. I would rather be knocked on the head now than wait forever without happiness."

With a cry of delight, he sprang onto the rope, much like a cat leaping for a table laden with fish. Every muscle in his frame ignited with energy. The only concession Kate could win was permission to fasten a lantern around his neck first.

"A light frightens ill spirits," she explained.

Thus, with his massive arms and legs moving with feather-light agility, Giles ascended faster than his brother had descended. The lantern at the nape of his neck transformed him into a living glowworm. His sister watched his progress with trembling anxiety. Suddenly, a female figure burst from the solid masonry, flying at her with supernatural speed.

Kate let out a weak cry, the only sound her terror allowed, as her tongue seemed to stick to her palate. She dropped her crutches and sank to her knees, hiding her face and moaning, "Take my body, but spare my soul!"

Margaret, panting, exclaimed, "Why, it is a woman!"

Kate, quivering, echoed, "Why, it is a woman!"

Margaret turned, startled. "How you scared me!"

Kate, still shaking, replied, "I am scared enough myself. Oh! oh! oh!"

"This is strange!" Margaret stammered. "But the fiery-headed thing? It was with you, and you are harmless! Why are you here at this hour?"

"Nay, why are YOU here?"

"Perhaps we are on the same errand? Ah! You are his good sister, Kate!"

"And you are Margaret Brandt."

"Yes."

"All the better. You love him; you are here. Then Giles was right. He has won his freedom."

Gerard stepped forward to confirm the truth, but their reunion was cut short by a horrible, unearthly noise, sounding like a sepulcher speaking through a ventriloquist's dummy:

With each repetition, the volume rose. They looked up to see the dwarf, his hands full of parchments, his face lit with fiendish joy and flushed with diabolical fire. The lantern at his neck made him appear as an infernal transparency. As he spoke, the awful imp hurled documents at the astonished figures below. Records rained down like wounded wild ducks; some collapsed instantly, others fluttered helplessly, while others spread out and wheeled slowly in airy circles. Hardly had they settled when the sepulchral roar returned—"Parchment!—parchment!"—and another flock of documents pattered and sailed down, whitening the grass. Finally, the fire-headed imp, with his light body and horny hands, slid down the rope like a falling star. Putting business before sentiment, he proposed an immediate settlement for the merchandise he had just delivered.

"Hush!" Gerard commanded. "You speak too loudly. Gather them up and follow us to a safer place."

"Will you come home with me, Gerard?" asked little Kate.

"I have no home."

"You shall not say so. Who is more welcome than you, after this cruel wrong, to your father's house?"

"Father! I have no father," Gerard said sternly. "He who was my father has become my gaoler. I have escaped his hands; I will never come within their reach again."

"An enemy did this, not our father," Kate insisted, recounting what she had overheard Cornelis and Sybrandt say. But the injury was too fresh to be soothed. Gerard displayed a bitterness of indignation he had previously seemed incapable of.

"Cornelis and Sybrandt are two ill curs that have shown me their teeth and their heart for a long while, but they could do no more. My father gave the burgomaster authority, or he would not have dared lay a finger on me, a free burgher of this town. So be it. I was his son; I am his prisoner. He has played his part; I shall play mine. Farewell to the burgh where I was born, lived honestly, and was imprisoned. While there is another town left in creation, I will never trouble you again, Tergou."

"Oh! Gerard! Gerard!"

Margaret whispered, "Do not gainsay him now. Give his anger time to cool!"

Kate turned quickly to her. "Let me look at your face?" The inspection seemed favorable, for she whispered, "It is a comely face, and no mischief-maker's."

"Fear me not," Margaret said in the same gentle tone. "I could not be happy without your love, as well as Gerard's."

"These are comfortable words," Kate sobbed. Then, looking up, she added, "I little thought to like you so well. My heart is willing, but my infirmity will not let me embrace you."

At this hint, Margaret gently wound her arms around Gerard's sister and kissed her lovingly.

"Often he has spoken of you to me, Kate; and often I longed for this."

"You, too, Gerard," Kate said. "Kiss me ere you go; for my heart lies heavy at parting with you this night."

Gerard kissed her, and she proceeded on her crutches toward home. The last thing they heard of her was a little, patient sigh. Then tears welled thick in Margaret's eyes, though Gerard, being a man, noticed neither the sigh nor the tears.

As they turned toward Sevenbergen, the dwarf nudged Gerard with his bundle of parchments and held out a concave claw.

Margaret dissuaded him. "Why take what is not ours?"

"Oh, spoil an enemy how you can."

"But may they not make this a handle for fresh violence?"

"How can they? Think you I shall stay in Tergou after this? The burgomaster robbed me of my liberty; I doubt I should take his life for it, if I could."

"Oh, fie! Gerard."

"What! Is life worth more than liberty? Well, I cannot take his life, so I take the first thing that comes to hand."

He gave Giles a few small coins, with which the urchin was gladdened, and shuffled after his sister. Margaret and Gerard were soon joined by Martin, and they departed for Sevenbergen.

The key to Gerard's prison lay heavy in Ghysbrecht Van Swieten's pouch, a cold weight against his hip. He waited until the clock struck ten, calculating the silence of the morning. "A little hunger sometimes does well; it breaks 'em," he muttered to the empty air, his voice rasping like dry leaves. At the appointed hour, he crept up the stairs, a loaf of bread and a pitcher of water tucked under his arm, trailed by his servant, Dierich Brower, who moved with the silent, armed readiness of a wolf guarding its kill. Ghysbrecht paused at the door, listening to the stillness within. A grim smile twisted his features. "By this time, he will be as down-hearted as Albert Koestein was," he thought, a cruel satisfaction warming his chest. He turned the lock and pushed the door open.

There was no sign of Gerard.

Ghysbrecht stood frozen, the blood draining from his face until he looked as though he might faint. Though his visage remained hidden in shadow, his entire body seemed to lose its vitality, stiffening into a statue of shock. Moments later, he collapsed, trembling violently against the wall. Behind him, Dierich sensed the shift in his master's demeanor and stepped forward, peering over the old man's shoulder. The sight of the empty cell, the discarded rope, and the iron bar hanging loose sent a cry of wonder through the servant's throat. His astonishment doubled when he saw his master disregard everything else, throwing himself to his knees before the empty chest. With quivering hands, Ghysbrecht frantically patted the wood, as if he feared the truth might vanish if he stopped touching it.

Dierich stared at him in utter bewilderment. "Why, master, what is the matter?"

Ghysbrecht's pale lips moved, forming words that never escaped his throat. His hands fell limp at his sides, and he fixed his gaze on the hollow space where the records should have been. "Why, master, what avails glaring into that empty box? The lad is not there. See here! Note the cunning of the young rogue; he hath taken out the bar—"

"GONE! GONE! GONE!" Ghysbrecht shrieked, the sound tearing from his throat.

"Gone! What is gone, Holy saints! Is he planet-struck?" Dierich stammered, panic rising in his voice.

"STOP THIEF!" Ghysbrecht roared, suddenly turning on his servant with the fury of a cornered beast. He grabbed Dierich by the collar, shaking him with a strength that belied his age and infirmity. "Do you stand there, knave, and watch your master robbed? Run! Fly! A hundred crowns to him who finds them for me again. No, no! 'Tis in vain. Oh, fool! Foolish fool to leave that in the same room with him! But none ever found the secret spring before. None ever would but he. It was to be. It is to be. Lost! Lost!" The frenzy burned bright and brief, only to be extinguished instantly by the crushing weight of his years. He sank back onto the chest, muttering the word over and over, "Lost! Lost!"

"What is lost, master?" Dierich asked, his voice softening with kindness.

"House and lands and good name," Ghysbrecht groaned, wringing his hands until they were red and feeble.

"WHAT?" Dierich cried, the word exploding in the quiet room.

That emphatic tone, laced with eager curiosity, struck a chord in Ghysbrecht's mind and revived his natural cunning. "I have lost the town records," he stammered, looking askance at the servant like a fox caught near a hen-roost.

"Oh, is that all?"

"Is't not enough? What will the burghers say to me? What will the burghs do?" Ghysbrecht's composure shattered again. "A hundred crowns to him who shall recover them; all, mind, all that were in this box. If one be missing, I give nothing."

"'Tis a bargain, master: the hundred crowns are in my pouch. See you not that where Gerard Eliassoen is, there are the pieces of sheepskin you rate so highly?"

"That is true; that is true, good Dierich: good faithful Dierich. All, mind, all that were in the chest."

"Master, I will take the constables to Gerard's house, and seize him for the theft."

"The theft? Ay! Good; very good. It is theft. I forgot that. So, as he is a thief now, we will put him in the dungeons below, where the toads are and the rats. Dierich, that man must never see daylight again. 'Tis his own fault; he must be prying. Quick, quick! Ere he has time to talk, you know, time to talk."

Within half an hour, Dierich Brower and four constables burst into the hosier's house, demanding young Gerard from the panic-stricken Catherine.

"Alas! What has he done now?" she cried, her voice trembling. "That boy will break my heart."

"Nay, dame, but a trick of youth," Dierich replied smoothly, masking his inner turmoil. "He hath but made off with certain skins of parchment, in a frolic doubtless. But the burgomaster is answerable to the burgh for their safekeeping, so he is in care about them; as for the youth, he will doubtless be quit for a reprimand."

This polished speech completely deceived Catherine, but her daughter, Kate, remained suspicious. Her doubts were sharpened by the disproportionate anger and disappointment Dierich displayed the moment he learned Gerard was not at home, nor had he been seen that night.

"Come away then," Dierich said roughly, dismissing the woman. "We are wasting time." He added vehemently, "I'll find him if he is above ground."

Affiction sharpens the wits, and often it has made an innocent person more than a match for the wily. As Dierich prepared to leave, Kate made a signal for him to speak with her privately. He bade his men wait outside the door and joined her.

"Hush!" she whispered. "My mother knows not. Gerard has left Tergou."

"How?"

"I saw him last night."

"Ay! Where?" Dierich cried, his interest piqued despite his frustration.

"At the foot of the haunted tower."

"How did he get the rope?"

"I know not; but this I know: my brother Gerard bade me there farewell, and he is many leagues from Tergou ere this. The town, you know, was always unworthy of him, and when it imprisoned him, he vowed never to set foot in it again. Let the burgomaster be content, then. He has imprisoned him, and he has driven him from his birthplace and from his native land. What need now to rob him and us of our good name?"

At another moment, this might have struck Dierich as sound logic; but he was too mortified by Gerard's escape and the potential loss of a hundred crowns to listen to reason.

"What need had he to steal?" he retorted bitterly.

"Gerard stole not the trash; he but took it to spite the burgomaster, who stole his liberty; but he shall answer to the Duke for it, he shall. As for these skins of parchment you keep such a coil about, look in the nearest brook or stye, and 'tis odds but you find them."

"Think ye so, mistress?—think ye so?" Dierich's eyes flashed with sudden suspicion. "Mayhap you know 'tis so."

"This I know, that Gerard is too good to steal, and too wise to load himself with rubbish, going a journey."

"Give you good day, then," Dierich said sharply. "The sheepskin you scorn, I value it more than the skin of any in Tergou."

With that, he hurried away on a false scent, leaving the house to its own devices.

Kate returned inside and drew Giles aside. "Giles, my heart misgives me; breathe not to a soul what I say to you. I have told Dirk Brower that Gerard is out of Holland, but much I doubt he is not a league from Tergou."

"Why, where is he, then?"

"Where should he be, but with her he loves? But if so, he must not loiter. These be deep and dark and wicked men that seek him. Giles, I see that in Dirk Brower's eye makes me tremble. Oh, why cannot I fly to Sevenbergen and bid him away? Why am I not lusty and active like other girls? God forgive me for fretting at His will; but I never felt till now what it is to be lame and weak and useless. But you are strong, dear Giles," she added coaxingly, her eyes pleading. "You are very strong."

"Yes, I am strong," thundered Perpusillus, then, catching sight of her meaning, "but I hate to go on foot," he added sulkily.

"Alas! Alas! Who will help me if you will not? Dear Giles, do you not love Gerard?"

"Yes, I like him best of the lot. I'll go to Sevenbergen on Peter Buyskens his mule. Ask you him, for he won't lend her me."

Kate remonstrated. The whole town would follow him. It would be known whither he was gone, and Gerard would be in worse danger than before.

Giles parried this by promising to ride out of the town the opposite way, and not turn the mule's head towards Sevenbergen until he had rid himself of the curious. Kate then assented and borrowed the mule. She charged Giles with a short but meaning message, and made him repeat it after her over and over, until he could say it word for word.

Giles mounted the mule, and little Kate retired, doing the last thing now in her power for her beloved brother—praying on her knees long and earnestly for his safety.

Gerard and Margaret hastened to Sevenbergen, buoyed by the fresh air of regained freedom and the thrill of their recent escape. Yet, this initial exhilaration quickly curdled into a bitter melancholy. Gerard was a fugitive, a man whose shadow hung over him, liable at any moment to be recaptured and punished. Consequently, separation became inevitable. Furthermore, a deep-seated loathing for his homeland had taken root in Gerard's heart. Margaret urged him to flee the country, yet the mere thought of him traveling to Italy caused her spirit to falter. Conversely, Gerard's willingness to leave her was tethered solely to his burning desire to visit Italy; he was convinced that there he could secure wealth and renown, clearing the path for their union. He recounted to her the warnings of the demoiselle Van Eyck, reminding her that the gold pieces were merely a seed fund for that specific journey. As a craftsman and artist stifled in this crude, unappreciative land, he believed Italy alone could recognize his worth. Moved by this selfless logic, the gentle girl finally consented, though her agreement was bought with a flood of tender tears. In turn, young and loving as she was, Gerard wept bitterly alongside her. Often, they clung to one another, bewildered by the conspiracy of fate that seemed to unite so many enemies against them, asking in despair what crime they had committed to deserve such torment.

Hand in hand, they sat until the clock struck midnight, oscillating between lamenting their cruel destiny and painting vivid, hopeful visions of a future together. In these moments, Margaret's sorrow would suddenly overwhelm her, and poor Gerard's eloquent pleas would dissolve into helpless sighs. By morning, they had resigned themselves to parting, yet neither possessed the courage to name the hour; indeed, it is doubtful whether the moment of separation would ever truly have arrived.

However, around three in the afternoon, Giles rode up to the door, having circled many miles to avoid detection. Both lovers rushed out, driven by urgent curiosity.

"Brother Gerard!" Giles cried out in his booming voice. "Kate commands you to run for your life! They accuse you of theft; you have handed them a weapon. Do not attempt to explain. Seek no justice in Tergou. The parchments you seized were merely a ruse. She has seen the verdict in the men's eyes; a price is on your head. Fly! For Margaret's sake, and for all who love you, do not waste another second of life, but flee!"

It was a thunderclap that left two pale faces staring in stunned silence at the terrifying messenger.

Then, Giles, who had hitherto spoken only as a mouthpiece for Catherine, added a word of his own volition. "All the constables were at our dwelling searching for you, and so was Dirk Brower. Kate is wise, Gerard. Listen to her counsel and fly!"

"Oh, yes, Gerard!" Margaret shrieked wildly. "Flee at once! Ah! Those parchments; my instincts warned me: why did I allow you to take them?"

"Margaret, they are but a blind," Gerard insisted, echoing Giles. "It matters not. That old villain shall never see them again. I will not depart until I have hidden his treasure where he can never find it." After thanking Giles warmly, he bid him farewell, instructing him to return and tell Kate that he was gone. "For I shall be gone before you reach home," he declared. He then shouted for Martin, explaining the peril and begging him to move a short distance toward Tergou and watch the road.

"Aye!" Martin agreed. "Should I spot Dirk Brower or any of his men, I will shoot an arrow into the oak-tree in our garden. Upon seeing that signal, you must dash into the nearby forest and meet me at the Weird Hunter's Spring. There, I will guide you through the woods."

With this contingency arranged, Gerard breathed a sigh of relief. He accompanied Margaret, and while she trembled, watching the oak-tree with every fiber of her being, fearing an arrow might strike its branches, Gerard began to dig a deep hole. His purpose was to bury the parchments.

He tossed them in one by one. Most were charters and records of the burgh, but one appeared to be a private deed between Floris Brandt, father of Peter, and Ghysbrecht.

"Why, this belongs to you just as much as to him," Gerard observed. "I will read this."

"Oh, not now, Gerard, not now!" Margaret cried out. "Every moment you lose fills me with dread. Look! Large drops of rain are beginning to fall, and the clouds are lowering."

Gerard yielded to her plea, yet he slipped the deed into his bosom before throwing earth over the others and stamping it down firmly. Just then, a flash of lightning illuminated the sky, followed by a roll of distant thunder, and the rain descended heavily. Margaret and Gerard fled into the house, swiftly followed by Martin.

"The road is clear," Martin reported, "and a violent storm is approaching."

His words proved true. The thunder grew louder, crashing overhead, while the lightning stroked the heavens like a whip, and the rain poured in torrents. Margaret covered her face, refusing to witness the blinding flashes. Seizing the opportunity, Gerard raised the rough shutter and lit a candle. The lovers conferred quietly, and Gerard blessed the tempest that granted them a few more hours of solitude with Margaret. The sun set unnoticed amidst the chaos, yet the thunder continued to boom and the lightning to flash as the rain lashed down. Supper was laid out, but neither could eat; the thought that this might be their final meal together choked them.

As the storm began to subside slightly, Peter retired to rest. Gerard, however, was to depart at the break of dawn, and neither he nor Margaret could afford to lose an hour to sleep. Martin remained seated for a while, carefully fitting a new string to his bow, a task he performed with meticulous care. The lovers murmured their sorrows and their undying love beside him.

Suddenly, the old man raised his hand, signaling for silence.

They stilled and listened, hearing nothing at first. But the next moment, a footstep crackled faintly upon the autumn leaves scattered across the garden behind the house. To those without fear, such a sound would be insignificant; to those hunted by enemies, it was a harbinger of doom. It was a foot attempting to be silent.

Martin nocked an arrow and hastily extinguished the candle. In that same instant, to their horror, they heard more than one footstep approaching the other door of the cottage. These steps were less stealthy than the first, yet still very cautious, followed by a deadly pause.

Their blood turned to ice in their veins.

"Oh, Kate, oh, Kate! You said to fly on the instant!" Margaret moaned, wringing her hands in anguish, terror, and wild remorse for having delayed Gerard's departure.

"Hush, girl!" Martin whispered sternly.

A heavy knock fell upon the door.

And upon the hearts within.

The back door crashed open with a violence that echoed through the quiet house, signaling the end of their sanctuary. In an instant, the room was hemmed in by the threat of intrusion. Yet, amidst the chaos, Margaret found a sudden reserve of composure. With a whisper that cut through the panic, she commanded, "Tell them he was here, but is gone." Seizing Gerard by the arm, she nearly dragged him up the rough wooden steps leading to her father's chamber, her own room lying just beyond.

The pounding on the door resumed, relentless and demanding.

"Who dares knock at this hour?" a voice called from within.

"Open, or you shall answer for it!"

"I will not open to thieves; honest men are all asleep."

"Open to the law, Martin Wittenhaagen, or you shall rue the day!"

"Why, that is Dirk Brower's voice," Martin muttered, recognizing the tone. "What brings you so far from Tergou?"

"Open, and you will know."

Slowly, deliberately, Martin drew the bolt. The door swung inward, and Dierich and four burly companions surged in, letting in the man who had entered through the rear.

"Now, Martin, where is Gerard Eliassoen?" Dierich demanded, his eyes scanning the room.

"Gerard Eliassoen? Why, he was here but moments ago!" Martin replied, his voice steady despite the tension.

"Was he here?" Dierich's face fell. "And where is he now?"

"They say he has fled to Italy. What is to be done?"

"No matter. When did he leave? Do not tell me he vanished in such a storm!"

A sneer curled Martin's lips. "There is a fuss about Gerard Eliassoen," he said with mock contempt. He lit a candle and sat calmly by the fire, beginning to wind fine silk around his bowstring where the arrow's notch had worn it thin. "Let me explain," he said casually. "Do you know his brother Giles? A little misbegotten imp, all head and arms? Well, he tore over here on a mule, screaming something I could not hear from my distance, only the noise. It startled Gerard. Immediately after he departed, there was much crying and kissing, and then Gerard left. They tell me he has gone to Italy—perhaps you know where that is, for I do not."

Dierich's expression darkened further at this account. There was no flaw in Martin's tale; a more cunning man might have lied too freely and raised suspicion, but Martin had done his task perfectly. He had spoken only the single falsehood he was instructed to utter, inventing nothing else.

"Folks," Dierich said, his voice grim, "I fear he speaks the truth. I warned the burgomaster of this very possibility. He met the dwarf galloping Peter Buyskens's mule from Sevenbergen. 'They have sent that imp to Gerard,' he said, 'so Gerard must be at Sevenbergen.' 'Ah, master!' I replied, 'it is too late now. We should have thought of Sevenbergen before wasting our time hunting the odd corners of Tergou for those cursed parchments we shall never find until we find the man who took them. If he was at Sevenbergen, and they sent the dwarf to him, it must have been to warn him we are after him. He is leagues away by now,' I told him. Confound that chalk-faced girl! She has outwitted us bearded men. So I told the burgomaster, but he would not hear reason. A wet jerkin apiece is all we shall get from this job."

Martin grinned coolly in response to Dierich's frustration.

"However," Dierich added, "to satisfy the burgomaster, we must search the house."

Martin's demeanor shifted instantly to grave seriousness. Dierich noted the change immediately. After a moment's reflection, he ordered, "Post two of you outside, one on each side of the house, to ensure no one jumps from the upper windows. The rest come with me."

Taking the candle, Dierich ascended the stairs, followed by three of his comrades, leaving Martin alone in the hallway.

The stout soldier hung his head, the initial success of their mission now seeming like a cruel illusion. Suddenly, a spark of hope ignited within him. Gerard must be in Peter's room or Margaret's; neither was high above the ground. Gerard would leap out. Dierich had left a guard below, but what then? For a fleeting half-minute, Gerard and Martin would be two against one, and in that brief span, anything might happen.

Martin held the back door ajar and watched. Light spilled from Peter's room. "Curse the fool!" he muttered. "Is he going to let them take him like a girl?"

The light then shifted to Margaret's bedroom. Still, no window was open. If Gerard had intended to escape that way, he would not have waited until the men were inside. Martin realized this immediately and left the door, descending to the foot of the stairs to listen.

Silence stretched, growing heavier with every passing second. He began to believe Gerard must have slipped out the window while the men occupied the rooms. But this conviction was suddenly and rudely shattered.

Faint cries drifted from the inner bedroom—Margaret's.

"They have taken him," Martin groaned. "They have caught him."

It flashed across his mind that if they removed Gerard, his life would be forfeit, and if harm came to him, Margaret's heart would break. Casting his eyes wildly around the room like a savage beast seeking an exit, he formed a resolution characteristic of those iron times and a soldier driven to the brink. He stepped to each door in turn, mimicking Dierich's voice sharply, "Watch the window!" Then, quietly, he closed and bolted both doors. He picked up his bow and six arrows, fitting one to the string while stowing the others in his quiver. He placed his knife on a chair behind him, hilt facing him, and waited at the foot of the stairs with a calm determination to slay those four men or be slain by them. He knew he could dispatch two with his arrows before they could reach him, leaving Gerard and himself to fight hand-to-hand with the remaining pair. Besides, he had seen men panic-stricken by such a sudden assault. Should Brower and his men hesitate even an instant before closing with him, Martin would shoot three instead of two, tipping the odds in their favor.

He need not wait long. Heavy footsteps echoed in Margaret's room, growing closer. The light advanced, accompanied by voices.

Martin's heart, though stout, hammered hard at the sound of men marching toward their deaths—and perhaps his own. Four against one in a battlefield of ten feet square was long odds, especially if Gerard was bound and powerless to aid. Yet this man, who had once trembled in his shoes at the sight of a lantern, never wavered in this awful moment of real danger. He stood there, his body braced for combat, his eye glowing, equally ready to take life and lose it. It was a desperate gamble; to win meant instant and lifelong exile, while to lose meant death upon that very floor.

Dierich Brower and his men found Peter in the first stages of sleep. They ransacked his cupboards, drove their knives into an alligator skin nailed to his wall, and searched beneath his bed. It was a large room, seemingly full of hiding places, yet Gerard was nowhere to be found.

Next, they moved to Margaret's room. The sight of it was discouraging—it was small, bare, and devoid of cupboards. However, there was a large fireplace and chimney. Dierich's gaze fell immediately upon these. There, sleeping on an old chest not a foot high, with no attempt to cover it, lay the beauty of Sevenbergen. The sheets were snowy white, as was Margaret's own linen. She lay there, looking like a lily fallen into a rut.

Soon, she awoke, sitting up in bed in amazement. Upon seeing the men, she began to scream faintly, praying for mercy.

Her presence made Dierich Brower ashamed of his errand.

"This is a to-do," he said, slightly confused. "We are not going to hurt you, my pretty maid. Lie still, shut your eyes, and think of your wedding-night while I look up this chimney to see if Master Gerard is there."

"Gerard! In my room?"

"Why not? They say that you and he—"

"Cruel! You know they have driven him away from me—driven him from his native place. This is a blind. You are thieves; you are wicked men; you are not men of Sevenbergen, or you would know Margaret Brandt better than to look for her lover in this room of all others in the world. Oh, brave! Four great hulking men, armed to the teeth, to insult one poor honest girl! The women that live in your own houses must be naught, or you would respect them too much to insult a girl of good character."

"There! Come away, before we hear worse," Dierich said hastily. "He is not in the chimney. Plaster will mend what a cudgel breaks, but a woman's tongue is a double-edged dagger, and a girl is a woman with her mother's milk still in her." He beat a hasty retreat. "I told the burgomaster how 'twould be."

Where is the woman who cannot play the part required of her? Where is she who refuses to deceive, even for the sake of the man she adores? Yet nature, in moments of supreme crisis, often grants the simplest of women the wit to cast dust into Solomon's eyes. As soon as the men had withdrawn, Margaret rose from her bed. She stripped off her nightgown, leaving only her skirt, petticoat, and stockings, then dragged the heavy chest—her makeshift bed—against the wall. She paused at the threshold, listening intently. The heavy tread of boots faded through her father's chamber and down the stone stairs until silence reclaimed the house.

Inside the chest lay a secret known only to her. To a stranger, the room appeared sound and solid, but Gerard, pressed for time and materials, had ingeniously cut a section from the floorboards to accommodate her hiding place, seamlessly repairing the gap above. In truth, the chest rested on the rafters a foot beneath the surface, making it nearly two feet deep despite its deceptive shallowness.

Quiet settled over the room. Margaret knelt, offering a silent prayer of gratitude to Heaven. Rising, she leaned over the lid and whispered softly, "Gerard!"

There was no answer.

She increased her volume slightly. "Gerard, all is safe. Thank God! You may rise, but be cautious!"

Silence.

She placed her hand upon his shoulder. "Gerard!"

Still nothing.

"Oh, what is this?" she cried, her fingers flying wildly across his face and chest. She gripped his shoulders, shook him, and lifted him, but he slipped from her trembling grasp, collapsing back not as a living man, but as a lifeless weight. A cold dread seized her. The lid had been lowered; she had lain upon him. The intruders had been in the room for some time. With a frenzy born of sheer terror, she tore him from the chest and carried him to the window. She threw it wide, letting the cool night air rush in. She laid him there, bathed in the silver moonlight. His face was the color of ashes; his limbs were limp and utterly still. She pressed her hand to his heart. Horror! It was as motionless as the rest of him. The horror of horrors: she had suffocated him with her own body.

The human mind cannot instantly accept such a sudden, terrible calamity. Gerard, who had barely crawled into that chest five minutes prior, how could he be dead?

She called upon every endearing name her heart could conjure. She kissed him, caressed him, and implored him to speak.

No response to words of love she had never dared utter before. Trembling violently, she began to babble foolish, terrible things over his ashen countenance.

"Oh, Gerard! I am so sorry you are dead. I am so sorry I have killed you. Forgive me for not letting the men take you; it would have been better than this. Oh, Gerard! I am very, very sorry for what I have done."

Then, suddenly, she began to rave.

"No! No! Such things cannot be, or there is no God. It is monstrous! How can my Gerard be dead? How can I have killed my Gerard? I love him. Oh, God, you know how I love him. He does not. I never told him. If he knew my heart, he would speak to me; he would not be so deaf to his poor Margaret. It must be a trick to make me scream and betray him! But no! I love him too well for that. I'll choke myself first." She clutched her own throat, stifling the wild urge to shriek in her agony.

"If he would but say one word. Oh, Gerard! Don't die without a word. Have mercy on me and scold me, but speak to me! If you are angry with me, curse me! I deserve it—the idiot who killed the man she loved better than herself. Ah, I am a murderess. The worst in all the world. Help! Help! I have murdered him. Ah! Ah! Ah! Ah! Ah!"

She tore at her hair, launching shriek after shriek so piercing and wild that they rang like a funeral knell in the darkened house. Dierich Brower and his men, alerted by the noise, sprang to their feet, staring at one another in confusion and alarm.

Martin Wittenhaagen stood frozen at the base of the stairwell, his bowstring pulled taut against his cheek, the arrow nocked and trembling near the fletching. Behind him, the heavy blade of his knife rested against his thigh. A profound shock seized him as the returning men shuffled past without Gerard. Confusion rippled through his features; he lowered the weapon, his mouth agape, staring at them as if they were ghosts. The men, in turn, halted, their faces etched with bewilderment at the sight of the old giant poised like a hunter about to strike.

"Why, mates," one of them chuckled nervously, "was the old fellow preparing to loose an arrow at us?"

"Silence!" Martin barked, forcing his stony demeanor back into place. "I was merely testing the tension of this new string. Behold!" He reached for the bowstring to loosen it, offering a display of feigned innocence.

"Hmph," grunted Dierich, his eyes narrowing with suspicion. "There is more to you than meets the eye, old man. Put a log upon the fire and let us warm our frozen hides before we ascend further."

A crackling blaze soon roared to life, casting dancing shadows against the stone walls. The men huddled around the heat, stripping off their cloaks and letting the flames lick at their long, matted hair until the scent of singed wool filled the air. It was then that the shrill, piercing cries erupted from Margaret's chamber above. Every head snapped upward in alarm. One of the men snatched a candle from the hearth and bolted up the narrow staircase.

Martin surged to his feet, his heart hammering against his ribs. The sudden screams, mingled with the intruder's frantic movements, ignited a surge of protective fury. Driven by a desperate need to shield the woman within, he lunged forward to intercept the man with the light.

Dierich moved with surprising speed, wrapping his arms around Martin's broad chest from behind and shouting for the others to hold him fast. The man with the candle slipped past their grasp, but the remaining men swarmed Martin. A brutal contest ensued, a chaotic tumble of limbs and curses where the three assailants rolled across the floor, pinning the massive Samson beneath them. With ropes meant for Gerard already in hand, they bound Martin's wrists and ankles, rendering the great man utterly helpless.

Martin groaned, a low sound of anguish tearing from his throat. He watched helplessly as the intruder, having navigated the struggle, slipped into Margaret's room. Now, he was a prisoner, his strength useless against the cords.

"Aye, grind your teeth, you old rascal," Dierich panted, wiping sweat from his brow. "You shall not use them again."

"It is my conviction, mates," another man muttered, "that our very lives hung by a thread while this old brute remained unbound."

"He makes me suspect Gerard is not far behind," someone else offered.

"No such fortune," Dierich retorted sharply. "Hark! Jorian Ketel has been lingering in that girl's chamber far too long. Some of us must ascend and see to him."

The rough laughter that followed this command had barely faded when the sound of hurried footsteps echoed loudly overhead, signaling the arrival of the intruder himself.

"Oh, here he comes at last," Dierich called out, his voice dripping with mockery. "Well, Jorian, pray tell us what mischief awaits us up there?"

Jorian Ketel pressed directly to Margaret's chamber, his heart hammering against his ribs with the frantic rhythm of a man who had run through the dark streets of a city holding his breath. He expected to find chaos, perhaps the man he sought fleeing or hiding in the shadows. Instead, he found a tableau of such stillness that the candle in his hand trembled, casting dancing, ghostly shadows against the walls.

There, in the center of the room, lay Gerard. He was pale as moonlight, motionless as a fallen statue, his head cradled in Margaret's lap. She knelt over him, rigid and silent, her eyes wide and dilated yet utterly glazed, seeing nothing of the world around her, hearing nothing of the man standing in the doorway. To her, the fire, the noise, the very air of the earth had ceased to exist; there was only the white, fragile face resting in her hands.

Jorian stood transfixed, awe-struck by the sheer weight of her despair. "Where was he, then, all this time?" he whispered, the question hanging heavy in the silence.

Margaret did not answer. She was lost in a trance of grief. Jorian stepped forward, his gaze falling on the empty chest that had once held the stolen goods. A cold realization settled over him, matching the chill in the room. He looked back at the girl, her body frozen in a posture of mute agony. "This is a sorry sight," he murmured, his voice thick with pity. "A black night's work, all for a few skins. She would have been safer with us. Poor wench, she is past answering me."

He hesitated, then moved to test the boy's condition. Taking down a small, round mirror from the wall, no larger than his own palm, he pressed it gently to Gerard's lips and nostrils, holding it steady. He watched the glass intently. When he withdrew it, the surface remained dull, clouded with condensation.

"There is life in him!" Jorian shouted to himself, the words bursting forth like a release of tension.

The sound struck Margaret like a physical blow. Though she could only mutter the words, the effect was instantaneous. As if a spell had broken upon a statue, she sprang to her feet, her form filling with sudden, fierce passion. She threw her arms around Jorian's neck, clutching him with an intensity that bordered on violence.

"Oh, bless the tongue that tells me so!" she cried, pressing her face against his rough coat, clinging to him as if he were the only anchor in a drowning sea.

"Easy now," Jorian said, trying to calm her trembling frame. "Let us lay him warm." In a moment, he lifted Gerard, placing him carefully upon the bedclothes. From his belt, he drew a flask and poured a generous measure of Schiedamze into his hand, flinging the pungent liquor sharply onto Gerard's face. The strong spirit seemed to wake the boy's dormant senses; he gave a faint, ragged sigh. It was a sound so full of relief that it brought fresh tears to Jorian's eyes. Margaret rushed forward, but stopped abruptly, quivering with fear that her touch might crush the fragile spark of life she had just witnessed. She had lost all confidence in her own strength.

"That is right—leave him alone," Jorian instructed gently. "Do not cuddle him as you did me, or you will drive his breath back. Let him rest; he is sure to come. He is not an old man."

Gerard sighed deeply, and a faint streak of color returned to his lips. Jorian turned toward the door, eager to escape the scene before suspicion could take root. Hardly had he reached the threshold when his legs were seized from behind.

It was Margaret. She curled around his knees like a desperate serpent, kissing his hand and fawning upon him with a mixture of gratitude and terror. "You won't tell? You have saved his life; you have not the heart to thrust him back into his grave, to undo your own good work?"

"No, no!" Jorian reassured her, his tone firm yet kind. "It is not the first time I have done you both a good turn; I told you in the church where we were to take him. Besides, what is Dierich Brower to me? I'll see him hanged ere I'll tell him. But I wish you'd tell me where the parchments are! There are a hundred crowns offered for them. That would be a good windfall for my Joan and the children, you know."

"They shall have those hundred crowns," Margaret promised, her voice trembling but resolute.

"What! Are the things in the house?" Jorian asked eagerly.

"No; but I know where they are. By God and St. Bavon, I swear you shall have them tomorrow. Come to me for them when you will, but come alone."

"I were made else," Jorian thought, his greed momentarily outweighing his caution. "What! Share the hundred crowns with Dirk Brower? And now may my bones rot in my skin if I let a soul know the poor boy is here."

He bolted from the room, terrified that any further delay might excite suspicion and draw the authorities back to the scene. Behind him, Margaret knelt, quivering from head to foot, praying beside Gerard and for Gerard.

Outside, the group regrouped. "What is to do?" Dierich Brower asked, his brow furrowed. "Why, we have scared the girl out of her wits. She was in a kind of fit."

"We had better all go and doctor her, then," someone suggested.

"Oh, yes! And frighten her into the churchyard," Jorian replied with his usual off-hand manner, which served to disarm all suspicion. "Her father is a doctor, and I have roused him to bring her round. Let us see the fire, will ye?"

Soon, the party agreed that the kitchen of the "Three Kings" was far warmer than Peter's house, and they departed, having first untied Martin. At the door, Dierich Brower chuckled. "Take note, mate, that I was right, and the burgomaster wrong. I said we should be too late to catch him, and we were too late."

Thus, Gerard, in one terrible night, had grazed the edge of the prison and the grave. How had he escaped? Not by cunningly contrived hiding places, nor by Margaret's ready wit, but by a good impulse in one of his captors, by the fragment of humanity left in a reckless fellow's heart, aided by a desire for gain. Human motives were so mixed and seemingly incongruous, and our shrewdest counsels so shortsighted.

Those whose moderate natures or gentle fates keep them from the fierce extremes of joy and anguish that human nature is capable of, are perhaps the best and certainly the happiest of mankind. But to such readers, I should try in vain to convey the unspeakable bliss that now settled upon these persecuted lovers. Even to those who have known great joy and suffered greatly, my feeble art can present but a pale reflection of Margaret's and Gerard's ecstasy.

To sit and watch a beloved face return from the grave to the world, to health and beauty, by swift gradations; to see the roses return to the loved cheek, love's glance to the loved eye, and his words to the loved mouth—this was Margaret's joy, a happiness to balance years of sorrow. For Gerard, it was to awake from a trance and find his head pillowed on Margaret's arm; to hear the woman he adored murmur new words of eloquent love, and to be showered with tears, tender kisses, and caresses. He never knew, until this sweet moment, how ardently, how tenderly she loved him. He thanked his enemies. They wreathed their arms sweetly around each other, and trouble and danger seemed a world, an age behind them. They called each other husband and wife. Were they not solemnly betrothed? Had they not stood before the altar together? Was not the blessing of Holy Church upon their union—and her curse on all who would part them?

Yet, as no woman's nerves can bear with impunity so terrible a strain, presently Margaret turned faint and sank on Gerard's shoulder, smiling feebly but quite unstrung. Gerard grew anxious and sought to seek assistance, but she held him with a gentle grasp, imploring him not to leave her for a moment.

"While I can lay my hand on you, I feel you are safe, not else," she whispered. "Foolish Gerard! Nothing ails me. I am weak, dearest, but happy, oh! so happy!"

Then it was Gerard's turn to support that dear head, with its great waves of hair flowing loose over him, to nurse her and soothe her, quivering on his bosom with soft encouraging words and murmurs of love. Sweetest of all her charms was a woman's weakness to a manly heart.

Poor things! They were happy. Tomorrow they must part, but that was nothing to them now. They had seen Death, and all other troubles seemed light as air. While there is life, there is hope; while there is hope, there is joy. Separation for a year or two, what was it to them, who were so young and had caught a glimpse of the grave? The future was bright, the present was Heaven; so passed the blissful hours.

Alas! Their innocence ran other risks besides the prison and the grave. They were in most danger from their own hearts and their inexperience, now that visible danger had vanished.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten lay awake, his mind a torment of restless dread, though the tempest raging outside offered him no solace. It was not the thunder that kept him from slumber, nor the flashing lightning; rather, it was a gnawing fear that had taken root in his chest. Had he been struck by such terror, he might have joined the frantic search for Peter, but his anxiety was of a different, more personal hue. The moment the storm broke and silence returned to the night, the burgomaster rose with a jerky, agitated motion. He crept down the creaking stairs, saddled his mule with trembling hands, and rode swiftly to the "Three Kings" inn at Sevenbergen.

There, he found his men sprawled in a drunken stupor—some slumped over chairs, others lying upon the tables, a few curled up on the floorboards. Ghysbrecht roused them with furious shouts, demanding answers for their failure. They recounted the tale of their unsuccessful search, weaving their report with exaggerated boasts of their own zeal.

"Fool!" the burgomaster cried, his voice cracking with rage. "To let you go without me! My life depends on it; he was there all the time. Did you look under the girl's bed?"

"No," one of the men replied, rubbing his eyes. "There was no room for a man there."

"How do you know that, if you did not look?" Ghysbrecht snarled, his face contorted. "You should have looked under her bed, inside it too, and sounded every panel with your knives. Come, rise now, and I shall show you the proper way to search."

Dierich Brower stood up, shaking the drowsiness from his frame. "If you find him, call me a horse and no man," he challenged, though his tone lacked true conviction.

Within moments, Peter's house was once again surrounded by the burghers. The fiery old man left his mule in the care of Jorian Ketel and, accompanied by Dierich and the rest, burst through the door.

The house was utterly empty. Not a soul remained within its walls, not even Peter. They climbed the stairs, their footsteps echoing in the hollow rooms, until suddenly, one of the men gave a sharp cry. He pointed through Peter's window, which stood wide open to the night air. The others leaned in, and there, visible in the moonlight at a short distance, walked the man they sought. He moved quietly across the fields, accompanied by Margaret and Martin.

With an exulting yell of triumph, Ghysbrecht descended the stairs and threw himself onto his mule. He and his men spurred their animals forward, setting off in hot pursuit of the fugitives.

The dawn broke gray and cold over the Dutch countryside, but Gerard Willems was already awake. The terror of the previous night's narrow escape had seared itself into his mind, a warning that silence was no longer enough. He roused Martin, the old soldier, with a urgency that startled the veteran. Martin, expecting to find Gerard fleeing through the window as he had last night, found instead a man driven by a fresh, desperate resolve.

"Listen to me," Gerard urged, his voice tight with anxiety. "We cannot stay. The net is closing. There is but one path of safety: the great forest. I know a bridle-road within its depths that leads swiftly to a hostelry. From there, a swift horse will carry us out of Holland before the sun has fully risen."

They moved quickly, leaving the safety of Peter's home behind. The distance to the forest edge was short, mere furlongs across the wet, churned earth. Margaret pinned up her kirtle and farthingale against the damp chill, her movements sharp with worry. Peter accompanied them only as far as his garden hedge, where he paused. With an emotion rarely seen on his weathered face, he placed a hand on Gerard's shoulder and offered a blessing, his gaze lingering on the young man who had become his son-in-law.

As they crossed the stony field toward the treeline, the sun was just beginning to peek over the horizon. Halfway across, Margaret stopped. Her eyes darted nervously toward the village, then toward the woods. A cry tore from her throat—a raw sound of instinctual terror—and she turned, running blindly toward the tree line, screaming.

Behind them, the ground trembled with the approach of Ghysbrecht and his men. The burgomaster was in hot pursuit.

Resistance would have been suicide. Without hesitation, Martin and Gerard followed Margaret's lead, plunging into the darkening shadows of the forest. The pursuers gained ground, their boots crunching loudly on the dry leaves, but Martin's voice cut through the panic, ringing out with a fierce command: "Only win the wood! Only win the wood!"

Their hearts leaped with hope. The great oaks seemed to stretch their branches like protective arms, their dense foliage forming a living screen between them and the world. For a moment, the forest was their sanctuary.

But the sanctuary was breached by an unforeseen danger. The fiery old burgomaster, Ghysbrecht, flung himself onto his mule, spurring the beast into a gallop. He did not merely chase his own men; he turned his mount directly toward the fugitives, aiming to cut them off from the deeper woods. The old man rode in a wide semicircle, positioning himself squarely in front of Gerard, while the others scrambled past.

Margaret shrieked, throwing her arms around Gerard in a futile attempt to shield him. He shook her off, his face grim and set.

In his blind rage, Ghysbrecht forgot the natural instinct of the hunted animal to turn on the hunter. He forgot that hatred breeds hatred. Instead of dodging the charging mule as the burgomaster expected, Gerard flew straight at him. With a savage, exulting cry that echoed through the trees, he raised his stout oak staff and struck with every ounce of his strength and soul.

The impact was catastrophic. The oak staff crashed down upon Ghysbrecht's face with a sound like thunder, felling the old man instantly. The burgomaster tumbled beneath his mule, his face streaming with blood, his collar spattered red, as the beast beat a frantic tattoo of hooves against the earth.

In the next heartbeat, the three fugitives were deep within the tree line. The yells of dismay and vengeful fury that erupted from Ghysbrecht's men signaled that the game had changed; it was now a race for life or death.

"Why run?" Gerard gasped, his chest heaving. "You have your bow, and I have this." He brandished the bloody staff.

"Boy!" Martin roared, his voice cracking with adrenaline. "The gallows! Follow me!" He vanished into the undergrowth.

Soon, the baying of hounds filled the air, a pack of hellhounds opening on the scent of the prey. The men were in the wood, their eyes scanning the trunks as they spotted the figures flitting among the trees. Margaret moaned, her breath coming in ragged gasps, while Gerard clenched his teeth, gripping his weapon until his knuckles turned white.

They reached a stiff hazel coppice. Martin dashed inside, shouldering aside the young wood as if it were standing corn. Within fifty yards, they faced a fork of four blind paths. Martin chose one, whispering, "Bend low." They glided along, half-creeping, until their path intersected with a labyrinth of tortuous trails.
