Taking another, they found themselves led back briefly before turning sharply into a thick pine grove. Here, the walking was difficult, the young firs so dense that visibility dropped to mere yards.

After some time, Martin sat down. Having learned in war to lose all impression of danger amidst the danger itself, he calmly produced a piece of bread and a slice of ham from his wallet, beginning to eat his breakfast.

The younger ones stared at him with dismay. Martin met their looks with a steady gaze.

"All Sevenbergen could not find you now," he said, chewing slowly. "You will lose your purse, Gerard, long before you reach Italy. Is that the way to carry a purse?"

Gerard looked down. Entangled by its chains to the buckle and strap of his wallet was a large triangular purse.

"This is none of mine," Gerard said, trying to detach it. In the struggle through the coppice, it had become inextricably bound. "It seems loath to leave me," he muttered, finally cutting it loose with his knife.

Upon examination, the purse was well-provided with silver coins of all sizes, though its bloated appearance was largely due to numerous pieces of brown paper folded and doubled inside. A light dawned in Gerard's eyes. "Why, it must be that old thief's! And see! Stuffed with paper to deceive the world!"

The mystery of how the burgomaster's purse ended up on Gerard was soon solved. It must have hung at Ghysbrecht's saddle-bow. In his rush to strike his enemy, Gerard had unconsciously torn it away, felling the man and robbing him in a single, fatal gesture.

Gerard was delighted by this feat of fortune, but Margaret was uneasy.

"Throw it away, Gerard, or let Martin take it back," she pleaded. "Already they call you a thief. I cannot bear it."

"Throw it away? Give it back? Not a stiver!" Gerard protested fiercely. "This is spoil lawfully won in battle from an enemy. Is it not, Martin?"

"Of course," Martin replied. "Send him back the brown paper, and you will; but the purse or the coin—that would be a sin."

"Oh, Gerard!" Margaret cried, her voice trembling. "You are going to a distant land. We need the goodwill of Heaven. How can we hope for that if we take what is not ours?"

But Gerard saw the matter in a different light.

"It is Heaven that gives it to me by a miracle," the pious youth declared. "I shall cherish it accordingly. Thus the favored people spoiled the Egyptians and were blessed."

"Take your own way," Margaret said humbly, her voice dropping to a murmur. "You are wiser than I am. You are my husband—is it for me to gainsay you?"

These humble words, coming from a woman who had until now held the whip-hand in their household, surprised Martin for a moment. They would recur to him later, surprising him even less then. Gerard kissed her tenderly in return for her wife-like docility, and they continued their journey hand in hand, with Martin leading the way into the depths of the huge forest. The farther they went, the more absolutely secure they felt. Indeed, the townspeople never ventured so far into the trackless part of the forest.

Yet, impetuous natures repent quickly. As soon as Gerard was out of immediate danger, his conscience began to prick him.

"Martin, would I had not struck quite so hard," he whispered.

"Whom? Oh! Let that pass; he is cheap served," Martin dismissed.

"Martin, I saw his grey hairs as my stick fell on him. I doubt they will not be from my sight this while."

Martin grunted with contempt. "Who spares a badger for his grey hairs? The greyer your enemy is, the older; and the older the craftier, and the craftier the better for a little killing."

"Killing? Killing, Martin? Speak not of killing!" Gerard shook all over, his face pale.

"I am much mistaken if you have not," Martin said cheerfully.

"Now Heaven forbid!"

"The old vagabond's skull cracked like a walnut. Aha!"

"Heaven and the saints forbid it!"

"He rolled off his mule like a stone shot out of a cart. Said I to myself, 'There is one wiped out,'" and the iron old soldier grinned ruthlessly.

Gerard fell to his knees and began to pray fervently for his enemy's life.

At this, Martin lost his patience. "Here's mummery. What! You that set up for learning, know you not that a wise man never strikes his enemy but to kill him? And what is all this coil about killing of old men? If it had been a young one, now, with the joys of life waiting for him—wine, women, and pillage! But an old fellow at the edge of the grave, why not shove him in? Go he must, today or tomorrow; and what better place for greybeards? Now, if ever I should be so mischancy as to last so long as Ghysbrecht did, and have to go on a mule's legs instead of Martin Wittenhaagen's, and a back like this—striking the wood of his bow—instead of this—striking the string—I'll thank and bless any young fellow who will knock me on the head, as you have done that old shopkeeper. Malison on his memory."

"Oh, culpa mea! Culpa mea!" cried Gerard, smiting his breast.

"Look there!" Martin cried to Margaret scornfully. "He is a priest at heart still—and when he is not in ire, St. Paul, what a milksop!"

"Tush, Martin!" Margaret reproached. Then she wreathed her arms round Gerard, comforting him with the double magic of a woman's sense and a woman's voice.

"Sweetheart," she murmured, "you forget: you went not a step out of the way to harm him, who hunted you to your death. You fled from him. He it was who spurred you on. Then did you strike; but in self-defense and a single blow, and with that which was in your hand. Malice had drawn knife, or struck again and again. How often have men been smitten with staves, not one but many blows, yet no lives lost! If then your enemy has fallen, it is through his own malice, not yours, and by the will of God."

"Bless you, Margaret; bless you for thinking so!"

"Yes; but, beloved one, if you have had the misfortune to kill that wicked man, the more need is there that you fly with haste from Holland. Oh, let us on."

"Nay, Margaret," said Gerard. "I fear not man's vengeance, thanks to Martin here and this thick wood: only Him I fear whose eye pierces the forest and reads the heart of man. If I but struck in self-defense, 'tis well; but if in hate, He may bid the avenger of blood follow me to Italy—to Italy? Ay, to earth's remotest bounds."

"Hush!" said Martin peevishly. "I can't hear for your chat."

"What is it?"

"Do you hear nothing, Margaret; my ears are getting old."

Margaret listened, and presently she heard a tuneful sound, like a single stroke upon a deep, ringing bell. She described it to Martin.

"Nay, I heard it," he said.

"And so did I," Gerard replied. "It was beautiful. Ah! There it is again. How sweetly it blends with the air. It is a long way off. It is before us, is it not?"

"No, no! The echoes of this wood confound the ear of a stranger. It comes from the pine grove."

"What! The one we passed?"

"Why, Martin, is this anything? You look pale."

"Wonderful!" said Martin, with a sickly sneer. "He asks me if it is anything? Come, on, on! At any rate, let us reach a better place than this."

"A better place—for what?"

"To stand at bay, Gerard," Martin said gravely. "And die like soldiers, killing three for one."

"What's that sound?"

"It is the avenger of blood."

"Oh, Martin, save him! Oh, Heaven be merciful! What new mysterious peril is this?"

"Girl, it's a bloodhound."

Courage in the common man is a narrow river; push it an inch from its bed, and it dries up. Martin's fortitude was iron against the familiar terrors of his rough life, yet here, confronted by a bloodhound's unerring instinct, it cracked. The supernatural chill of the hunt combined with the novelty of the beast to paralyze him. He leaned heavily on his bow, his spirit leaking away like water through a sieve. Gerard, seeing the danger only as a distant shadow, urged them to flee.

"What good is flight?" Martin sighed, his voice heavy with resignation. "If we escape the wood, we die cheap. But here, close at hand, I know a place where we may die with honor."

"Despair not, good Martin!" Gerard cried, though his own resolve wavered. "There must be a way."

"Oh, Martin!" Margaret interjected, her voice trembling with a fierce, desperate logic. "What if we part ways? Gerard's life is forfeit alone. Is there no way to draw the hound upon us two, letting him pass safe?"

"Girl, you know not the nature of this beast," Martin snapped, his head sinking low. "It does not track a man; it tracks blood. They have taken him to where Ghysbrecht fell, and from that dead man's stain, the hound will lead them straight to the killer, whether Gerard runs through an army or swims the Meuse."

As he spoke, the hound's mellow, haunting cry echoed through the trees—a sound so tunable it seemed to mock the horror of the moment. It was a paradox of beauty and death, like the eye of a constrictor that fascinates before it strikes. To Martin, standing bewildered and unnerved, the sound was a physical weight. Gerard, too, felt the paralysis set in; he had struck a man and spilled his blood, and now the four-legged avenger hunted him. Was this not the finger of God?

While the men were numbed by dread, Margaret's mind raced with activity. Her love for the soldier burned brighter than their fear.

"Lend me your knife," she commanded Martin.

"It will be little use in your hands," he replied.

With a sly, swift motion, Margaret stepped behind Gerard. Drawing the blade, she cut her own arm, letting the blood flow freely. She stooped, smearing her hose and shoes until they were dark with crimson, doing so with such adroitness that neither man noticed. Then she seized Gerard's arm.

"Come, be a man!" she urged. "Let this end. Take us to a thick place where numbers cannot help our foes."

"I am going," Martin grumbled, resigned to his fate. "Hurry serves no purpose; we cannot outrun the hound, and the place is near." Turning left, he led them forward like men walking to execution.

He guided them to a dense hazel coppice, similar to the one that had aided their escape hours prior.

"There," Martin said, pointing to the thicket. "It is but a furlong wide, but it will serve."

"What do we do?"

"Pass through and wait on the other side. As they emerge, I will shoot three, knock two on the head, and the rest will kill us."

"That is all you can think of?" Gerard asked, his patience fraying.

"That is all."

"Then, Martin Wittenhaagen, I take the lead, for you have lost your head. Can you obey a young man like me?"

"Oh, yes, Martin," Margaret cried, seizing the moment. "Do not gainsay Gerard! He is wiser than his years."

Martin gave a sullen assent.

"Do as you see me do," Gerard ordered. Drawing his massive knife, he hacked at hazel shoots, twisting them breast-high behind him to create a barrier. Martin followed, moving with a dogged, hopeless air. As they labored through the brush, the hound's bay grew louder, less melodic, and sterner.

Margaret trembled. Martin dropped to his stomach to listen.

"I hear horse's feet," he whispered.

"No," Gerard corrected grimly. "It is a mule. That cursed Ghysbrecht is still alive; none else would follow so bitterly."

"Never strike your enemy unless you mean to slay him," Martin said gloomily.

"I shall hit harder this time, if Heaven grants me the chance," Gerard vowed.

Finally, they broke through the coppice into an open wood. The trees were large but sparse, offering no cover. Soon, the hound's cry mingled with a dozen voices hooping and hallooing.

"The whole village is out after us," Martin declared.

"I care not," Gerard said calmly. "Listen. I have smoothed the path for the dog but roughened it for the men, so we may deal with them separately. The hound will gain on the men; as soon as he emerges, we kill him."

"The hound? There are more than one."

"I hear but one."

"Ay, but one speaks while the others run silent. If the leader loses the scent, another will take up the cry. There are at least two dogs, or devils in dog's skins."

"Then we must kill two. The moment they fall, we retreat into the coppice and go right back."

"That is a good thought, Gerard," Martin said, a spark of hope returning to his eyes.

"Hush! The men are in the wood."

Gerard whispered his plan. "Stand with your bow by the side of the coppice, in the ditch. I will move a few yards to that oak and hide behind it. The dogs will follow me; you shoot as many as you can, and I will brain the rest as they circle the tree."

Martin's eyes flashed with renewed purpose. They took their positions.

The hooping grew closer, the rustling of the undergrowth intensifying until the inevitable struggle for life hung in the air. Suddenly, a trembling hand touched Gerard's shoulder, causing him to start violently.

"Martin says if we are forced to part, make for the high ash-tree we entered by," Margaret whispered urgently.

"Yes! Yes! But go back! For Heaven's sake, do not come here, all out in the open!"

She turned to run back to Martin, but before she could reach him, a huge dog burst from the coppice, standing erect for a heartbeat. Margaret cowered, but the beast ignored her entirely; scent was his world. He lowered his nose, then, with an awful yell, sprang straight for Gerard's hiding spot. Before he could reach the tree, Martin loosed an arrow. The shaft found its mark, and the hound rolled head-over-heels, dead as a stone, spitted by the bowstring's twang.

Simultaneously, a second hound emerged, smelling his fallen comrade. Gerard rushed out with his cudgel, but before he could strike, a streak of white lightning seemed to flash across the beast, leaving him groveling in the dust, desperately wounded and howling piteously, but not dead.

Gerard had no time to finish the job; the coppice rustled dangerously close, seeming to breathe. Pointing wildly for Martin to retreat, Gerard ran a few yards right and crawled into the thick brush just as three men burst forth. These had gained on their comrades; the rest followed at varying distances. Gerard crawled back on all fours, and instinct taught Martin and Margaret to do the same along their line of retreat. Within yards, pursuers and pursued passed each other on opposite tracks.

A loud cry announced the discovery of the dead and wounded dogs. Voices swelled as fresh hunters arrived. The hunters wasted time in surprise; the hunted used it to escape.

"I hear no more hounds," Martin whispered to Margaret, his fear subsiding.

Now it was Margaret's turn to tremble.

"Oh, why did we leave Gerard? They will kill my Gerard, and I will not be near him."

"Nay, nay! The head to catch him is not on their shoulders. You bade him meet us at the ash-tree?"

"And so I did. Bless you, Martin, for thinking of that. To the ash-tree!"

"Ay, but with less noise."

They reached the coppice's edge when suddenly, hooping and hallooing erupted behind them. The men had realized the fugitives were inside and were beating back into the thicket.

"No matter," Martin whispered. "We have time to win clear and slip back by hard running. Ah!"

He stooped suddenly. Just as he prepared to burst from the brush, his eye caught a figure keeping sentinel. It was Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, seated on his mule. A bloody bandage crossed his broken nose, yet his eyes peered keenly through the gore, having heard the fugitives' rustling. Martin muttered a terrible oath, cautiously strung his bow, and fitted his final arrow. Margaret covered her face, her heart pounding against her throat. She knew this man must die or Gerard must.

The bow rose, the arrow drawn to its head, when an active figure leaped upon Ghysbrecht from behind with the speed of a hawk on a pigeon. A kerchief flew over the burgomaster's head, muffling his cries as he was whirled from his seat and fell heavily, groaning in terror. Gerard jumped down after him.

"Hist, Martin! Martin!"

Martin and Margaret emerged, the former shouting, "Now fly! Fly! While they are all in the thicket; we are saved!"

At this critical moment, when safety seemed within grasp, Margaret, who had borne so bravely, began to succumb, partly from the loss of blood.

"Oh, my beloved, fly!" she gasped. "Leave me, for I am faint."

"No! No!" Gerard cried. "Death together, or safety. Ah! The mule! Mount her, you, and I will run by your side."

In an instant, Martin mounted Ghysbrecht's mule. Gerard lifted the fainting girl into his arms and placed her on the saddle, relieving Martin of his bow.

"Help! Treason! Murder! Murder!" shrieked Ghysbrecht, suddenly rising on his hamstrings.

"Silence, cur!" Gerard roared, trampling the burgomaster down by the throat as one crushes an adder.

"Now, have you secured her? Then fly! For our lives! For our lives!"

But even as the mule, spurred by Martin's heel, scattered the flints with his hind hooves before reaching a canter, and even as Gerard withdrew his foot from Ghysbrecht's throat to run, Dierich Brower and his five men, who had returned for orders and heard the burgomaster's cries, burst roaring out of the coppice upon them.

Speech is often the vessel for human thought, yet there exist emotions so primal and overwhelming that they spill forth not in articulate words, but in raw, eloquent cries. In such moments, man sheds his civilized veneer, revealing himself as nothing more than a creature of instinct; for when beasts are driven to the brink, they too utter sounds, though they lack the gift of speech.

What followed was a spectacle of pure, terrifying animality. The roar of triumph erupted from the thicket as Dierich Brower and his men burst forth upon their quarry, while a sharp, piercing shriek of terror tore from the lips of the fugitives. The pursuers' hands grasped at empty air mere feet behind them as the trio—Gerard, Margaret, and the mule—darted away for their lives. For a heartbeat, the hunters stood stunned, like lions who have missed their spring, allowing Gerard and the beast to gain a ten-yard advantage. Then, weapons raised, they surged forward once more. They were certain of capture; this was not the first time these groups had tested their mettle against one another. On the open ground earlier that morning, the pursuers had visibly closed the gap, and now, in this renewed chase, victory would be decided solely by speed. A hundred yards vanished in the blink of an eye, yet that ten-yard buffer remained stubbornly intact.

This sudden surge in the fugitives' velocity baffled Dierich. The explanation lay in the dynamics of their flight. When three flee together, their pace is dictated by the slowest member. From Peter's house to the forest's edge, Gerard had been tethered to Margaret's slower stride. But now, freed from her burden, he ran at his own youthful best; moreover, the mule was swift enough to leave them all in the dust. Furthermore, the benefits of youth and a life of chastity began to manifest. Light seemed to grow perceptibly between the hunted and the hunters. Desperate to close the distance, Dierich made a final, frantic effort, gaining two yards, only for Gerard to steal them back within seconds. The pursuers cursed under their breath.

Martin heard the commotion, and a glimmer of hope ignited in his face. "Courage, Gerard! Courage, brave lad! They are straggling!"

It was true. Dierich now led only one man, while another had fallen entirely to the rear.

They ascended a long, gentle rise. Here, the advantages of youth, grit, and sober living proved decisive. Before Gerard reached the summit, the weight of Dierich's forty years pressed upon him like forty leaden bullets. "Our prey is dough," he gasped, abandoning the run to follow at a walk. Jorian Ketel trailed off next, followed by others one by one. Gerard ran them all to a standstill, save for one who pressed on with the tenacity of a bloodhound, though steadily losing ground. His name, if my memory serves, was Eric Wouverman. Following him, they approached a shorter but far steeper rise within the wood.

"Hand on the mane!" Martin cried.

Gerard obeyed instantly, and the mule hauled him up the incline faster than he had ever run before.

Witnessing this maneuver, Dierich's remaining man lost his nerve. Now nearly eighty yards behind Gerard and significantly ahead of his nearest comrade, he halted abruptly. Obedient to Dierich's silent command, he lowered his crossbow, leveled it with deliberate precision, and, just as the trio sank out of sight over the crest of the hill, released the bolt. It whistled through the air.

A cry of dismay rang out. Then, as if struck by a thunderbolt, they all collapsed onto the earth, mule and rider alike, brought low by the sudden violence.

The shot struck with such sudden, magical force that the marksman himself stood stupefied for a heartbeat. Recovering quickly, he hailed his companions to join in the pursuit and turned up the hill. But before he had covered half the distance, the figure of Martin Wittenhaagen rose from the treeline, a bent bow held ready in his hand. Eric Wouverman, recognizing the lethal skill of that weapon and recalling the dog already lying dead, did not hesitate. He darted behind a thick trunk, shrinking himself against the bark until he was as small as possible.

Wouverman peered cautiously around the rough surface; there, still aimed directly at him, was the gleaming point of the arrow. It shone with a cold, deadly light. Terrified, he dared not stir from his shelter. Minutes passed in tense silence before his companions arrived in great force, their numbers swelling with every step. Then, with a scornful laugh, Martin vanished into the woods, and shortly thereafter, the sound of hooves announced his departure on the mule.

All the hunters surged forward together. From the high ground, they commanded a view of a narrow, almost interminable glade. Far below, they spotted Gerard and Margaret running at a prodigious pace, appearing no larger than gnats against the vast landscape. Behind them, Martin galloped with frantic energy, driving the mule *ventre à terre*.

The hunters were outwitted as much as they were outrun. To understand Martin's conduct, one must look to the chain of coincidences that guided fate. As often happens, such coincidences can be deceitful. Just as a hare might tumble over a briar at the exact moment a gun fires, raising false hopes before scudding away untouched, so too did the burgomaster's mule stumble. At the precise instant the crossbow bolt whizzed harmlessly overhead, the beast stepped into a hidden rabbit-hole. She fell, throwing both riders to the earth. Gerard caught Margaret in his arms, but her weight and momentum dragged him down with her. In a moment, the soil was strewn with the principal characters of this drama.

The docile mule rose immediately, standing trembling amidst the dust. Martin was next to recover, and upon looking around, he saw that only one pursuer remained. Seizing the opportunity, he urged the young lovers to flee on foot while he checked the lone enemy, as previously recorded. He then galloped after his companions. After a long, exhausting chase, when he finally caught up to them, he instantly placed Gerard and Margaret back upon the mule. He ran alongside them until his breath failed, then took his turn to ride, passing the reins to the runner who needed rest. By this rotation, the runner was always fresh, and long before they relaxed their speed, all sound and trace of them was hopelessly lost to Dierich and his men. The hunters returned crestfallen, leaving behind their chief and their winged bloodhound to face the silence of the forest.

Life and freedom, when unthreatened, pass unnoticed, taken for granted as the midday sun that blinds rather than warms. It is only when the shadow lengthens, when the light fractures into shards of topaz beneath the elm, that men truly perceive its value. Yet in that twilight, the light is dimmer, overshadowed by the encroaching gloom. It was this very contrast that ignited a fierce, trembling gratitude within Gerard and Margaret. Though their hearts still echoed the barking of hounds and the brutality of voices that had nearly claimed them, they moved now through a sanctuary of trees, bathed in a joy so profound it made their spirits glow.

Then, Gerard's gaze fell upon the dark stains blooming on Margaret's ankles.

"Martin! Martin, help! They have wounded her! The crossbow!" he cried, his voice cracking with panic.

"No, no!" Margaret laughed softly, a sound meant to soothe his terror. "I am unhurt, my love. Not a scratch."

"But what is this, then?" Gerard demanded, agitation seizing his throat. "In Heaven's name, explain!"

"Do not scold me!" she blushed, her eyes dropping.

"Have I ever scolded you?"

"Not once, dear Gerard." She paused, her expression shifting to one of desperate explanation. "Well, Martin told me the dogs followed the scent of blood. So, thinking if I could but offer them a little red upon my shoes, they might chase me instead of you... I scratched my arm with Martin's knife. Forgive me! Whose else could I use? Yours, Gerard? Ah, no. You forgive me?" she pleaded, her face a mask of loving submission.

"Let me see this wound," Gerard choked out, his composure fracturing. "There. I knew it. A scratch? I call it a gash—a deep, cruel, terrible cut." He shuddered, unable to look away from the crimson mark.

"She could have used her bodkin," the soldier muttered, his tone sharp with irritation. "A milksop, sickened by the sight of a mere drop of blood."

"No, no. I could stare upon a sea of blood, but not upon hers," Gerard insisted, his voice rising in anguish. "Oh, Margaret! How could you be so cruel?"

Margaret smiled, a radiant expression of ineffable love. "Foolish Gerard," she murmured, wrapping her guilty arm around his neck. "To make such a fuss over nothing." She pressed herself against him. "As if I would not give every drop of blood in my heart for you, let alone a few from my arm." Under the weight of his recent peril, tears streamed down her face, mingling with his own as they wept together for the pain they had inflicted upon one another.

"I must leave her," he sobbed, the reality crashing down. "We who love so deeply—one in Holland, one in Italy. Ah, me! Ah, me!"

Margaret wept anew, but her tears were silent, patient, born of an instinct that knew her words would only deepen his sorrow. To speak her thoughts would be to add to his grief, so she remained in quiet mourning.

Suddenly, they broke from the trees onto a beaten path. Martin halted, hanging his head.

"This is the bridle-road I spoke of," he said, pointing toward the distant inn. "And there lies the hostelry."

Gerard and Margaret exchanged a terrified glance.

"Come a step with me, Martin," Gerard whispered, pulling the soldier aside. Once they were hidden, he spoke in a broken voice. "Good Martin, watch over her for me! She is my wife, yet I leave her. Look, Martin! Here is gold—it was for my journey. I cannot ask her to take it; she would refuse. But you will take it for her, will you not? Oh, Heaven! Is this all I can offer? Money? But poverty is a curse. You will ensure she wants for nothing, dear Martin? The burgomaster's silver is enough for me."

"You are a good lad, Gerard," the soldier replied, his rough demeanor softening. "Neither want nor harm shall touch her. I care for her little finger more than for all the world; and were she nothing to me, even for your sake, I would be a father to her. Go with a stout heart, and may God be with you, going and returning." The rough warrior squeezed Gerard's hand, turning his head away with an emotion he rarely showed.

For a moment, he hesitated to return to the couple, but Gerard stopped him. "No, good Martin; pray, stay here behind this thicket. Turn your head away from us, while I—oh, Martin! Martin!"

Thus, Gerard escaped the witness of his agony in leaving the woman he loved, and Martin spared himself the sight of their despair. He saw nothing of the young pair kneeling before Heaven to renew the holy vows cruel men had interrupted. He saw nothing of them clinging together as one, then failing to part, returning to each other like drowning souls, holding fast in mutual desperation. He heard only the raw sobbing of Gerard and the mournful moans of Margaret.

Finally, a hoarse cry pierced the air, followed by the patter of feet on the hard road.

Martin sprang up. There stood Gerard, running wildly, hands clasped above his head in frantic prayer. Beside him, Margaret tottered backward, palms extended in a pitiful plea for aid, her cheeks ashen, her eyes fixed on empty space.

He caught her in his arms, speaking words of comfort, but her mind could not grasp them. Only the sound of his voice caused her to moan, to hold him tightly, and to tremble violently.

He lifted her onto the mule, his arm wrapped securely around her frame. What had once been strong as a boy's, she was now relaxed and powerless. Supporting her, he guided her slowly and sadly home. She shed not a single tear, spoke not a single word.

At the edge of the wood, he dismounted her and bade her cross to her father's house. She obeyed without question.

Martin rode on to Rotterdam; Sevenbergen had become too hot for him.

Gerard, severed from the love of his life, moved through the world like a dreamer. At the inn, he hired a horse and a guide, riding swiftly toward the German frontier. Yet everything was mechanical; his senses felt dulled. Trees, houses, and men drifted past him like objects viewed through a thick veil. His companions spoke to him twice, receiving no answer. Only once did he cry out savagely, "Shall we never be out of this hateful country?"

Hours later, they reached the brow of a steep hill, with a small brook running at the bottom.

"Halt!" the guide cried, pointing across the valley. "Here is Germany."

"Where?"

"On the other side of the stream. No need to ride down the hill, I trow."

Gerard dismounted without a word, drawing the burgomaster's purse from his girdle. As he opened it, the guide said half-sulkily, "You will soon be out of this hateful country. Perhaps the one you seek will like you no better; but once across that stream, though it be a church you have robbed, they cannot take you."

Such words, at another time, would have earned the speaker a rebuke or a blow. Now, they fell on Gerard like idle air. He paid the lad in silence and descended the hill alone. The brook was silvery, murmuring over pebbles varnished by clear water. He sat and stared stupidly at them, then drank from the stream. He washed his hot feet and hands in the icy water; it woke him.

Rising, he took a run and leaped across the stream into Germany. Even as his foot touched the strange soil, he turned suddenly and looked back.

"Farewell, ungrateful country!" he cried. "But for her, it would cost me nothing to leave you forever, and all my kin, and—the mother who bore me, and my playmates, and my little native town. Farewell, fatherland—welcome the wide world! *Omne solum fortipatratra*." With these brave words on his lips, he suddenly drooped, arms and legs weak, sitting down to sob bitterly upon the foreign earth.

When the young exile had sat for a while bowed in grief, he rose and wiped the tears from his eyes like a man. Casting not a single glance behind him to weaken his resolve, he stepped out into the wide world.

His love and heavy sorrow left no room for vulgar misgivings. Compared with the tearing of himself from Margaret, walking to Italy in that rude age seemed a small price to pay.

"All nations meet in a convent," he thought, recalling the monks who had aided him. "Thanks to them and my thirst for knowledge, I can speak most of the tongues needed on this long road." He told himself, "I will soon be at Rome; the sooner the better."

After walking a good league, he arrived where four serpentine country roads met, a junction that had puzzled many a neighbor. Gerard took out the little dial Peter had given him, set it in the autumn sun, and steered unhesitatingly for Rome. He was inexperienced as a young swallow flying south, yet unlike the swallow, he wandered south alone.

Gerard encountered a small group on the road. Two men in sober suits leaned lazily against a horse, conversing idly. The rider, clad in a silk doublet and a bright green jerkin of English cloth, lay flat on his stomach in the afternoon sun, resembling an enormous lizard. His flaming yellow velvet cloak was carefully spread over the horse's loins.

"Is aught amiss?" Gerard inquired.

"Not that I wot of," one of the servants replied.

"But your master lies like a corpse. Are ye not ashamed to let him grovel on the ground?"

"Go to; the bare ground is the best cure for his disorder. If you get sober in bed, it gives you a headache; but you leap up from the hard ground like a lark in spring. Eh, Ulric?"

"He speaks sooth, young man," Ulric said warmly.

"What, is the gentleman drunk?" Gerard asked.

The servants burst into a hoarse laugh at the simplicity of his question. Suddenly, Ulric stopped, eyeing Gerard gravely. "Who are you, and where born, that know not the Count is ever drunk at this hour?" Gerard found himself a suspected character.

"I am a stranger," Gerard said, "but a true man, and one that loves knowledge; therefore ask I questions, and not for love of prying."

"If you be a true man," Ulric said shrewdly, "then give us trinkgeld for the knowledge we have given you."

Gerard looked blank but put on a good face. "Trinkgeld you shall have, such as my lean purse can spare, an if you will tell me why ye have ta'en his cloak from the man and laid it on the beast."

Inspired by the prospect of payment, two solutions were instantly offered. First, that if the Count came to himself and found his horse sweating in the cold while a cloak lay idle, he would fall to cursing and perhaps violence. Second, that a horse is a poor milksop requiring warmth, whereas a master, filled with good beer, possesses an inward heat that renders a cloak a mere shred of idle vanity. Each speaker fell in love with his theory, and having taken a hair or two of the dog that had bitten their master to the brain, their voices rose so high that the green sot began to growl instead of snoring.

Ere long, the argument took a turn certain to enliven a discussion in that age. Hans, holding the bridle, gave Ulric a sound cuff; Ulric returned it with interest. They went at it, ding dong, over the horse's mane, pummelling one another and jagging the poor beast until he ran backward, treading with an iron heel upon a promontory of the green lord. Like a toad stung by Ithuriel's spear, the Count started up howling, one hand clapped to the smart and the other tugging at his hilt. The servants, amazed with terror, let the horse go. He galloped off whinnying, the men in pursuit crying out with fear, and the green noble after them, volleying curses, his naked sword in hand, his body rebounding from hedge to hedge in his headlong but zigzag career down the narrow lane.

"In which hurtling" Gerard turned his back on them all, went calmly south, glad to have saved the four tin farthings he had prepared for trinkgeld, but far too heavy-hearted even to smile at their drunken extravagance.

The sun was nearly setting, and Gerard, who had hoped in vain to find an inn, was very ill at ease. Black clouds gathered over the sky. Gerard quickened his pace almost to a run. It was in vain; down came the rain in torrents, drenching the bewildered traveller and seeming to extinguish the very sun. Gerard trudged on, dark, wet, and in an unknown region. "Fool! to leave Margaret," he said.

Presently, the darkness thickened. He was entering a great wood. Huge branches shot across the narrow road, and the benighted stranger groped his way in what seemed an interminable and inky cave with a rugged floor, on which he stumbled repeatedly. On, and on, and on, with shivering limbs and an empty stomach, till the wolves rose from their lairs and bayed all round the wood. His hair bristled, but he grasped his cudgel, prepared to sell his life dear.

There was no wind; his excited ear heard light feet patter over the newly fallen leaves and low branches rustle with creatures gliding swiftly past. Presently, in the sea of ink, there was a great fiery star close to the ground. He hailed it as he would his patron saint. "CANDLE! a CANDLE!" he shouted, trying to run. But the dark and rugged way soon stopped him. The light was more distant than he had thought. At last, in the very heart of the forest, he found a house with lighted candles and loud voices inside. He looked up for a signboard; there was none. "Not an inn after all!" he said sadly. "No matter; what Christian would turn a dog out into this wood to-night?" With this, he made for the door leading to the voices. He opened it slowly and put his head in timidly. He drew it out abruptly, as if slapped in the face, and recoiled into the rain and darkness.

He had peeped into a large but low room, the middle of which was filled by a huge round stove or clay oven reaching to the ceiling. Round this, wet clothes were drying—some on lines, and some more compendiously on rustics. These latter habiliments, impregnated with the wet of the day, the dirt of a life, and lined with what another foot traveller called "rammish clowns," evolved rank vapours and compound odours inexpressible in steaming clouds. In one corner was a travelling family, a large one; thence flowed into the common stock the peculiar sickly smell of neglected brats. Garlic filled the interstices of the air. All this, with closed windows and the intense heat of the central furnace, created the breath of at least forty persons.

They had just supped. Now Gerard, like most artists, had sensitive organs, and the potent effluvia struck dismay into him. But the rain lashed him outside, and the light and fire tempted him in. He could not force his way all at once through the palpable perfumes, but he returned to the light again and again, like a singed moth. At last he discovered that the various smells did not entirely mix; odour of family predominated in two corners, stewed rustic reigned supreme in the centre, and garlic in the noisy group by the window. He found, too, by hasty analysis, that of these the garlic described the smallest aerial orbit, and the scent of reeking rustic darted farthest—a flavour as if ancient goats or the fathers of all foxes had been drawn through a river and dried by Nebuchadnezzar.

So Gerard crept into a corner close to the door. But though the solidity of the main fetors isolated them somewhat, the heat and reeking vapours circulated, making the walls drip. The home-nurtured novice found something like a cold snake wind about his legs, his head turning to a great lump of lead, and next he felt like choking, sweetly slumbering, and dying, all in one. He was within an ace of swooning, but recovered to a deep sense of disgust and discouragement, settling to go back to Holland at peep of day. This resolution formed, he plucked up a little heart; being faint with hunger, he asked one of the men of garlic whether this was not an inn after all.

"Whence come you, who know not 'The Star of the Forest'?" was the reply.

"I am a stranger; and in my country inns have aye a sign."

"Droll country yours! What need of a sign to a public-house—a place that every soul knows?"

Gerard was too tired and faint for the labour of argument, so he turned the conversation, asking where he could find the landlord. At this fresh display of ignorance, the native's contempt rose too high for words. He pointed to a middle-aged woman seated on the other side of the oven and turned to his mates, letting them know what an outlandish animal was in the room. Thereat the loud voices stopped, one by one, as the information penetrated the mass; and each eye turned, as on a pivot, following Gerard, and his every movement, silently and zoologically.

The landlady sat on a chair an inch or two higher than the rest, between two bundles. From the first, a huge heap of feathers and wings, she was taking the downy plumes, pulling the others from the quills, filling bundle two littering the floor ankle-deep, and contributing to the general stock a stuffy little malaria. Gerard asked her if he could have something to eat.

She opened her eyes with astonishment. "Supper is over this hour and more."

"But I had none of it, good dame."

"Is that my fault? You were welcome to your share for me."

"But I was benighted, and a stranger; and belated sore against my will."

"What have I to do with that? All the world knows 'The Star of the Forest' sups from six till eight. Come before six, ye sup well; come before eight, ye sup as pleases Heaven; come after eight, ye get a clean bed, and a stirrup cup, or a horn of kine's milk, at the dawning."

Gerard looked blank. "May I go to bed, then, dame?" he said sulkily, "for it is ill sitting up wet and fasting, and the byword saith, 'He sups who sleeps.'"

"The beds are not come yet," replied the landlady. "You will sleep when the rest do. Inns are not built for one."

It was Gerard's turn to be astonished. "The beds were not come! what, in Heaven's name, did she mean?" But he was afraid to ask for every word he had spoken hitherto had amazed the assembly, and zoological eyes were upon him—he felt them. He leaned against the wall and sighed audibly.

At this fresh zoological trait, a titter went round the watchful company. "So this is Germany," thought Gerard; "and Germany is a great country by Holland. Small nations for me." He consoled himself by reflecting it was to be his last, as well as his first, night in the land. His reverie was interrupted by an elbow driven into his ribs. He turned sharp on his assailant, who pointed across the room. Gerard looked, and a woman in the corner was beckoning him. He went towards her gingerly, being surprised and irresolute, so that to a spectator her beckoning finger seemed to be pulling him across the floor with a gut-line. When he had got up to her, "Hold the child," she said in a fine hearty voice; and in a moment she plumped the bairn into Gerard's arms.

He stood transfixed, jelly of lead in his hands, and sudden horror in his elongated countenance. At this ruefully expressive face, the lynx-eyed conclave laughed loud and long.

"Never heed them," said the woman cheerfully; "they know no better; how should they, bred an' born in a wood?" She was rummaging among her clothes with the two penetrating hands, one of which Gerard had set free. Presently she fished out a small tin plate and a dried pudding; and resuming her child with one arm, held them forth to Gerard with the other, keeping a thumb on the pudding to prevent it from slipping off.

"Put it in the stove," she said; "you are too young to lie down fasting."

Gerard thanked her warmly. But on his way to the stove, his eye fell on the landlady. "May I, dame?" he said beseechingly.

"Why not?" she said.

The question was evidently another surprise, though less startling than its predecessors. Coming to the stove, Gerard found the oven door obstructed by "the rammish clowns." They did not budge. He hesitated a moment. The landlady saw, calmly put down her work, and coming up, pulled a hircine man or two hither and pushed a hircine man or two thither, with the impassive countenance of a housewife moving her furniture. "Turn about is fair play," she said; "ye have been dry this ten minutes and better."

Her experienced eye was not deceived; Gorgonii had done stewing and begun baking. Debarred the stove, they trundled home, all but one, who stood like a table, where the landlady had moved him to, like a table. And Gerard baked his pudding; and getting to the stove, burst into steam.

The door opened, and in flew a bundle of straw. It was hurled by a hind with a pitchfork. Another and another came flying after it, till the room was like a clean farmyard. These were then dispersed round the stove in layers, like the seats in an arena, and in a moment the company was all on its back. The beds had come.

Gerard took out his pudding and found it delicious. While he was relishing it, the woman who had given it him, and who was now abed, beckoned him again. He went to her bundle side. "She is waiting for you," whispered the woman. Gerard returned to the stove and gobbled the rest of his sausage, casting uneasy glances at the landlady, seated silent as fate amid the prostrate multitude. The food bolted, he went to her, and said, "Thank you kindly, dame, for waiting for me."

"You are welcome," she said calmly, making neither much nor little of the favour; and with that began to gather up the feathers. But Gerard stopped her. "Nay, that is my task;" and he went down on his knees and collected them with ardour. She watched him demurely.

"I wot not whence ye come," she said, with a relic of distrust; adding, more cord

Denys caught at Gerard, halting his descent, though it was doubtful whether this grasp alone could have spared him a broken neck or shattered limb. His only salvation was the dying bear, whose hairy carcass bore the weight of Denys's head and shoulders. With frantic strength, Denys tore the beast from him. It was futile; the animal panted, her limbs trembling, yet she was no harmless hare. Soon, she drew her final breath. The sensible Denys propped Gerard against her soft, cooling form and fanned him. Gerard regained consciousness gradually, though confused and disoriented by the sensation of the bear around him, until he rolled away in a scream.

"Courage!" cried Denys. "The devil is dead."

"Is it dead? Quite dead?" Gerard inquired from behind a tree, his courage feverish and his chill returning.

"Behold," said Denys, pulling the brute's ear playfully, opening her jaws, and inserting his head with other insulting antics. In the midst of this, Gerard was violently sick.

Denys laughed at him.

"What is the matter now?" he asked. "Also, why tumble off your perch just when we had won the day?"

"I swooned, I trow."

"But why?"

Receiving no answer, Denys continued, "Green girls faint as soon as they look at you, but then they choose time and place. What woman ever fainted up a tree?"

"She sent her nasty blood all over me. I think the smell must have overpowered me! Faugh! I hate blood."

"I do believe it potently."

"See what a mess she has made of me."

"But with her blood, not yours. I pity the enemy that strives to satisfy you."

"You need not brag, Maitre Denys; I saw you under the tree, the color of your shirt."

"Let us distinguish," said Denys, blushing. "It is permitted to tremble for a friend."

Gerard, in answer, flung his arms around Denys's neck in silence.

"Look here," whined the stout soldier, moved by this sudden gush of nature and youth. "Was ever anything so like a woman? I love thee, little milksop—go on. Good! Behold him on his knees now. What new caprice is this?"

"Oh, Denys, ought we not to return thanks to Him who has saved both our lives against such fearful odds?" Gerard knelt and prayed aloud. Presently, he found Denys kneeling quietly beside him, his hands crossed over his bosom in the fashion of his nation, with a face as long as his arm. When they rose, Gerard's countenance was beaming.

"Good Denys," he said, "Heaven will reward thy piety."

"Ah, bah! I did it out of politeness," said the Frenchman. "It was to please thee, little one. *C'est égal*, 'twas well and orderly prayed, and edified me to the core while it lasted. A bishop had scarce handled the matter better; so now our evensong being sung, and the saints enlisted with us—*marchons*."

Before taking two steps, he stopped. "By-the-by, the cub!"

"Oh, no, no!" cried Gerard.

"You are right. It is late. We have lost time climbing trees, tumbling off them, swooning, vomiting, and praying; and the brute is heavy to carry. And now I think on't, we shall have Papa after it next; these bears make such a coil about an odd cub. What is this? You are wounded! You are wounded!"

"Not I."

"He is wounded; miserable that I am!"

"Be calm, Denys. I am not touched; I feel no pain anywhere."

"You? You only feel when another is hurt," cried Denys, with great emotion. Throwing himself on his knees, he examined Gerard's leg with glistening eyes.

"Quick! Quick! Before it stiffens," he cried, hurrying him on.

"Who makes the coil about nothing now?" inquired Gerard composedly.

Denys's reply was very indirect.

"Be pleased to note," said he, "that I have a bad heart. You were man enough to save my life, yet I must sneer at you, a novice in war. Was not I a novice once myself? Then you fainted from a wound, and I thought you swooned for fear, and called you a milksop. Briefly, I have a bad tongue and a bad heart."

"Denys!"

"Plait-il?"

"You lie."

"You are very good to say so, little one, and I am eternally obliged to you," mumbled the remorseful Denys.

Ere they had walked many furlongs, the muscles of the wounded leg contracted and stiffened, until Gerard could barely put his toe to the ground, and that with great pain.

At last he could bear it no longer.

"Let me lie down and die," he groaned, "for this is intolerable."

Denys argued that it was afternoon and the nights were now frosty, that cold and hunger were ill companions, and that it would be unreasonable to lose heart, especially since a certain great personage was notoriously defunct. So Gerard leaned upon his axe and hobbled on; but presently he gave in all at once and sank helpless in the road.

Denys drew him aside into the wood. To his surprise, he gave Gerard his crossbow and bolts, enjoining him strictly to lie quiet. If any ill-looking fellows should find him and come to him, he was to bid them keep aloof; and should they refuse, to shoot them dead at twenty paces. "Honest men keep the path; and, knaves in a wood, none but fools do parley with them." With this, he snatched up Gerard's axe and set off running—not, as Gerard expected, toward Düsseldorf, but on the road they had come.

Gerard lay aching and smarting. To him, Rome, which seemed so near at the start, looked far, far off now that he was two hundred miles nearer it. But soon all his thoughts turned toward Sevenbergen. How sweet it would be one day to hold Margaret's hand and tell her all he had gone through for her! The very thought of it, and of her, soothed him; and amidst pain and nerve irritation, he lay resigned, sweetly though faintly smiling.

He had lain thus more than two hours when suddenly there were shouts. The next moment, something struck a tree hard by and quivered in it.

He looked; it was an arrow.

He started to his feet. Several missiles rattled among the boughs, and the wood echoed with battle cries. Whence they came he could not tell, for noises in these huge woods are so reverberated that a stranger is always at fault regarding their source; but they seemed to fill the whole air. Presently there was a lull, then he heard the fierce galloping of hooves; and still louder shouts and cries arose, mingled with shrieks and groans. Above all, strange and terrible sounds, like fierce claps of thunder, bellowed loudly and then died off in cracking echoes. Red tongues of flame shot out ever and anon among the trees, and clouds of sulphurous smoke drifted over his head. And all was still.

Gerard was struck with awe. "What will become of Denys?" he cried. "Oh, why did you leave me? Oh, Denys, my friend! My friend!"

Just before sunset, Denys returned, almost sinking under a hairy bundle. It was the bear's skin.

Gerard welcomed him with a burst of joy that astonished him.

"I thought never to see you again, dear Denys. Were you in the battle?"

"No. What battle?"

"The bloody battle of men, or fiends, that raged in the wood a while ago;" and with this he described it to the life, more fully than I have done.

Denys patted him indulgently on the back.

"It is well," said he. "Thou art a good limner; and fever is a great spur to the imagination. One day I lay in a cart-shed with a cracked skull and saw two hosts manoeuvre and fight a good hour on eight feet square, the which I did fairly describe to my comrade in due order, only not so gorgeously as thou, for want of book learning."

"What, then, you believe me not? When I tell you the arrows whizzed over my head, and the combatants shouted, and—"

"May the foul fiends fly away with me if I believe a word of it."

Gerard took his arm and quietly pointed to a tree close by.

"Why, it looks like—it is—a broad arrow, as I live!" He went close and looked up at it.

"It came out of the battle. I heard it, and saw it."

"An English arrow."

"How know you that?"

"Marry, by its length. The English bowmen draw the bow to the ear; others only to the right breast. Hence the English loose a three-foot shaft, and this is one of them, perdition seize them! Well, if this is not glamour, there has been a trifle of a battle. And if there has been a battle in so ridiculous a place for a battle as this, why then 'tis no business of mine, for my Duke hath no quarrel hereabouts. So let's to bed," said the professional. With this, he scraped together a heap of leaves and made Gerard lie on it, his axe by his side. He then lay down beside him, with one hand on his arbalest, and drew the bear-skin over them, hair inward. They were soon as warm as toast and fast asleep.

But long before the dawn, Gerard woke his comrade.

"What shall I do, Denys? I die of famine?"

"Do? Why, go to sleep again incontinent: *qui dort dine*."

"But I tell you I am too hungry to sleep," snapped Gerard.

"Let us march, then," replied Denys, with paternal indulgence.

He had a brief paroxysm of yawns, then made a small bundle of bear's ears, rolling them up in a strip of the skin cut for the purpose; and they took the road.

Gerard leaned on his axe, propped by Denys on the other side, hobbling along, not without sighs.

"I hate pain," said Gerard viciously.

"Therein you show judgment," replied Papa smoothly.

It was a clear starlight night, and soon the moon rising revealed the end of the wood at no great distance: a pleasant sight, since Düsseldorf they knew was but a short league further.

At the edge of the wood, they came upon something so mysterious that they stopped to gaze at it before going up to it. Two white pillars rose in the air, distant a few paces from each other; and between them stood many figures that looked like human forms.

"I go no farther till I know what this is," said Gerard, in an agitated whisper. "Are they effigies of the saints for men to pray to on the road? Or live robbers waiting to shoot down honest travelers? Nay, living men they cannot be, for they stand on nothing that I see. Oh! Denys, let us turn back till daybreak; this is no mortal sight."

Denys halted and peered long and keenly. "They are men," he said at last. Gerard was for turning back all the more. "But men that will never hurt us, nor we them. Look not to their feet, for that they stand on!"

"Where, then, i' the name of all the saints?"

"Look over their heads," said Denys gravely.

Following this direction, Gerard presently discerned the outline of a dark wooden beam passing from pillar to pillar. As the pair got nearer, walking now on tiptoe, one by one dark, snake-like cords came out in the moonlight, each pendent from the beam to a dead man, tight as wire.

Now as they came under this awful monument of crime and wholesale vengeance, a light air swept by, and several of the corpses swung, or gently gyrated, and every rope creaked. Gerard shuddered at this ghastly salute. So thoroughly had the gibbet, with its sickening load, seized and held their eyes, that it was but now they perceived a fire right underneath, and a living figure sitting huddled over it. His axe lay beside him, the bright blade shining red in the glow. He was asleep.

Gerard started, but Denys only whispered, "Courage, comrade, here is a fire."

"Ay! But there is a man at it."

"There will soon be three;" and he began to heap some wood on it that the watcher had prepared. During which, the prudent Gerard seized the man's axe and sat down tight on it, grasping his own and examining the sleeper. There was nothing outwardly distinctive in the man. He wore the dress of the country folk and the hat of the district, a three-cornered hat called a Brunswicker, stiff enough to turn a sword cut, and with a thick brass hat-band. The weight of the whole thing had turned his ears entirely down, like a fancy rabbit's in our century; but even this, though it spoiled him as a man, was nothing remarkable. They had of late met scores of these dog's-eared rustics. The peculiarity was, this clown watching under a laden gallows. What for?

Denys, if he felt curious, would not show it. He took out two bear's ears from his bundle, running sticks through them, and began to toast them. "'Twill be eating coined money," said he; "for the burgomaster of Düsseldorf had given us a rix-dollar for these ears, as proving the death of their owners; but better a lean purse than a lere stomach."

"Unhappy man!" cried Gerard. "Could you eat food here?"

"Where the fire is lighted there must the meat roast, and where it roasts there must it be eaten; for nought travels worse than your roasted

The afternoon following the injury found Gerard no longer dizzy, but plagued by a restless irritability and a succession of vivid, troubling fancies. In one such fit, he implored Denys to procure a lemon for him to suck. Denys, transformed from a rough soldier into a figure of tender, grandfatherly affection, rose hastily. "Lemons shall be yours in the twinkling of a quart pot," he promised, rushing out to scour the shops. They were rare commodities in the North, and his absence stretched long. Gerard's patience wore thin as the minutes ticked by. Finally, the door creaked open, but it was not Denys who entered.

Instead, a commanding figure glided softly into the room. He was an elderly gentleman clad in a long, sober gown trimmed with rich fur, wearing cherry-colored hose and pointed shoes. A sword hung at his side in a morocco scabbard, and around his neck sat a ruff, starched with severe rigidity and treacherously stiffened into deep furrows by hidden wooden frameworks. Upon his head rested a four-cornered cap with a fur border, and a majestic white beard framed his chin and bosom. Gerard needed no introduction; barring the weapon, this was the full dress of a physician. Trailing behind him was a boy carrying a basket filled with phials, lint, and surgical tools that seemed to invite inspection.

The old gentleman approached the bedside with a mild, hushed tone. "How fares it with thee, my son?"

Gerard answered gratefully that his wound caused little pain, though his throat felt parched and his head heavy.

"A wound!" the doctor exclaimed, feigning surprise. "They told me not of that. Let me see it. Ay, ay, a clean bite. The mastiff had sound teeth that took this out, I warrant me." His sympathy seemed to drift entirely toward the canine culprit he had conjured, his jackal.

"This must be cauterized immediately, or we shall find you retreating from water and performing somersaults in bed under our hands. 'Tis the year for raving curs, and one has done your business; but we shall baffle him yet. Boy, heat the iron."

"But, sir," Gerard interjected, "it was no dog, but a bear."

"A bear! Young man," the senior chided severely, "consider your words. It is ill jesting with the man of art who brings his grey hairs and long study to heal you. A bear, quotha! Had you dissected as many bears as I, or even a tenth thereof, and drawn their teeth to keep your hand in, you would know that no bear's jaw ever made this trifling wound. I tell you 'twas a dog, and since you force my hand, I even deny it was a dog of magnitude, but merely one of these furious curs so rife, running devious paths, biting manly legs and laying their wearers low. Were it not for me and my learned brethren, who still stay the mischief with knife and cautery, you would be lost."

"Alas, sir!" Gerard protested. "When did I say 'bear's jaw'? I said, 'A bear'; it was his paw, now."

"And why didst not tell me that at once?"

"Because you kept telling me instead."

"Never conceal anything from your leech, young man," the senior continued, a good talker though one of the worst listeners in Europe. "Well, it is an ill business. All the horny excrescences of animals—claws of tigers, panthers, badgers, cats, bears, and the like—and the horn of deer, and the nails of humans, especially children, are imbued with direst poison. You would have been better bitten by a cur than gored by a bull or stag, or scratched by a bear. Nevertheless, you shall receive a good biting cataplasm for your leg. Meanwhile, keep the body cool: put out your tongue! Good! Fever. Let me feel your pulse: good! Fever. I ordain flebotomy, and on the instant."

"Flebotomy! That is bloodletting: humph! Well, no matter, if it is sure to cure me, for I will not lie idle here." The doctor assured him that flebotomy was infallible, especially in this case.

"Hans, fetch the necessary things, and I will entertain the patient with reasons in the meantime."

The man of art then explained to Gerard that in disease, the blood becomes hot, distempered, and poisonous. By removing a portion of this unhealthy fluid, Nature is compelled to create a purer one to fill the void. Thus, bleeding served as both a cooler and a purifier, a specific remedy for all diseases, for all diseases were febrile, regardless of what empirics claimed.

"But think not," he said warmly, "that bleeding alone suffices. Any paltry barber can open a vein (though not all can close it again). The art lies in knowing which vein to empty for which disease. The other day, they brought me one tormented with earache. I let him bleed from the right thigh, and away flew his pain. By the way, he has since died. Another came with toothache. I bled him behind the ear, and relieved him instantly. He too has since passed away, as fate would have it. I bled our bailiff between the thumb and forefinger for rheumatism. Soon he returned with a headache and drumming in his ears, holding out his hand over the basin. I smiled at his folly, bled him in the left ankle against his will, and made his head as light as a nut."

Diverging from the immediate theme with the enthusiasm of a zealot, the reverend teacher proceeded:

"Know, young man, that two schools of art contend throughout Europe at this moment. The Arabian school, whose ancient oracles are Avicenna, Rhazes, and Albucazis; and its revivers are Chauliac and Lanfranc. And the Greek school, whose modern champions are Bessarion, Platinus, and Marsilius Ficinus, but whose pristine doctors were medicine's very oracles: Phoebus, Chiron, Aesculapius, and his sons Podalinus and Machaon, Pythagoras, Democritus, Praxagoras, who invented the arteries, and Dioctes, 'qui primus urinae animum dedit.' All these taught orally. Then came Hippocrates, the eighteenth from Aesculapius, and of him we have manuscripts. To him we owe 'the vital principle.' He also invented the bandage and tapped for water on the chest; and above all, he dissected—yet only quadrupeds, for the brutal prejudices of the pagan vulgar withheld the human body from the knife of science. Him followed Aristotle, who gave us the aorta, the largest blood-vessel in the human body."

"Surely, sir," Gerard objected humbly, "the Almighty gave us all that is in our bodies, and not Aristotle, nor any Grecian man."

"Child! Of course He gave us the thing; but Aristotle gave us the name of the thing. But young men will still talk. The next great light was Galen; he studied at Alexandria, then the home of science. He, justly malcontent with quadrupeds, dissected apes, as coming nearer to man, and bled like a Trojan. Then came Theophilus, who gave us the nerves, the lacteal vessels, and the pia mater."

This worried Gerard. "I cannot lie still and hear it said that mortal man bestowed the parts which Adam our father took from Him, who made him of the clay, and us his sons."

"Was ever such perversity?" the doctor asked, his color rising. "Who is the real donor of a thing to man? He who plants it secretly in the dark recesses of man's body, or the learned wight who reveals it to his intelligence, enriching his mind with the knowledge of it? Comprehension is your only true possession. Are you answered?"

"I am put to silence, sir."

"And that is better still; for garrulous patients are ill to cure, especially in fever. I say, then, that Eristratus gave us the cerebral nerves and the milk vessels; nay, more, he was the inventor of lithotomy, whatever you may say. Then came another whom I forget; you do somewhat perturb me with your petty exceptions. Then came Ammonius, the author of lithotrity, and here comes Hans with the basin—to stay your volubility. Blow thy chafer, boy, and hand me the basin; 'tis well. Arabs, quotha! What are they but a sect of yesterday who, about the year 1000, fell in with the writings of those very Greeks and read them awry, having no concurrent light of their own? For their demigod, and camel-driver, Mahound, impostor in science as in religion, had strictly forbidden them anatomy, even of lower animals, the which he who severeth from medicine, 'tollit solem e mundo,' as Tully quoth. Nay, wonder not at my fervour, good youth; where the general weal stands in jeopardy, a little warmth is civic, humane, and honourable. Now there is settled of late in this town a pestilent Arabist, a mere empiric, who, despising anatomy and scarce knowing Greek from Hebrew, hath yet spirited away half my patients; and I tremble for the rest. Put forth thine ankle; and thou, Hans, breathe on the chafer."

Just as matters reached this posture, Denys entered with the lemons, standing surprised. "What sport is toward?" he asked, raising his brows.

Gerard colored slightly and told him the learned doctor was going to flebotomize and cauterize him; that was all.

"Ay! Indeed; and yon imp, what blows he hot coals for?"

"What should it be for," the doctor said to Gerard, "but to cauterize the vein when opened and the poisonous blood let free? 'Tis the only safe way. Avicenna indeed recommends a ligature of the vein; but how 'tis to be done he saith not, nor knew he himself I wot, nor any of the spawn of Ishmael. For me, I have no faith in such tricky expedients; and take this with you for a safe principle: 'Whatever an Arab or Arabist says is right, must be wrong.'"

"Oh, I see now what 'tis for," said Denys. "And art thou so simple as to let him put hot iron to thy living flesh? Didst ever keep thy little finger but ten moments in a candle? And this will be as many minutes. Art not content to burn in purgatory after thy death? Must thou needs buy a foretaste on't here?"

"I never thought of that," said Gerard gravely. "The good doctor spake not of burning, but of cautery; to be sure 'tis all one, but cautery sounds not so fearful as burning."

"Imbecile! That is their art; to confound a plain man with dark words till his hissing flesh lets him know their meaning. Now listen to what I have seen. When a soldier bleeds from a wound in battle, these leeches say, 'Fever. Blood him!' and so they burn the wick at the other end too. They bleed the bled. Now at fever's heels comes desperate weakness; then the man needs all his blood to live; but these prickers and burners, having no forethought, recking nought of what is sure to come in a few hours, and seeing like brute beasts only what is under their noses, having meantime robbed him of the very blood his hurt had spared him to battle that weakness withal; and so he dies exhausted. Hundreds have I seen so scratched and pricked out of the world, Gerard, and tall fellows too; but lo! if they have the luck to be wounded where no doctor can be had, then they live; this too have I seen. Had I ever outlived that field in Brabant but for my most lucky mischance, lack of chirurgery? The frost chocked all my bleeding wounds, and so I lived. A chirurgeon had pricked yet one more hole in this my body with his lance, and drained my last drop out, and my spirit with it. Seeing them thus distraught in bleeding of the bleeding soldier, I place no trust in them; for what slays a veteran may well lay a milk-and-water bourgeois low."

"This sounds like common sense," sighed Gerard languidly, "but no need to raise your voice so; I was not born deaf, and just now I hear acutely."

"Common sense! Very common sense indeed," shouted the bad listener. "Why, this is a soldier; a brute whose business is to kill men, not cure them." He added in very tolerable French, "Woe be to you, unlearned man, if you come between a physician and his patient; and woe be to you, misguided youth, if you listen to that man of blood."

"Much obliged," said Denys, with mock politeness. "But I am a true man, and would rob no man of his name. I do somewhat in the way of blood, but not worth mention in this presence. For one I slay, you slay a score; and for one spoonful of blood I draw, you spill a tubful. The world is still gulled by shows. We soldiers vapour with long swords, and even in war be-get two foes for every one we kill; but you smooth gownsmen, with soft phrases and bare bodkins, 'tis you that thin mankind."

"A sick chamber is no place for jesting," cried the physician.

"No, doctor, nor for bawling," said the patient peevishly.

"Come, young man," said the senior kindly, "be reasonable. *Cuilibet in sua arte credendum est*. My whole life has been given to this art. I studied at Montpellier; the first school in France, and by consequence in Europe. There learned I Dririmancy, Scatomancy, Pathology, Therapeus

The worthy physician returned home, his face a mask of feigned agony, claiming to his housekeeper that he suffered from a "bad burn." It was a classic case of professional self-inflicted injury; while he could safely cauterize the digits of his neighbors, his own fingers bore the brunt of the heat. His housekeeper, a woman of simple wisdom, applied a remedy as mild as milk, to which he submitted with the docility of a lamb, his sole concern being the patient's cure. In the interim, he drafted his bill for the shattered phials and took immediate steps to have the travelers imprisoned. Before a magistrate, he swore a solemn oath that these strangers, owing him money, had plotted instant flight from the township.

Alas, it proved to be his unluckiest day. His sincere desire and honest endeavor to commit perjury were baffled by a circumstance he had never foreseen nor deemed possible. He had spoken the truth. And IN AN AFFIDAVIT!

The officers, arriving at "The Silver Lion," discovered the birds had already flown. They descended to the river, gathering intelligence that prompted a hot pursuit up the bank. This temporary escape owed everything to Denys's good sense and sharp observation. After a peal of laughter that served as a cordial tonic, and after venting his watchword three times, Denys turned grave. "Gerard Dusseldorf is no place for us," he declared. "That old fellow went off unnaturally silent for such a babbler. We are strangers here; the bailiff is his friend. In five minutes, we shall lie in a dungeon for assaulting a Dusseldorf dignity. Are you strong enough to hobble to the water's edge? It is hard by. Once there, you have but to lie down in a boat instead of a bed; and what is the odds?"

"The odds, Denys? Untold, and all in favor of the boat," Gerard replied, his feverish mind already racing. "I pine for Rome; for Rome is my road to Sevenbergen. Then we shall lie in the boat, but ON the Rhine, the famous Rhine—the cool, refreshing Rhine. I feel its breezes coming; the very sight will cure a little hop-'o-my-thumb fever like mine. Away! Away!"

Finding his excitable friend in this mood, Denys hastily settled with the landlord, and they hurried to the river. Inquiry revealed to their dismay that the public boat had departed an hour prior, and no others would start until evening. By dint of asking many questions and gathering a crowd, however, they secured an offer of a private boat from an old man and his two sons.

This arrangement was duly ridiculed by a bystander. "The current is too strong for three oars."

"Then my comrade and I will help row," said the invalid.

"No need," said the old man. "Bless your silly heart, he owns the other boat."

A powerful breeze blew right astern; the boatmen set a broad sail and rowed also, departing at a spanking rate.

"Are ye better, lad, for the river breeze?"

"Much better. But indeed the doctor did me good."

"The doctor? Why, you would none of his cures."

"No, but I mean—you will say I am nought—but knocking the old fool down—somehow—it soothed me."

"Amiable dove! How thy little character opens more and more every day, like a rosebud. I read thee all wrong at first."

"Nay, Denys, mistake me not. I trust I had borne with his idle threats, though in sooth his voice went through my poor ears; but he was an infidel, or next door to one, and such I have been taught to abhor. Did he not as good as say we owed our inward parts to men with long Greek names, and not to Him, whose name is but a syllable, but whose hand is over all the earth? Pagan!"

"So you knocked him down forthwith—like a good Christian."

"Now, Denys, you will still be jesting. Take not an ill man's part. Had it been a thunderbolt from Heaven, he had met but his due; yet he took but a sorry bolster from this weak arm."

"What weak arm?" inquired Denys, with twinkling eyes. "I have lived among arms, and by Samson's hairy power never saw I one more like a catapult. The bolster wrapped round his nose and the two ends kissed behind his head, and his forehead resounded, and had he been Goliath, or Julius Caesar, instead of an old quacksalver, down he had gone. St. Denys guard me from such feeble opposites as thou! And above all from their weak arms—thou diabolical young hypocrite."

The river took many turns, and this sometimes brought the wind on their side instead of right astern. Then they all moved to the weather side to prevent the boat heeling over too much, save for a child of about five years old, the grandson of the boatman and his darling. This urchin had slipped on board at the moment of starting; being too light to affect the boat's trim, he was above, or rather below, the laws of navigation.

They sailed merrily on, little conscious that they were pursued by a whole posse of constables armed with the bailiff's writ, and that their pursuers were coming up with them; for if the wind was strong, so was the current.

And now Gerard suddenly remembered that this was a very good way to Rome, but not to Burgundy. "Oh, Denys," said he, with an almost alarmed look, "this is not your road."

"I know it," said Denys quietly; "but what can I do? I cannot leave thee till the fever leaves thee; and it is on thee still, for thou art both red and white by turns; I have watched thee. I must e'en go on to Cologne, I doubt, and then strike across."

"Thank Heaven," said Gerard joyfully. He added eagerly, with a little touch of self-deception, "'Twere a sin to be so near Cologne and not see it. Oh, man, it is a vast and ancient city such as I have often dreamed of, but ne'er had the good luck to see. Me miserable, by what hard fortune do I come to it now? Well then, Denys," continued the young man less warmly, "it is old enough to have been founded by a Roman lady in the first century of grace, and sacked by Attila the barbarous, and afterwards sore defaced by the Norman Lothaire. And it has a church for every week in the year, forbye chapels and churches innumerable of convents and nunneries, and above all, the stupendous minster yet unfinished, and therein, but in their own chapel, lie the three kings that brought gifts to our Lord: Melchior gold, and Gaspar frankincense, and Balthazar the black king, he brought myrrh; and over their bones stands the shrine, the wonder of the world. It is of ever-shining brass, brighter than gold, studded with images fairly wrought, and inlaid with exquisite devices, and brave with colors; and two broad stripes run to and fro, of jewels so great, so rare, each might adorn a crown or ransom its wearer at need; and upon it stand the three kings curiously counterfeited, two in solid silver, richly gilt; these be bareheaded; but he of Aethiop ebony, and beareth a golden crown; and in the midst our blessed Lady, in virgin silver, with Christ in her arms; and at the corners, in golden branches, four goodly waxen tapers do burn night and day. Holy eyes have watched and renewed that light unceasingly for ages, and holy eyes shall watch them in saecula. I tell thee, Denys, the oldest song, the oldest Flemish or German legend, found them burning, and they shall light the earth to its grave. And there is St. Ursel's church, a British saint's, where lie her bones and all the other virgins her fellows; eleven thousand were they who died for the faith, being put to the sword by barbarous Moors, on the twenty-third day of October, two hundred and thirty-eight. Their bones are piled in the vaults, and many of their skulls are in the church. St. Ursel's is in a thin golden case, and stands on the high altar, but shown to humble Christians only on solemn days."

"Eleven thousand virgins!" cried Denys. "What babies German men must have been in days of yore. Well, would all their bones might turn flesh again, and their skulls sweet faces, as we pass through the gates. 'Tis odds but some of them are wearied of their estate by this time."

"Tush, Denys!" said Gerard; "why wilt thou, being good, still make thyself seem evil? If thy wishing-cap be on, pray that we may meet the meanest she of all those wise virgins in the next world, and to that end let us reverence their holy dust in this one. And then there is the church of the Maccabees, and the cauldron in which they and their mother Solomona were boiled by a wicked king for refusing to eat swine's flesh."

"Oh, peremptory king! and pig-headed Maccabees! I had eaten bacon with my pork liefer than change places at the fire with my meat."

"What scurvy words are these? It was their faith."

"Nay, bridle thy choler, and tell me, are there nought but churches in this thy so vaunted city? for I affect rather Sir Knight than Sir Priest."

"Ay, marry, there is an university near a hundred years old; and there is a market-place, no fairer in the world, and at the four sides of it houses great as palaces; and there is a stupendous senate-house all covered with images, and at the head of them stands one of stout Herman Gryn, a soldier like thyself, lad."

"Ay. Tell me of him! What feat of arms earned him his niche?"

"A rare one. He slew a lion in fair combat, with nought but his cloak and a short sword. He thrust the cloak in the brute's mouth, and cut his spine in twain, and there is the man's effigy and eke the lion's to prove it. The like was never done but by three more, I ween; Samson was one, and Lysimachus of Macedon another, and Benaiah, a captain of David's host."

"Marry! Three tall fellows. I would like well to sup with them all to-night."

"So would not I," said Gerard drily.

"But tell me," said Denys, with some surprise, "when wast thou in Cologne?"

"Never but in the spirit. I prattle with the good monks by the way, and they tell me all the notable things both old and new."

"Ay, ay, have not I seen your nose under their very cowls?
