But when I speak of matters that are out of sight, my words they are small, and the thing it was big; now thy words be as big or bigger than the things; art a good limner with thy tongue; I have said it; and for a saint, as ready with hand, or steel, or bolster—as any poor sinner living; and so, shall I tell thee which of all these things thou hast described draws me to Cologne?"

"Ay, Denys."

"Thou, and thou only; no dead saint, but my living friend and comrade true; 'tis thou alone draws Denys of Burgundy to Cologne?"

Gerard hung his head.

At this juncture, one of the younger boatmen suddenly inquired what was amiss with "little turnip-face?"

His young nephew, thus described, had just come aft grave as a judge, and burst out crying in the midst without more ado. On this phenomenon, so sharply defined, he was subjected to many interrogatories, some coaxingly uttered, some not. Had he hurt himself? Had he over-eaten himself? Was he frightened? Was he cold? Was he sick? Was he an idiot?

To all and each he uttered the same reply, which English writers render thus, oh! oh! oh! and French writers thus, hi! hi! hi! So fixed are Fiction's phonetics.

"Who can tell what ails the peevish brat?" snarled the young boatman impatiently. "Rather look this way and tell me whom be these after!"

The old man and his other son looked, and saw four men walking along the east bank of the river. At the sight, they left rowing awhile and gathered mysteriously in the stern, whispering and casting glances alternately at their passengers and the pedestrians.

The sequel may show they would have employed speculation better in trying to fathom the turnip-face mystery; I beg pardon of my age: I mean the deep mind of dauntless infancy.

"If 'tis as I doubt," whispered one of the young men, "why not give them a squeak for their lives; let us make for the west bank."

The old man objected stoutly. "What," said he, "run our heads into trouble for strangers! Are ye mad? Nay, let us rather cross to the east side; still side with the strong arm! That is my rede. What say you, Werter?"

"I say, please yourselves."

What age and youth could not decide upon, a puff of wind settled most impartially. Came a squall, and the little vessel heeled over; the men jumped to windward to trim her; but to their horror they saw in the very boat from stem to stern a ditch of water rushing to leeward, and the next moment they saw nothing, but felt the Rhine, the cold and rushing Rhine.

"Turnip-face" had drawn the plug.

The officers unwound the cords from their waists.

Gerard could swim like a duck; but the best swimmer, canted out of a boat capsized, must sink ere he can swim. The dark water bubbled

Good and evil balance themselves in a manner both remarkable and universal. The steel bow of the arbalest, strapped to the rescuer's back and raised high, had nearly claimed the victim; yet, in that very position, it fell into Gerard's grasp. One hand seized the weapon, the other slipped between the bow and its cord, securing the tool of salvation. With a desperate, eager jerk, Denys was hauled from the water by his crossbow until half his body bobbed free.

"Now, grip me not! Grip me not!" Gerard cried, his heart hammering against his ribs in mortal terror of the fatal mistake he had almost made.

"Pas si bete," Denys gurgled, his voice thick with water.

Seeing the sturdy stuff he had to work with, Gerard's fear vanished, replaced by a sharp, commanding calm. "On thy back," he ordered. Seizing the heavy arbalest, he stroked forward, aiding the soldier's ascent. "Hand on my shoulder! Slap the water with the other! No—downward motion. Do nothing more than I bid thee." Gerard had clamped his teeth onto the end of Denys's long hair, twisting it tight, and with the strong muscles of his youthful neck, he held the soldier's head aloft while striking out lustily against the current. For a moment, he hesitated, unsure which bank to aim for, little realizing the terrible importance of that simple choice. Seeing the west shore slightly nearer, he steered toward it, refusing to swim straight to jail like a good boy and thus providing a novel incident for the authorities. Driven by the river's force, they slanted considerably. After covering nearly a hundred yards, Denys murmured uneasily, "How much more of this?"

"Courage," Gerard mumbled. "Whatever a duck knows, a Dutchman knows; thou art safe as in bed."

Suddenly, to their surprise, they found themselves in shallow water and waded ashore. Once on solid ground, they stared at one another from head to toe as if eyes could devour, then, by one impulse, flung arms around each other's necks and panted there, hearts too full for speech. In this sacred moment, life was sweet as heaven to both; sweetest perhaps to the exiled lover who had just saved his friend. Oh, joy to whose height has any poet yet soared? To save a human life, and that life a beloved one. Such moments are worth living for, yes, three score years and ten. Calmer now, they took hands and walked along the bank, hand in hand like sweethearts, scarce knowing or caring whither they went.

The boatmen were all safe on the overturned craft, Herr Turnip-face, the "Inverter of things," sitting in the middle. The entire commotion seemed not to have deranged his habits; he greeted them as they shot into the Rhine and again as they emerged.

"Shall we wait till they right the boat?"

"No, Denys, our fare is paid; we owe them nought. Let us go, and briskly."

Denys assented, noting they could walk the whole way to Cologne on this bank.

"I fare not to Cologne," came the calm reply.

"Why, whither then?"

"To Burgundy."

"To Burgundy? Ah, no! That is too good to be true."

"Sooth 'tis, and sense into the bargain. What matters it to me how I go to Rome?"

"Nay, nay; thou but sayest so to please me. The change is too sudden. Think me not so ill-hearted as to take thee at thy word. Did I not see thy eyes sparkle at the wonders of Cologne? The churches, the images, the relics..."

"How dull art thou, Denys; that was when we were to enjoy them together. Churches! I shall see plenty, go Rome-ward how I will. The bones of saints and martyrs; alas! the world is full of them; but a friend like thee, where on earth's face shall I find another? No, I will not turn thee farther from the road that leads to thy dear home, and her that pines for thee. Neither will I rob myself of thee by leaving thee. Since I drew thee out of the Rhine, I love thee better than I did. Thou art my pearl: I fished thee, and must keep thee. So gainsay me not, or thou wilt bring back my fever; but cry courage, and lead on; and hey for Burgundy!"

Denys gave a joyful caper. "Courage! Va pour la Bourgogne. Oh! Soyez tranquille! Cette fois il est bien décidément mort, ce coquin-là." And they turned their backs on the Rhine.

As this decision became clear, a commotion arose across the river among the little party watching the discussion. They had not taken many steps before a voice rang out over the water. "HALT!"

Gerard turned, seeing one of the four men holding out a badge of office and a parchment slip. His heart sank; for he was a good citizen, accustomed to obey the voice that now bade him return to Düsseldorf—the Law's.

Denys shared no such scruples. He was a Frenchman, despising every other nation, its laws, inmates, and customs. He was a soldier, viewing the situation through a military lens: superior force opposed, a river between, the rear open; retreat was made easy. He saw at a glance that the boat still drifted in mid-stream and no ferry was nearer than Düsseldorf. "I shall beat a quick retreat to that hill," he said, "and then, being out of sight, quick step."

They sauntered off.

"Halt! In the bailiff's name," cried a voice from the shore.

Denys turned, ostentatiously snapped his fingers at the bailiff, and proceeded.

"Halt! In the archbishop's name."

Denys snapped his fingers at his Grace and proceeded.

"Halt! In the emperor's name."

Denys snapped his fingers at his Majesty and proceeded.

Gerard watched this needless pantomime with regret. As soon as they passed the brow of the hill, he declared, "There is now but one course; we must run to Burgundy instead of walking," and set off, running the best part of a league without stopping.

Denys was fairly blown and inquired what had become of Gerard's fever. "I begin to miss it sadly," he said drily.

"I dropped it in the Rhine, I trow," was the reply.

Presently they reached a small village, where Denys purchased a loaf and a huge bottle of Rhenish wine. "For," he said, "we must sleep in some hole or corner. If we lie at an inn, we shall be taken in our beds." This was no more than common prudence on the old soldier's part. The official network for catching law-breakers, especially plebeians, was very close in that age, though the cooperation of the public was almost null, at least upon the Continent. Innkeepers were everywhere under close surveillance regarding their travelers, for whose acts they were even in some degree responsible, more so it would seem than for their sufferings.

Both friends were glad when the sun set, and delighted when, after a long trudge under the stars—for the moon, if memory serves, did not rise until about three in the morning—they arrived at a large barn belonging to a house at some distance. A quantity of barley had recently been thrashed; the heap of straw on one side of the floor was almost as high as the unthrashed corn on the other.

"Here be two royal beds," said Denys. "Which shall we lie on, the mow, or the straw?"

"The straw for me," said Gerard.

They sat on the heap, ate their brown bread, and drank their wine. Then Denys covered his friend in straw, heaping it high above him, leaving only a breathing hole. "Water, they say, is death to fevered men; I'll make warm water on it, anyhow."

Gerard bade him make his mind easy. "These few drops from the Rhine cannot chill me. I feel heat enough in my body now to parch a kennel, or boil a cloud if I were in one." With this epigram, his consciousness faded so rapidly he might truly be said to "fall asleep."

Denys, lying awake a while, heard that which made him nestle closer. Horses' hoofs came ringing up from Düsseldorf, and the wooden barn vibrated as they rattled past, howling in a manner too well known and understood in the fifteenth century, but as unfamiliar in Europe now as a Red Indian's war-whoop.

Denys shook where he lay.

Gerard slept like a top.

It all swept by, and troop and howls died away.

The stout soldier drew a long breath, whistled in a whisper, closed his eyes, and slept like a top too.

In the morning, he sat up and put out his hand to wake Gerard. It lighted on the young man's forehead and found it quite wet. Denys, in his quality of nurse, forbore to wake him. "It is ill to check sleep or sweat in a sick man," he said. "I know that far, though I ne'er minced ape nor gallows-bird."

After waiting a good hour, he felt desperately hungry; so he turned, and in self-defense went to sleep again. Poor fellow, in his hard life he had often been driven to this maneuver. At high noon, he was waked by Gerard moving and found him sitting up with the straw smoking round him like a dung-hill. Animal heat versus moisture. Gerard called him "a lazy loon." He quietly grinned.

They set out, and the first thing Denys did was give Gerard his arbalest and mount a high tree on the road. "Coast clear to the next village," he said, and on they went.

Drawing near the village, Denys halted and suddenly inquired of Gerard how he felt.

"What! Can you not see? I feel as if Rome were no further than yon hamlet."

"But thy body, lad; thy skin?"

"Neither hot nor cold; and yesterday 'twas hot one while and cold another. But what I cannot get rid of is this tiresome leg."

"Le grand malheur! Many of my comrades have found no such difficulty."

"Ah! There it goes again; itches consumedly."

"Unhappy youth," said Denys solemnly, "the sum of thy troubles is this: thy fever is gone, and thy wound is—healing. Sith so it is," he added indulgently, "I shall tell thee a little piece of news I had otherwise withheld."

"What is't?" asked Gerard, sparkling with curiosity.

"Oh!"

Gerard stood frozen, the sudden news striking him with such force that his complexion flickered between pale fear and flushed anger. He ground his teeth, his spirit rising like a wild boar cornered by relentless hunters. He knew that if one pressed too closely upon a man of spirit, eventually that man would turn and charge, tearing through the very pack that sought to crush him. Yet, as he braced himself for battle, a crushing realization brought his face down again. "My axe lies in the Rhine," he whispered, his voice heavy with despair.

Denys and Prudence conferred quickly. Prudence urged caution, advising them to skirt the village entirely, while a gnawing hunger demanded they seek sustenance. In the end, hunger roared the loudest, and Prudence's logic proved most compelling. They resolved to cut across the open fields.

Stopping beside a towering haystack, they borrowed two bundles, using them as a makeshift bed in a dry ditch hidden from view, though stinging nettles pricked their skin. Taking turns venturing forth, they returned with turnips, munching sparingly throughout their flight until the sun dipped below the horizon.

Then, shivering violently, they crept out into the driving rain and encroaching darkness, emerging onto the road beyond the village. The night was a dismal abyss, black as pitch and whipped by a fierce wind. They were invisible, unheard, and unseen, moving like ghosts past their enemies, whom they almost forgot amidst the primal terrors of the storm. It felt as though they were groping and hacking their way through solid black marble. When the moon finally rose, casting its pale light over the landscape, they had traveled many leagues from Düsseldorf. Yet, they did not stop. Their weary feet carried them onward until they approached a massive structure looming in the gloom.

"Courage!" Denys cried, his voice cutting through the wind. "I believe I recognize this convent. Yes, it is so. We have entered the see of Juliers; Cologne holds no sway here."

In that instant, the walls of the sanctuary became their shield, and they stepped safely within.

In the quiet sanctuary of the prior's garden, Gerard found a companion in a monk of singular ingenuity. This brother, a master of mechanics, had crafted the great sundial, a wheel to draw water from the well, and a winnowing machine for the grain. His workshop was a testament to restless invention; he had already built several psalteries and two dulcimers, and was currently striving to construct a set of regalles, small organs intended for the choir.

Gerard played the humble psaltery with modest skill, but the monk responded with a divine touch, revealing to Gerard his own profound ignorance of music. Yet, where the monk faltered in illumination, Gerard excelled in the art of writing. In an age where true accomplishment flowed from natural bent rather than forced study, their differences dissolved. Youth and Age stood side by side like brothers, and Gerard was warmly pressed to remain for the night. He sought the counsel of Denys, who, with a rueful shrug, granted his consent.

Gerard recounted his journey to his old friend, softening the harsher edges of their recent adventures. "Alack!" sighed the good old man. "I have traveled far in my day, yet never was I molested." He advised Gerard to shun the inns, which were haunts of rogues and rioters that could harm the soul even if the body escaped, urging him instead to seek refuge in peaceful monasteries. Suddenly, his demeanor shifted; he looked at Gerard with the sharp intensity of a needle and asked how long it had been since he had confessed. Gerard flushed, answering weakly, "Better than a fortnight."

"And thou an exorcist!" the monk exclaimed. "No wonder perils have overtaken thee. Come, thou must be absolved at once."

"Yes, father," Gerard replied, sinking to his knees with hands joined. But the monk stopped him, half-fretful. "Not to me! Not to me! I am as full of the world as any man living. My soul is bound to these wooden pipes and sorry leather stops, which shall perish with those whose minds are fixed on such vanities."

"Dear father," Gerard pleaded, "they are for the use of the Church, and surely that sanctifies the labor?"

"That is precisely what the devil has been whispering in my ear," the monk retorted, shaking a finger playfully yet threateningly. "He quoted Solomon building the Lord a house with rare hangings, claiming it was gracious and sinless. Oh, he can quote Scripture rarely! But I am not so simple a monk as you think, my lad," he cried, turning his gaze not to Gerard but to the empty space beside him. "This one toy finished, vigils, fasts, and prayers for me—prayers standing, prayers lying on the chapel floor, and prayers in a right good tub of cold water." He nudged Gerard and winked knowingly. "Nothing he hates more than seeing us monks at our orisons up to our chins in cold water. For *corpus domat aqua*. So now, go confess thy little trumpery sins, pardonable in youth and secularity, and leave me to mine, sweet to me as honey, to be expiated in proportion."

Gerard bowed his head, unable to resist asking, "Where shall I find a confessor more holy and clement?"

"In each of these cells," the monk replied simply as they moved into the corridor. "Go to Brother Anselm, yonder."

Gerard hastened toward the indicated cell, but the doors stood close together, and he seemed to have misjudged his destination. Just as he raised his hand to knock, he heard his old friend crying out in an agitated whisper, "Nay! Nay! Nay!" He turned to find the monk pacing anxiously before his own door, beating the air with double hands like a sawyer. Gerard feared the cell he sought housed some wild beast, or perhaps the very personage whose presence had been so distinctively proclaimed. Looking back inquiringly, he moved to the next door. Then, his old friend nodded rapidly, his face bursting into a blissful expression before shooting back into his den. He took his hourglass, turned it, and returned to his regalles, muttering, "Well-a-day, the sands how swift they run when the man is bent over earthly toys."

Brother Anselm was a venerable monk with an ample head and a face radiating dignity and love. As Gerard confessed, responding to the gentle yet searching questions, he found himself distracted by the sheer nobility of the man. "Here is a head!" he thought. "Oh dear! Oh dear! I wonder whether you will let me draw it when I have done confessing." His own mind grew confused, and he forgot certain crimes. Yet, he did not lower his guard regarding the nature of his past, nor did he diminish the pagan character of the stories he had bolstered.

The penance assigned was brief: Gerard was to enter the convent church, prostrate himself, and kiss the lowest step of the altar three times. Then, kneeling on the floor, he was to recite three *Paternosters* and a *Credo*, after which he must return immediately.

Having performed this short mortification, Gerard returned to find Father Anselm spreading plaster. "After the soul comes the body," the monk said. "Know that I am the chirurgeon here, for want of a better. This is going on thy leg; to cool it, not to burn it; the saints forbid."

During the operation, the monastic leech, having taken interest in the Düsseldorf branch of Gerard's confession, sided with Denys on the matter of bleeding. "We Dominicans seldom let blood nowadays," he explained. "The lay leeches say it is from timidity and want of skill; but in sooth, we have long found that simples will cure most ills that can be cured at all. Besides, they never kill in capable hands, whereas other remedies slay like thunderbolts. As for the blood, the Vulgate expressly says it is the life of a man.' And in medicine or law, as in divinity, to be wiser than the All-Wise is to be a fool. Moreover, simples are mighty. The little four-footed creature that kills the poisonous snake, if bitten herself, finds an herb powerful enough to quell that poison, though stronger and of swifter operation than any mortal malady; and we, taught by her wisdom and our own traditions, still search and try the virtues of those plants the good God hath strewed this earth with, some to feed men's bodies, some to heal them. Only in desperate ills we mix heavenly with earthly virtue. We steep the hair or the bones of some dead saint in the medicine, and thus work marvellous cures."

"Think you, father, it is along of the relics? For Peter a Floris, a learned leech and no pagan, denies it stoutly."

"What knows Peter a Floris? And what know I?" the monk countered. "I take not on me to say we can command the saints, and will they nill they, can draw corporal virtue from their blest remains. But I see that the patient drinking thus in faith is often bettered as by a charm. Doubtless faith in the recipient is for much in all these cures. But so 'twas ever. A sick woman, that all the Jewish leeches failed to cure, did but touch Christ's garment and was healed in a moment. Had she not touched that sacred piece of cloth she had never been healed. Had she without faith not touched it only, but worn it to her grave, I trow she had been none the better for't. But we do ill to search these things too curiously. All we see around us calls for faith. Have then a little patience. We shall soon know all. Meantime, I, thy confessor for the nonce, do strictly forbid thee, on thy soul's health, to hearken to learned lay folk on things religious. Arrogance is their bane; with it they shut heaven's open door in their own faces. Mind, I say, learned laics. Unlearned ones have often been my masters in humility, and may be thine. Thy wound is cared for; in three days 'twill be but a scar. And now God speed thee, and the saints make thee as good and as happy as thou art thoughtful and gracious."

Gerard hoped there was no need to part, for he was to dine in the refectory. But Father Anselm, with a shade of regret just perceptible, told him he would not leave his cell that week, being himself in penitence. With this, he took Gerard's head delicately in both hands, kissed him on the brow, and almost before the cell door had closed, was back to his pious offices. Gerard went away chilled to the heart by the isolation of the monastic life, and saddened too. "Alas!" he thought. "Here is a kind face I must never look to see again on earth; a kind voice gone from mine ear and my heart forever. There is nothing but meeting and parting in this sorrowful world. Well-a-day! Well-a-day!"

This pensive mood was interrupted by a young monk who came for him and took him to the refectory. There he found several monks seated at a table, and Denys standing like a poker, being examined as to the towns he should pass through. The friars then clubbed their knowledge, marking out the route and noting all the religious houses on or near that road, which they gave to Gerard. After supper, the old monk carried Gerard to his cell, and they had an eager chat. The friar incidentally revealed the cause of his pantomime in the corridor. "Ye had well-nigh fallen into Brother Jerome's clutches. Yon was his cell."

"Is Father Jerome an ill man, then?"

"An ill man!" the friar crossed himself. "A saint, an anchorite, the very pillar of this house! He had sent ye barefoot to Loretto. Nay, I forgot, ye are bound for Italy; the spiteful old saint upon earth had sent ye to Canterbury or Compostella. But Jerome was born old and with a cowl; Anselm and I were boys once, and wicked beyond anything you can imagine." Gerard wore a somewhat incredulous look. "This keeps us humble more or less, and makes us reasonably lenient to youth and hot blood."

Then, at Gerard's earnest request, one more heavenly strain upon the psalterion, and so to bed, the troubled spirit calmed, and the sore heart soothed.

I have described in full this day, marked only by contrast, a day that came like oil on waves after so many passions and perils—because it must stand in this narrative as the representative of many such days which now succeeded to it. For our travelers on their weary way experienced that which most of my readers will find in the longer journey of life, viz., that stirring events are not evenly distributed over the whole road, but come by fits and starts, and as it were, in clusters. To some extent this may be because they draw one another by links more or less subtle. But there is more in it than that. It happens so. Life is an intermittent fever. Now all narrators, whether of history or fiction, are compelled to slur these barren portions of time or else line trunks. The practice, however, tends to give the unguarded reader a wrong arithmetical impression, which there is a particular reason for avoiding in these pages as far as possible. I invite therefore your intelligence to my aid, and ask you to try and realize that, although there were no more vivid adventures for a long while, one day's march succeeded another; one monastery after another fed and lodged them gratis with a welcome always charitable, sometimes genial; and though they met no enemy but winter and rough weather, antagonists not always contemptible, yet they trudged over a much larger tract of territory than that, their passage through which I have described so minutely. And so the pair, Gerard bronzed in the face and travel-stained from head to foot, and Denys with his shoes in tatters, stiff and footsore both of them, drew near the Burgundian frontier.

Gerard felt a fervor for this promised land nearly matching Denys's own. The soldier was a relentless evangelist for Burgundy, singing its praises at every turn. Whenever a minor inconvenience arose, he would remind Gerard that "they did things better in Burgundy," specifically playing upon the Hollander's deepest weakness: the promise of clean bedclothes. "I ask no more," Gerard would sigh, "than to think I have not slept in a naked bed since leaving home! When I gaze upon their linen, I wish my eyes and nose were sealed shut."

Denys carried his patriotism to absurd lengths. He had once walked twenty leagues in shoes that had long since blown apart, refusing to purchase new ones from a German peasant who, in his view, threw every conceivable obstacle—incivility, poor food, and physical discomfort—in a traveler's path.

As the sun began to dip below the horizon, they found themselves equidistant between a small town and a monastery, though the latter lay off the main road. Denys favored the inn; Gerard preferred the convent. Denys yielded, but only on the condition that once they crossed into Burgundy, they would henceforth stay only at inns. Gerard agreed all the more readily, for his chart of safe havens ended precisely here. They turned off the road.

Now, Gerard asked with genuine surprise where this sudden aversion to places that had fed and lodged them freely so often had come from. The soldier hesitated, hemming and hawing, until finally, his grievances burst forth. It was not a sudden dislike, he confessed, but an ancient and abiding horror he had suppressed with infinite difficulty and mere politeness.

"I saw they had put powder in your drink," he said, "so I forbore them. However, since this is the last, why not ease my mind? Know then I have been like a fish out of water in all those great dungeons. You straightway leave with some old friar; so you see not my purgatory."

"Forgive me! I have been selfish."

"Ay, ay, I forgive thee, little one; 'tis not thy fault: thou art not the first fool that has been priest-rid and monk-hit. But I will not forgive them my misery." Then, speaking of a century before Henry VIII.'s commissioners, he delivered his indictment. These gloomy piles were all built alike. Inns differed, but here all was monotony. A great gate, a little gate, so many steps, and then a gloomy cloister. There was the dortoir, the great cold refectory where one must sit silent, or at least inaudible, if one wished to speak one's mind.

"And then," he continued, "nobody is a man here, but all are slaves, and of what? Of a peevish, tinkling bell that never sleeps. An 'twere a trumpet now, sounding alarums, 'twouldn't freeze a man's heart so. *Tinkle, tinkle, tinkle*, and you must sit to meat with perhaps no stomach for food. Ere your meat settles in your stomach, *tinkle, tinkle!* and ye must go to church with perhaps no stomach for devotion: I am not a hog at prayers, for one. *Tinkle, tinkle*, and now you must go to bed with your eyes open. Well, by then you have contrived to shut them, some uneasy imp of darkness has got to the bell-rope, and *tinkle, tinkle!* it behoves you say a prayer in the dark, whether you know one or not. If they heard the sort of prayers I mutter when they break my rest with their tinkle! Well, you drop off again and get about an eyeful of sleep: lo, it is *tinkle, tinkle* for matins."

"And the only clapper you love is a woman's," put in Gerard, half-contemptuously.

"Because there is some music in that even when it scolds," was the stout reply. "And then to be always checked. If I do but put my finger in the salt-cellar, straightway I hear, 'Have you no knife that you finger the salt?' And if I but wipe my knife on the cloth to save time, then 'tis, 'Wipe thy knife dirty on the bread, and clean upon the cloth!' Oh, small of soul! These little peevish pedantries fall chill upon good fellowship like wee icicles melting down from strawen eaves."

"I hold cleanliness no pedantry," said Gerard. "Thou shouldst learn better manners once for all."

"Nay; 'tis they who lack manners. They stop a fellow's mouth at every word."

"At every other word, you mean; every obscene or blasphemous one."

"Exaggerator, go to! Why, at the very last of these dungeons, I found the poor travelers sitting all chilled and mute round one friar, like rogues awaiting their turn to be hanged; so to cheer them up, I did but cry out, 'Courage, tout le monde, le dia--'"

"Connu! What befell?"

"Marry, this. 'Blaspheme not!' quoth the executioner. 'Plait-il,' say I. Doesn't he wheel and wryte on me in a sort of Alsatian French, turning all the P's into B's? I had much ado not to laugh in his face."

"Being thyself unable to speak ten words of his language without a fault."

"Well, all the world ought to speak French. What avail so many jargons except to put a frontier atwixt men's hearts?"

"But what said he?"

"What signifies it what a fool says?"

"Oh, not all the words of a fool are folly, or I should not listen to you."

"Well, then, he said, 'Such as begin by making free with the devil's name, aye end by doing it with all the names in heaven.' 'Father,' said I, 'I am a soldier, and this is but my consigne or watchword.' 'Oh, then, it is just a custom?' said he. I, not divining the old fox, and thinking to clear myself, said, 'Ay, it was.' 'Then that is ten times worse,' said he. ''Twill bring him about your ears one of these days. He still comes where he hears his name often called.' Observe! No gratitude for the tidings which neither his missals nor his breviary had ever let him know. Then he was so good as to tell me soldiers do commonly commit the crimes for which all other men are broken on the wheel; a savoir murder, rape, and pillage."

"And is't not true?"

"True or not, it was ill manners," replied Denys guardedly. "And so says this courteous host of mine, 'Being the foes of mankind, why make enemies of good spirits into the bargain, by still shouting the names of evil ones?' and a lot more stuff."

"Well, but, Denys, whether you hearken his rede or slight it, wherefore blame a man for raising his voice to save your soul?"

"How can his voice save my soul, when he keeps turning of his P's into B's."

Gerard was staggered. Ere he could recover from this thunderbolt of Gallicism, Denys went triumphant off at a tangent, stigmatizing all monks as hypocrites. "Do but look at them, how they creep about and cannot eye you like honest men."

"Nay," said Gerard eagerly, "that modest downcast gaze is part of their discipline, 'tis custodia oculorum."

"Cussed toads eating hoc hac horum? No such thing; just so looks a cutpurse. Can't meet a true man's eye. Doff cowl, monk; and behold, a thief; don cowl thief, and lo, a monk. Tell me not they will ever be able to look God Almighty in the face, when they can't even look a true man in the face down here. Ah, here it is, black as ink! Into the well we go, comrade. Misericorde, there goes the tinkle already. 'Tis the best of tinkles though; 'tis for dinner: stay, listen! I thought so: the wolf in my stomach cried 'Amen!'" This last statement he confirmed with two oaths, and marched like a victorious gamecock into the convent, thinking by Gerard's silence he had convinced him, and not dreaming how profoundly he had disgusted him.

The silence of the refectory was broken not by the clatter of spoons, but by a hushed reference to the sudden passing of the scribe, the monk tasked with transcribing the monastery's charter and rules. Gerard, seated at the table, felt the weight of the news settle upon him. Seizing the moment with a trembling humility, he offered his services. "Nay, not for hire, my lords," he stammered, his voice barely rising above the murmur, "but for love, and as a humble token of gratitude for the countless nights of shelter the brethren of your order have granted to a poor wayfarer."

A monk, his face softening with approval, shook his head gently. "The late brother was a master of the pen," he murmured, "and his work cannot be continued save by a master."

Gerard's heart hammered against his ribs. With a gesture of trepidation, he reached into his wallet and withdrew a vellum deed. He had spent hours cleaning its back and practicing upon it, creating a specimen of his finest hand. As the monk's eyes fell upon the script, he gave a sharp intake of breath. In a flurry of motion, he rose and hurried up the long hall to the high table. Bending his knee just enough to graze the fifth and tenth steps, he presented the deed to the Prior, his voice trembling with excitement.

Instantly, a dozen knowing eyes locked onto the page. A low buzz of conversation rippled through the hall. Gerard watched as the Prior pointed repeatedly, his expression shifting from scrutiny to delight. Soon, the monk returned, his face flushed with pride, bearing a savory *crustade royale*—a game pie rich with gravy and spices—for Gerard, alongside a silver grace cup brimming with *pimentum*.

Gerard accepted the cup, bowed low first to the distant Prior, then to his companions, and raised it in a toast before circulating it among the brothers. To his astonishment, the entire table erupted in greeting. "Art convent-bred? Deny it not!" they called out.

He acknowledged their words with a surge of relief, thanking Heaven profusely. Without this recognition, he would have remained as rough and ignorant as his former comrades, Sybrandt and Cornelis.

"But 'tis passing strange how you could know," one monk asked, leaning forward.

"You drank with the cup in both hands," two others chimed in simultaneously.

For some time, a raucous laughter had echoed from a table at the far end of the hall, but now it burst forth with such obstreperous volume that the Prior dispatched the Sub-Prior to quell the disturbance and assign penance to the offenders. Gerard's cheeks burned with shame; amidst the unruly merriment, his ear had caught the word "courage!" and the distinct, trumpet-like tones of Denys of Burgundy.

Later, Gerard was settled into Feu Werter's cell, illuminated by wax lights and equipped with a writing frame adjustable to any angle, surrounded by the finest caligraphic materials. Yet, the work proved too taxing for a single evening. The question arose: how could he ask Denys, the self-proclaimed monk-hater, to remain longer?

"Tell me," Gerard urged gently, "and I shall abide by your decision."

To his delight, Denys replied with gracious ease, "A day's rest will harm neither of us. Write thou, and I shall pass the time as I may."

Gerard's labor was met with vast admiration. They agreed that the monastery's records had been grievously diminished by poor Werter's death. The Sub-Prior pressed a *rix-dollar* into Gerard's hand, along with several brushes and pigments from the convent's ample stock. Warm-hearted and buoyed by this good omen, Gerard resumed his march, for he relied upon his pen to secure his fortune and win back his beloved.

"Come, Denys," he said with a cheerful smile, displaying his gifts. "See what the good monks have bestowed upon me. Now, do try to be fairer to them; for, to be blunt, it chilled my friendship for a moment to hear even you call my benefactors 'hypocrites.'"

"I recant," Denys declared instantly.

"Thank you! Thank you, good Denys!" Gerard exclaimed.

"I was a scurrilous vagabond," the soldier admitted.

"Nay, nay, say not so!" Gerard protested warmly.

"But we soldiers are rude and hasty," Denys continued, his tone sincere. "I give myself the lie and offer my highest esteem to those I misunderstood. 'Tis unjust that thousands should be defamed for the hypocrisy of a few."

"Now you speak reasonably," Gerard said, pleased. "Have you pondered what I said?"

"Nay, it is their own doing," Denys replied.

Gerard crowed softly; all men delight in being proven right. He listened intently as Denys recounted the tale of his conversion.

"Well then," Denys began, his voice animated, "at dinner on the first day, a young monk beside me opened his jaws and laughed right out, most musically. 'Good,' said I, 'at last I have fallen upon a man and not a shorn ape.' To test him further, I slapped his broad back and administered my consigne. 'Heaven forbid!' he cried. I stared. For the dog looked as sad as Solomon; a better mime you never saw, even at a Mystery play. 'I see war is no sharpener of the wits,' he said. 'What are the clergy for but to fight the foul fiend? And what else are the monks for?'

'You may plough up the convents,' he continued, 'and we poor monks shall have naught to do—but turn soldiers, and so bring him to life again.' Then there was a great laugh at my expense. 'Well, you are the monk for me,' said I. 'And you are the crossbowman for me,' he retorted. 'And I'll be bound you could tell us tales of the war that would make our hair stand on end.' 'Excusez! The barber has put that out of the question,' I quipped, and then I had the laugh."

"What wretched ribaldry!" Gerard observed, his brow furrowing pensively.

The candid Denys immediately admitted he had witnessed merrier jests hatched with less cackle. "'Twas a great matter to have got rid of hypocrisy," he said. "'So,' said I, 'I can give you the *chaire de poule*, if that may content ye.' 'That we will see,' was the cry, and a signal went round."

Denys then burst into a gleeful description of his bedtime ordeal. He had been taken to a cell instead of the great dormitory and strictly forbidden to sleep. To aid his vigil, a book of pictures depicting a hundred merry adventures of monks pursuing the female laity was lent to him. In due course, he was led barefoot to the parlour, where a supper fit for a duke awaited him, served by twelve jolly friars—the roaringest boys he had ever met in peace or war.

The story, the toast, the jest, and the wine-cup circulated freely. Some played cards with a gorgeous pack where Saint Theresa and Saint Catherine, bedizened with gold, stood for the four queens, while black, white, grey, and crutched friars represented the four knaves. They staked their very rosaries, swearing like troopers when they lost. At midnight, a sly monk stole out, only to be cunningly followed into the garden, where he was seen thrusting his hand into the ivy and pulling out a rope-ladder. He scaled the ten-foot wall with surprising nimbleness, and a russet kirtle popped up from the outer world as quickly as he. Thus began a scene of billing and cooing that struck Denys as rather feline than ecclesiastical, drawing from him the appropriate comment of a "mew!" The monks joined the musical chorus, and the lay visitor shrieked in discomfort. But Abelard merely cried, "What, are ye there, ye jealous miauling knaves? Ye shall caterwaul to some tune tomorrow night. I'll fit every man-jack of ye with a fardingale." This brutal threat had reconciled him to stay another day—at Gerard's request.

Gerard groaned.

Meanwhile, unable to disconcert such a brazen monk, and the demoiselle beginning to whimper, they had danced caterwauling in a circle, bestowed a solemn benediction on the two wall-flowers, and retired to the parlour. There, they found a pair lying dead drunk and two others affectionate to tears. They had straightway carried off the inanimate, dragged the weeping ones away, and kicked them all merrily into their respective cells.

"And so shut up in measureless content," Denys concluded.

Gerard was disgusted and voiced his displeasure.

Denys chuckled and proceeded to describe the next day's exploits. He and the young monks had drained the fish-ponds, secreting much pike, carp, tench, and eel for their own use. In the dead of night, he had been taken shoeless by crooked paths into the chapel—a ghost-like place, dark and eerie—and then down steps into a crypt below the chapel floor, where suddenly paradise had burst upon him.

"'Tis there the holy fathers retire to pray," Gerard interjected solemnly.

"Not always," Denys corrected. "Wax candles by the dozen were lighted, and princely cheer. Fifteen soups *maigre*, with marvelous twangs of venison, grouse, and hare, and twenty different fishes—being Friday—cooked with wondrous art. Each dish was served between two buxom lasses, and each lass between two lads with a cowl; all but me: and to think I had to woo by interpreter. I doubt the knave put in three words for himself and one for me; if he didn't, hang him for a fool. And some of the weaker vessels were novices, not wont to hold good wine; had to be coaxed ere they would put it to their white teeth; *mais elles s'y faisaient*; and the story, the jest, and the cup went round (by-the-by, they had flagons made to simulate breviaries). A monk touched the cittern and sang ditties with a voice tunable as a lark in spring. The posies did turn the faces of the women folk bright red at first: but *elles s'y faisaient*."

Here Gerard exploded.

"Miserable wretches! Corrupters of youth! Perverters of innocence! But for your being there, Denys, who have been taught no better, oh, would God the church had fallen on the whole gang. Impious, abominable hypocrites!"

"Hypocrites?" Denys cried, with unfeigned surprise. "Why, that is what I clept them ere I knew them: and you withstood me. Nay, they are sinners; all good fellows are that; but, by St. Denys his helmeted skull, no hypocrites, but right jolly roaring blades."

"Denys," Gerard said solemnly, "you little know the peril you ran that night. That church you defiled amongst you is haunted; I had it from one of the elder monks. The dead walk there; their light feet have been heard to patter o'er the stones."

"Merci-corde!" Denys whispered.

"Ay, more," Gerard lowered his voice almost to a whisper. "Celestial sounds have issued from the purlieus of that very crypt you turned into a tavern. Voices of the dead holding unearthly communion have chilled the ear of midnight. And at times, Denys, the faithful in their nightly watches have even heard music from dead lips; and chords, made by no mortal finger, swept by no mortal hand, have rung faintly, like echoes, deep among the dead in those sacred vaults."

Denys wore a look of dismay. "Ugh! If I had known, mules and wain-ropes had not hauled me thither; and so" (with a sigh) "I had lost a merry time."

Whether further discussion might have thrown any more light upon these ghostly sounds, who can tell? For up came a "bearded brother" from the monastery, spurring his mule and waving a piece of vellum in his hand. It was the deed between Ghysbrecht and Floris Brandt. Gerard valued it deeply as a remembrance of home; he turned pale at first, fearing he had so nearly lost it. To Denys's infinite amusement, he not only gave a piece of money to the lay brother but kissed the mule's nose.

"I'll read you now," Gerard said, "were you twice as ill written; and—to make sure of never losing you"—here he sat down, taking out needle and thread, and sewed it with feminine dexterity to his doublet. His mind, and heart, and soul were away to Sevenbergen.

They reached the promised land, and Denys, who was in high spirits, doffed his bonnet to all the females, who curtsied and smiled in return; he fired his consigne at most of the men, at which some stared, some grinned, and some did both; and finally, he landed his friend at one of the long-promised Burgundian inns.

"It is a little one," he said, "but I know it of old for a good one; *Les Trois Poissons*." But what is this writ up? I mind not this," he added, pointing to an inscription running across the whole building in a single line of huge letters. "Oh, I see. *Ici on loge à pied et à cheval*," Denys said, going minutely through the inscription and looking bumptious when he had effected it.

Gerard did look, and the sentence in question ran thus:

"ON NE LOGE CEANS A CREDIT; CE BONHOMME EST MORT, LES MAUVAIS PAIEURS L'ONT TUE."

The landlord appeared in the passage, bowing low as he removed his cap. "Welcome, messieurs," he said.

"Come, we are not in Germany," Gerard replied, his tone sharp.

Inside the public room, they encountered the mistress, a robust woman of forty. She curtsied warmly and smiled. "Give yourself the trouble of sitting ye down, fair sir," she addressed Gerard, dusting two chairs with her apron despite their apparent cleanliness.

"Thank you, dame," Gerard murmured, inwardly sighing. *This is a polite nation,* he thought. *The trouble of sitting down? I shall endure it with singular patience. And presently, the labour of eating, the toil of digestion, and finally, by Hercules' aid, the strain of going to bed, and the struggle of sinking fast asleep.*

"Why, Denys, what are you doing? Ordering supper for only two?"

"Why not?"

"What, can we sup without waiting for forty more? Burgundy forever!"

"Aha! Courage, camarade. Le dia—"

"C'est convenu."

The Salic Law seemed absent from this French inn. Here, wimples and kirtles reigned supreme; doublets and hose were few and feeble. The landlord wandered aimlessly, eternally removing his cap for want of thought, while the women, passing him in turn, shoved him aside quietly without a glance, as one removes a live twig while bustling through a wood.

A maid brought in supper, followed by the mistress, empty-handed.

"Fall to, my masters," she said cheerily. "Y'have but one enemy here; and he lies under your knife." (I shrewdly suspect this of formula.)

They ate. The mistress drew her chair closer, providing company as well as meat, gossiping genially like old acquaintances. Once this formality passed, she departed, sending in her daughter, a beautiful young woman of about twenty. She was not quite as broad and genial as the elder, but gentle and cheerful, showing womanly tenderness upon learning the distance the poor boy had traveled and the journey he must undertake. She stayed nearly half an hour, and when she left, Gerard remarked, "This an inn? Why, it is like home."

"Qui fit Francois il fit courtois," said Denys, bursting with gratified pride.

"Courteous? Nay, Christian; to welcome us like home guests and old friends, us vagrants, here to-day and gone to-morrow. But indeed, who better merits pity and kindness than the worn traveller far from his folk? Hola! Here's another."

The newcomer was the chambermaid, a woman of about twenty-five, with a cocked nose, a large laughing mouth, and a sparkling black eye, her bare arm stout but not shapely.

The moment she entered, one traveler passed a somewhat free jest on her; the next, the whole company was roaring at his expense, so swiftly had her practiced tongue done his business. Even as, in a passage of arms between a novice and a master of fence, foils clash—the novice pinked. On this another, and then another, must break a lance with her; but Marion stuck her great arms upon her haunches and held the whole room in play. This country girl possessed in perfection that rude and ready humor which looks mean and vulgar on paper but carries all before it spoken: not wit's rapier, its bludgeon. Nature had done much for her in this way, and daily practice in an inn the rest.

Yet shall she not be photographed by me, but feebly indicated: for it was just four hundred years ago, the raillery was coarse, she returned every stroke in kind, and though a virtuous woman, said things without winking which no decent man of our day would say even among men.

Gerard sat gaping with astonishment. This was to him almost a new variety of "that interesting species," homo. He whispered, "Denys, now I see why you Frenchmen say 'a woman's tongue is her sword,'" just then she levelled another assailant; and the chivalrous Denys, to console and support "the weaker vessel," the iron kettle among the clay pots, administered his consigne, "Courage, ma mie, le—" etc.

She turned on him directly. "How can he be dead as long as there is an archer left alive?" (General laughter at her ally's expense.)

"It is 'washing day,' my masters," she said with sudden gravity.

"Apres? We travellers cannot strip and go bare while you wash our clothes," objected a peevish old fellow by the fireside, who had kept mumchance during the raillery but crept out into the sunshine of commonplaces.

"I aimed not your way, ancient man," replied Marion superciliously. "But since you ask me" (here she scanned him slowly from head to foot), "I trow you might take a turn in the tub, clothes and all, and no harm done" (laughter). "But what I spoke for, I thought this young sire might like his beard starched."

Poor Gerard's turn had come; his chin crop was thin and silky.

The loudest of all the laughers this time was the traitor Denys, whose beard was of a good length and singularly stiff and bristly; so that Shakespeare, though he never saw him, hit him in the bull's eye.

"Full of strange oaths, and bearded like the pard."
--As You Like It.

Gerard bore the Amazonian satire mighty calmly. He had little personal vanity. "Nay, 'chambriere,'" said he with a smile, "mine is all unworthy your pains; take you this fair growth in hand!" and he pointed to Denys's vegetable.

"Oh, time for that, when I starch the besoms."

Whilst they were all shouting over this palpable hit, the mistress returned, and in no more time than it took her to cross the threshold, did our Amazon turn to a seeming Madonna meek and mild. Mistresses are wonderful subjugators. Their like I think breathes not on the globe. Housemaids, decide! It was a waste of histrionic ability though; for the landlady had heard, and did not at heart disapprove, the peals of laughter.

"Ah, Marion, lass," she said good-humouredly, "if you laid me an egg every time you cackle, 'L'es Trois Poissons' would never lack an omelet."

"Now, dame," said Gerard, "what is to pay?"

"What for?"

"Our supper."

"Where is the hurry? Cannot you be content to pay when you go? Lose the guest, find the money, is the rule of 'The Three Fish.'"

"But, dame, outside 'The Three Fish' it is thus written—'Ici-on ne loge—'"

"Bah! Let that flea stick on the wall! Look hither," and she pointed to the smoky ceiling, which was covered with hieroglyphics. These were accounts, vulgo scores; intelligible to this dame and her daughter, who wrote them at need by simply mounting a low stool and scratching with a knife so as to show lines of ceiling through the deposit of smoke. The dame explained that the writing on the wall was put there to frighten moneyless folk from the inn altogether, or to be acted on at odd times when a non-paying face should come in and insist on being served. "We can't refuse them plump, you know. The law forbids us."

"And how know you mine is not such a face?"

"Out fie! It is the best face that has entered 'The Three Fish' this autumn."

"And mine, dame?" said Denys; "dost see no knavery here?"

She eyed him calmly. "Not such a good one as the lad's; nor ever will be. But it is the face of a true man. For all that," added she drily, "an I were ten years younger, I'd as lieve not meet that face on a dark night too far from home."

Gerard stared. Denys laughed. "Why, dame, I would but sip the night dew off the flower; and you needn't take ten years off, nor ten days, to be worth risking a scratched face for."

"There, our mistress," said Marion, who had just come in, "said I not t'other day you could make a fool of them still, an if you were properly minded?"

"I dare say ye did; it sounds like some daft wench's speech."

"Dame," said Gerard, "this is wonderful."

"What? Oh! No, no, that is no wonder at all. Why, I have been here all my life; and reading faces is the first thing a girl picks up in an inn."

Marion. "And frying eggs the second; no, telling lies; frying eggs is the third, though."

The Mistress. "And holding her tongue the last, and modesty the day after never at all."

Marion. "Alack! Talk of my tongue. But I say no more. She under whose wing I live now deals the blow. I'm sped—'tis but a chambermaid gone. Catch what's left on't!" and she staggered and sank backwards onto the handsomest fellow in the room, which happened to be Gerard.

"Tic! tic!" cried he peevishly; "there, don't be stupid! That is too heavy a jest for me. See you not I am talking to the mistress?"

Marion resumed her elasticity with a grimace, made two little bounds into the middle of the floor, and there turned a pirouette. "There, mistress," said she, "I give in; 'tis you that reigns supreme with the men, leastways with male children."

"Young man," said the mistress, "this girl is not so stupid as her deportment; in reading of faces, and frying of omelets, there we are great. 'Twould be hard if we failed at these arts, since they are about all we do know."

"You do not quite take me, dame," said Gerard. "That honesty in a face should shine forth to your experienced eye, that seems reasonable: but how by looking on Denys here could you learn his one little foible, his insanity, his miserable mulierosity?" Poor Gerard got angrier the more he thought of it.

"His mule--his what?" (crossing herself with superstitious awe at the polysyllable).

"Nay, 'tis but the word I was fain to invent for him."

"Invent? What, can a child like you make other words than grow in Burgundy by nature? Take heed what ye do! Why, we are overrun with them already, especially bad ones. Lord, these be times. I look to hear of a new thistle invented next."

"Well then, dame, mulierose—that means wrapped up, body and soul, in women. So prithee tell me; how did you ever detect the noodle's mulierosity?"

"Alas! good youth, you make a mountain of a molehill. We that are women be notice-takers; and out of the tail of our eye see more than most men can, glaring through a prospect glass. Whiles I move to and fro doing this and that, my glance is still on my guests, and I did notice that this soldier's eyes were never off the womenfolk: my daughter, or Marion, or even an old woman like me, all was gold to him: and there a sat glowering; oh, you foolish, foolish man! Now you still turned to the speaker, her or him, and that is common sense."

Denys burst into a hoarse laugh. "You never were more out. Why, this silky, smooth-faced companion is a very Turk—all but his beard. He is what d'ye call 'em oser than ere an archer in the Duke's body-guard. He is more wrapped up in one single Dutch lass called Margaret, than I am in the whole bundle of ye, brown and fair."

"Man alive, that is just the contrary," said the hostess. "Yourn is the bane, and hisn the cure. Cling you still to Margaret, my dear. I hope she is an honest girl."

"Dame, she is an angel."

"Ay, ay, they are all that till better acquainted. I'd as lieve have her no more than honest, and then she will serve to keep you out of worse company. As for you, soldier, there is trouble in store for you. Your eyes were never made for the good of your soul."

"Nor of his pouch either," said Marion, striking in, "and his lips, they will sip the dew, as he calls it, off many a bramble bush."

"Overmuch clack! Marion overmuch clack."

"Ods bodikins, mistress; ye didn't hire me to be one o' your three fishes, did ye?" and Marion sulked thirty seconds.

"Is that the way to speak to our mistress?" remonstrated the landlord, who had slipped in.

"Hold your whisht," said his wife sharply; "it is not your business to check the girl; she is a good servant to you."

"What, is the cock never to crow, and the hens at it all day?"

"You can crow as loud as you like, my man out o' doors. But the hen means to rule the roost."

"I know a byword to that tune," said Gerard.

"Do ye, now? Out wi't then."

"Femme veut en toute saison,
Estre dame en sa mason."

"I never heard it afore; but 'tis as sooth as gospel. Ay, they that set these bywords a rolling had eyes and tongues, and tongues and eyes. Before all the world give me an old saw."

"And me a young husband," said Marion. "Now there was a chance for you all, and nobody spoke. Oh! It is too late now, I've changed my mind."

"All the better for some poor fellow," suggested Denys.

And now the arrival of the young mistress, or, as she was called, the little mistress, was the signal for

"Where are the true men?"

"Here we stand. God bless you all!"

A rush surged toward the stairs, where half a dozen hands, hard yet warm with brotherhood, reached out to grasp the rescuers.

"You have saved our lives, lads," cried Denys, his voice thick with relief. "You have saved us this very night."

The scene that greeted the rescued pair was wild and stark. Torches flared, casting dancing shadows against the glittering breastplates of the archers, their faces bronzed by the fire and the fight. Bound and silent lay the thieves, their white cheeks a pale contrast to the violence around them. In the center of the carnage lay the bleeding giant, his life extinguished, left to rest in his own gore.

Gerard moved among the soldiers, taking each by the hand, his eyes glistening with unshed tears. In return, they all kissed him. Turning to a handsome archer of his own age, Gerard spoke softly, "Prithee, good soldier, keep watch over me. A strange drowsiness overtakes me. Let no one cut my throat while I sleep—for pity's sake."

The archer laughed, thinking Gerard jesting, and promised faithfully. Almost immediately, Gerard sank into a deep, heavy sleep.

Denys watched, surprised but unmoved, for it served his immediate purpose. Elsewhere, two archers were turning the Abbot's body over with their boots, asking which of the two had thrown the enormous rogue from the upper storey. They sought the trick of his arm.

At first, Denys scoffed, unwilling to play the braggart, fearing the young vagabond would claim it was the finger of Heaven and make him look a fool. But seeing Gerard unconscious, he suddenly supplied the required tale.

"Well, then, comrades," Denys declared, "I ran my sword through this one to the hilt. Others came buzzing about me; I needed my sword or I must die. So I planted my foot against his stomach, gave a tug with my hand, a spring with my foot, and sent him flying to kingdom come! He died in the air, and his carrion rolled down among you without ceremony—made you jump, I warrant. But pikes and pillage! What avail prattling of trifles once they are gone? Drink, comrades, drink!"

The archers noted it was easy to say "drink" where no liquor existed, but harder to do it.

"Nay, I'll soon find you liquor," Denys insisted. "My nose has a natural alacrity for scenting wine. Follow me, and bring a torch!"

They descended a short flight of stone steps into a large, low, damp cellar. The air was close and dank; the walls were encrusted with what looked like cobwebs but proved to be saltpetre oozing from the damp stones.

"Oh! the fine mouldy smell," Denys murmured. "In such places still lurks the good wine. Advance thy torch. Diable! What is that in the corner? A pile of rags? No—it is a man."

They gathered round with the torch. There, crouched on a heap in the corner, pale as ashes and shivering, was a figure.

"Why, it is the landlord," Denys said.

"Get up, thou craven heart!" shouted an archer.

"Why, man, the thieves are bound, and we are dry that bound them. Up! Show us thy wine; for no bottles see here."

"What, be the rascals bound?" stammered the pale landlord. "Good news. Wine? That will I, honest sirs."

He rose with unsure joints to lead the way, but Denys interposed. "You are all in the dark, comrades. He is in league with the thieves."

"Alack, good soldier, me in league with the accursed robbers! Is that reasonable?"

"The girl said so anyway."

"The girl! What girl? Ah! Curse her, traitress!"

"Well," interposed the other archer, "the girl is not here, but gone to the bailiff. So let the burghers settle whether this craven be guilty or no; for we caught him not in the act. Let him draw us our wine."

"One moment," said Denys shrewdly. "Why cursed he the girl? If he be a true man, he should bless her as we do."

"Alas, sir!" said the landlord. "I have but my good name to live by, and I cursed her to you because you said she had belied me."

"Humph! I trow thou art a thief, and where is the thief that cannot lie with a smooth face? Therefore hold him, comrades: a prisoner can draw wine an if his hands be not bound."

The landlord offered no objection, hoping they would pay for what they drank, noting his rent was two months overdue. The archers smiled grimly at his simplicity. One laid a quiet, firm hand on his shoulder; the other led on with the torch.

They reached the threshold when Denys cried, "Halt!"

"What is't?"

"Here be bottles in this corner; advance thy light."

The torch-bearer moved forward, having just removed his scabbard to probe the heap the landlord had crouched upon.

"Nay, nay," cried the landlord. "The wine is in the next cellar. There is nothing there."

"Nothing is mighty hard, then," said Denys, drawing a hand from the heap.

It proved to be only a bone.

Denys threw it on the floor; it rattled.

"There is nought there but the bones of the house," said the landlord.

"Just now 'twas nothing. Now that we have found something, 'tis nothing but bones. Here's another. Humph? Look at this one, comrade; and you come too, and bring your smooth knave along."

The archer with the torch, Philippe, held the bone to the light, turning it round and round.

"Well?" said Denys.

"Well, if this was a field of battle, I should say 'twas the shankbone of a man; no more, no less. But 'tisn't a battlefield, nor a churchyard; 'tis an inn."

"True, mate; but yon knave's ashy face is as good a light to me as a field of battle. I read the bone by it. Bring yon face nearer, I say. When the chine is amissing, and the house dog can't look at you without his tail creeping between his legs, who was the thief? Good brothers mine, my mind it doth misgive me. The deeper I thrust, the more there be. Mayhap if these bones could tell their tale, they would make true men's flesh creep that heard it."

"Alas! young man, what hideous fancies are these! The bones are bones of beeves, sheep, and kids, not as you think, of men and women. Holy saints preserve us!"

"Hold thy peace! Thy words are air. Thou hast not got burghers by the ear, that know not a veal knuckle from their grandsire's ribs; but soldiers-men that have gone to look for their dear comrades, and found their bones picked as clean by the crows as these I doubt have been by thee and thy mates. Men and women, saidst thou? And prithee, when spake I a word of women's bones? Wouldst make a child suspect thee. Field of battle, comrade! Was not this house a field of battle half an hour ago? Drag him close to me, let me read his face: now then, what is this, thou knave?"

He thrust a small object suddenly into the landlord's face.

"Alas! I know not."

"Well, I would not swear neither: but it is too like the thumb bone of a man's hand. Mates, my flesh it creeps. Churchyard! How know I this is not one?"

He drew his sword and began to rake the heap of earth, broken crockery, and bones onto the floor.

The landlord assured him he but wasted his time. "We poor innkeepers are sinners," he pleaded. "We give short measure and baptize the wine; we are fain to do these things; the laws are so unjust to us. But we are not assassins. How could we afford to kill our customers? May Heaven's lightning strike me dead if there be any bones there but such as have been used for meat. 'Tis the kitchen wench that flings them here. I swear by God's holy mother, by holy Paul, by holy Dominic, and Denys my patron saint—ah!"

Denys held out a bone under his eye in dead silence. It was a bone no man, however ignorant or lying, could confound with those of sheep or oxen. The sight of it shut the lying lips and palsied the heartless heart.

The landlord's hair rose visibly on his head like spikes, and his knees gave way as if his limbs had been struck from under him. The archers dragged him fiercely up, keeping him erect under the torch, staring fascinated at the dead skull which, white as the living cheek opposed, but no whiter, glared back at its murderer. The pale lip opened and opened, but could utter no sound.

"Ah!" said Denys solemnly, trembling now with rage. "Look on the sockets out of which thou hast picked the eyes, and let them blast thine eyes, that crows shall pick them out ere this week shall end. Now, hold thou that while I search on. Hold it, I say, or here I rob the gallows—" and he threatened the quaking wretch with his naked sword until, with a groan, he took the skull and held it, almost fainting.

Oh! that every murderer and contriver of murder could see him, sick and staggering with terror, hair on end, holding the cold skull, feeling that his own head would soon be like it. Soon the heap was scattered, and alas! not one nor two, but many skulls were brought to light, the culprit moaning at each discovery.

Suddenly Denys uttered a strange cry of distress from so bold and hard a man. He held up to the torch a mass of human hair. It was long, glossy, and golden—a woman's beautiful hair. At the sight of it, the archers instinctively shook the craven wretch in their hands, and he whined.

"I have a little sister with hair just so fair and shining as this," gulped Denys. "Jesu! if it should be hers! There, quick, take my sword and dagger, and keep them from my hand, lest I strike him dead and wrong the gibbet. And thou, poor innocent victim, on whose head this most lovely hair did grow, hear me swear this, on bended knee, never to leave this man till I see him broken to pieces on the wheel even for thy sake."

He rose from his knee. "Ay, had he as many lives as here be hairs, I'd have them all, by God," and he put the hair into his bosom. Then, in sudden fury, he seized the landlord fiercely by the neck, forced him to his knees, and ground his face savagely among the bones of his victims where they lay thickest. The assassin first yelled, then whined and whimpered, just as a dog first yells, then whines, when his nose is forced into some leveret he has killed.

"Now lend me thy bowstring, Philippe!" He passed it through the eyes of a skull alternately and hung the ghastly relic of mortality and crime round the man's neck. Then he pulled him up and kicked him industriously into the kitchen, where one of the aldermen of the burgh had arrived with constables, taking an archer's deposition.

The grave burgher was much startled at the sight of the landlord driven in, bleeding from a dozen scratches inflicted by the bones of his own victims, carrying his horrible collar. But Denys came panting after, and in a few fiery words soon made all clear.

"Bind him like the rest," said the alderman sternly. "I count him the blackest of them all."

While his hands were being bound, the poor wretch begged piteously that the skull be taken from him.

"Humph!" said the alderman. "Certes I had not ordered such a thing to be put on mortal man. Yet being there, I will not lift voice nor finger to doff it. Methinks it fits thee truly, thou bloody dog. 'Tis thy ensign, and hangs well above a heart so foul as thine."

He then inquired if Denys thought they had secured the whole gang or but a part.

"Your worship," said Denys, "there are but seven of them, and this landlord. One we slew upstairs, one we trundled down dead, the rest are bound before you."

"Good! Go fetch the dead one from upstairs, and lay him beside him I caused to be removed."

Here a voice like a guinea-fowl's broke peevishly in. "Now, now, now, where is the hand? That is what I want to see." The speaker was a little pettifogging clerk.

"You will find it above, nailed to the door-post by a crossbow bolt."

"Good!" said the clerk. He whispered to his master, "What a goodly show will the 'pieces de conviction' make!" and with this he wrote them down, enumerating them in separate squeaks as he penned them: Skulls—Bones—a woman's hair—a thief's hands, an axe, two carcasses, one crossbow bolt. This done, he itched to search the cellar himself; there might be other invaluable morsels of evidence, an ear, or even an earring. The alderman assenting, he caught up a torch and hurried thither, when an accident stopped him, indeed carrying him a step or two in the opposite direction.

The constables had gone up the stair in single file. But the head constable no sooner saw the phosphorescent corpse seated by the bedside than he stood stupefied

Gerard opened his eyes to find Denys leaning over him, his face etched with a quiet worry. "It is you for sleeping!" Denys exclaimed, glancing toward the window where the sun hung high in the sky. "'Tis well past noon."

"It was a blessed slumber," Gerard replied, his voice thick with lingering awe. "Methinks Heaven itself granted it to me, drawing a veil between my spirit and that dreadful nightmare. To sit here, alive and breathing, feels like a miracle. The terror of the vision still clings to me."

"Ay, lad," Denys said gently, patting his shoulder. "That is the only wise way to view such things once the dust has settled; they are but shadows, fleeting and false. Eat your breakfast, and the memory will fade. Besides, I promised to escort you to the town by midday to meet with his Worship."

Gerard dipped a piece of coarse rye bread into a cup of red wine and consumed it quickly to banish the last tremors of his dream. He then walked with Denys back over the site of the recent skirmish, shuddering at the echoes of violence, before they set out again. As they traveled, Gerard kept peering anxiously through the hedgerows, expecting ambushes that never came, until they finally reached the small Burgundian town.

Denys led him straight to "The White Hart."

"No fear of cut-throats here," Denys assured him, ushering him inside. "I have known this landlord for years; he is a burgess and likely serves as bailiff. It was here I intended to rest last night, but we lost time, and darkness overtook us—and..."

"And you saw a woman at the door," Gerard interrupted, his eyes lighting up. "She proved wiser than a Jeanneton, telling us there was no danger."

"Why, what saved our lives if not that woman? She risked her own safety to do it."

"That is true, Denys," Gerard said, his heart swelling with gratitude. "Though I hold little regard for women in general, I long to thank this poor soul and reward her handsomely, even if I must part with every coin in my purse to do so. Do you not agree?"

"Parbleu," Denys nodded vigorously.

"Where might we find her?"

"Perhaps the alderman can tell us. We must seek him out first."

The alderman received them with a countenance so singular and inexplicable that it seemed almost theatrical. Yet, after a brief pause for reflection, a grim smile softened his features. He proceeded to interrogate Gerard in detail, noting every answer with a quill. Once finished, he declared that they must remain in the town until the thieves were tried, warning them that failure to appear would result in fines and imprisonment. They stared at him, bewildered by the severity.

"However," the alderman continued, "it will not be long; the culprits were caught red-handed." He added casually, "And you know, in any case, you could not leave this place this week."

Denys stared at the remark, confused, while Gerard smiled inwardly at the old gentleman's simplicity. To him, the idea that a provincial town of Burgundy held any attraction strong enough to detain him from Rome and Margaret seemed a delightful delusion.

Now, he turned his attention to the matter nearest both their hearts.

"Your Worship," Gerard said earnestly, "we cannot find our benefactress within the town."

"Nay, but who is your benefactress?" the alderman asked.

"Who? Why, the good girl who came to you by night and saved our lives at the peril of her own existence," Gerard explained, his voice trembling with emotion. "Oh, sir, our hearts burn within us to thank and bless her. Where is she?"

"In prison, sir; good lack, for what misdeed?"

"Well, she is a witness, and may be a necessary one."

"Why, Messire Bailiff," interjected Denys, "you do not lay all your witnesses by the heels, I trow."

The alderman, pleased at being addressed as bailiff, grew communicative. "In a case of blood, we detain all testimony likely to give us leg bail and thus defeat justice; that is why we still keep the women folk. A man, at odd times, hides a week in one mind, but a woman, if she do her duty to the realm on Friday, shall undo it before Sunday, or try. Could you see that wench now, you should find her a-blubbering at having betrayed five males to the gallows. Had they been females, we might have trusted to a subpoena. For they despise one another, and there they show some sense. But now I think on't, there were other reasons for laying this one by the heels. Hand me those depositions, young sir." He adjusted his spectacles. "Ay! she was implicated; she was one of the band."

A loud disclaimer burst from Denys and Gerard simultaneously.

"No need to deafen me," said the alderman. "Here 'tis in black and white. 'Jean Hardy (that is one of the thieves), being questioned, confessed that—humph? Ay, here 'tis. 'And that the girl Manon was the decoy, and her sweetheart was Georges Vipont, one of the band; and hanged last month: and that she had been dejected ever since, and had openly blamed the band for his death, saying if they had not been rank cowards, he had never been taken, and it is his opinion she did but betray them out of very spite, and—'"

"His opinion," cried Gerard indignantly. "What signifies the opinion of a cut-throat, burning to be revenged on her who has delivered him to justice? And if you go to that, what avails his testimony? Is a thief never a liar? Is he not always a liar? And here a motive to lie? Revenge, why, 'tis the strongest of all passions. And oh, sir, what madness to question a detected felon and listen to him lying away an honest life—as if he were a true man swearing in open day, with his true hand on the Gospel laid!"

"Young man," said the alderman, "restrain thy heat in presence of authority! I find by your tone you are a stranger. Know then that in this land we question all the world. We are not so weak as to hope to get at the truth by shutting either our left ear or our right."

"And so you would listen to Satan belying the saints!"

"Ta! ta! The law meddles but with men and women, and these cannot utter a story all lies, let them try ever so. Wherefore we shut not the barn-door (as the saying is) against any man's grain. Only having taken it in, we do winnow and sift it. And who told you I had swallowed the thief's story whole like fair water? Not so. I did but credit so much on't as was borne out by better proof."

"Better proof?" Gerard looked blank. "Why, who but the thieves would breathe a word against her?"

"Marry, herself."

"Herself, sir? What, did you question her too?"

"I tell you we question all the world. Here is her deposition; can you read?—Read it yourself, then."

Gerard looked at Denys and read him Manon's deposition.

"I am a native of Epinal. I left my native place two years ago because I was unfortunate: I could not like the man they bade me. So my father beat me. I ran away from my father. I went to service. I left service because the mistress was jealous of me. The reason that she gave for turning me off was, because I was saucy. Last year I stood in the marketplace to be hired with other girls. The landlord of 'The Fair Star' hired me. I was eleven months with him. A young man courted me. I loved him. I found out that travellers came and never went away again. I told my lover. He bade me hold my peace. He threatened me. I found my lover was one of a band of thieves. When travellers were to be robbed, the landlord went out and told the band to come. Then I wept and prayed for the travellers' souls. I never told. A month ago my lover died.

"The soldier put me in mind of my lover. He was bearded like him I had lost. I cannot tell whether I should have interfered, if he had had no beard. I am sorry I told now."

The paper almost dropped from Gerard's hands. Now, for the first time, he saw that Manon's life was in mortal danger. He knew the dogged law, and the dogged men that executed it. He threw himself suddenly on his knees at the alderman's feet. "Oh, sir! Think of the difference between those cruel men and this poor weak woman! Could you have the heart to send her to the same death with them; could you have the heart to condemn us to look on and see her slaughtered, who, but that she risked her life for ours, had not now been in jeopardy? Alas, sir! Show me and my comrade some pity, if you have none for her, poor soul. Denys and I be true men, and you will rend our hearts if you kill that poor simple girl. What can we do? What is left for us to do then but cut our throats at her gallows' foot?"

The alderman was tough, but mortal; the prayers and agitation of Gerard first astounded, then touched him. He showed it in a curious way. He became peevish and fretful. "There, get up, do," said he. "I doubt whether anybody would say as many words for me. What ho, Daniel! Go fetch the town clerk." And on that functionary entering from an adjoining room, "Here is a foolish lad fretting about yon girl. Can we stretch a point? Say we admit her to bear witness, and question her favourably."

The town clerk was one of your "impossibility" men.

"Nay, sir, we cannot do that: she was not concerned in this business. Had she been accessory, we might have offered her a pardon to bear witness."

Gerard burst in, "But she did better. Instead of being accessory, she stayed the crime; and she proffered herself as witness by running hither with the tale."

"Tush, young man, 'tis a matter of law." The alderman and the clerk then had a long discussion, the one maintaining, the other denying, that she stood as fair in law as if she had been accessory to the attempt on the travellers' lives.
