And this was lucky for Manon: for the alderman, irritated by the clerk reiterating that he could not do this, and could not that, and could not do t'other, said "he would show him he could do anything he chose," And he had Manon out, and upon the landlord of "The White Hart" being her bondsman, and Denys depositing five gold pieces with him, and the girl promising, not without some coaxing from Denys, to attend as a witness, he liberated her, but eased his conscience by telling her in his own terms his reason for this leniency.

"The town had to buy a new rope for everybody hanged, and present it to the bourreau, or compound with him in money: and she was not in his opinion worth this municipal expense, whereas decided characters like her late confederates, were." And so Denys and Gerard carried her off, Gerard dancing round her for joy, Denys keeping up her heart by assuring her of the demise of a troublesome personage, and she weeping inauspiciously. However, on the road to "The White Hart" the public found her out, and having heard the whole story from the archers, who naturally told it warmly in her favour, followed her hurrahing and encouraging her, till finding herself backed by numbers she plucked up heart. The landlord too saw at a glance that her presence in the inn would draw custom, and received her politely, and assigned her an upper chamber: here she buried herself, and being alone rained tears again.

Poor little mind, it was like a ripple, up and down, down and up, up and down. Bidding the landlord be very kind to her, and keep her a prisoner without letting her feel it, the friends went out: and lo! as they stepped into the street they saw two processions coming towards them from opposite sides. One was a large one, attended with noise and howls and those indescribable cries by which rude natures reveal at odd times that relationship to the beasts of the field and forest, which at other times we succeed in hiding. The other, very thinly attended by a few nuns and friars, came slow and silent.

The prisoners going to exposure in the market-place. The gathered bones of the victims coming to the churchyard.

And the two met in the narrow street nearly at the inn door, and could not pass each other for a long time, and the bier, that bore the relics of mortality, got wedged against the cart that carried the men who had made those bones what they were, and in a few hours must die for it themselves. The mob had not the quick intelligence to be at once struck with this stern meeting: but at last a woman cried, "Look at your work, ye dogs!" and the crowd took it like wildfire, and there was a horrible yell, and the culprits groaned and tried to hide their heads upon their bosoms, but could not, their hands being tied. And there they stood, images of pale hollow-eyed despair, and oh how they looked on the bier, and envied those whom they had sent before them on the dark road they were going upon themselves! And the two men who were the cause of both processions stood and looked gravely on, and even Manon, hearing the disturbance, crept to the window, and, hiding her face, peeped trembling through her fingers, as women will.

This strange meeting parted Denys and Gerard. The former yielded to curiosity and revenge, the latter doffed his bonnet, and piously followed the poor remains of those whose fate had so nearly been his own. For some time he was the one lay mourner: but when they had reached the suburbs, a long way from the greater attraction that was filling the market-place, more than one artisan threw down his tools, and more than one shopman left his shop, and touched with pity or a sense of our common humanity, and perhaps decided somewhat by the example of Gerard, followed the bones bareheaded, and saw them deposited with the prayers of the Church in hallowed ground.

After the funeral rites Gerard stepped respectfully up to the cure, and offered to buy a mass for their souls.

Gerard, son of Catherine, always looked at two sides of a penny: and he tried to purchase this mass a trifle under the usual terms, on account of the pitiable circumstances. But the good cure gently but adroitly parried his ingenuity, and blandly screwed him up to the market price.

In the course of the business they discovered a similarity of sentiments. Piety and worldly prudence are not very rare companions: still it is unusual to carry both so far as these two men did. Their collision in the prayer market led to mutual esteem, as when knight encountered knight worthy of his steel. Moreover the good cure loved a bit of gossip, and finding his customer was one of those who had fought the thieves at Domfront, would have him into his parlour and hear the whole from his own lips. And his heart warmed to Gerard, and he said "God was good to thee. I thank Him for't with all my soul. Thou art a good lad." He added drily, "Shouldst have told me this tale in the churchyard. I doubt, I had given thee the mass for love. However," said he (the thermometer suddenly falling), "'tis ill luck to go back upon a bargain. But I'll broach a bottle of my old Medoc for thee: and few be the guests I would do that for." The cure went to his cupboard, and while he groped for the choice bottle, he muttered to himself, "At their old tricks again!"

"Plait-il?" said Gerard.

"I said nought. Ay, here 'tis."

"Nay, your reverence. You surely spoke: you said, 'At their old tricks again!'"

"Said I so in sooth?" and his reverence smiled. He then proceeded to broach the wine, and filled a cup for each. Then he put a log of wood on the fire, for stoves were none in Burgundy. "And so I said 'At their old tricks!' did I? Come, sip the good wine, and, whilst it lasts, story for story, I care not if I tell you a little tale."

Gerard's eyes sparkled.

"Thou lovest a story?"

"As my life."

"Nay, but raise not thine expectations too high, neither. 'Tis but a foolish trifle compared with thine adventures."

"Once upon a time, then, in the kingdom of France, and in the duchy of Burgundy, and not a day's journey from the town where now we sit a-sipping of old Medoc, there lived a cure. I say he lived; but barely. The parish was small, the parishioners greedy; and never gave their cure a doit more than he could compel. The nearer they brought him to a disembodied spirit by meagre diet, the holier should be his prayers in their behalf. I know not if this was their creed, but their practice gave it colour.

"At last he pickled a rod for them.

"One day the richest farmer in the place had twins to baptize. The cure was had to the christening dinner as usual; but ere he would baptize the children, he demanded, not the christening fees only, but

The condemned were ordered to stand pinioned in the marketplace for two hours, a punishment designed to allow any witnesses to identify them for future trials. They endured the full duration, standing like statues in the square, for they were creatures of the night—vampires who preyed upon weary travelers in the dark. Yet, just as the officers prepared to release them, a scream tore through the crowd. A woman pointed a trembling finger at one of the men, her eyes wide with horror, before collapsing into the arms of those who rushed to support her.

When she regained consciousness, the crowd erupted in speculation. "It was him!" someone cried. "No, it was the other!" another insisted. "Nay, it was the hangdog with the hair!" a third shouted. But their conjectures were cut short. The woman, her mind clearing in a flash of maternal instinct, lunged at the man with the golden hair hanging from his neck like a lioness. "My child! Give me back my child!" she shrieked, tearing the hair from his neck and burying her face in it. In that moment of confusion, she realized her daughter was dead. She sank to the ground, sobbing over the lock of hair, and the mob surged forward with a savage roar. The assassin's life ended instantly; in those days, every man carried death at his girdle.

Denys drew his sword immediately, shouting, "A moi, camarades!" and held the frenzied crowd at bay. "Who lays a finger on him dies," he commanded. Supported by archers, he kept the mob at a distance while appealing to their reason. "What sort of vengeance is this?" he asked, his voice cutting through the noise. "Would you rob the wheel and give the vermin an easy death?" The steel of the archers kept the mob passive, though they growled with flashing eyes. Seeing the danger, the municipal officers gathered with the guards and prudently escorted the accused back to the gaol. The mob hooted them and the prisoners alike, but Denys ensured the latter were safely lodged before heading to "The White Hart" to find Gerard.

On his way, Denys paused at a first-floor window where two girls were working. He saluted them, and they smiled, their manners easy and their complexions high. He invited them to dine at the inn; they initially refused, then consented. In this charming society, Denys momentarily forgot his concern for Gerard, who was currently being carted off to prison.

Gerard, meanwhile, had recovered his presence of mind and demanded to know by whose authority he was arrested. "By the vice-baillie's," the constable replied. "The vice-baillie?" Gerard protested, feigning innocence. "Alas! what have I, a stranger, done to offend a vice-baillie? This charge of sorcery must be a blind. I am a poor true lad far from my home." The officer's disgust was evident. "Show him the capias, Jacques," he ordered.

Jacques held the writ a yard and a half from Gerard's face, while the large constable pinned him down, fearing the prisoner might snatch the document, which they regarded with superstitious dread. Gerard, however, had no such thoughts. He craned his neck, read the writ, and to his infinite surprise discovered that the vice-bailiff who had signed it was the friendly alderman. Taking courage, he assured his captors there was an error and demanded to be taken before the alderman.

"What say you to that, Jacques?" the officer asked.
"Impossible. We have no orders to take him before his worship," Jacques replied.
"Nay, but good kind fellows, what harm can it be? I will give you each an ecu," Gerard pleaded.
"Jacques, what say you to that?"
"Humph! I say we have no orders *not* to take him to his worship," Jacques countered.
"Then say we take him to prison round by his worship."
It was agreed. They accepted the money and bade Gerard observe they were doing him a favor. Gerard saw they wanted gratitude as well as silver, but feigning was not his forte.

He entered the alderman's presence with his heart in his mouth, begging to know what offense he had committed since leaving the room with Manon and Denys. "Nought that I know of," the alderman replied. Upon seeing the writ, he explained he had signed it at daybreak, forgetting the specific offense until Gerard reminded him. "All is well. You are he I committed for sorcery. Stay! ere you go to gaol, you shall hear what your accuser says: run and fetch him."

The accuser could not be found immediately. The impatient alderman then informed Gerard that the main charge was setting a dead body ablaze with diabolical fire that flamed but did not consume. "And if 'tis true, young man, I'm sorry for thee, for thou wilt assuredly burn with fire of good pine logs in the market-place of Neufchasteau."

"Oh, sir, for pity's sake let me have speech with his reverence the cure," Gerard pleaded.
The alderman advised against it, noting that the Church was harder upon sorcerers than the corporation. "Officer, go with him to the cure; but see he 'scape you not," the alderman commanded.

They found the cure repairing a wheelbarrow. Gerard told him his tale, appealing piteously to his mercy. "Just for using a little phosphorus in self-defence against cut-throats they are going to hang."

Lucky for Gerard, he had already told his full story to the cure, who now held the situation firmly. The corporation held it by the ferule; the cure held it by the stick. The cure looked grave, summoning Gerard to a private room and ordering the officer to guard the door. The big constable stood outside, quaking, expecting to see the room fly away and leave a stench of brimstone. Instantly alone, the cure unlocked his countenance and became curious.

"Show me the trick on't," he said.
"I cannot, sir, unless the room be darkened."
The cure quickly closed out the light with a wooden shutter. "Now, then."
"But on what shall I put it?" Gerard asked. "Here is no dead face. 'Twas that made it look so dire."
The cure groped about the room and found an image of his patron saint. "Good; here is an image."
"Heaven forbid! That were profanation," Gerard objected.
"Pshaw! 'twill rub off, will't not?"
"Ay, but it goes against me to take such liberty with a saint."
"Fiddlestick!" said the divine.
"To be sure, by putting it on his holiness will show your reverence it is no Satanic art."
"Mayhap 'twas for that I did propose it," the cure said subtly.

Encouraged, Gerard fired the eyes and nostrils of the image, making the cure jump. He then lighted up the hair in patches, setting the whole face shining like a glow-worm's. "By'r Lady," shouted the cure, " 'tis strange, and small my wonder that they took you for a magician, seeing a dead face thus fired. Now come thy ways with me!"

He donned his grey gown and great hat, and in a few minutes they stood before the alderman. By his side stood the accuser, a singular figure in red hose, red shoes, a black gown with blue bands, and a cocked hat. After saluting the alderman, the cure turned to the accuser, Mangis, with good humor. "So, Mangis, at thy work again, babbling away honest men's lives! Come, your worship, this is the old tale! two of a trade can ne'er agree. Here is Mangis, who professes sorcery, and would sell himself to Satan to-night, but that Satan is not so weak as to buy what he can have gratis. This Mangis, who would be a sorcerer, but is only a quacksalver, accuses of magic a true lad, who did but use in self-defence a secret of chemistry well-known to me and all churchmen."

"But he is no churchman, to dabble in such mysteries," the alderman objected.
"He is more churchman than layman, being convent bred, and in the lesser orders," the ready cure retorted. "Therefore, sorcerer, withdraw thy plaint without more words!"
"That I will not, your reverence," Mangis replied stoutly. "A sorcerer I am, but a white one, not a black one. I make no pact with Satan... I ne'er profane the sacraments... nor turn myself into a cat and go sucking infants' blood... I but tell the peasants when their cattle and their hens are possessed... above all, it is my art and my trade to detect the black magicians..."

"Ay, Mangis. And what is the upshot of that famous fire thy tongue did kindle?" the cure asked.
"Why, their ashes were cast to the wind."
"Ay. But the true end of thy comedy is this. The parliament of Dijon hath since sifted the matter, and found they were no sorcerers, but good and peaceful citizens... Now 'tis our custom in this land, when we have slain the innocent by hearkening false knaves like thee, not to blame our credulous ears, but the false tongue that gulled them. Therefore bethink thee that, at a word from me to my lord bishop, thou wilt smell burning pine nearer than e'er knave smelt it and lived..."

The cure pointed at Mangis with his staff. "That is true i'fegs," said the alderman, "for red and black be the foul fiendys colours."
By this time, the white sorcerer's cheek was as colourless as his dress was fiery. He stammered, "I respect Holy Church and her will; he shall fire the churchyard, and all in it, for me: I do withdraw the plaint."
"Then withdraw thyself," said the vice-bailiff.

The moment Mangis was gone, the cure took a conversational tone, telling the alderman that the accused had received the chemical substance from Holy Church and had restored it by giving it all to him. "Then 'tis in good hands," the alderman replied. "Young man, you are free. Let me have your reverence's prayers."
"Doubt it not! Humph! Vice-bailiff, the town owes me four silver franks, this three months and more."
"They shall be paid, cure, ay, ere the week be out."

On this good understanding, Church and State parted. As soon as he was in the street, Gerard caught the priest's hand and kissed it. "Oh, sir! Oh, your reverence. You have saved me from the fiery stake. What can I say, what do? what?"
"Nought, foolish lad. Bounty rewards itself. Natheless--Humph?--I wish I had done't without leasing. It ill becomes my function to utter falsehoods."
"Falsehood, sir?" Gerard was mystified.
"Didst not hear me say thou hadst given me that same phosphorus? 'Twill cost me a fortnight's penance, that light word." The cure sighed, his eye twinkling cunningly.
"Nay, nay," cried Gerard eagerly. "Now Heaven forbid! That was no falsehood, father: well you knew the phosphorus was yours, is yours." And he thrust the bottle into the cure's hand. "But alas, 'tis too poor a gift: will you not take from my purse somewhat for Holy Church?" and he held out his purse with glistening eyes.
"Nay," said the other brusquely, putting his hands quickly behind him; "not a doit. Fie! fie! art pauper et exul. Come thou rather each day at noon and take thy diet with me; for my heart warms to thee;" and he went off very abruptly.

They itched. But they itched in vain. Where there's a heart there's a Rubicon.

Gerard went hastily to the inn to relieve Denys of the anxiety so long and mysterious an absence must have caused him. He found him seated at his ease, playing dice with two young ladies whose manners were unreserved and complexion high. Gerard was hurt. "N'oubliez point la Jeanneton!" he said, coloring up.
"What of her?" Denys asked, gaily rattling the dice.
"She said, 'Le peu que sont les femmes.'"
"Oh, did she? And what say you to that, mesdemoiselles?"
"We say that none run women down, but such as are too old, or too ill-favoured, or too witless to please them."
"Witless, quotha? Wise men have not folly enough to please them, nor madness enough to desire to please them," said Gerard loftily. "But 'tis to my comrade I speak, not to you, you brazen toads, that make so free with a man at first sight."
"Preach away, comrade. Fling a byword or two at our heads. Know, girls, that he is a very Solomon for bywords. Methinks he was brought up by hand on 'em."
"Be thy friendship a byword!" retorted Gerard. "The friendship that melts to nought at sight of a farthingale."
"Malheureux!" cried Denys, "I speak but pellets, and thou answerest daggers."
"Would I could," was the reply. "Adieu."
"What a little savage!" said one of the girls.
Gerard opened the door and put in his head. "I have thought of a byword," he said spitefully.
"Qui hante femmes et dez / Il mourra en pauvretez."
"There." And having delivered this thunderbolt of antique wisdom, he slammed the door viciously ere any of them could retort.

Exhausted by his anxieties, Gerard went to the bar for a morsel of bread and a cup of wine. The landlord would sell

The snow outside the town was fresh, trampled into a chaotic carpet by the innumerable wolves that haunted the road.

"We did well to heed the old man's counsel, Denys," Gerard said, his voice barely rising above the wind.

"Aye, we did," Denys replied, his breath pluming in the cold air. "Now that I think upon it, I heard them last night scurrying beneath our window, whining and howling for human flesh in that market square. Yet no fat burgher showed pity, dropping a crust to the poor vagabonds."

Gerard offered a smile, but it lacked warmth, clouded by a profound abstraction. They trudged on in silence, the crunch of snow the only sound accompanying their heavy boots.

"What weighs so heavily on your mind?" Denys asked eventually.

"Your goodness," Gerard answered simply.

Denys bristled, his good humor evaporating instantly. He was a man of immense patience, capable of enduring much impertinence, yet he flared with unprovoked anger at times. "A civil inquiry deserves a civil response," he said dryly.

"I meant no offense," Gerard insisted.

"Then why feign that you ponder my virtue, knowing full well there is no goodness beneath my skin?" Denys challenged. "If another had spoken thus, I would have retorted, 'Thou liest.' But to thee, I say, 'Thou hast no eye for men's qualities, save for women's.' Once more, I defy thy unreasonable temper. I was thinking of thy kindness of last night. Thou wouldst have wedded me to the 'Tête d'Or,' or rather to the 'tête de veau dorée,' and left thyself alone."

"Oh, are you there, lad?" Denys recovered his cheerfulness in an instant. "Truly, I meant it not for goodness, but for friendship and true fellowship. Let me confess, my young master, my conscience pricks me now for allowing you to turn your back on fortune and peaceful days. A truer friend might have steered you differently, perhaps hamstrung you slightly. Then you would have lain smarting at the 'Tête d'Or' for a month or two; that skittish lass would have nursed you tenderly, and all would have been well. I had the blade in hand to do it, but remembering how thou hatest pain, even a mere scratch, my craven heart failed me at the critical moment." Denys wore an expression of humble apology for his lack of virtuous resolve when the path of duty seemed so clear.

Gerard raised his eyebrows in astonishment at this monstrous yet thoroughly characteristic revelation. This delicate point of friendship—whether one ought to sever the Achilles tendon of a beloved friend in the name of love—was never discussed further. An incident intervened before the topic could be explored.

"Behold one riding in our rear upon his neighbor's mule!" Denys shouted.

Gerard turned. "And how do you know it is not his own?"

"Oh, blind fool! Because he rides it without discretion."

Indeed, the man came galloping like a fury. What astonished the friends most was that upon reaching them, the rustic rider's eyes widened saucer-like, and he pulled the reins so suddenly and powerfully that the mule reared, sticking out her forelegs before sliding between the pedestrians like a four-legged table on castors.

"I trow ye are from the 'Tête d'Or'?" the rider called out. They assented. "Which of you is the younger?"

"He that was born the later," Denys winked at his companion.

"Gramercy for the news."

"Come, divine then!"

"And shall. Thy beard is ripe, thy fellow's is green; he shall be the younger. Here, youngster." He held out a paper packet. "Ye left this at the 'Tête d'Or,' and our mistress sends it to you."

"Nay, good fellow, methinks I left naught." Gerard felt his pouch.

"Would ye make our burgess a liar?" the rustic reproached, his tone sharpening. "And shall I have no *pourboire*?" (even more reproachfully); "and came *ventre à terre*."

"Nay, thou shalt have *pourboire*," Gerard said, handing him a small coin.

"*À la bonne heure*," cried the clown, his features beaming with disproportionate joy. "The Virgin go with ye; come up, Jenny!" and back he went "stomach to earth," as his nation is pleased to call it.

Gerard undid the packet. It was about six inches square, and inside it he found another packet, which contained a packet, and so on. At the fourth layer, he hurled the whole thing into the snow. Denys retrieved it and rebuked his petulance. He excused himself on the grounds of hating affectation.

Denys attested, "'The great toe of the little daughter of Herodias'—there was no affectation here, but only woman's good wit. Doubtless the wraps contained something which, out of delicacy, or her sex's lovely cunning, she would not have her hind see bestowed upon a young man; thy garter, to wit."

"I wear none."

"Her own then; or a lock of her hair. What is this? A piece of raw silk fresh from the worm. Well, of all the love tokens!"

"Now who but thee ever dreamed that she is so naught as to send me love tokens? I saw no harm in her—barring her hands."

"Stay, here is something hard lurking in this soft nest. Come forth, I say, little nestling! Saints and pikestaves! Look at this!"

It was a gold ring with a great amethyst glowing and sparkling, full-colored but pure as crystal.

"How lovely!" said Gerard innocently.

"And here is something writ; read it thou! I read not so glib as some, when I know not the matter beforehand."

Gerard took the paper. "'Tis a posy, and fairly enough writ." He read the lines, blushing like a girl. They were very naive, and may be thus Englished:

*Come back to 'the Hedde of gold.'"

"The little dove!" purred Denys.

"The great owl! To go and risk her good name thus. However, thank Heaven she has played this prank with an honest lad that will ne'er expose her folly. But oh, the perverseness! Could she not bestow her nauseousness on thee?" Denys sighed and shrugged. "On thee that art as ripe for folly as herself?"

Denys confessed that his young friend had harped his very thought. 'Twas passing strange to him that a damsel with eyes in her head should pass by a man, and bestow her affections on a boy. Still, he could not but recognize in this the bounty of Nature. Boys were human beings after all, and but for this occasional caprice of women, their lot would be too terrible; they would be out of the sun altogether, blighted, and never come to anything; since only the fair could make a man out of such unpromising materials as a boy.

Gerard interrupted this flattering discourse to beg the warrior-philosopher's acceptance of the lady's ring. He refused it flatly, insisting that Gerard go back to the "Tête d'Or" at once, ring and all, like a man, and not letting a poor girl hold out her arms to him in vain.

"Her hands, you mean."

"Her hand, with the 'Tête d'Or' in it."

Failing in this, he was for putting the ring on his friend's finger. Gerard declined. "I wear a ring already."

"What, that sorry gimcrack? Why, 'tis pewter, or tin at best: and this virgin gold, forbye the jewel."

"Ay, but 'twas Margaret gave me this one; and I value it above rubies. I'll neither part with it nor give it a rival," and he kissed the base metal, bidding it fear nought.

"I see the owl hath sent her ring to a goose," said Denys sorrowfully. However, he prevailed on Gerard to fasten it inside his bonnet. To this, indeed, the lad consented very readily. For sovereign qualities were universally ascribed to certain jewels; and the amethyst ranked high among these precious talismans.

When this was disposed of, Gerard earnestly requested his friend to let the matter drop, since speaking of the other sex to him made him pine so for Margaret, and almost unmanned him with the thought that each step was taking him farther from her. "I am no general lover, Denys. There is room in my heart for one sweetheart, and for one friend. I am far from my dear mistress; and my friend, a few leagues more, and I must lose him too. Oh, let me drink thy friendship pure while I may, and not dilute it with any of these stupid females."

"And shalt, honey-pot, and shalt," said Denys kindly. "But as to my leaving thee at Remiremont, reckon thou not on that! For" (three consecutive oaths) "if I do. Nay, I shall propose to thee to stay forty-eight hours there, while I kiss my mother and sisters, and the females generally, and on go you and I together to the sea."

"Denys! Denys!"

"Denys nor me! 'Tis settled. Gainsay me not! or I'll go with thee to Rome. Why not? His Holiness the Pope hath ever some little merry pleasant war toward, and a Burgundian soldier is still welcome in his ranks."

On this, Gerard opened his heart. "Denys, ere I fell in with thee, I used often to halt on the road, unable to go farther: my puny heart so pulled me back: and then, after a short prayer to the saints for aid, would I rise and drag my most unwilling body onward. But since I joined company with thee, great is my courage. I have found the saying of the ancients true, that better is a bright comrade on the weary road than a horse-litter; and, dear brother, when I do think of what we have done and suffered together! Savedst my life from the bear, and from yet more savage thieves; and even poor I did make shift to draw thee out of Rhine, and somehow loved thee double from that hour. How many ties tender and strong between us! Had I my will, I'd never, never, never, never part with my Denys on this side the grave. Well-a-day! God His will be done."

"No, my will shall be done this time," shouted Denys. "Le bon Dieu has bigger fish to fry than you or me. I'll go with thee to Rome. There is my hand on it."

"Think what, you say! 'Tis impossible. 'Tis too selfish of me."

"I tell thee, 'tis settled. No power can change me. At Remiremont I borrow ten pieces of my uncle, and on we go; 'tis fixed."

They shook hands over it. Then Gerard said nothing, for his heart was too full; but he ran twice round his companion as he walked, then danced backwards in front of him, and finally took his hand, and so on they went hand in hand like sweethearts, till a company of mounted soldiers, about fifty in number, rose to sight on the brow of a hill.

"See the banner of Burgundy," said Denys joyfully; "I shall look out for a comrade among these."

"How gorgeous is the standard in the sun," said Gerard. "And how brave are the leaders with velvet and feathers, and steel breastplates like glassy mirrors!"

When they came near enough to distinguish faces, Denys uttered an exclamation: "Why, 'tis the Bastard of Burgundy, as I live. Nay, then; there is fighting afoot since he is out; a gallant leader, Gerard, rates his life no higher than a private soldier's, and a soldier's no higher than a tomtit's; and that is the captain for me."

"And see, Denys, the very mules with their great brass frontlets and trappings seem proud to carry them; no wonder men itch to be soldiers;" and in the midst of this innocent admiration the troop came up with them.

"Halt!" cried a stentorian voice. The troop halted. The Bastard of Burgundy bent his brow gloomily on Denys: "How now, arbalestrier, how comes it thy face is turned southward, when every good hand and heart is hurrying northward?"

Denys replied respectfully that he was going on leave, after some years of service, to see his kindred at Remiremont.

"Good. But this is not the time for't; the duchy is disturbed. Ho! bring that dead soldier's mule to the front; and thou mount her and forward with us to Flanders."

"So please your highness," said Denys firmly, "that may not be. My home is close at hand. I have not seen it these three years; and above all, I have this poor youth in charge, whom I may not, cannot leave, till I see him shipped for Rome."

"Dost bandy words with me?" said the chief, with amazement, turning fast to wrath. "Art weary o' thy life? Let go the youth's hand, and into the saddle without more idle words."

Denys made no reply; but he held Gerard's hand the tighter, and looked defiance.

At this the Bastard roared, "Jarnac, dismount six of thy archers, and shoot me this white-livered cur dead where he stands—for an example."

The young Count de Jarnac, second in command, gave the order, and the

The rough roads of travel hold a peculiar charm for the English soul, even for those who wander not as pilgrims of love. There is a fascination in the records of such journeys, much like the interest one finds in a fossil—antique friendship preserved in stone. Yet, since the true heart of our story beats in Holland, it is time to return there, to the ordinary lives and common incidents that have shaped human history in every age.

Jorian Ketel arrived at Peter's house to claim Margaret's promise, but he found her confined to her bed by illness. Peter, upon hearing his purpose, treated him with open affront, warning him to leave the premises. Those standing nearby were ready to dunk him in the water, for both father and daughter were beloved figures, and the entire town of Sevenbergen was in a state of hot, undiscriminating agitation, fueled by popular sympathy.

Humiliated and angry, Jorian departed in dudgeon, repenting his earlier kindness. Such penitence is not uncommon and often carries the sincerity of genuine remorse. Elsewhere, Dierich Brower, caught at "The Three Kings" attempting to buy information about Martin Wittenhaagen through drunkenness, was seized and thrown into a horsepond, threatened with further punishment if he ever returned to the burgh. Municipal jealousy soon flared; the burgomaster of Sevenbergen sent a formal letter to his counterpart in Tergou, reminding him that he had overstepped the law and inviting him to handle any future complaints against Sevenbergen's citizens.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, suppressing his rage at this rebuke, replied with feigned civility. He claimed the man he pursued was a Tergovian named Gerard who had stolen the town records. Since Gerard had fled to foreign lands, likely taking the documents with him, the matter was supposedly closed. Thus, Ghysbrecht made a virtue of necessity, though his calm was merely a veil. Baffled by the failure in Sevenbergen, he redirected his emissaries to the Tergou family to discover where Gerard had fled. To his infinite surprise, they knew nothing. This uncertainty gnawed at him; he feared Gerard was lurking nearby, waiting to make a discovery and exact a terrible revenge. From this moment, Dierich and others noted a darkening in Ghysbrecht's demeanor. He became moody and irritable, haunted by a dread that caused his eyes to cast furtive glances, as if expecting a blow from an unknown quarter. The misery he inflicted on others had failed to bring him happiness, for rarely does cruelty satisfy the soul.

The small family at Tergou, which might have resolved its differences without exiling Gerard to a distant land, retained its outward appearance but lost its inner warmth. Little Kate knew the role Cornelis and Sybrandt played in banishing Gerard. Though she never spoke of it to her mother to avoid further strife, she shuddered at their sight and blushed at their hypocritical regrets. Catherine, observing with a woman's vigilance and subtlety, said nothing but watched closely. The brothers, trying to share the family's gloom, succeeded in deceiving their father and Giles, yet the demure satisfaction beneath their actions could not escape the feminine eyes of the household.

Mistrust and suspicion now sat at the table, poor substitutes for Gerard's intelligent face, which had once brightened the entire circle. The old hosier, wounded in his pride by his son's disobedience, bore himself stiffly, refusing to mention Gerard's name. Yet beneath his Spartan exterior, Nature tugged at his heart-strings. He could not conceal one anxiety: "If I but knew where the boy is, and that his life and health are safe, my care would be small," he would sigh, followed by a deep breath. One imagines that if Gerard had walked in at that moment, there would have been many tears and embraces, and few, if any, reproaches.

The family was taken by surprise by the sudden publicity surrounding Gerard's departure. Within a week, his adventures were the talk of the town. Popular sympathy rallied warmly behind the lovers and against Gerard's cruel parents and the meddling burgomaster who had interfered in matters that did not concern him.

"Mother," Kate said softly, "it is known throughout the town that Margaret is suffering from a fever—a burning fever. Her father fears for her greatly."

"Margaret? What Margaret?" Catherine asked, feigning a treacherous calm.

"Oh, Mother! Whom else could I mean? Gerard's Margaret."

"Gerard's Margaret!" Catherine screamed. "How dare you utter such a word in this house? Never mention that hussy's name again. She has laid bare my life. She is the ruin of my poor boy, the flower of my flock. Because of her, he is not a holy priest among us but roams the world, leaving me a desolate, broken-hearted mother." She paused, seeing Kate's distress, and softened her tone. "Do not cry, my girl. I speak harshly because I bear up before you all; I must swallow my fears. But at night, I see him in my dreams, always in trouble. Sometimes torn by wild beasts, other times held by robbers with cruel knives raised to strike his pale face. Oh, Kate! When I remember that while I sit here in comfort, my poor boy may lie dead in some savage place because of that girl... her name is ratsbane to me. I tremble when I hear it."

"I will say nothing to grieve you further, Mother," Kate replied tenderly, though a sigh escaped her.

Outside, Margaret was widely spoken of and even pitied. The young men and maidens of Sevenbergen cast pitying glances at the little window where the village beauty lay "dying for love." In this familiar phrase, they underestimated her spirit and selflessness. Gerard was not dead, and she remained too loyal to doubt his constancy. Her father was dear and helpless, and but for her physical weakness, her love for Gerard would not have prevented her from fulfilling her duties, though she performed them with a drooping head and heavy heart. However, the strain of physical and mental excitement had triggered a violent fever. Only her youth and constitution saved her. Though the malady eventually left her, it did so leaving her in a terrible state of bodily weakness, where life felt like a burden.

It is in such moments that love and friendship become mortal angels, bringing comfort with their voices and healing with their hands. But this poor girl had to find a way back to life and vigor on her own. Many days she lay alone, watching heavy hours roll over her like leaden waves. In her enfeebled state, existence seemed a weight, and life a thing already gone by. She lacked the strength to strive for recovery, for Gerard was gone, and she had no one left to recover for. Often she lay motionless for hours, tears welling gently from her eyes.

One day, waking from an uneasy sleep, she found two women in her room. One was a servant; the other, distinguished by the deep fur on her collar and sleeves, was a woman of consideration. A narrow band of silvery hair spared by her coiffure revealed she was past the age when sensible women conceal their years. Both women looked kind and friendly. Margaret tried to raise herself in bed, but the old lady gently placed a hand to stop her.

"Lie still, sweetheart. We come not to put you about, but to comfort you, God willing. Cheer up a bit, and tell us, who do you think we are?"

"Nay, madam, I know you, though I have never seen you before. You are the demoiselle Van Eyck, and this is Reicht Heynes. Gerard has often spoken of you and your goodness to him. Madam, he has no friend like you near him now," she said, sinking back as tears welled instantly.

The good-natured Reicht Heynes began to cry for company, but her mistress chided her gently. "Well, you are a pretty one for a sick-room," she said, employing innocent art to cheer the patient, with some success. An old woman who has seen life and all its troubles is a sovereign blessing beside a sorrowful young woman. She knows what to say and what to avoid, how to soothe and interest. Within an hour, Margaret's head rested on the old lady's shoulder instead of the pillow, her soft eyes dwelling there with gentle gratitude.

"Ahh! This is hair," the old lady said, running her fingers through it. "Come and look at it, Reicht!"

Reicht approached and handled it, praising it sincerely. The poor girl who owned it was not entirely immune to flattery, perhaps because she was not yet dead.

"In sooth, madam, I used to think it hideous; but he praised it, and ever since then I have been almost vain of it, saints forgive me. You know how foolish those are that love."

"They are greater fools that don't," the old lady replied sharply.

Margaret opened her lovely eyes to seek her meaning. This was only the beginning of many visits. Either Margaret Van Eyck or Reicht came nearly every day until the patient was convalescent, and she improved rapidly under their care. Reicht attributed this largely to the nourishing dishes she prepared in Peter's kitchen, but Margaret valued more the kind words and eyes that constantly reminded her she had friends to live for.

Meanwhile, Martin Wittenhaagen went straight to Rotterdam to take the bull by the horns. The bull was a biped with a crown for horns: Philip the Good, Duke of Burgundy. Upon arriving in Rotterdam, Martin learned the court was at Ghent. He went there seeking an audience but was baffled by lackeys and pages. Undeterred, he threw himself in the sovereign's path while hunting and, contrary to all court precedents, began the conversation by roaring lustily for mercy.

"Why, where is the peril, man?" the Duke asked, looking around and laughing.

"Grace for an old soldier hunted down by burghers!" Martin cried.

Kings differ in character like other people, yet they share one trait: a mighty inclination to be affable toward men of very low estate. These subjects do not vie with them in anything, so jealousy cannot creep in; they amuse themselves with the bluntness and novelty of such men, refreshing them with a touch of nature—a rarity in courts. Philip the Good reined in his horse and granted Martin almost a private conversation. Martin reminded him of a certain battlefield where he had received an arrow intended for his sovereign. The Duke remembered the incident perfectly and was graciously pleased to view it cheerfully. He could afford to laugh, for he had not been the one hit.

Martin then recounted his Majesty's capture in the church, his imprisonment in the tower, the maneuver that freed him, and all the details of the hunt. Whether he told it better than I have, or the Duke had not heard so many good stories, it is certain that the sovereign became so engrossed that when courtiers galloped up to interrupt, he swore like a costermonger and threatened, half in jest, to cut off the next head that came between him and a good story. When Martin finished, the Duke cried out:

"St. Luke! What sport goes on in this mine earldom, ay! in my own woods, and I see it not. You base fellows have all the luck." He was indignant at the partiality of Fortune. "Lo you now! This was a man-hunt," he said. "I never had the luck to be at a man-hunt."

"My luck was none so great," Martin replied bluntly. "I was on the wrong side of the dogs' noses."

"Ah! So you were; I forgot that." Royalty was more reconciled to its lot. "What would you then?"

"A free pardon, your highness, for myself and Gerard."

"For what?"

"For prison-breaking."

"Go to; the bird will fly from the cage. 'Tis instinct. Besides, why coop a young man up for loving a young woman? These burgomasters must be void of common sense. What else?"

"For striking down the burgomaster."

"Oh, the hunted boar will turn to bay. 'Tis his right; and I hold him less than man that grudges it him. What else?"

"For killing of the bloodhounds."

The Duke's countenance fell.

"'Twas their life or mine," Martin said eagerly.

"Ay! But I can't have my bloodhounds, my beautiful bloodhounds, sacrificed to—

"No, no, no! They were not your dogs."

"Whose dogs, then?"

"The ranger's."

"Oh. Well, I am very sorry for him, but as I was saying, I can't have my old soldiers sacrificed to his bloodhounds. Thou shalt have thy free pardon."

"And poor Gerard."

"And poor Gerard too, for thy sake. And more, tell thou this burgomaster his doings mislike me: this is to set up for a king, not a burgomaster. I'll have no kings in Holland but one. Bid him be more humble; or by St. Jude I'll hang him before his own door, as I hanged the burgomaster of some town in Flanders, no, 'twas somewhere in Brabant—no matter—I hanged him, I remember that much—for oppressing poor folk."

The Duke then beckoned his chancellor, a pursy old fellow who rode like a sack, and ordered him to write out a free pardon for Martin and one Gerard.

This precious document was drawn up in form and signed the next day. Martin hastened home with it.

Margaret had left her bed a few days prior and was sitting pale and pensive by the fireside when he burst in, waving the parchment and crying, "A free pardon, girl, for Gerard as well as me! Send for him back when you will; all the burgomasters on earth daren't lay a finger on him."

She flushed all over with joy, her hands trembling with eagerness as she took the parchment and devoured it with her eyes. She kissed it again and again, flung her arms around Martin's neck, and kissed him. When she was calmer, she told him that Heaven had raised her a friend in

Margaret Van Eyck's heart remained as green and vigorous as her namesake, Margaret Brandt. Though forty years had passed, the old artist possessed a youthful ardour for the younger woman. This affection was no fleeting caprice; Margaret Van Eyck, sharp-eyed and discerning, recognized in Margaret Brandt a genuineness rare among women of her sex. Beyond this sterling quality, the younger woman held a powerful ally in the bosom of the elder, an ally rooted in the strange, immutable laws of human nature.

It is a curious truth, noted by Greek philosophers and immortalized by the British Homer, that our hearts warm more readily to those we have benefited than to our benefactors. The bard recalled how memories of shared battles, of shielding friends from arrows and bearing them to shore, swept away even the strongest purposes of revenge. Here, the assassin's hand was stayed not by gratitude for favors received, but by the memory of kindnesses conferred.

Margaret Van Eyck had indeed been wonderfully kind. She had broken her own habits to visit the sickbed, nursing and soothing Margaret Brandt until the recipient of her goodness loved her more tenderly than she had ever loved Gerard. Thus, when the old lady saw the roses blooming on Margaret's cheek and read the parchment that caused them, she surrendered her own plans without a murmur.

"Sweetheart," she said gently, "I desired he should stay in Italy five or six years, return rich, and become a master artist. But your happiness comes first. Since you cannot live without him, we must bring him home as soon as possible."

"Oh, madam! You see my very thoughts," the young woman replied, hanging her head and blushing. "But how shall I let him know? That passes my skill. He is gone to Italy, though I know not which city he visits. He mentioned Florence and Rome... but surely a letter addressed 'My Gerard—Italy' might miscarry?" She looked imploringly at her friend for counsel.

"You are come to the right place and at the right time," the old lady declared. "Hans Memling was with me today. He is going to Italy no later than next week, 'to improve his hand,' he says. Not before 'twas needed, I assure you."

"But how shall he find my Gerard?"

"Why, he knows your Gerard, child. They have supped here more than once; they are like hand and glove. Since his business is the same as Gerard's, he will visit the same places and must soon fall in with him. Get you a long letter written, copy this pardon into it, and I will answer for the messenger. In six months at farthest, Gerard shall receive it. When he does, he will kiss it, press it to his bosom, and fly home. What are you smiling at? And why are your cheeks so red? You are smothering me, I cannot think. Yes! Happy days are coming to my little pearl."

Meanwhile, in the kitchen, Martin sat before the black-jack while Reicht Heynes spun beside him. The night was spent pumping Martin for information, and the atmosphere grew heavy with anticipation.

Hans Memling, an old pupil of Jan Van Eyck and his sister, was a painter of considerable talent and a good soul, save for one fault: he loved the "nipperkin, canakin, and the brown bowl" more than they deserved. This penchant prevented him from amassing fortune and often led him to seek meals or a groat from Margaret Van Eyck. Yet, this dependence gave her a claim on him; she knew he would not trifle with any commission she entrusted to him.

The letter was duly written and left with the old artist. The following week, Hans Memling returned from Flanders. Margaret Van Eyck handed him the letter and a piece of gold for his travels. He seemed in a hurry to depart.

"All the better," said the old artist. "He will be the sooner in Italy."

But as horses that burn to start often require the whip after the first yard, so Hans's urgency cooled into inaction once he reached the principal hostelry of Tergou. There, he found two of his boon companions sitting in the bay window. He entered for a parting glass, but when he offered to pay, they refused. They insisted on treating him, for he was going on a long journey.

The result was that Hans's tongue became as loose as if the wine were oil. Confiding in his convivial crew, he boasted of showing the Italians how to paint, sang of his exploits in battle with the pike, and recounted his amorous successes. His speech flowed in a torrent of miscellaneous matters until, in a moment of drunken blabbering, he revealed he was entrusted with a letter to a townsman of theirs, one Gerard, a good fellow. He added, "You are all good fellows," and slapped Sybrandt on the back so heartily that he drove the breath from the man's body.

Sybrandt circled the table to avoid this muscular approval but listened intently, learning for the first time that Gerard had gone to Italy. To confirm it, he feigned doubt.

"My brother Gerard is never in Italy."

"Ye lie, ye cur!" roared Hans, instantly turning irascible and failing to recognize the man he had just praised as the very person he sought. "If he is ten times your brother, he is in Italy. What call ye this? There, read me that superscription!" He flung the letter onto the table.

Sybrandt examined it gravely but claimed he could not read. However, one of the company, boasting of a rare accomplishment, took the paper and read aloud:

"To Gerard Eliassoen, of Tergou. These by the hand of the trusty Hans Memling, with all speed."

"'Tis excellently well writ," said the reader, inspecting every letter.

"Ay!" Hans declared bombastically. "'Tis writ by a famous hand; by Margaret, sister of Jan Van Eyck. Blessed and honoured be his memory! She is an old friend of mine."

After diversifying into forty topics, Hans departed. Sybrandt stole out to find Cornelis. Together, they plotted. Italy was an immense distance; if they could only keep Gerard there, they might secure his share of their father's substance.

"Keep him there? Nothing would keep him long from his Margaret," Cornelis countered.

"Curse her!" Sybrandt snapped. "Why didn't she die when she was about it?"

"She die? She would outlive the pest to vex us," Cornelis replied, wroth at her selfishness.

These two black sheep, tainting each other with their corruption, conceived a plan to keep Gerard in Italy forever. Yet, when they had planned it, they were no nearer execution; iniquity required talent, and they lacked it. Then, as if Satan whispered simultaneously into their ears, they both burst out:

They went to Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, who received them eagerly. Suspense is intolerable; certainty, though painful, offers relief.

"You have news of Gerard?" he asked eagerly.

They told him of the letter and Hans Memling. Ghysbrecht listened with restless eyes. "Who wrote the letter?"

"Margaret Van Eyck," they replied, assuming the contents matched the superscription.

"Are ye sure?" Ghysbrecht asked, drawing forth a paper written by Margaret while negotiating for her house. "Was it writ like this?"

"Yes. 'Tis the same writing," Sybrandt said boldly.

"Good. And now, what would ye of me?" Ghysbrecht asked, his heart beating beneath a mask of feigned carelessness. The brothers fumbled with their bonnets, stammered, and hesitated, gradually revealing their desire for a letter to keep Gerard in Italy—a letter to substitute in Hans Memling's wallet.

While the brothers vacillated between respect for the burgomaster and suspicion of his own roguishness, pros and cons coursed through Ghysbrecht's spirit. Vengeance urged him to let Gerard return and feel the weight of the law; prudence advised keeping him a thousand miles away. But prudence also warned against dirty work on a doubtful chance, lest these rogues tarnish his name. Finally, convinced that Gerard possessed a secret that could wound him to the quick, Ghysbrecht found words: "It is my duty to aid the citizens that cannot write. But for their matter, I will not be responsible. Tell me, then, what I shall write."

"Something about this Margaret," Sybrandt suggested.

"Ay, ay! That she is false, that she is married to another, I'll go bail."

"Nay, burgomaster, nay! Not for all the world!" Sybrandt cried. "Gerard would not believe it, or but half, and then he would come back to see. No; say that she is dead."

"Dead! What, at her age, will he credit that?"

"Sooner than the other. Why she was nearly dead; so it is not to say a downright lie, after all."

"Humph! And you think that will keep him in Italy?"

"We are sure of it, are we not, Cornelis?"

"Ay," said Cornelis. "Our Gerard will never leave Italy now he is there. It was always his dream to get there. He would come back for his Margaret, but not for us. What cares he for us? He despises his own family; always did."

"This would be a bitter pill to him," said the old hypocrite.

"It will be for his good in the end," replied the young one.

"What avails Famine wedding Thirst?" Cornelis asked.

"And the grief you are preparing for him so coolly?" Ghysbrecht spoke sarcastically, yet tasting his own vengeance.

"Oh, a lie is not like a blow with a curtal axe. It hacks no flesh, and breaks no bones."

"A curtal axe?" Sybrandt asked, shooting a sly, envenomed glance at the burgomaster's broken nose. "No, nor even like a stroke with a cudgel."

Ghysbrecht's face darkened with ire when the adder's tongue struck his wound. But it told, as intended; the old man bristled with hate.

"Well," he said, "tell me what to write for you, and I must write it; but take notice, you bear the blame if aught turns amiss. Not the hand which writes, but the tongue which dictates, doth the deed."

The brothers assented warmly, sneering within. Ghysbrecht drew his inkhorn, laid the specimen of Margaret's writing before him, and made inquiries regarding the size and shape of the letter. Just then, an unlooked-for interruption occurred. Jorian Ketel burst hastily into the room, looking vexed at not finding him alone.

"Thou seest I have matter on hand, good fellow," Ghysbrecht said.

"Ay; but this is grave. I bring good news; but 'tis not for every ear."

The burgomaster rose and drew Jorian aside into the embrasure of his deep window. The brothers heard them converse in low but eager tones. It ended with Ghysbrecht sending Jorian out to saddle his mule. He then addressed the brothers with a sudden coldness that amazed them.

"I prize the peace of households; but this is not a thing to be done in a hurry: we will see about it, we will see."

"But, burgomaster, the man will be gone. It will be too late."

"Where is he?"

"At the hostelry, drinking."

"Well, keep him drinking! We will see, we will see." And he sent them off discomfited.

To explain this, we must retrograde a step. That very morning, Margaret Brandt had met Jorian Ketel near her own door. He passed her with a scowl, which struck her.

"Stay," she said. "Yes! It is the good man who saved him. Oh! Why have you not been near me since? And why have you not come for the parchments? Was it not true about the hundred crowns?"

Jorian gave a snort but, seeing her candid face, began to think there might be a mistake. He told her he had come and how he had been received.

"Alas!" she said. "I knew nought of this. I lay at Death's door. She then invited him to follow her, took him into the garden, and showed him the spot where the parchments were buried. Martin was for taking them up, but I would not let him. He put them there; and I said none should move them but you, who had earned them so well of him and me."

"Give me a spade!" Jorian cried eagerly. "But stay! No; he is a suspicious man. You are sure they are there still?"

"I will openly take the blame if human hand hath touched them."

"Then keep them but two hours more, I prithee, good Margaret," said Jorian, and ran off to the Stadthouse of Tergou a joyful man.

The burgomaster jogged along towards Sevenbergen with Jorian striding beside him, giving assurance that the missing parchments would be in his hand within an hour.

"Ah, master!" Jorian said. "Lucky for us it wasn't a thief that took them."

"Not a thief? Not a thief? What call you him, then?"

"Well, saving your presence, I call him a jackdaw. This is jackdaw's work, if ever there was: 'take the thing you are least in need of, and hide it'—that's a jackdaw. I should know," Jorian added oracularly, "for I was brought up along with a chough. He and I were born the same year, but he cut his teeth long before me, and wow! but my life was a burden for years all along of him. If you had but a hole in your hose no bigger than a groat, in went his beak like a gimlet; and, for stealing, Gerard all over. What he wanted least, and any poor Christian in

Denys, positioned squarely between his companions to prevent any desperate flight, was borne away in a torment of grief and self-reproach. For his sake, Gerard had forsaken the German road to Rome; and what recompense awaited him? To be cast adrift, utterly alone in the heart of Burgundy. This thought drove Denys to madness, causing him to rage against the heavens and the earth, or to lapse into a silence so dark and menacing that his captors deemed it wise to subdue him. They seized their leader just beyond the town walls, and the entire cavalcade halted at the "Tête d'Or" to rest.

The young landlady, though preoccupied with the Count and even more so with the Bastard, spotted Denys and queried him anxiously regarding the fate of his youthful companion. With a sudden outburst of sorrow, Denys recounted everything, imploring her to dispatch someone in search of Gerard. "Now that he is severed from me, he may yet heed my counsel," he declared. "Poor wretch, he cannot bear solitude."

The landlady shook her head dismissively. "I believe I have already shown you too much kindness," she replied, flushing slightly, before turning her back.

"You will not?" Denys pressed.

"Not I."

"Then," he erupted, unleashing a torrent of curses and invectives upon her.

She wheeled away, retreating while whimpering that she was unused to such treatment, and immediately ordered her servant to saddle two mules. Denys rode north with his troop, head bowed and spirit crushed, unaware that the truthful young woman had donned her riding gear in a mere forty minutes, driven by urgency, and spurred off with her servant in the exact opposite direction.

By nightfall, after a grueling march, the Bastard and his men arrived at "The White Hart." Their entrance stirred a tremendous commotion, and it took some time before Manon discerned her old friend amidst the crowd. When she finally recognized him, her reaction was marked only by a deepening flush; she made no move to acknowledge the acquaintance. The poor soul had already begun to despise her.

"The base steps by which she climbed."

Denys observed her but could not muster a smile. The inn brought memories of Gerard too sharply to the forefront.

Before night fully fell, the wind shifted. Manon entered the room and beckoned him with a finger. He rose sullenly, followed by his guards.

"Nay, I wish to speak a word to thee in private."

She led him to a corner of the chamber and whispered, asking if he would grant her a favor.

He answered aloud, "No, I would not; I am not in the mood to bestow kindnesses on man or woman. If I were to show kindness, it should be to a dog; and even then, only if I could help it."

"Alas, good archer, I once did you a kindness, you and your pretty comrade," Manon said humbly.

"You did, dame, you did; well then, for his sake—what is required?"

"You know my tale. I was unfortunate. Now I am honored. But a woman cast him in my teeth this day. And so he shall hang there forever. I would have him taken down; alas!"

"With all my heart."

"And none dare I ask but thee. Wilt thou do it?"

"Not I, even were I not a prisoner."

Upon this stern refusal, the tender Manon sighed and clasped her palms together in despair. Denys assured her she need not fret. There were soldiers of a lower caliber who would not hesitate to take two bites out of such a cherry. It was merely a matter of coin; if she could provide two angels, he would find two soldiers to perform the dirty work at "The White Hart."

This proposition was not entirely palatable. Yet, reflecting that soldiers were transient creatures, drinking here tonight and knocked on the head tomorrow, she whispered softly, "Send them out to me. But pray, tell them it is for one who is my friend; let them not think it is for me; I should sink into the earth; times have changed."

Denys found warriors eager to win an angel apiece so easily. He dispatched them and instantly dismissed the subject with contempt, sitting instead to brood over his lost friend.

Manon and the warriors soon reached a general understanding. But what were they to do with the body once removed? She murmured, "The river is near—the place."

"Fling him in, eh?"

"Nay, nay; be not so cruel! Could ye not place him—gently—and—with somewhat weighty?"

She must have been contemplating the matter in detail, for she was not one to whom ideas came swiftly.

All was quickly agreed upon, save for the timing of payment. The mail-clad warriors itched for it and sought it in advance. Manon hesitated at that.

"What? Did she doubt their word?" Then let her come along with them, or watch them from a distance.

"Me?" Manon exclaimed with horror. "I would sooner die than witness it done."

"Which yet you would have done."

"Ay, for sore is my need. Times have changed."

She had already forgotten her precept to Denys.

An hour later, the disagreeable relic of caterpillar existence ceased to fester the worshipful matron's public life, and the grim eyes of the past ceased to cast malignant glances down into the white hind's clover field.

In total, she made the landlord an average wife, a prime house-dog, and outlived everyone.

Her troops, upon returning from executing with medieval naivety the precept "Off wi' the auld love," received a shock. They found the market-place black with groups; it had been empty an hour prior. Conscience struck them. This was the result of meddling with the dead. However, the bolder of the two, encouraged by the darkness, stole forward alone and slyly mingled with a group. He soon returned to his companion, saying in a tone of reproach that was not strictly reasonable,

"You born fool, it is only a miracle."

The church wall bore the scorch marks of letters, a supernatural exclamation asking why no mass was held for the Duke during this time of turmoil. The spectacle had drawn a crowd, their faces fixed in gaping wonder until the phenomenon faded, leaving only the silence of the stone. Yet, the Corporation, unwilling to lag behind divine loyalty to a temporal sovereign, promptly commissioned masses. From this pious transaction, the Cure, an old friend of ours, extracted a handsome sum—a fact he certainly did not regret, given his discerning taste for hard cash. But I shall not detain you with such trivial miracles.

Denys pleaded for his arms. "Why disgrace him as well as break his heart?" he asked.

"Then swear on the cross of thy sword not to leave the bastard's service until the sedition is quelled." Reluctantly yielding to necessity, Denys delivered three volleys of oaths, reclaiming his weapons and his liberty.

The troops halted at an inn known as "The Three Fish." Marion, seeing him, cried out, "I'm out of luck; who would have thought to see you again?" Noticing his somber demeanor rather than amusement at her blunt jest, she inquired what troubled him. He confessed his plight. She, however, took a bright view of the case. Gerard was too handsome and well-behaved to come to harm; moreover, the women would always be on his side. She reasoned that things must either go well or ill. If well, he would join a company bound for Rome; if ill, he would return home, perhaps arriving before his friend, for he had fighting to do in Flanders by all accounts. She then produced gold pieces, which he steadfastly refused, urging her repeatedly to keep them. Denys was somewhat convinced by her logic, for she concurred with his own wishes, and he found comfort in her honesty. It made him think a little better of that world in which his poor little friend walked alone.

Small detachments of foot soldiers, reduced to pairs and trios, were already straggling toward Flanders, many penniless, passing from town to town under the bailiffs' orders for food and lodging. Anthony of Burgundy overtook these scattered groups, gathering them under his standard until he entered Flanders at the head of six hundred men. Upon crossing the frontier, he was met by his brother Baldwyn, bringing men, arms, and provisions. Anthony organized his force and marched in battle array through several towns, meeting not only no impediment but great acclamations. This display of loyalty drew comments that were not altogether gracious.

"This rebellion of ours is a bite," growled a soldier named Simon, who had elected himself Denys's comrade.

Denys said nothing, but made a silent vow to St. Mars to shoot Anthony of Burgundy dead should the rebellion, which had cost him Gerard, prove anything other than a rebellion.

That afternoon, they came in sight of a strongly fortified town. A whisper ran through the little army that this was a disaffected place. Yet, when they drew near, the great gate stood open, and the flanking towers were manned by a single sentinel apiece. The advancing force broke formation, marching carelessly forward.

Within a furlong, the drawbridge across the moat rose slowly and creaking until it stood vertical against the fort. The very moment it settled into this warlike attitude, the portcullis rattled down at the gate, and the towers and curtains bristled with lances and crossbows. A stern hum ran through the Bastard's front rank and spread to the rear.

"Halt!" cried he. The word traveled down the line, and they stopped. "Herald to the gate!" A pursuivant spurred out, halted twenty yards from the gate, raised his bugle with the herald's flag hanging round it, and blew a summons. A tall figure in brazen armor appeared over the gate. Fiery words passed between him and the herald, audible only in their import: the herald blew a single keen, threatening note at the walls and galloped back with war in his face. The Bastard moved out of the line to meet him. Their heads had not been together two seconds ere he turned in his saddle and shouted, "Pioneers, to the van!" In a moment, hedges were leveled, the force took the field, and encamped just out of shot from the walls. Mounted officers flew south, east, and west to friendly towns for catapults, palisades, mantelets, raw hides, tar-barrels, carpenters, provisions, and all materials for a siege.

The bright perspective mightily cheered one drooping soldier. At the first clang of the portcullis, his eyes brightened and his temples flushed. When the herald returned with battle in his eye, he saw it instantly, and for the first time in many days cried, "Courage, tout le monde, le diable est mort."

If that great warrior heard, how he must have grinned!

The besiegers encamped a furlong from the walls and made roads. They kept their pikemen in camp ready for assault and sent forward their sappers, pioneers, catapultiers, and crossbowmen. These opened the siege by filling the moat, mining, or breaching the wall. Since much of this work had to be done under close fire of arrows, quarrels, bolts, stones, and rocks, these artists "had need of a hundred eyes." They acted in concert with a vigilance, individual intelligence, daring, and skill that made a siege very interesting, even amusing to lookers-on.

First, they advanced their carpenters behind rolling mantelets to erect a stockade high and strong on the very edge of the moat. Some lives were lost, but not many; for a strong force of crossbowmen, including Denys, rolled their mantelets up and shot over the workmen's heads at every besieged who showed his nose, and at every loophole or aperture commanding the spot. Covered by this condensed fire, they soon raised a high palisade between themselves and the ordinary missiles from the pierced masonry.

But the besieged expected this. At night, they ran out their boards or wooden penthouses on top of the curtains. The curtains were built with square holes near the top to receive beams supporting these structures—the true defense of medieval forts. From here, the besieged delivered missiles with far more freedom and variety of range than they could shoot through the oblique but immovable loopholes of the curtain or the sloping crenelets of the higher towers. On this, the besiegers brought up mangonels, setting them hurling huge stones at these woodworks and battering them to pieces. Contemporaneously, they built a triangular wooden tower as high as the curtain, keeping it ready for use just out of shot.

This was a terrible sight to the besieged. These wooden towers had taken many a town. They began to mine underneath the part of the moat the tower stood frowning at, making other preparations to give it a warm reception. The besiegers also mined at another part, aiming to get under the square barbican and throw it down. All this time, Denys was behind his mantelet with another arbalestrier, protecting the workmen and making excellent shots. These ended by earning him the esteem of an unseen archer, who every now and then sent a winged compliment quivering into his mantelet. One struck within an inch of the narrow slit through which Denys was squinting.

"Peste," cried he. "You shoot well, my friend. Come forth and receive my congratulations! Shall merit such as thine hide its head? Comrade, it is one of those cursed Englishmen, with his half-ell shaft. I'll not die till I've had a shot at London wall."

On the side of the besieged was a figure that soon attracted great notice by promenading under fire. It was a tall knight, clad in complete brass, carrying a light but prodigiously long lance, with which he directed the movements of the besieged. When any disaster befell the besiegers, this tall knight and his long lance were pretty sure to be concerned in it.

My young reader will say, "Why did not Denys shoot him?" Denys did shoot him; every day of his life; other arbalestriers shot him; archers shot him. Everybody shot him. He was there to be shot, apparently. But the abomination was, he did not mind being shot. Worse, he became so demoralized as not to seem to know when he was hit. He walked his battlements under fire, as some stout skipper paces his deck in a suit of Flushing, calmly oblivious of the April drops falling on his woollen armor. At last, the besiegers grew spiteful and would not waste any more good steel on him, cursing him and his impervious coat of mail. He took those missiles like the rest.

Gunpowder has spoiled war. War was always detrimental to the solid interests of mankind. But in old times it was good for something: it painted well, sang divinely, furnished Iliads. But invisible butchery, under a pall of smoke a furlong thick, leaves no one the better for it. A poet with his notebook may repeat, "Suave etiam belli certamina magna tueri"; but the sentiment is hollow and savors of cuckoo. You cannot "tueri" anything but a horrid row. He didn't say, "Suave etiam ingentem caliginem tueri per campos instructam." They managed better in the Middle Ages.

This siege was a small affair, yet a writer or minstrel could see it and turn an honest penny by singing it; so far, the sport was reasonable and served an end.

It was a bright day, clear but not quite frosty. The efforts of the besieging force were concentrated against a space of about two hundred and fifty yards, containing two curtains and two towers. One was the square barbican; the other had a pointed roof built to overlap, resting on a stone machicolade, by which means a row of dangerous crenelets between the roof and the masonry grinned down at the nearer assailants, looking not unlike the grinders of a modern frigate with each port nearly closed. The curtains were overlapped with penthouses somewhat shattered by mangonels, trebuchets, and other slinging engines. On the besiegers' edge of the moat was what seemed at first sight a gigantic arsenal, longer than it was broad, peopled by human ants and full of busy, honest industry, displaying all the various mechanical science of the age in full operation. Here the lever at work, there the winch and pulley, here the balance, there the capstan. Everywhere heaps of stones, piles of fascines, mantelets, and rows of fire-barrels. Mantelets rolled, the hammer tapped all day, horses and carts in endless succession rattled up with materials. Only, on looking closer into the hive of industry, one might observe that arrows were constantly flying to and fro, that the cranes did not tenderly deposit their masses of stone but flung them with an indifference to property, though on scientific principles, and that among the tubs full of arrows, tar-barrels, beams, fagots, and other utensils, here and there a workman or soldier lay flatter than is usual in limited naps, with something more or less feathered sticking in him, and blood and other essentials oozing out.

At the edge of the moat opposite the wooden tower, a strong penthouse, which they called "a cat," might be seen stealing toward the curtain, gradually filling up the moat with fascines and rubbish which the workmen flung out at its mouth. It was advanced by two sets of ropes passing round pulleys, each worked by a windlass at some distance from the cat. The knight burnt the first cat by flinging blazing tar-barrels on it. So the besiegers made the roof of this one very steep and covered it with raw hides, so the tar-barrels could not harm it. Then the knight made signs with his spear, and a little trebuchet behind the walls began dropping stones just clear of the wall into the moat. At last they got the range, and a stone went clean through the roof of the cat, making an ugly hole.

Baldwyn of Burgundy saw this, losing his temper, ordered the great catapult battering the wood-work of the curtain opposite to be turned and leveled slantwise at this invulnerable knight. Denys and his Englishman went to dinner. These two worthies, eternally on the watch for one another, had made a sort of distant acquaintance and conversed by signs, especially on a topic that in peace or war maintains the same importance. Sometimes Denys would put a piece of bread on the top of his mantelet, and then the archer would hang something of the kind out by a string; or the order of invitation would be reversed. Anyway, they always managed to dine together.

Now the engineers proceeded to the unusual step of slinging fifty-pound stones at an individual. This catapult was a scientific, simple, and beautiful engine, very effective in vertical fire at the short ranges of the period.

Imagine a fir-tree cut down and set to turn round a horizontal axis on lofty uprights, but not in equilibrium; three-fourths of the tree being on the hither side. At the shorter and thicker end was fastened a weight of half a ton. This butt end, just before discharge, pointed toward the enemy. By means of a powerful winch, the long tapering portion of the tree was forced down to the very ground and fastened by a bolt; the stone placed in a sling attached to the tree's nose. This process, of course, raised the butt end with its huge weight high in the air, keeping it there struggling in vain to come down. The bolt was now drawn. Gravity, an institution which flourished even then, resumed its sway. The short end swung furiously down, the long end went as furiously round up, and at its highest elevation flung the huge stone out of the sling with a tremendous jerk. In this case, the huge mass missed the knight but came down near him on the penthouse, going through it like paper and making an awful gap in roof and floor. Through the latter fell out two inanimate objects: the stone itself and the mangled body of a besieger it had struck. They fell down the high

Margaret Brandt had undergone a profound transformation. She moved through her household duties as if drifting in a fevered dream, her senses dulled by a pervasive dread. A mere sharp word from Peter sent her heart hammering against her ribs, her eyes wide with terror as she stared at him. Her visits to her old friend, Margaret Van Eyck, became rare and fraught with discomfort; instead of meeting the elder woman's warm embraces, Margaret would stand passive and trembling, sometimes shrinking back as if burned. Yet, the most striking change was her refusal to venture outdoors in daylight. She would only slip away to Tergou after dusk, returning before the sun could rise again, avoiding even the morning prayers.

Peter, though usually oblivious to such subtleties, eventually noticed her absence and pressed her for an explanation.

"I fear the people all stare at me," she whispered.

One afternoon, Margaret Van Eyck, concerned, asked her directly what troubled her. Margaret offered only a frightened glance and a torrent of tears. The old lady spoke gently, urging her to share her burdens. "What, sweetheart, afraid to confide your sorrows to me?"

"I have no sorrows, madam, but ones of my own making," Margaret replied, her voice thin. "I am treated with more kindness than I deserve, especially within these walls."

"Then why not come to me oftener, my dear?"

"I come oftener than I deserve," she sighed, the weight of her guilt heavy upon her.

"There, Reicht is crying for you," Margaret Van Eyck said, pointing to the child. "Go, child—what on earth can it be?"

Speculating on the cause, Margaret Van Eyck suspected Margaret felt mortified by the contempt shown to her by Gerard's family. Resolved to intervene, she decided to reprimand the women of the household. The following day, hooded and cloaked, accompanied by Reicht, she marched to the hosier's house. Catherine received her with respectful tears, thanking her for her past kindness to Gerard. However, when Margaret Van Eyck attempted to speak of Margaret Brandt, Catherine's demeanor hardened instantly. Her eyes dried, her lips thinned, and she adopted the stubborn, impenetrable expression of an ignorant woman. To try to soften such a face was like attempting to melt a brick wall. Margaret Van Eyck, unused to being thwarted, grew provoked. She finally withdrew with an abrupt, truncated curtsey, which Catherine returned with an air of defiant challenge.

Stepping outside, Margaret Van Eyck found Reicht talking with a pale girl leaning on crutches. As the old lady pushed past them, her face flushed with indignation and her head tossed in scorn toward the entire family, a small, delicate hand suddenly glided timidly into hers. Turning, Margaret saw two dove-like eyes filled with tears, looking up with a gaze that was both grateful and imploring. Reading this complex expression, Margaret's anger dissolved instantly. She stopped, kissed Kate's brow, and said, "I see. Then, I leave it to you."
