Returning home, she reflected, "I visited a house today where I witnessed both the common and the uncommon. I saw a stupid, obstinate woman, and I also saw an angel in the flesh—a face so beautiful that if I had my brushes here, I would take them down again and try to paint it."

Little Kate did not disappoint the high opinion Margaret had formed of her. Waiting for a quiet moment, she told her mother what she had learned from Reicht Heynes: that Margaret had shed her very blood for Gerard in the woods.

"See, mother, how she loves him," Kate said softly.

"Who would not love him?"

"Oh, mother, think of it! Poor thing."

"Ay, wench. She has her own troubles, no doubt, just as we have mine. I cannot bear the sight of blood, let alone my own."

This was a significant step forward, but when Kate tried to press further, she was met with silence.

About a month later, a soldier of the Dalgetty tribe, returning from service in Burgundy, delivered a letter to the hosier's house. He was on business elsewhere, but the family sat down to supper. The soldier placed the letter on the table beside Catherine and, refusing any payment, departed for Sevenbergen.

The letter was unfolded. Curiously, though none of them could read the script, they all knew immediately it was Gerard's handwriting.

"And your father must be away," cried Catherine. "Are you not ashamed of yourselves? Not one of you can read your brother's letter."

Though the words were hieroglyphics to them, the letter held a meaning they could decipher. There exists an art that speaks without words, unfettered by the limits of the pen, capable of stealing through the eye straight into the heart and mind of the learned and the unlearned alike. It crosses frontiers and seas without losing its essence, needing no translator, for it writes a universal language.

When they beheld this, a simple sketch of two hands clasped together, drawn by Gerard between the two short paragraphs of his text, they understood. It went straight to their hearts. Gerard was bidding them farewell.

As they gazed upon that simple drawing, recognizing his manner in every turn and line, Gerard seemed present, saying goodbye. The women wept until they could barely see the paper.

Giles murmured, "Poor Gerard!" in a voice lower than seemed natural for him. Even Cornelis and Sybrandt felt a sudden pang of remorse, sitting silent and gloomy.

But how could they get the words read to them? They were reluctant to show their ignorance and their raw emotion to a stranger.

"The Dame Van Eyck?" Kate suggested timidly.

"And so I will, Kate," her father agreed. "She has a good heart. She loves Gerard, too. She will be glad to hear of him. I was short with her when she came here, but I will make my submission, and then she will tell me what my poor child says to me."

Soon, Catherine was at Margaret Van Eyck's house. Reicht led her into a room and said, "Wait a moment; she is at her prayers."

Inside, a young woman sat pensively by the stove. She rose and courteously made way for the visitor.

"Thank you, young lady; the winter nights are cold, and your stove is a treat," Catherine said, warming her hands. As she did so, she inspected her companion furtively from head to toe. The young woman wore an ordinary wimple, but her gown was trimmed with fur—a sign of superior rank or wealth in those days. What struck Catherine most, however, was the candor and modesty of the girl's face. Feeling sure of sympathy from such a good countenance, she began to chat.

"Now, what brings me here, young lady? It is a letter! A letter from my poor boy who is far away in some savage land. And I take shame to say that none of us can read it. I wonder whether you can?"

"Yes."

"Can you, now? It is much to your credit, my dear. I dare say she won't be long, but every minute feels like an hour to a poor longing mother."

"I will read it to you."

"Bless you, my dear; bless you!"

In her unfeigned eagerness, Catherine never noticed the suppressed urgency with which the hand was slowly extended to take the letter. She did not see the tremor as the fingers closed around it.

"Come, then, read it to me, prithee. I am wearying for it."

"The first words are, 'To my honoured parents.'"

"Ay! And he always did honour us, poor soul."

"'God and the saints have you in His holy keeping, and bless you by night and by day. Your one harsh deed is forgotten; your years of love remembered.'"

Catherine laid her hand on her bosom and sank back in her chair with one long sob.

"Then comes this, madam. It speaks for itself: 'a long farewell.'"

"Ay, go on; bless you, girl, you give me sorry comfort. Still, it is comfort."

"'To my brothers Cornelis and Sybrandt—Be content; you will see me no more!'"

"What does that mean? Ah!"

"'To my sister Kate. Little angel of my father's house. Be kind to her—for poor Gerard's sake: since grief to her is death to me.'"

Catherine gasped, "Ah!" Nature, resenting the poor girl's struggle for unnatural composure, suddenly gave way. She sank from her chair and lay insensible, the letter still in her hand, her head resting on Catherine's knees.

In the quiet chamber, where the air grew thick with the scent of unwashed linen and sudden terror, Catherine did not falter. She was a woman of experience, one who knew that fainting was rarely a ghostly visitation but merely the body's refusal to bear a heavy load. With brisk efficiency, she laid the young girl flat upon the floorboards, her head resting squarely on the wood, and threw open the shutter to invite the cool night air. Only after securing the girl's dress and loosening her bodice did she turn to the door, her voice ringing out with commanding clarity.

"Come here, if you please."

Margaret Van Eyck and Reicht entered to find the scene exactly as described: the girl prone and pale, and Catherine striking her palms together in a rhythm of agitation.

"Oh, my poor girl! What have you done to her?" Margaret cried, her face flushing with a mixture of shock and anger.

"Me?" Catherine snapped, her eyes flashing. "What has happened, then?"

"Nothing, madam," Catherine replied, her tone hardening into stone. "Nothing more than is natural in her situation."

Margaret's cheeks burned hotter, the color rising to her temples in ire. "You do well to speak so coolly," she retorted, pointing an accusing finger. "You that are the cause of her situation."

"That I am not," Catherine declared bluntly, her voice steady despite the turmoil. "Nor is any woman born."

"Was it not you and your husband that kept them apart?" Margaret pressed, her voice trembling with suppressed grief. "And now he has gone to Italy all alone. 'Situation' indeed! You have broken her heart amongst you."

"Why, madam? Who is it then? In Heaven's name!" Catherine exclaimed, genuinely baffled. "To hear you, one would think this was my Gerard's lass. But that can't be. This fur never cost less than five crowns the ell; besides, this young gentlewoman is a wife; or ought to be."

"Of course she ought," Margaret insisted, her composure cracking. "And who is the cause she is none? Who came before them at the very altar?"

"God forgive them, whoever it was," Catherine said gravely, shaking her head. "Me it was not, nor my man."

"Well," the older woman sighed, her anger softening into concern as she noticed the girl's eyelids fluttering. "Now you have seen her, perhaps you will not be quite so bitter against her, madam. She is coming to, thank Heaven."

"Me bitter against her?" Catherine laughed softly, the sound devoid of malice. "No, that is all over. Poor soul! Trouble behind her and trouble afore her; and to think of my setting her, of all living women, to read Gerard's letter to me. Ay, and that was what made her go off, I'll be sworn. She is coming to. What, sweetheart! Be not afeared, none are here but friends."

They lifted the girl into an easy chair. As the rosy hue began to creep back to her cheeks and lips, Catherine drew Margaret Van Eyck aside, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper.

"Is she staying with you, if you please?"

"No, madam."

"I wouldn't let her go back to Sevenbergen to-night, then."

"That is as she pleases. She still refuses to bide the night."

"Ay, but you are older than she is; you can make her. There, she is beginning to notice."

Catherine leaned close, pressing her lips to Margaret Van Eyck's ear for a fleeting moment. It seemed too brief to whisper a full sentence, yet between women, communication often flashes like lightning, bypassing the need for words.

The old lady recoiled slightly, her eyes widening. "It's false! It is a calumny! It is monstrous! Look at her face. It is blasphemy to accuse such a face."

"Tut! Tut! Tut!" Margaret dismissed her with a wave of her hand. "You might as well say this is not my hand. I ought to know; and I tell ye it is so."

Then, to Margaret's utter astonishment, she stepped forward, took the young girl by the neck, and kissed her warmly.

"I suffered for Gerard," Margaret whispered fiercely, tears welling in her eyes. "And you shed your blood for him, I do hear; his own words show me that I have been to blame, the very words you have read to me. Ay, Gerard, my child, I have held aloof from her; but I'll make it up to her once I begin. You are my daughter from this hour."

Another embrace, warm and desperate, sealed this hasty compact, and the impulsive woman departed.

Margaret lay back in the chair, a feeble smile stealing over her face. Gerard's mother had kissed her and claimed her as a daughter; but the next moment, she saw her old friend regarding her with a deeply vexed expression.

"I wonder you let that woman kiss you," the old lady murmured, her tone sharp with disapproval.

"His mother!" Margaret replied, half reproachful, half confused.

"Mother, or no mother, you would not let her touch you if you knew what she whispered in my ear about you."

"About me?" Margaret asked faintly.

"Ay, about you, whom she never saw till to-night." The old lady hesitated, choosing her words carefully as she prepared to share her indignation, when an unexpected interruption silenced her.

The young woman slid from the chair to her knees, bowing her head low. She began to pray piteously for pardon. From the fervor of her words and the anguish in her manner, a bystander would have gathered that she had inflicted some cruel wrong, some intolerable insult, upon her venerable friend.

The gathering at the hosier's dwelling paused mid-discourse as the mother entered, her presence instantly silencing the room. With a gaze that sliced through the air, she swept over the assembled men before laying a letter flat upon the table. She recited its contents from memory, tracing the invisible lines with a trembling finger, a desperate ritual to convince herself—and them—that she could still decipher the script. Then, her head snapped up, and her eyes locked onto Cornelis and Sybrandt. Their gazes dropped to the floor.

In that instant, the tempest that had been gathering in the cellar broke loose.

Catherine seemed to inflate with a fury akin to a hen ruffling her plumage, unleashing a torrent of wisdom and nonsense, of grand and petty invectives, that no man in history could have voiced in a single breath, let alone a woman. A sampling of her words follows, though she delivered them all in one relentless rush:

"I have long suspected that you kindled the fire between Gerard and your father, setting that old rogue, Ghysbrecht, to stoke it. Now, here are Gerard's own words to confirm your treachery. You have driven your own flesh and blood into a distant land, stealing from this mother who bore you her darling, the pride of her sight, the joy of her heart. Yet, you remain one and the same from start to finish. When we were all boys, my others were a comfort; you were a curse—mischievous and sly, requiring a woman half a day to mend your torn garments. Why? For labor wears cloth, but play cuts it. Prudence arrives with the beard, but it never came to you. You were the last to retire and the last to rise, for honesty sleeps early and industry wakes before dawn; where two sluggards dwell in a house, there dwells a pair of ne'er-do-wells. I have often sat watching your ways, wondering whence you sprang. You bear no resemblance to your father, and you are no more like me than a wasp is to an ant. Surely, you were swapped in the cradle, or a cuckoo dropped you upon my floor, for you lack our hands and our hearts. Of all my blood, none but you ever mocked those God afflicted. Often, with my back turned, I heard you sneer at Giles for not being as large as others, and at my lily Kate for lacking the strength of a Flemish mare. After that, you would rob a church! For in the eyes of Him who made both Kate and Giles, and in mine who suffered for them as I did for you, you paltry, unfeeling, treasonable curs, you can be no worse!

"No, I will not hush, my daughter; they have filled the cup to the brim. It takes a great deal to turn a mother's heart against the sons she has nursed upon her knees. Many times I have winked, choosing not to see too much, biting my tongue lest your father discover what I knew; he would have cast you out that very moment. But now, the cup is full. Where did you acquire this wealth? For the past month, you have rolled in it without a single stitch of honest work. I pray I may never hear from another mouth how you obtained it. It is since that night you stayed out so late, returning with a head swollen with pride, Cornelis. Sloth and greed are a fatal pairing, my masters. Those who love money must either sweat or steal. Well, if you robbed any poor soul of it, it was surely some woman, for I'll go bail a man would drive you with his bare hand. Be that as it may, it serves one purpose. It has revealed how you would manage our affairs if they ever fell to your keeping. I have watched you, my lads, for some time. You have spent a groat between you today. I spend scarce a groat a week and yet keep you all, good and bad. No, I give up waiting for the shoes that might walk behind your coffins. This shop and this house shall never be yours. Gerard is our heir; poor Gerard, whom you have banished and done your utmost to destroy. After that, never call me mother again! But you have made him tenfold dearer to me. My poor lost boy! I shall soon see him again, hold him in my arms, and set him upon my knees. Yes, you may stare! You are too crafty, yet not crafty enough. You severed the stalk, but you left the seed—the seed that shall outgrow you and outlive you. Margaret Brandt is quick, and the child is Gerard's, and what is Gerard's is mine. I have prayed the saints it may be a boy, and it will—it must. Kate, when I learned the truth, my bowels yearned over her unborn child as if it were my own. He is our heir. He will outlive us. You will not; for a bad heart in a carcass is like a worm in a nut, swiftly bringing the body to dust. So, Kate, take down Gerard's bib and tucker from the drawer you know of, and one of these days we shall carry them to Sevenbergen. We will borrow Peter Buyskens' cart and go to comfort Gerard's wife under her burden. She is his wife. Who is Ghysbrecht Van Swieten? Can he stand between a couple and the altar, and sunder those whom God and the priest have joined? She is my daughter, and I am as proud of her as I am of you, Kate, almost. As for you, keep out of my way for a while, for you are like the black dog in my eyes."

Cornelis and Sybrandt took the hint and slunk from the room, their hearts aching with remorse, impenitence, and a deep-seated hatred. They avoided her gaze as much as possible, and for many days thereafter, she spoke not a single word—good, bad, or indifferent—to either of them. She had finally freed her spirit.

Catherine was a woman of domestic routine, rarely venturing beyond the walls of her home, and when she did, chaos invariably followed. Acutely aware of this fragility, she had postponed her journey to Sevenbergen for days, waiting for a lull in household affairs. Finally, on an afternoon when Peter Buyskens' mule was conveniently out of the way, the opportunity arose. Yet, before she could depart, Eli summoned the family.

"Eli asked her publicly if she intended to visit Margaret Brandt."

"Ay, my man," she admitted.

"Then I forbid it," Eli declared.

"Oh, do you?" she challenged, her face flushing.

"I do," he replied sternly.

"There is nothing more to be said," she conceded, though her defiance simmered beneath the surface. Alone later with her daughter, Catherine berated herself for her lack of discretion. "I acted like a cat cornered by a robin," she lamented. "Next time, let us act immediately and say nothing until the deed is done. If questioned, we can claim ignorance. For two pins, I would have gone there openly."

Such wild defiance staggered Kate. "Nay, mother, with patience father will come round."

"And so will Michaelmas," Catherine retorted. "But when? I was determined you see the girl. Say what they will, one judges a beast only by the eye. Were I to have fifty sons, I would never thwart one's fancy until I had seen the woman myself. Life is a school, and the lesson never ends; we put down one fault and pick up another until we stumble into our graves."

"Mother," Kate whispered timidly.

"What is coming now? No good news by your look. What frightens the poor wretch?"

"An avowal she must make," Kate faltered.

"A noble word," Catherine said proudly. "Gerard taught you that. Speak."

"Well then, truthfully, I have seen her."

"And?"

"And spoken with her as well."

"And never told me? After this, miracles are dust."

"Mother, you were so angry against her. I waited until I could speak without worsening your temper."

"Ay," Catherine said, half sadly, half bitterly. "Like mother, like daughter; cowardice is our bane. I buffet the others, or how would the house fare? Did you, Kate, ever receive a harsh word from your poor mother that you... Nay, I will not have you cry. Rise, brave heart, and tell me all. Better late than never. First and foremost, when and how did you go to Sevenbergen with your crutches, and I not know?"

"I never was there in my life," Kate replied. "And, mammy dear, to say that I never wished to see her that I will not, but I never went nor sought to see her."

"There now," Catherine disputed. "Did I not say it was unlike my girl to seek her secretly? Come, I am all agog."

"Thus it was. It came to my ears, pray do not ask how, that Gerard was a prisoner in the Stadthouse tower."

"Ah," Catherine sighed, a sound almost of a moan. "Blacker than I thought."

"Giles and I went out at night to bid him be of good cheer. At the foot of the tower stood a brave lass, quite strange to me, on the same errand."

"Lookee there now, Kate."

"At first we frightened one another, the place being filled with his bad name and our heads full of devils, and we paled in the moonlight. But the next moment, she said, 'You are Margaret.' And I said, 'And you are Kate.' Think on't!"

"Did one ever?" Catherine marveled. "Gerard must have been talking backwards and forwards of you to her, and her to you."

In return, Kate bestowed upon Catherine one of nature's prettiest gifts—the composite kiss. She pressed a single kiss to her cheek that conveyed: *Quite correct; good, clever mother for guessing so quickly; how sweet for us to be of one mind again.*

"Now then, speak thy mind, child. Gerard is not here. Alas, what am I saying? Would to Heaven he were."

"Well then, mother, she is comely and wrongs her picture but little."

"Eh, dear; hark to young folk! I am for good acts, not good looks. Does she love my boy as he ought to be loved?"

"Sevenbergen is farther from the Stadthouse than we are," Kate thoughtfully noted. "Yet she was there before me."

Catherine nodded intelligently.

"Nay, more, she had gotten him out ere I came. Ay, down from the captive's tower."

Catherine shook her head in disbelief. "The highest tower for miles! It is not feasible."

"'Tis sooth though," Kate insisted. "She and an old man found means and wit to send him up a rope. There he dangled from his prison, and our Giles went up it. When first I saw it hang, I said, 'This is glamour.' But when the frank lass's arms came round me, and her bosom beat against mine, and her cheeks wet, then said I, 'Tis not glamour: 'tis love.' For she is not like me, but lusty and able. Even I, poor frail creature, feel sometimes as I could move the world for them I love: I love you, mother. And she loves Gerard."

"God bless her for't! God bless her!"

"But..."

"But what, lamb?"

"Her love, is it for very certain honest? 'Tis most strange; but that very thing which hath warmed your heart hath somewhat cooled mine towards her. Poor soul. She is no wife, you know, mother, when all is done."

"Humph! They have stood at the altar together."

"Ay, but they went as they came, maid and bachelor."

"The parson saith so?"

"Nay, for that I know not."

"Then I'll take no man's word but his in such a tangled skein." After reflection, she added, "Natheless, art right, girl. I'll go to Sevenbergen alone. A wife I am, but not a slave. We are all in the dark here. She holds the clue. I must question her, and no one by; least of all you. I'll not take any lily to a house with a spot, no, not to a palace of gold and silver."

The more Catherine pondered this conversation, the more she felt drawn towards Margaret. Curiosity, a potent and nearly omnipotent passion, seized her. One fine day after dinner, she whispered to Kate, "Keep the house from going to pieces, an ye can," and donned her best kirtle and hood, her scarlet clocked hose, and new shoes. She trudged briskly off to Sevenbergen, troubling no man's mule.

Upon arrival, she inquired where Margaret Brandt lived. The first person she asked shook his head, claiming the name was strange to him. She walked a little farther and asked a girl of about fifteen standing at a door. "Father," the girl called into the house, "here is another after that magician's daughter." The man emerged and informed Catherine that Peter Brandt's cottage was just outside the town on the east side. "You may see the chimney hence," he pointed out. "But you will not find them there, neither father nor daughter; they have left the town this week, bless you."

"Say not so, good man, and me walken all the way from Tergou."

"From Tergou? Then you must have met the soldier."

"What soldier? Ay, I did meet a soldier."

"Well, then, yon soldier was here seeking that self-same Margaret."

"Ay, and wasn't mad with us because she was gone?" the girl interjected. "His long beard and her cheek are no strangers, I warrant."

"Say no more than ye know," Catherine said sharply. "You are young to slander your elders. Stay! Tell us more about this soldier, good man."

"Nay, I know no more than that he came hither seeking Margaret Brandt, and I told him she and her father had made a moonlight flight this day sennight, and that some thought the devil had flown away with them, being magicians. 'And,' says he, 'the devil fly away with thee for thy ill news;' that was my thanks. 'But I doubt 'tis a lie,' said he. 'An you think so,' said I, 'go and see.' 'I will,' said he, and burst out with a handful of gibberish; my wife thinks 'twas curses; and hied him to the cottage. Presently back he comes, and sings the other tune. 'You were right and I was wrong,' says he, and shoves a silver coin in my hand. Show it the wife, some of ye; then she'll believe me; I have been called a liar once to-day."

"It needs not," said Catherine, inspecting the coin all the same.

"And he seemed quiet and sad like, didn't he now, wench?"

"That he did," said the young woman warmly. "And, dame, he was just as pretty a man as ever I clapped eyes on. Cheeks like a rose, and shining beard, and eyes in his head like sloes."

"I saw he was well bearded," Catherine said. "But, for the rest, at my age I scan them not as when I was young and foolish. But he seemed right civil: doffed his bonnet to me as I had been a queen, and I did drop him my best reverence, for manners beget manners. But little I wist he had been her light of love, and most likely the... Who bakes for this town?"

The man, not being acquainted with her, opened his eyes at this swift transition.

"Well, dame, there be two; John Bush and Eric Donaldson, they both bide in this street."

"Then, God be with you, good people," she said, proceeding. But her sprightly foot came flat on the ground now, and no longer struck it with little jerks and cocking heel. She asked the bakers whether Peter Brandt had gone away in their debt. Bush said they were not customers. Donaldson said, "Not a stiver: his daughter had come round and paid him the very night they went. Didn't believe they owed a copper in the town." So Catherine got all the information of that kind she wanted with very little trouble.

"Can you tell me what sort this Margaret was?" she asked as she turned to go.

"Well, somewhat too reserved for my taste. I like a chatty customer—when I'm not too busy. But she bore a high character for being a good daughter."

"'Tis no small praise. A well-looking lass, I am told?"

"Why, whence come you, wyfe?"

"From Tergou."

"Oh, ay. Well you shall judge: the lads clept her 'the beauty of Sevenbergen;' the lasses did scout it merrily, and terribly pulled her to pieces, and found so many faults no two could agree where the fault lay."

"That is enough," said Catherine. "I see, the bakers are no fools in Sevenbergen, and the young women no shallower than in other burghs."

She bought a manchet of bread, partly out of sympathy and justice (she kept a shop), partly to show her household how much better bread she gave them daily; and returned to Tergou dejected.

Kate met her outside the town with beaming eyes.

"Well, Kate, lass, it is a happy thing I went; I am heartbroken. Gerard has been sore abused. The child is none of ourn, nor the mother from this hour."

"Alas, mother, I fathom not your meaning."

"Ask me no more, girl, but never mention her name to me again. That is all."

Kate acquiesced with a humble sigh, and they went home together.

They found a soldier seated tranquilly by their fire. The moment they entered the door he rose, and saluted them civilly. They stood and looked at him; Kate with some little surprise, but Catherine with a great deal, and with rising indignation.

"What makes you here?" was Catherine's greeting.

"I came to seek after Margaret."

"Well, we know no such person."

"Say not so, dame; sure you know her by name, Margaret Brandt."

"We have heard of her for that matter—to our cost."

"Come, dame, prithee tell me at least where she bides."

"I know not where she bides, and care not."

Denys felt sure this was a deliberate untruth. He bit his lip. "Well, I looked to find myself in an enemy's country at this Tergou; but maybe if ye knew all ye would not be so dour."

"I do know all," replied Catherine bitterly. "This morn I knew nought." Then suddenly setting her arms akimbo, she told him with a raised voice and flashing eyes she wondered at his cheek sitting down by that hearth of all hearths in the world.

"May Satan fly away with your hearth to the lake of fire and brimstone," shouted Denys, who could speak Flemish fluently. "Your own servant bade me sit there till you came, else I had ne'er troubled your hearth. My malison on it, and on the churlish roof-tree that greets an unoffending stranger this way," and he strode scowling to the door.

"Oh! oh!" ejaculated Catherine, frightened, and also a little conscience-stricken; and the virago sat suddenly down and burst into tears. Her daughter followed suit quietly, but without loss of time.

A shrewd writer, now unhappily lost to us, has somewhere the following dialogue:

She. "I feel all a woman's weakness."
He. "Then you are invincible."

Denys, by anticipation, confirmed that valuable statement; he stood at the door looking rue

The following day, Denys seized a moment to reveal the remainder of the tale to his mother and daughter, carefully excluding Cornelis and Sybrandt from the conversation. "It is not my place to tarnish their names," he explained with his characteristic reserve, "for they are of good stock. Yet Gerard views them as enemies in this affair. As his comrade, I adhere to the soldier's code: never to speak truth to an adversary, but to lie instead."

Catherine sighed deeply but offered no rebuttal.

The adventures Denys recounted stirred a tempest of agitation and sorrow within them; they wept bitterly at the memory of the friends' parting, a scene that still caused Denys to falter in his narration. Yet, eventually, their grief merged into a hopeful anticipation of Gerard's swift return. Denys shared this confidence, though he gently reminded them that Gerard's final wishes must not be neglected. Indeed, if Gerard returned next week to find no Margaret, what manner of figure would he cut?

For the first time, Catherine felt a profound kinship toward the absent Margaret. Her anxiety to locate her and welcome her back before Gerard's arrival matched Denys's fervor, yet she could not agree with his plan to scour the entire province of Holland. "She must have confided in someone where she is going," she reasoned. "These are not dishonest fugitives fleeing debt; they owe not a stiver in Sevenbergen. And, dear heart, Denys, you cannot hunt all of Holland for one girl."

"Can I not?" Denys asked grimly. "We shall see." After a moment's reflection, he proposed a division of labor: she would remain here with eyes and ears wide open, while he would ransack every town in Holland if necessary. "But she will not be many leagues from here," he insisted. "Three do not fly as fast, nor as far, as one."

"That is sensible," Catherine conceded, though she pressed him to visit the demoiselle Van Eyck first. "She and our Margaret were bosom friends. She knows where the girl has gone, if she will but tell us." Eager to depart immediately, Denys was intercepted by Catherine, who, with a deft turn of her hand, smoothed her dress and led him into the house.

They were received graciously by the elderly lady in a richly furnished chamber. As the tapestry slipped from Margaret Van Eyck's hands, she exclaimed, "Gone? Gone from Sevenbergen without telling me? The thankless girl!"

This reaction startled the visitors. "What, you know not? When was she last here?"

"Perhaps ten days ago," the old lady replied. "I had taken out my brushes after so many years to paint her portrait. I did not do it, though; for reasons."

Catherine noted the strangeness of her departure, bag and baggage, without leave or explanation. "Was ever anything so untoward? Just when our hearts are warm toward her, and here comes Gerard's mate from the ends of the earth with comfort, only to find her missing, while Gerard himself is expected. What am I to do? Surely she did not leave without cause. Can you not give us the clue, my good demoiselle? Prithee now."

"I have no clue to give," the elder lady said, her tone becoming peevish.

"Then I can," declared Reicht Heynes, appearing in the doorway with flushed cheeks.

"So you have been listening all this time, have you?"

"What are my ears for, mistress?"

"True. Well, then, shed the light of your wisdom upon this dark matter."

"There is no darkness here," Reicht asserted. "And the clue, if you call it a two-ply twine, has its ends right here in this room. Oh, mistress, I wonder at you pretending."

"Marry, come up," the mistress said, her own cheek flushing nearly as red as the servant's. "So it was I who drove the foolish girl away."

"You did your share, mistress. What greeting did you give her the last time she came? Did she fail to notice it, being all friendless? And you said, 'I have altered my mind about painting you,' turning your nose up at her."

"I did not turn up my nose. It is not shaped like yours for looking heavenward."

"Oh, all our noses can follow our hearts' bent, for that matter. Poor soul. She came to the kitchen to me. 'I am not to be painted now,' she said, tears in her eyes. She said no more. But I knew well what she meant. I had seen you."

"Well," Margaret Van Eyck admitted, "I confess as much and make you the judge, madam. These young girls can do nothing of their own heads; they are most apt to mimic their sweethearts. Your Gerard is reasonably handy at many things, including the illuminator's craft. Margaret is his pupil, a patient one: what marvel? Having a woman's eye for color and a lover to ape. 'Tis a trick I despise at heart; for by it the great art of color, which should be royal, aspiring, and free, becomes a poor slave to the petty crafts of writing and printing, fettered and imprisoned to match the littleness of books, going to church in a rich fool's pocket. Nevertheless, affection rules us all. When the poor wench brought me her thorn leaves, lilies, ivy, dewberries, ladybirds, and butterfly grubs—all the scum of Nature stuck fast in gold-leaf like wasps in a honey-pot—along with her diurnal book showing she had pored over each page for hundreds of hours, I was wroth that an immortal soul and much manual skill should be flung away on Nature's trash. But having bowels, I restrained my better feelings and looked kindly at the work to see how it might be bettered. I said, 'Since Heaven has doomed us to spend time, soul, and color on great letters and little beetles, omitting saints and heroes, why not present the scum naturally?' I told her the grapes I saw hanging in the air did not stick in walls, and even these insects pass their noxious lives at large, not wedged miserably in metal prisons. 'Ah, my dear friend,' she said, 'I see now whither you drive; but this ground is gold; whereon we may not shade.' 'Who said so?' I asked. 'All teachers of this craft,' she replied, and to make an end of me at once, 'Gerard himself!' 'That for Gerard himself,' I said, 'and all the gang; give me a brush!'

"Then I chose to shade her fruit and reptiles with a color false in nature but true relative to that monstrous ground of glaring gold. In five minutes, out came a bunch of raspberries, stalk and all, almost flying into your mouth, and a butterfly grub so truly presented it might turn the stoutest stomach. My lady threw her arms around my neck and said, 'Oh!'"

"Did she now?"

"The little love!" observed Denys, finally managing to wedge in a word.

Margaret Van Eyck stared at him and then smiled. She continued to tell them how she had been led step by step to promise to resume the art she had laid aside with a sigh when her brothers died, and to paint the Madonna once more—with Margaret as the model. Incidentally, she revealed how girls are turned into saints. "Thy hair is adorable," I said. "'Tis red,' she replied. 'Ay,' I said, 'but what a red! How brown! How glossy! Most hair is not worth a straw to us painters; thine is the artist's very hue. But thy violet eyes, which smack of earth, being now languid for lack of one Gerard, now full of fire in hopes of the same Gerard, these will I lift to heaven in fixed and holy meditation. And thy nose, which doth already somewhat aspire that way (though not so piously as Reicht's), will I debase a trifle and somewhat enfeeble thy chin.'"

"Enfeeble her chin? Alack! What may that mean? Ye go beyond me, mistress."

"'Tis a resolute chin. Not a jot too resolute for this wicked world; but when ye come to a Madonna? No thank you."

"Well, I never. A resolute chin."

Denys. "The darling!"

"And now comes the rub. When you told me she was—the way she is, it gave me a shock; I dropped my brushes. Was I going to turn a girl, that couldn't keep her lover at a distance, into the Virgin Mary, at my time of life? I love the poor ninny still. But I adore our blessed Lady. Say you, 'a painter must not be peevish in such matters'? Well, most painters are men; and men are fine fellows. They can do aught. Their saints and virgins are neither more nor less than their lemans, saving your presence. But know that for this very reason half their craft is lost on me, which finds beneath their angels' white wings the very trollops I have seen flaunting it on the streets, bejewelled like Paynim idols, and put on like the queens in a pack of cards. And I am not a fine fellow, but only a woman, and my painting is but one-half craft, and the other half devotion. So now you may read me. 'Twas foolish, maybe, but I could not help it; yet am I sorry." The old lady ended despondently a discourse she had commenced in a mighty defiant tone.

"Well, you know, dame," observed Catherine, "you must think it would go to the poor girl's heart, and she so fond of you?"

Margaret Van Eyck only sighed.

The Frisian girl, after biting her lips impatiently for a moment, turned upon Catherine. "Why, dame, do you think 'twas for that alone that Margaret and Peter left Sevenbergen? Nay."

"For what else, then?"

"What else? Why, because Gerard's people slight her so cruelly. Who would bide among hard-hearted folk that have driven her lad to Italy, and now he is gone, relented not, but face it out, and ne'er come nigh her that is left?"

"Reicht, I was going."

"Oh, ay, going, and going, and going. Ye should have said less or else done more. But with your words you did uplift her heart and let it down with your deeds. 'They have never been,' said the poor thing to me, with such a sigh. Ay, here is one who can feel for her: for I too am far from my friends, and often, when first I came to Holland, I used to take a hearty cry all to myself. But ten times liefer would I be Reicht Heynes with nought but the leagues between me and all my kith, than be as she is in the midst of them that ought to warm to her, and yet fare as lonesome as I."

"Alack, Reicht, I did go but yestreen, and had gone before, but one plaguy thing or t'other did still come and hinder me."

"Mistress, did aught hinder you to eat your dinner any one of those days? I trow not. And had your heart been as good towards your own flesh and blood as 'twas towards your flesher's meat, nought had prevailed to keep you from her that sat lonely, watching the road for you and comfort, with your child's child beating 'neath her bosom."

Here this rude young woman was interrupted by an incident not uncommon in a domestic's bright existence. The Van Eyck had been nettled by the attack on her, but with due tact had gone into ambush. She now sprang out of it. "Since you disrespect my guests, seek another place!"

"With all my heart," said Reicht stoutly.

"Nay, mistress," put in the good-natured Catherine. "True folk will still speak out. Her tongue is a stinger." Tears came into the speaker's eyes by way of confirmation. "But better she said it than thought it. So now 't won't rankle in her. And part with her for me, that shall ye not. Beshrew the wench, she wots she is a good servant, and takes advantage. We poor wretches which keep house must still pay 'em tax for value. I had a good servant once, when I was a young woman. Eh dear, how she did grind me down into the dust. In the end, by Heaven's mercy, she married the baker, and I was my own woman again. 'So,' said I, 'no more good servants shall come hither, a hectoring o' me.' I just get a fool and learn her; and whenever she knoweth her right hand from her left, she sauceth me: then out I bundle her neck and crop, and take another dunce in her place. Dear heart, 'tis wearisome, teaching a string of fools by ones; but there—I am mistress:" here she forgot that she was defending Reicht, and turning rather spitefully upon her, added, "and you be mistress here, I trow."

"No more than that stool," said the Van Eyck loftily. "She is neither mistress nor servant; but Gone. She is dismissed the house, and there's an end of her. What, did ye not hear me turn the saucy baggage off?"

"Ay, ay. We all heard ye," said Reicht, with vast indifference.

"Then hear me!" said Denys solemnly.

They all went round like things on wheels and fastened their eyes on him.

"Ay, let us hear what the man says," urged the hostess. "Men are fine fellows, with their great hoarse voices."

"Mistress Reicht," said Denys, with great dignity and ceremony, indeed so great as to verge on the absurd, "you are turned off. If on a slight acquaintance I might advise, I'd say, since you are a servant no more, be a mistress, a queen."

"Easier said than done," replied Reicht bluntly.

"Not a jot. You see here one

Catherine sat in a paroxysm of dread, her mind racing through the terrifying arithmetic of time. Gerard would return within a week, perhaps no more than a month at the utmost. The thought paralyzed her: how could she confess to him that she had failed to watch over his betrothed? The uncertainty gnawed at her, twisting into a sickening certainty of the worst possible outcome.

"Oh, Kate," she groaned, her voice trembling, "what if she has already gone and made herself away!"

"Mother, she would never be so wicked," Little Kate pleaded, her small hands clutching her mother's apron.

"Ah, my lass, you know not the folly of young hearts untethered by a mother's hand," Catherine replied, her eyes clouded with a mixture of maternal wisdom and deep-seated cynicism. "They will cast themselves into the water for a man whom the next stranger would cure in a week. I have seen them leap in like brass one moment and scream for help the next, unable to command their own minds even over such a trifling matter. Worse still, there are times when their bodies ail in ways unknown to any other creature, tightening their feelings until the patience they possess seems to vanish entirely, leaving them to plunge into the water without a second thought. Therefore, I say, men are monsters."

"Mother!"

"Monsters, and no less," Catherine insisted, her tone sharpening. "They dig these vast canals solely to tempt poor women. They know we shall not cut our throats, for we hate the sight of blood and value our skins and lives too highly. As for hanging, while she waits for the nail to be fixed and the noose to be made, she has time to change her mind. But a jump into a canal is no different than jumping into bed; the water does all the washing, whether you will it or not. Why, look at me, the mother of nine—was I not eager to make a hole in our canal just now?"

"Nay, mother, I will never believe it of you."

"You may, though," Catherine laughed, a dry, hollow sound. "It was in the first year we kept house together. Eli had not yet discovered my weak stitches, nor I his; so we made a rent, pulling in opposite directions, until we had a quarrel. I ran crying to tell some gossiping fool what I had no business saying outside the house, save to the saints. There stood one of our precious canals. Do they take us for teal? Oh, how tempting it looked! I told myself, 'Since he has let me go out of his door quarreling, he shall see me drowned next, and then he will change his key. He will weep a good one, and I shall look down from heaven—and see him take on, and oh, but that will be sweet!' I was on tiptoes, ready to step in, until suddenly I thought better of it. I had a new gown being made, after all, and nearly finished. So I went home instead. What was Eli's first word? 'Let yon flea stick in the wall, my lass,' he said. 'Not a word of all I said to anger thee was true, but this: I love thee.' These were his very words; I remembered them because it was our first quarrel. I threw my arms around his neck and sobbed a little, thinking of the canal, and he was no colder to me than I to him, being a man and a young one. That was better than lying in the water. I spoiled my wedding kirtle and my fine new shoes, made by old John Bush, who keeps the shop now and is his uncle. And what was my grief compared to hers?"

Little Kate, seeking comfort in logic, hoped that Margaret loved her father too deeply to consider leaving him so old and alone. "He is father and mother and all to her, you know."

"Nay, Kate," Catherine countered, her voice dropping to a grim whisper. "They forget all such things in a moment of despair when the sky seems black above them. I place more faith in him that is unborn than in him that is ripe for the grave to keep her out of mischief. For certainly, it goes sore against us to die when there is a little innocent pulling at our hearts to let it live, feeding at our very veins."

"Well, then, keep up a good heart, mother." Catherine added, trying to soothe her daughter's anxiety, that very likely all these fears were exaggerated. She ended by solemnly entreating her mother not to persist in naming the sex of Margaret's infant. It was so unlucky, all the gossips told her; "dear heart, as if there were not as many girls born as boys."

This reflection, though reasonable, was met with a clamor of protest.

"Have you the cruelty to threaten me with a girl!!?" Catherine cried out, her temper flaring. "I want no more girls while I have you. What use would a lass be to me? Can I set her on my knee and see my Gerard again as I can a boy? I tell thee 'tis all settled."

"How may that be?"

"In my mind," Catherine declared firmly. "And if I am to be disappointed in the end, 'tis not for you to disappoint me beforehand by telling me it is not to be a child, but only a girl."

Margaret Brandt had always kept herself aloof from the bustling life of Sevenbergen, a reserve that the townsfolk mistook for haughty pride. Whispers of jealousy and malice had reached her ears, and she knew that many hearts were festering with resentment. She feared the moment her condition could no longer be hidden; she dreaded the triumph of her enemies. This thought gnawed at her day and night, driving her to seclude herself within the house, avoiding the daylight even when she ventured abroad.

In her secret heart, however, Margaret did not mistake her moral standing, unlike the unlearned observers of her time. Though she lacked knowledge of the fine legal distinctions of the era, she understood that a betrothal was a binding marriage contract, as unbreakable as any written agreement witnessed by others. She knew that marrying another party would have been annulled by both Church and State, and that betrothed couples often united without further ceremony, their children deemed legitimate.

What crushed her simple medieval mind was not the law, but the absence of the contract itself. It was not in her possession; Gerard had taken it, and he was far away. She hated and despised herself for the miserable oversight that had left her at the mercy of false opinion. Though she had never heard Horace's famous couplet regarding the slowness of anger, she possessed a Horatian clarity of judgment, a trait often found in women when feeling does not blind them. She measured the world's understanding precisely. With her marriage lines lost in Italy, they could never balance her visible pregnancy or the sight of her unborn child. What story would she tell to silence slanderous tongues? "I have my marriage lines, but I cannot show them to you." Which woman would believe her, or even pretend to? As one of the most modest girls in Holland, she craved the good opinion of women, not men.

Slander attacks a woman's sex with a certain advantage, but here was slander wearing a face of truth. The concept of the "strong-minded woman" had not yet been invented. Margaret, though naturally resolute and accustomed to managing a household, was weak as water in this specific trial. Like the elite of her sex, she was a fragile leaf trembling at every gust of public opinion, whether true or false. Much misery lies in few words. It is doubtful that any man's pen could describe the anguish of a virtuous woman foreseeing herself paraded as a frail one. Had she been frail at heart, she might have brazened it out. But she was pure as snow, and she saw the pitch approaching. The poor girl sat listless for hours, moaning with inner anguish. Often, when her father spoke to her and she offered mechanical replies, her cheek would burn like fire, and the old man would wonder what had discomposed her. Nothing. His words were less than air to her; it was the ever-present dread that sent the color of shame into her burning face, regardless of her topic.

Shame and fear reached a climax when she returned that night from Margaret Van Eyck's house. Her condition had been discovered by women of her own sex. The old artist, secluded like herself, might not betray her, but Catherine, a gossip at the center of a family and a thick neighborhood? One spark of hope remained. Catherine had spoken kindly, even lovingly. The situation admitted no middle course; Gerard's mother, thus roused, would either be her best friend or her worst enemy. She waited in racking anxiety for news. None came. Hope faded. Catherine was not going to be her friend. Then she would expose her, for she possessed no strong, kindly feeling to balance the natural love of babbling.

From that moment, the desire to flee this neighborhood grew, gnawing at her until it became a wild, passionate longing. But how persuade her father to leave? Old people cling to their places. He was very old and infirm to change his abode. There was no choice but to make him her confidant; running away from him would be the alternative. Now, between her uncontrollable desire to hide and her invincible aversion to speak to a man, even her father, she vibrated in a suspense full of lively torture. Presently, betwixt these two forces came the fatal thought: "End all!" Foolishly worded thoughts are not always foolish. One of Catherine's bugbears, the numerous canals, sorely tempted this fluctuating girl. She stood on the bank one afternoon, eyeing the calm, deep water. It seemed an image of repose, and she was so harassed. No more trouble. No more fear of shame. If Gerard had not loved her, she doubted she would have ended there.

As it was, she knelt by the water's edge and prayed fervently to God to keep such wicked thoughts from her. "Oh! selfish wretch," she whispered, "to leave thy father. Oh, wicked wretch, to kill thy child, and make thy poor Gerard lose all his pain and peril undertaken for thy sight. I will tell father all, ay, ere this sun shall set." She hurried home with eager haste, lest her good resolution ooze away before she arrived.

In domestic matters, the learned Peter was simple as a child, while Margaret, since the age of sixteen, had governed the house gently but absolutely. It was therefore strange to see her young, despotic mistress address him with such faltering irresolution—a manner less suited to him than to Martin Wittenhaagen or even the little girl who washed the vessels for a pittance.

"Father, I would speak to thee."

"Speak on, girl."

"Wilt you listen to me? And—and—try to excuse my faults?"

"We have all our faults, Margaret, thou no more than the rest of us; but fewer, unless parental feeling blinds me."

"Alas, no, father: I am a poor foolish girl, that would fain do well, but have done ill, most ill, most unwisely; and now must bear the shame. But, father, I love you, with all my faults, and will not you forgive my folly, and still love your motherless girl?"

"That ye may count on," said Peter cheerfully.

"Oh, well, smile not. For then how can I speak and make you sad?"

"Why, what is the matter?"

"Father, disgrace is coming on this house: it is at the door. And I the culprit. Oh, father, turn your head away. I—I—father, I have let Gerard take away my marriage lines."

"Is that all? 'Twas an oversight."

"'Twas the deed of a mad woman. But woe is me! that is not the worst."

Peter interrupted her. "The youth is honest, and loves you dear. You are young. What is a year or two to you? Gerard will assuredly come back and keep troth."

"And meantime know you what is coming?"

"Not I, except that I shall be gone first for one."

"Worse than that. There is worse pain than death. Nay, for pity's sake turn away your head, father."

"Foolish wench!" muttered Peter, but he turned his head.

She trembled violently, and with her cheeks on fire, she began to falter, "I did look on Gerard as my husband—we being betrothed—and he was in so sore danger, and I thought I had killed him, and I—oh, if you were but my mother I might find courage: you would question me. But you say not a word."

"Why, Margaret, what is all this coil about? and why are thy cheeks crimson, speaking to no stranger, but to thy old father?"

"Why are my cheeks on fire? Because—because—father, kill me; send me to heaven! bid Martin shoot me with his arrow! And then the gossips will come and tell you why I blush so this day. And then, when I am dead, I hope you will love your girl again for her mother's sake."

"Give me thy hand, mistress," said Peter, a little sternly.

She put it out to him trembling. He took it gently and began, with some anxiety in his face, to feel her pulse.

"Alas, nay," said she. "'Tis my soul that burns, not my body, with fever. I cannot, will not, bide in Sevenbergen." And she wrung her hands impatiently.

"Be calm now," said the old man soothingly, "nor torment thyself for nought. Not bide in Sevenbergen? What need to bide a day, as it vexes thee, and puts thee in a fever: for fevered thou art, deny it not."

"What!" cried Margaret, "would you yield to go hence, and—and ask no reason but my longing to be gone?" Suddenly throwing herself on her knees beside him, in a fervor of supplication she clutched his sleeve, and then his arm, and then his shoulder, imploring him to quit this place and not ask her why. "Alas! what needs it? You will soon see it. And I could never say it. I would liever die."

"Foolish child, who seeks thy girlish secrets? Is it I, whose life hath been spent in searching Nature's? And for leaving Sevenbergen, what is there to keep me in it, thee unwilling? Is there respect for me here, or gratitude? Am I not yclept quacksalver by those that come not near me, and wizard by those I heal? And give they not the guerdon and the honor they deny me to the empirics that slaughter them? Besides, what is't to me where we sojourn? Choose thou that, as did thy mother before thee."

Margaret embraced him tenderly, and wept upon his shoulder.

She was respited.

Yet as she wept, respited, she almost wished she had had the courage to tell him everything.

After a while, nothing would content him but her taking a medicament he went and brought her. She took it submissively, to please him. It was the least she could do. It was a composing draught, and though administered under an error, and a common one, it did her more good than harm: she awoke calmed by a long sleep, and that very day began her preparations.

Next week they went to Rotterdam, bag and baggage, and lodged above a tailor's shop in the Brede-Kirk Straet.

Only one person in Tergou knew whither they were gone.

The Burgomaster.

He locked the information in his own breast.

The use he made of it ere long, my reader will not easily divine: for he did not divine it himself.

But time will show.

Among strangers, Margaret Brandt found a comparative happiness, until a new and unexpected cause of contentment arose. A civic dignitary, fanciful in proportion to his illness, wandered from doctor to doctor until he reached the brink of death. Desperate, he summoned Peter. The old man found the patient reduced to nothing by excessive bleeding and purging. With characteristic vigor, Peter flung a battalion of bottles from the window, leaving them to shatter; he beat egg yolks in neat Schiedam wine, administered them in small doses, and followed this with meat stewed in red water, shredded with mild febrifugal herbs. Finally, the patient recovered, looking somewhat like a man stuffed into a pillow-case. Being a voluble dignitary, he spread Peter's fame through every street. The artist, who had long merited a reputation in vain, suddenly made one by luck. Things looked bright. The old man's pride was cheered, and his purse began to fill. Yet he spent much of his gain on sovereign herbs and choice drugs, intending to invest them all, but Margaret, white-mailed by necessity, secured a portion. The victory came too late; its happy excitement proved fatal.

One evening, bidding her good-night, his voice seemed inarticulate. The next morning, he was found speechless and barely sensible. Margaret, her father's attentive pupil for years, immediately recognized a paralytic stroke. Distrusting herself, she ran for a doctor. One who had not been obstructed by Peter came cheerfully, confirming her fears, and proposed bleeding the patient. She declined. "He was always against bleeding," she said, "especially the old." Peter lived, but was never the same man again. His memory became much affected, rendering him untrustworthy to prescribe. Several patients, bent on being cured by the new doctor and no other, awaited his convalescence. Misery stared her in the face. Resolved to seek advice and comfort, she turned to her cousin William Johnson, from whom she had hitherto kept aloof out of pride and poverty.

She found him and his servant sitting in the same room, neither the better for liquor. Mastering her surprise, she greeted them and announced she had come to discuss a family matter, glancing quietly at the servant by way of hint. The woman took it, but not as expected. "Oh, you can speak before me, can she not, my old man?" At this familiarity, Margaret turned very red. "I cry you mercy, mistress. I knew not my cousin had fallen into the custom of this town. Well, I must take a fitter opportunity," she said, rising to go. "I wot not what ye mean by custom o' the town," the woman bounced up, retorting, "But this I know; 'tis the part of a faithful servant to keep her master from being preyed on by his beggarly kin." Margaret retorted sharply: "Ye are too modest, mistress. Ye are no servant. Your speech betrays you. 'Tis not till the ape hath mounted the tree that she shows her tail so plain. Nay, there sits the servant; God help him! And while so it is, fear not thou his kin will ever be so poor in spirit as come where the likes of you can flout their dole." Casting a look of mute reproach at her cousin for his passive silence, she turned and went haughtily out, nor shedding a single tear until she reached home. Now there were two men to be lodged and fed by one pregnant girl, and another mouth coming into the world. But this last, though the most helpless, was their best friend.

Nature was strong in Margaret Brandt; that same nature which makes brutes, birds, and insects cunning at providing food and shelter for their progeny. Stimulated by this instinct, she sat and brooded, thinking how to be beforehand with destitution. Though she still had five gold pieces left, she saw starvation approaching with inevitable foot. Her sex, when deviating from custom to think with male intensity, thinks just as much to the purpose. She rose, bade Martin move Peter to another room, made her own chamber neat and clean, polished a glass globe, suspended it from the ceiling, dusted a crocodile, and nailed it to the outside wall. After instructing Martin, she set him to play the lounging sentinel at the street door, telling the crocodile-bitten that a great, aged, and learned alchemist abode there, who in his moments of recreation would sometimes amuse himself by curing mortal diseases.

Patients soon came, demanding to see the leech. "That might not be. He was deep in his studies, searching for the grand elixir, and not princes could have speech of him. They must tell her their symptoms and return in two hours." And oh, mysterious powers! When they returned, the drug or draught was always ready. Sometimes, with a worshipful patient, she would scan his face, feel his pulse and skin, and hear his story, then go softly to Peter's room. There, thinking and asking herself how her father would have treated the case, she would write an illegible scrawl with cabalistic letters, bring it down reverently, and show it to the patient. "Could he read that?" she would ask. It would be either, "I am no reader," or, with admiration, "Nay, mistress, nought can I make on't." "Ay, but I can. 'Tis sovereign. Look on thyself as cured!" If she had materials, she would let the patient see her compound them, anxiously consulting the sacred prescription lest great Science should suffer in her hands. Thus she sent them away relieved of cash but with pockets full of medicine and minds full of faith, humbugged to their hearts' content. *Populus vult decipi.* When they were gone, she took down two little boxes Gerard had made her; on one she wrote "To-day," and on the other "To-morrow," putting smaller coins into "To-day" and larger ones into "To-morrow," along with surviving gold pieces from Sevenbergen and housekeeping expenses. She met current needs and laid by for the rainy day she foresaw, mixing drugs with simples and vice with virtue. On this last score, her conscience pricked her sore, and after each day's comedy, she knelt to pray God to forgive her "for the sake of her child." But lo and behold, cures were reported; so her conscience began to harden.

Martin Wittenhaagen had become a dead weight on her hands. Like most men who had endured great hardships, he had stiffened rather suddenly. Though less supple, he was as strong as ever and could have carried the doctor herself round Rotterdam. He carried her slops instead. In this new business, he showed the qualities of a soldier: unreasoning obedience, punctuality, accuracy, despatch, and drunkenness. He fell among "good fellows"; the blackguards plied him with Schiedam. He babbled and bragged. Doctor Margaret had risen very high in his estimation. All this brandishing of a crocodile for a standard, setting a dotard in ambush, and getting rid of slops struck him not as Science but as Strategy. He boasted in his cups how "me and my General we are a biting of the burghers."

When this revelation had time to leaven the city, his General, Doctor Margaret, received a call from the constables. They took her, trembling and begging subordinate machines to forgive her before the burgomaster, where real physicians in long robes and square caps accused her of practicing unlawfully on the duke's lieges. At first, she was too frightened to speak. Novice-like, the very name of "Law" paralyzed her. But questioned closely, she told the truth: she had long been her father's pupil, following his system, and had cured many. "And it is not for myself in very deed, sirs, but I have two poor helpless honest men at home upon my hands, and how else can I keep them? Ah, good sirs, let a poor girl make her bread honestly; ye hinder them not to make it idly and shamefully; and oh, sirs, ye are husbands, ye are fathers; ye cannot but see I have reason to work and provide as best I may." Ere this appeal left her lips, she would have given the world to recall it, standing with one hand upon her heart and one before her face, hiding it, but not the tears trickling underneath. All went to the wrong address. Perhaps a female bailiff might have yielded, but the burgomaster asked only if she pledged to break the law no more. On this, Doctor Margaret clasped her hands and vowed most penitently never, never, never to cure body or beast again. Being dismissed with the constables to pay the fine, she turned at the door, curtsied, and thanked the gentlemen for their forbearance.

To pay the fine, the "To-morrow box" must be opened instantly. With excess of caution, she had nailed it up, fearing temptation. Now she could not draw the nails. The constables grew impatient, doubting its contents, and said, "Let us break it for you." She refused. "Ye will break it worse than I shall." Taking a hammer, she struck too faintly, lost all strength for a minute, and wept hysterically. At last, she broke it, and a little cry bubbled from her when it shattered. She paid the fine, using all her unlawful gains and two gold pieces to boot. When the men were gone, she drew the broken pieces of the box and the little money they had left her onto the table. Her arms went round them, her rich hair escaped and fell loose, and she bowed her forehead on the wreck, sobbing, "My love's box it is broken, and my heart withal." Martin Wittenhaagen came in; she could not lift her head but sighed out to him what had befallen her, ending, "My love his box is broken, and so mine heart is broken."

Martin was not so sad as wroth. Some traitor had betrayed him. What stony heart had told and brought her to this pass? Whoever it was should feel his arrow's point. "Idle chat! idle chat!" moaned Margaret, without lifting her brow. "When you have slain all the gossips in this town, can we eat them? Tell me how to keep you all, or prithee hold thy peace, and let the saints get leave to whisper me." Martin held his tongue, casting uneasy glances at his defeated General.

Towards evening, she rose, washed her face, and did up her hair, doggedly biding Martin take down the crocodile and put out a basket instead. "I can get up linen better than they seem to do it in this street," she said. "You must carry it in the basket." "That will I for thy sake," said the soldier. "Good Martin! forgive me that I spake shrewishly to thee." Even while they talked, a male came for advice. Margaret told him the mayor had forbidden her to sell drugs. "But," she said, "I will gladly iron and starch your linen for you, and I will come and fetch it from your house." "Are ye mad, young woman?" said the male. "I come for a leech, and ye proffer me a washerwoman." It went out in dudgeon. "There is a stupid creature," said Margaret sadly.

Presently, a female came to tell the symptoms of her sick child. Margaret stopped her. "We are forbidden by the bailiff to sell drugs. But I will gladly wash, iron, and starch your linen... and I will come and fetch it from your house." "Oh, ay," said the female. "Well, I have some smocks and ruffs foul. Come for them; and when you are there, you can look at the boy." An introduction is an introduction. Two or three patients out of all those denied medicine made Doctor Margaret their washerwoman.

"Now, Martin, you must help. I'll no more cats than can slay mice." "Mistress, the stomach is not awanting for't, but the headpiece, worst luck." "Oh! I mean not the starching and ironing; that takes a woman and a handy one. But the bare washing; a man can surely contrive that. Why, a mule has wit enough in's head to do't with his hoofs, an' ye could drive him into the tub. Come, off doublet, and try." "I am your man," said the brave old soldier, stripping for the unwonted toil. "I'll risk my arm in soapsuds, an you will risk your glory." "My what?" "Your glory and honour as a--washerwoman." "Gramercy! if you are man enough to bring me half-washed linen t' iron, I am woman enough to fling't back i' the suds." And so the brave girl and the brave soldier worked with a will, keeping the wolf from the door. More they could not do. Margaret had repaired the "To-morrow box," and as she leaned over the glue, her tears mixed with it, cementing her exiled lover's box with them. A smile is allowable, but an intelligent smile tipped with pity, please, and not the empty guffaw of the nineteenth-century-jackass, burlesquing Bibles. But when mended, it stood unreplenished. They kept the weekly rent paid and the pot boiling, but no more.

Now came a concatenation. Recommended from one to another, Margaret washed for the mayor. Bringing home clean linen one day, she heard in the kitchen that his worship's only daughter was stricken with disease, not like to live. Poor Margaret could not help cross-questioning, and a female servant gave her such symptoms as she had observed. They were too general. However, one gossip would add one fact, and another another. Margaret pondered them all.

At last, she met the mayor himself. He recognized her directly. "Why, you are the unlicensed doctor." "I was," she said, "but now I'm your worship's washerwoman." The dignitary coloured, saying that was rather a come-down. "Nay, I bear no malice; for your worship might have been harder. Rather would I do you a good turn. Sir,

The air in Margaret's chamber felt thick with the weight of revelation, a day that offered both solace and sorrow. She had found in Denys a true comrade, a balm to her weary spirit, yet the news he carried was sharp: Gerard was utterly alone in that distant, strange land. Hope, fragile as glass, shattered within her; she could not share Denys's naive belief that he would return soon. Indeed, if he had intended to come home, he would have arrived long ago.

Denys, however, was a relentless source of cheer. He embraced her as his "she-comrade," filling the room with his formulas of comfort and boisterous laughter. Margaret indulged him, doting on his presence just as she did on Martin, though her temper flared when he lingered too long over her provisions. She would not allow him to eat a single meal in her house, nor would she tolerate his rough tongue. "It spoils you, Denys," she chided, her voice trembling with suppressed emotion. "Good lack, to hear such ugly words escape such a comely head! Forbear, or I shall be angry; be civil." Instantly, Denys switched to his best manners, the struggle between his native politeness and his acquired ruffianism playing out as a ludicrous pantomime.

Yet, as the saying goes, when it rains, it pours. Other visitors soon sought Margaret's friendship. Among them was Margaret Van Eyck, who had sent a humble letter confessing her loss of favor and vowing to keep her distance, yet pleading for secrecy regarding her current residence. She begged that her whereabouts not be revealed, especially not to Gerard's people, whom she despised for driving him away. She wrote bitterly of their cruelty, noting that even after his departure, their hearts had not softened toward her as they might have if they had repented.

The Van Eycks were perplexed by her sudden coldness. Eventually, they confided in Reicht, whose quick wit carried the secret like water through a pipe straight to Catherine.

"Ay, and is she turned so bitter against us?" Catherine asked, her tone laced with confusion. "She stole our son from us, and now she hates us for not rushing into her arms. Nevertheless, it is a blessing she lives and is no farther away than Rotterdam."

Meanwhile, the English princess, now Countess Charolois, made a stately progress through the northern duchy, accompanied by her stepdaughter, Marie de Bourgogne, the young heiress. The old Duke, the most magnificent prince in Europe, displayed his full splendor. Troops of dazzling knights and bevy of gorgeously attired ladies attended the procession, followed by minstrels, jongleurs, bards, musicians, actors, and tumblers. Fencing matches, dances, and merriment filled every town they visited. Richart invited all his people to gather in Rotterdam to witness the pageant.

Days passed in Rotterdam before Denys accidentally encountered Catherine in the street. After a warm greeting, he crowed that he had finally found her "she-comrade." But Catherine cooled his enthusiasm, pointing out that he would have found her sooner had he stayed quietly at Tergou rather than vagabonding across Holland. "And being found, what the better are we?" she asked sharply. "Her heart is set dead against us now."

"Oh, let that flea stick," Denys retorted, undeterred. "Come with me to her house."

"No," Catherine refused, sensing the chill of rejection. "Those who come unbidden sit unseated." She insisted that Denys act as mediator, bringing the parties to a good understanding. He accepted the task with great pomp and confidence, trotting off to Margaret.

"She-comrade," he announced, "I met today a friend of yours."

"You did look into the Rotter then, and see yourself?" Margaret replied dryly.

"Nay, 'twas a female, and one who seeks your regard. 'Twas Catherine, Gerard's mother."

"Oh, was it?" Margaret said, her voice hardening. "Then you may tell her she comes too late. There was a time I longed and longed for her; but she held aloof in my hour of most need. So now, we will be as we have been."

Denys tried to coax her, but she remained bitter and sullen, unmoved by his pleas. He then scolded her, and at that, she broke down into hysterics. Frightened by the result of his own eloquence and caught off guard, Denys allowed himself to be trapped into a solemn promise never to mention the subject again. He returned to Catherine crestfallen and recounted the reception of his overtures. Catherine flew into a rage, informing the family how her advances had been rebuffed. The feud ignited, burning fiercer each day, until little Kate alone dared to make excuses for Margaret.

The very next day, another visitor arrived. Margaret found her household in a state of military enslavement and degradation: Martin was elbow-deep in soapsuds, Denys ironing with clumsy ineptitude, and Margaret herself plaiting ruffs, her eyes scanning her raw recruits with the critical gaze of a mistress. Into this scene stepped an old man, venerable at first glance but keen and wizened upon closer inspection.

"Ah!" cried Margaret. Swiftly, she turned her back on him, hiding her face with invincible repugnance. "Oh, that man! That man!"

"Nay, fear me not," said Ghysbrecht gently. "I come on a friend's errand. I bring you a letter from foreign parts."

"Mock me not, old man," she said slowly, turning around.

"Nay, see," he replied, holding out an enormous envelope.

Margaret darted forward, snatching the letter with trembling hands and glistening eyes. It was Gerard's handwriting.

"Oh, thank you, sir, bless you for this," she whispered, forgiving him for all the ill he had ever wrought upon her. Pressing the letter to her bosom with one hand, she glided swiftly from the room, leaving Ghysbrecht behind.

As she did not return, Ghysbrecht departed, casting a scowl at Martha. Margaret was left alone with her letter for hours, the silence of the room amplifying the thunder of her emotions.

Margaret descended the stairs a transformed creature. The storm of tears had washed away her bitterness and feverish excitement, leaving her eyes wet but serene.

"Denys," she whispered, "I possess my orders. I am to read my lover's letter to his kin."

"You will never do that?" Denys asked, his voice trembling with disbelief.

"I shall."

"I perceive something in that letter has softened your heart toward them."

"Not a single jot, Denys. Not a jot. Yet, though I loathed them as poison, I could not disobey my love. Denys, it is sweet to obey, but sweeter still to obey one who stands afar, unable to enforce command, forced to trust solely in the fidelity of my love. Ah, Gerard, my darling; had you stood before me, I might have slighted your commands, repelled by your people as I have cause to be. But now, if you had bidden me wade into the raging sea to read your sweet letter to the sharks, I would have gone. Therefore, Denys, tell his mother I have the letter. If she and hers wish to hear it, I am their servant. Let them name the hour, and I will seat them as best I can and welcome them with all my power."

Denys hastened to Catherine with this tidings. He found the family gathered at dinner and announced that a long letter from Gerard had arrived. Amidst the rising joy, he added, "And her heart is softened; she will read it to you herself. You may choose your own time."

"What does she suppose no one else can read it?" Catherine asked. "Let her send the letter, and we shall read it."

"Nay, mother," little Kate interjected, "perhaps she cannot bear to part with it from her hand; she loves him so dearly."

"Do you think she fears we shall steal it?"

Cornelis suggested she merely sought to wedge herself into the family through this token of affection.

Denys cast a look of scorn upon the speaker. "There spoke a base heart," he declared. "La camarade hates you all like poison. Oh, mistake me not, dame; I defend her not, for so it is. Yet, despite her spleen at a word from Gerard, she offers to read you his letter with her own pretty mouth, possessing a voice like honey—surely a fair offer."

"'Tis so, mine honest soldier," the father, Eli, replied gravely. "It merits a civil response. Hold your tongues, ye women, and I shall answer her. Tell her that I, his father, setting aside all past grudges, do hereby thank her for this grace. If she desires double thanks, let her send my son's letter by your faithful hand. I shall read it to his flesh and blood, and then return to her so surely and faithfully as I am Eli a Dierich, a William a Luke, free burgher of Tergou, like my forbears, and like them, a man of my word."

"Ay, and a man who is better than his word," cried Catherine, "the only one I have ever encountered."

"Hold thy peace, wife."

"Art a man of sense, Eli, a dirk, a chose," shouted Denys. "The she-comrade will be right glad to obey Gerard yet avoid facing you all, whom she hates as wormwood, save in your presence. Bless ye, the world hath changed; she is all submission today: 'Obedience is honey,' quoth she; and in truth, 'tis a sweetmeat she cannot but savor, eating so little of it. What with her fair face and mellow tongue, and what with flying in fits to terrify us soldiers to death when we thwart her, and what with chiding us one moment and petting us like lambs the next, she hath made two of the crawlingest slaves ever you saw out of two honest swashbucklers. I am the ironing ruffian, the other washes."

"What next?"

"Why, whenever the brat enters the world, I shall rock the cradle, and the other knave shall wash the tucker and bib. So, I shall fetch the letter on the instant. Will you let me stay and hear it read?"

"Else our hearts were black as coal," said Catherine.

Denys departed for the letter and returned crestfallen. "She will not let it out of her hand, neither to me nor you, nor any living soul."

"I knew she would not," Cornelis remarked.

"Hush! Hush!" Eli commanded. "Let Denys tell his story."

"Nay," Denys replied, recounting the exchange, "'be ruled by me.' 'Not I,' quoth she. 'Well, but that same honey Obedience ye spake of.' 'You are a fool,' she said; 'obedience to Gerard is sweet, but obedience to any other body, whoever said that was sweet?' At last she seemed to soften a bit and gave me a written paper for you, mademoiselle. Here it is."

"For me?" little Kate blushed, taking the paper.

"Give that here!" Eli demanded, scanning the writing and speaking almost in a whisper, "These be words from the letter. Hearken!"

"And, sweetheart, if these lines should travel safe to thee, make thou trial of my people's hearts withal. Maybe they are somewhat turned towards me, being far away. If 'tis so they will show it to thee, since now to me they may not. Read, then, this letter! But I do strictly forbid thee to let it from thy hand; and if they still hold aloof from thee, why, then say nought, but let them think me dead. Obey me in this; for, if thou dost disrespect my judgment and my will in this, thou lovest me not."

A heavy silence fell upon the room as Gerard's words, copied by Margaret, were handed round and inspected.

"Well," said Catherine, "that is another matter. But methinks 'tis for her to come to us, not we to her."

"Alas, mother! what odds does that make?"

"Much," Eli replied. "Tell her we are over many to come to her, and bid her hither, the sooner the better."

When Denys had gone, Eli confessed it was a bitter pill for him. "When that lass crosses my threshold, all the mischief and misery she hath made here will seem to come in doors in one heap. But what could I do, wife? We must hear the news of Gerard. I saw that in thine eyes, and felt it in my own heart.
