And she is backed by our undutiful but still beloved son, and so is she stronger than we, bringing our noses down to the grindstone, the sly, cruel jade. But never heed. We will hear the letter; and then let her go unblessed as she came unwelcome."

"Make your mind easy," said Catherine. "She will not come at all." A tone of regret was visible in her voice.

Shortly thereafter, Richart arrived from Amsterdam, grave and dignified in his burgher's robe, gold chain, ruff, and furred cap. He was received not only with affection but with respect, for he had risen a step higher than his parents, and such ascents were marked in medieval society almost as visibly as those on staircases.

Admitted to the family council, he showed plainly, though not discourteously, that his pride was deeply wounded by their having deigned to treat with Margaret Brandt. "I see the temptation," he said. "But which of us hath not at times to wish one way and do another?" This threw a considerable chill over the older members. Little Kate ventured a word. "Vex not thyself, dear Richart. Mother says she will not come."

"All the better, sweetheart. I fear me, if she do, I shall hie me back to Amsterdam."

Just then, Denys popped his head into the door and announced, "She will be here at three on the great dial."

They all looked at one another in silence.

"Nay, Richart," Catherine finally said, her voice trembling with a mixture of maternal instinct and suppressed fury, "for Heaven's sake, let not this one sorry wench set us all by the ears. Hath she not made ill blood enough already?"

"In very deed she hath," Richart replied, his tone steady despite the storm brewing in the room. "Fear me not, good mother. Let her come and read the letter of the poor boy she hath by devilish arts bewitched, and then let her go. Give me your words to show her no countenance beyond decent and constrained civility; less we may not, being in our own house. And I will say no more."

On this understanding, they waited for the foe. Catherine, for her part, prepared for the interview in a spirit little less hostile. When Denys brought word that they would not come to her, but would receive her, her lip curled in disdain. She bade him observe how in them every feeling, however small, was larger than the love for Gerard. "Well," she said, her voice sharp, "I have not that excuse; so why mimic the pretty burgher's pride, the pride of all unlettered folk? I will go to them for Gerard's sake. Oh, how I loathe them!"

Thus, poor good-natured Denys was unwittingly bringing into one house the materials of an explosion.

Margaret made her toilet in the same spirit that a knight of her day dressed for battle—he to parry blows, and she to parry glances. Glances of contempt at her poverty, or of irony at her extravagance. Her kirtle was of English cloth, dark blue, and her farthingale and hose of the same material, but a glossy roan, or claret colour. Not an inch of pretentious fur about her, but plain snowy linen wristbands, and curiously plaited linen from the bosom of the kirtle up to the commencement of the throat; it did not encircle her throat, but framed it, being square, not round. Her front hair still peeped in two waves, much after the fashion which Mary Queen of Scots revived a century later; but instead of the silver net, which would have ill become her present condition, the rest of her head was covered with a very small tight-fitting hood of dark blue cloth, hemmed with silver. Her shoes were red; but the roan petticoat and hose prepared the spectator's mind for the shock, and they set off the arched instep and shapely foot.

Beauty knew its business then as now.

And with all this she kept her enemies waiting, though it was three by the dial.

At last she started, attended by her he-comrade. And when they were halfway, she stopped and said thoughtfully, "Denys!"

"Well, she-general?"

"I must go home" (piteously).

"What, have ye left somewhat behind?"

"What?"

"My courage. Oh! oh! oh!"

"Nay, nay, be brave, she-general. I shall be with you."

"Ay, but wilt keep close to me when I be there?"

Denys promised, and she resumed her march, but gingerly.

Meantime they were all assembled, and waiting for her with a strange mixture of feelings. Mortification, curiosity, panting affection, aversion to her who came to gratify those feelings, yet another curiosity to see what she was like, and what there was in her to bewitch Gerard and make so much mischief.

At last Denys came alone, and whispered, "The she-comrade is without."

"Fetch her in," said Eli gravely. "Now whisht, all of ye. None speak to her but I."

They all turned their eyes to the door in dead silence. A little muttering was heard outside; Denys's rough organ and a woman's soft and mellow voice. Presently that stopped; and then the door opened slowly, and Margaret Brandt, dressed as I have described, and somewhat pale, but calm and lovely, stood on the threshold, looking straight before her.

They all rose but Kate, and remained mute and staring.

"Be seated, mistress," said Eli, and motioned to a seat that had been set apart for her.

She inclined her head, and crossed the apartment; and in so doing her condition was very visible, not only in her shape, but in her languor. Cornelis and Sybrandt hated her for it. Richart thought it spoiled her beauty. It softened the women somewhat.

She took her letter out of her bosom, and kissed it as if she had been alone; then disposed herself to read it, with the air of one who knew she was there for that single purpose.

But as she began, she noticed they had seated her all by herself like a leper. She looked at Denys, and putting her hand down by her side, made him a swift furtive motion to come by her. He went with an obedient start as if she had cried "March!" and stood at her shoulder like a sentinel; but this zealous manner of doing it revealed to the company that he had been ordered thither; and at that she coloured. And now she began to read her Gerard, their Gerard, to their eager ears, in a mellow, clear voice, so soft, so earnest, so thrilling, her very soul seemed to cling about each precious sound. It was a voice as of a woman's bosom set speaking by Heaven itself.

"I do nothing doubt, my Margaret, that long ere this shall meet thy beloved eyes, Denys, my most dear friend, will have sought thee out, and told thee the manner of our unlooked for and most tearful parting. Therefore I will e'en begin at that most doleful day. What befell him after, poor faithful soul, fain, fain would I hear, but may not. But I pray for him day and night next after thee, dearest. Friend more stanch and loving had not David in Jonathan, than I in him. Be good to him, for poor Gerard's sake."

At these words, which came quite unexpectedly to him, Denys leaned his head on Margaret's high chair, and groaned aloud. She turned quickly as she sat, and found his hand, and pressed it. And so the sweetheart and the friend held hands while the sweetheart read.

"I went forward all dizzied, like one in an ill dream; and presently a gentleman came up with his servants, all on horseback, and had liked to have rid o'er me. And he drew rein at the brow of the hill, and sent his armed men back to rob me. They robbed me civilly enough and took my purse and the last copper, and rid gaily away. I wandered stupid on, a friendless pauper."

There was a general sigh, followed by an oath from Denys.

"Presently a strange dimness came o'er me; I lay down to sleep on the snow. 'Twas ill done, and with store of wolves hard by. Had I loved thee as thou dost deserve, I had shown more manhood. But oh, sweet love, the drowsiness that did crawl o'er me desolate, and benumb me, was more than nature. And so I slept; and but that God was better to us, than I to thee or to myself, from that sleep I ne'er had waked; so all do say. I had slept an hour or two, as I suppose, but no more, when a hand did shake me rudely. I awoke to my troubles. And there stood a servant girl in her holiday suit. 'Are ye mad,' quoth she, in seeming choler, 'to sleep in snow, and under wolves' nosen? Art weary o' life, and not long weaned? Come, now, said she, more kindly, 'get up like a good lad;' so I did rise up. 'Are ye rich, or are ye poor?' But I stared at her as one amazed. 'Why, 'tis easy of reply,' quoth she. 'Are ye rich, or are ye poor?' Then I gave a great, loud cry; that she did start back. 'Am I rich, or am I poor? Had ye asked me an hour agone, I had said I am rich. But now I am so poor as sure earth beareth on her bosom none poorer. An hour agone I was rich in a friend, rich in money, rich in hope and spirits of youth; but now the Bastard of Burgundy hath taken my friend, and another gentleman my purse; and I can neither go forward to Rome nor back to her I left in Holland. I am poorest of the poor.' 'Alack!' said the wench. 'Natheless, an ye had been rich ye might ha' lain down again in the snow for any use I had for ye; and then I trow ye had soon fared out o' this world as bare as ye came into it. But, being poor, you are our man: so come wi' me.' Then I went because she bade me, and because I recked not now whither I went. And she took me to a fine house hard by, and into a noble dining-hall hung with black; and there was set a table with many dishes, and but one plate and one chair. 'Fall to!' said she, in a whisper. 'What, alone?' said I. 'Alone? And which of us, think ye, would eat out of the same dish with ye? Are we robbers o' the dead?' Then she speered where I was born. 'At Tergou,' said I. Says she, 'And when a gentleman dies in that country, serve they not the dead man's dinner up as usual, till he be in the ground, and set some poor man to it?' I told her, 'nay.' She blushed for us then. Here they were better Christians.' So I behoved to sit down. But small was my heart for meat. Then this kind lass sat by me and poured me out wine; and tasting it, it cut me to the heart Denys was not there to drink with me. He doth so love good wine, and women good, bad, or indifferent. The rich, strong wine curled round my sick heart; and that day first I did seem to glimpse why folk in trouble run to drink so. She made me eat of every dish. ''Twas unlucky to pass one. Nought was here but her master's daily dinner.' 'He had a good stomach, then,' said I. 'Ay, lad, and a good heart. Leastways, so we all say now he is dead; but, being alive, no word on't e'er heard I.' So I did eat as a bird, nibbling of every dish. And she hearing me sigh, and seeing me like to choke at the food, took pity and bade me be of good cheer. I should sup and lie there that night. And she went to the hind, and he gave me a right good bed; and I told him all, and asked him would the law give me back my purse. 'Law!' quoth he; 'law there was none for the poor in Burgundy. Why, 'twas the cousin of the Lady of the Manor, he that had robbed me. He knew the wild spark. The matter must be judged before the lady; and she was quite young, and far more like to hang me for slandering her cousin, and a gentleman, and a handsome man, than to make him give me back my own. Inside the liberties of a town a poor man might now and then see the face of justice; but out among the grand seigneurs and dames--never.' So I said, 'I'll sit down robbed rather than seek justice and find gallows.' They were all most kind to me next day; and the girl proffered me money from her small wage to help me towards Rhine."

"Oh, then, he is coming home! he is coming home!" shouted Denys, interrupting the reader. She shook her head gently at him, by way of reproof.

"I beg pardon, all the company," said he stiffly.

"'Twas a sore temptation; but being a servant, my stomach rose against it. 'Nay, nay,' said I. She told me I was wrong. ''Twas pride out o' place; poor folk should help one another; or who on earth would?' I said if I could do aught in return 'twere well; but for a free gift, nay: I was overmuch beholden already. Should I write a letter for her? 'Nay, he is in the house at present,' said she. 'Should I draw her picture, and so earn my money?' 'What, can ye?' said she. I told her I could try; and her habit would well become a picture. So she was agog to be limned, and give it her lad. And I set her to stand in a good light, and soon made sketches two, whereof I send thee one, coloured at odd hours. The other I did most hastily, and with little conscience daub, for which may Heaven forgive me; but time was short. They, poor things, knew no better, and were most proud and joyous; and both kissing me after their country fashion, 'twas the hind that was her sweetheart, they did bid me God-speed; and I towards Rhine."

Margaret paused here, and gave Denys the coloured drawing to hand round. It was eagerly examined by the females on account of the costume, which differed in some respects from that of the Dutch domestic: the hair was in a tight linen bag, a yellow half kerchief crossed her head from ear to ear, but threw out a rectangular point that descended the centre of her forehead, and it met in two more points over her bosom. She wore a red kirtle with long sleeves, kilted very high in front, and showing a green farthingale and a great red leather purse hanging down over it; red stockings, yellow leathern shoes, ahead of her age; for they were low-quartered and square-toed, secured by a strap buckling over the instep, which was not uncommon, and was perhaps the rude germ of the diamond buckle to come.

Margaret continued:--

"But oh! how I missed my Denys

Margaret awoke with her head resting upon Catherine's arm, surrounded by the sturdy, honest members of the household who had gathered like a protective wall around her. Among them stood Giles, who roared with boisterous encouragement, insisting that she cease her lamentations. "Gerard is alive and well!" he declared, his voice booming with conviction. "If he were not, this letter—the largest, most magnificent, and most moving document ever penned by mankind—would not exist!"

Margaret sighed weakly, her voice trembling. "Ay, good Master Giles, he lives. But how can I know what has befallen him since? Why did he leave Holland to wander among strangers fierce as lions? And why did I not drive him from me sooner, rather than part him from his own flesh and blood? Forgive me, you who are his mother!"

Gently removing Catherine's arm, Margaret made a feeble attempt to slide from the chair to her knees. After a brief struggle against gravity and her own weakness, she found herself settled upon Catherine's lap. With a faint but sweet smile, she extended the letter to Eli. "I will trust it to your hand now," she whispered. "In truth, I am little fit to read any more... and loth to leave this comfort." She then wound her other arm tightly around Catherine's neck.

"Read thou, Richart," Eli instructed gently. "Thine eyes are younger than mine."

Richart accepted the parchment. "Well," he remarked, examining the script. "Such writing I have never seen. It is penned with a needle's point, clear and legible. Why is he not in my counting-house at Amsterdam instead of vagabonding it out there?"

"When I came to myself," Richart began reading, "I was seated in the litter, my good merchant holding my hand. I babbled nonsense, then shuddered in silence. He put a horn of wine to my lips."

"Bless him! Bless him!" Catherine exclaimed.

"Whisht!" Eli cautioned softly.

"I told him what had befallen," Richart continued. "He examined my leg; it was sprained sore and swollen at the ankle. All my points were broken; I could scarce keep up my hose. I said, 'Sir, I shall be but a burden to you, and can make you no harmony now; my poor psaltery is broken.' I grieved over my broken music, the companion of so many weary leagues. But he patted my cheek, bidding me not fret. He placed my leg on a pillow and tended me like a kind father."

"January 19," Richart read on. "I sit all day in the litter, for we push forward with haste. At night, the good, kind merchant sends me to bed and forbids me to work. Strange! Whenever I fall in with men like fiends, the next moment God sends me some good man or woman, lest I turn away from humankind. Oh, Margaret! How strangely mixed they are, and how old I feel compared to what I was three months ago. And lo! If good Master Fugger has not already bought me a psaltery."

"Eli, my man," Catherine interrupted, her eyes brightening, "if that merchant comes our way, let us buy a hundred ells of cloth from him, and not haggle."

"That will I, take your oath on't!" Eli replied eagerly.

As Richart prepared to continue, Kate glanced at her mother. With a faint blush, she drew a piece of work from under her apron and began to sew, her head bowed slightly lower than necessary. Her mother followed suit, pulling a similar piece from her pocket. Both specimens, now exposed to observation for the first time, were babies' caps, more than half finished. They told a silent tale; horror-struck, one might say they resembled little monks' cowls in their shape and delicacy.

"January 20," Richart read. "Laid up in the litter, as good as blind, yet halting to bait, the Lombardy plains burst upon me. Oh, Margaret! A land flowing with milk and honey; all sloping plains, goodly rivers, jocund meadows, delectable orchards, and blooming gardens. Though winter, it looks warmer than poor beloved Holland at midsummer, making the wanderer's face shine and his heart leap for joy to see earth so kind and smiling. Here be vines, cedars, olives, and cattle plenty, but three goats to a sheep. The draught oxen wear white linen on their necks, and standing by dark green olive-trees, each one is a picture. The folk, especially women, wear delicate strawen hats with flowers and leaves fairly imitated in silk, mixed with silver. This day we crossed a river prettily in a chained ferry-boat. On either bank was a windlass, and a single man, by turning it, drew our whole company to his shore, which I admired greatly, being a stranger. Passed over with us some country folk. An old woman, looking at a young wench, hid her face with her hand and held her crucifix out like a knight holding his sword in a tourney, dreading the evil eye."

"January 25," Richart continued, his voice taking on a scholarly tone. "Safe at Venice. A place whose strange and passing beauty is well known to thee by report of our mariners. Dost mind too how Peter would oft fill our ears withal? We handed beneath the table, and he still discoursed of this sea-enthroned and peerless city, in shape a bow, and its great canal and palaces on piles, and its watery ways plied by scores of gilded boats; and that market-place of nations, *orbis non urbis*, forum, St. Mark, his place? And his statue with the peerless jewels in his eyes, and the lion at his gate? But I, lying at my window in pain, may see none of these beauties as yet, but only a street, fairly paved, which is dull, and houses with oiled paper and linen in lieu of glass, which is rude; and the passers-by, their habits and their gestures, wherein they are superfluous. Therefore, not to miss my daily comfort of whispering to thee, I will e'en turn mine eyes inward, and bind my sheaves of wisdom reaped by travel. For I love thee so, that no treasure pleases me not shared with thee; and what treasure so good and enduring as knowledge? This then have I, Sir Footsore, learned: that each nation hath its proper wisdom, and its proper folly. Methinks, could a great king, or duke, tramp like me, and see with his own eyes, he might pick the flowers, and eschew the weeds of nations, and go home and set his own folk on Wisdom's hill. The Germans in the north were churlish, but frank and honest; in the south, kindly and honest too. Their general blot is drunkenness, which they carry even to mislike and contempt of sober men. They say commonly, 'Kanstu niecht sauffen und fressen so kanstu kienem hern wol dienen.' In England, the vulgar sort drink as deep, but the worshipful hold excess in this a reproach, and drink a health or two for courtesy, not gluttony, and still sugar the wine. In their cups the Germans use little mirth or discourse, but ply the business sadly, crying 'Seyte frolich!' The best of their drunken sport is 'Kurlemurlehuff,' a way of drinking with touching deftly of the glass, the beard, the table, in due turn, intermixed with whistlings and snappings of the finger so curiously ordered as 'tis a labour of Hercules, but to the beholder right pleasant and mirthful. Their topers, by advice of German leeches, sleep with pebbles in their mouths. For, as of a boiling pot the lid must be set ajar, so with these fleshy wine-pots, to vent the heat of their inward parts: spite of which many die suddenly from drink; but 'tis a matter of religion to slur it, and gloze it, and charge some innocent disease therewith. Yet 'tis more a custom than very nature, for their women come among the tipplers, and do but stand a moment, and as it were, kiss the wine-cup; and are indeed most temperate in eating and drinking, and of all women, modest and virtuous, and true spouses and friends to their mates; far before our Holland lasses, that being maids, put the question to the men, and being wived, do lord it over them. Why, there is a wife in Tergou, not far from our door. One came to the house and sought her man. Says she, 'You'll not find him: he asked my leave to go abroad this afternoon, and I did give it him.'"

"'Tis sooth! 'Tis sooth! 'Twas Beck Hulse, Jonah's wife," Catherine confirmed. "This comes of a woman wedding a boy."

"In the south where wine is, the gentry drink themselves bare; but not in the north: for with beer a noble shall sooner burst his body than melt his lands. They are quarrelsome, but 'tis the liquor, not the mind; for they are none revengeful. And when they have made a bad bargain drunk, they stand to it sober. They keep their windows bright; and judge a man by his clothes. Whatever fruit or grain or herb grows by the roadside, gather and eat. The owner seeing you shall say, 'Art welcome, honest man.' But an ye pluck a wayside grape, your very life is in jeopardy. 'Tis eating of that Heaven gave to be drunken. The French are much fairer spoken, and not nigh so true-hearted. Sweet words cost them nought. They call it *payer en blanche*."

"Les coquins! ha! ha!" Denys laughed.

"Natheless, courtesy is in their hearts, ay, in their very blood. They say commonly, 'Give yourself the trouble of sitting down.' And such straws of speech show how blows the wind. Also at a public show, if you would leave your seat, yet not lose it, tie but your napkin round the bench, and no French man or woman will sit here; but rather keep the place for you."

"Gramercy! That is manners. France for me!" Catherine declared.

Denys rose and placed his hand gracefully to his breastplate.

"Natheless, they say things in sport which are not courteous, but shocking. 'Le diable t'emporte!' 'Allez au diable!' and so forth. But I trow they mean not such dreadful wishes: custom belike. Moderate in drinking, and mix water with their wine, and sing and dance over their cups, and are then enchanting company. They are curious not to drink in another man's cup. In war the English gain the better of them in the field; but the French are their masters in attack and defence of cities; witness Orleans, where they besieged their besiegers and hashed them sore with their double and treble culverines; and many other sieges in this our century. More than all nations they flatter their women, and despise them. No. She may be their sovereign ruler. Also they often hang their female malefactors, instead of drowning them decently, as other nations use. The furniture in their inns is walnut, in Germany only deal. French windows are ill. The lower half is of wood, and opens; the upper half is of glass, but fixed; so that the servant cannot come at it to clean it. The German windows are all glass, and movable, and shine far and near like diamonds. In France many mean houses are not glazed at all. Once I saw a Frenchman pass a church without unbonneting. This I ne'er witnessed in Holland, Germany, or Italy. At many inns they show the traveller his sheets, to give him assurance they are clean, and warm them at the fire before him; a laudable custom. They receive him kindly and like a guest; they mostly cheat him, and whiles cut his throat. They plead in excuse hard and tyrannous laws. And true it is their law thrusteth its nose into every platter, and its finger into every pie. In France worshipful men wear their hats and their furs indoors, and go abroad lighter clad. In Germany they don hat and furred cloak to go abroad; but sit bareheaded and light clad round the stove.

"The French intermix not the men and women folk in assemblies, as we Hollanders use. Round their preachers the women sit on their heels in rows, and the men stand behind them. Their harvests are rye, and flax, and wine. Three mules shall you see to one horse, and whole flocks of sheep as black as coal.

"In Germany the snails be red. I lie not. The French buy minstrelsy, but breed jests, and make their own mirth. The Germans foster their set fools, with ear-caps, which move them to laughter by simulating madness; a calamity that asks pity, not laughter. In this particular I deem that lighter nation wiser than the graver German. What sayest thou? Alas! canst not answer me now.

"In Germany the petty laws are wondrous wise and just. Those against criminals, bloody. In France bloodier still; and executed a trifle more cruelly there. Here the wheel is common, and the fiery stake; and under this king they drown men by the score in Paris river, Seine yclept. But the English are as peremptory in hanging and drowning for a light fault; so travellers report. Finally, a true-hearted Frenchman, when ye chance on one, is a man as near perfect as earth affords; and such a man is my Denys, spite of his foul mouth."

"My foul mouth! Is that so writ, Master Richart?" Denys asked, inspecting the letter gravely.

"Ay, in sooth; see else," Richart replied.

"Humph! Ahem! Why, just the contrary," Denys countered. "It is kittle work perusing of these black scratches men are agreed

Two months prior to this moment, the inhabitants of a small coastal town nestled between Naples and Rome gathered on the beach, their gaze fixed seaward upon a vessel struggling against a ferocious gale that blew directly onto the shore. The ship appeared to weather the storm, prompting cheers from the crowd, only for the wind and surf to drive her visibly closer, eliciting a secret satisfaction among the spectators they would never admit to.

The vessel, though unsuited for open-sea navigation by scientific standards, was perfectly engineered for grounding, boasting an extravagant poop that caught the wind and lines resembling a cocked hat turned inside out. To the onlookers, the battering wooden frame seemed alive, panting against death; yet those on deck knew better, sensing a multitude of hearts and natures exposed to the terrifying hour.

Mariners stumbled wildly, handling ropes with chaotic intent, alternating curses and prayers. Passengers huddled around the mast—some seated, some kneeling, others prostrate—clutching the bulwarks as the ship rolled in mighty waves. Among them stood Gerard, a handsome young man whose ashen cheeks and compressed lips revealed a fierce battle between terror and self-respect. He held a shroud tightly, wincing with every swell. Amid the trembling throng, prayers and vows rose like incense, creating a cacophony of deities. While sailors relied on a single goddess, shifting her titles from "Queen of Heaven" to "Haven of Safety," the landsmen embraced polytheism with fervor. An English merchant vowed gold to Our Lady of Walsingham; a Genoese pledged a silver collar to Our Lady of Loretto; a Tuscan noble promised wax lights to Our Lady of Ravenna. Each pledged allegiance to a specific local manifestation of the divine rather than the universal truth.

Suddenly, a violent gust caught the sail at a disadvantage. Rotten shrouds snapped, and the sail tore away with a loud crack, fluttering downwind like a sheet of paper until it vanished into the sea. Before the helmsman could adjust, a wave crashed over the quarter, drenching the crew to the bone and offering a foretaste of chill death. Vows erupted anew: one man vowed to become a Carthusian monk if Saint Thomas saved him; another promised a pilgrimage to Compostela in armor and bare feet if Saint James intervened. Others invoked Thomas, Dominic, Denis, and especially Catherine of Siena.

Two Neapolitan traders shivered nearby. One shouted, "I vow a waxen image of St. Christopher's own weight if I reach land safely!" His companion nudged him, warning, "Brother, take heed; selling all you possess won't buy that much wax." "Silence, fool," the first retorted, whispering, "If I survive, I'll not give him a straw."

Others lay flat, praying to the sea itself: "Oh, merciful sea! Oh, generous sea! Be gentle, preserve us!" Wails of animal terror rose as the ship became a mere plaything in the arms of the waves.

Seated amidst the wailing was a humble Roman woman with her child at her breast. Her cheek was pale, her eyes calm, her lips moving in silent prayer. She neither wept nor bargained with the gods. When the ship seemed to vanish beneath them and bearded men squealed, she simply kissed her child. She sat patiently, suckling him in the jaws of death, refusing to deny him any joy she could offer. In her veins flowed undiluted Roman blood; the instinct of her ancient race taught her to meet death with dignified composure.

A gigantic friar stood on the poop, feet planted wide like the Colossus of Rhodes, ignoring the peril rather than defying it. Reciting verses from the Canticles with an unwavering voice, he invited passengers to confess. Some knelt, receiving absolution as if in a secure sacristy. Gerard, drawn by an instinct to seek the impregnable, moved closer. The friar's composure dwarfed the courage of heroes facing fleshly odds. Here, two figures maintained the dignity of their race: a woman, tender yet heroic, and a monk steeled by faith against mortal fear.

With the sail gone, sailors cut down the mast, and its fall seemed to relieve the ship slightly. Yet the hull, no longer propelled by canvas, could not outrun the sea. Waves struck the poop repeatedly, blows as heavy as those from a rocky mountain. The captain, pale as death, left the helm to cry, "Lighten her! Fling everything overboard, or we shall founder before we strike!" As sailors executed the order, the captain spoke with a calmness alien to an English skipper in peril. "Friends," he said, "last night a globe of fire approached us. If both Castor and Pollux appear, we are lucky; if one comes alone, we are doomed. Prepare to die."

A loud wail answered him. When asked how long they had to prepare, he replied, "Perhaps half an hour; after that, it is impossible. She leaks like a sieve." Passengers seized everything on deck and threw it overboard. They grabbed a heavy sack from under an elderly, seasick man. As they dragged it to the side, the owner leaped up with an unearthly shriek. "Holy Moses! What are you doing? It is my all! My silver candlesticks, plates, brooches!" "Let go, hoary villain," cried others. "Shall we all die for your ill-gotten gear?" "Fling him in with it," shouted one. "It is this Hebrew we Christians are drowned for." They wrestled the man away, and the sack splashed into the waves. The owner cried out in anguish, his white hair streaming, and lunged to jump after it. But it sank, and he staggered, tearing his hair and cursing the ship, the sea, and all powers of heaven and hell.

"Now," cried the captain, "see a church in sight. Steer for it, and pray to the saint, whoever he may be." They steered toward the church, praying to an unknown god. A tremendous sea broke over them, snapping the rudder and jamming it immovable while flooding the deck.

Superstitious terror gripped some, who turned to Gerard. "Here is the cause," they cried. "He has never invoked a saint. He is a heathen, a pagan aboard." "Alas, good friends, say not so," Gerard stammered, teeth chattering. "Call these heathens who pray to the sea. I honor the saints, but I dare not pray to them now; there is no time. What avail Dominic, Thomas, and Catherine? Nearer God's throne sits St. Peter, and if I pray to him, I shall be drowned before he can plead my case. Oh, sweet Jesus, pitiful Jesus, who walked on Genezareth when Peter sank and wept for Lazarus, save poor Gerard—for dear Margaret's sake!"

At that moment, sailors prepared to desert the sinking ship in the lifeboat. A rush of egotists ensued, crowding thirty souls into the vessel. Three remained bewildered, running to and fro, unable to act. Two sat paralyzed by terror, like heaps of wet rags. One knelt, praying over a wooden statue of the Virgin Mary, which the sailors had detached from the mast. The statue washed about the deck as water poured in, and the poor soul followed it on his knees, clasping his hands to it. Beside him sat the Jew, palsied not by fear but by a different affliction. No longer capable of petty passions, he sat cross-legged, bemoaning his lost bag, shaking his fist at the spray and cursing the Nazarenes, their gods, their devils, and their waters to eternity.

The gigantic Dominican, having shriven the entire ship, stood calmly communing with his spirit. The Roman woman sat pale and patient, drawing her child closer as death approached.

Gerard saw this and his manhood awakened. "See! See!" he cried. "They have taken the boat, leaving the poor woman and child to perish." His heart set his wit working. "Wife, I will save thee, please God." He ran to find a cask or plank, finding none. His eye fell on the wooden image of the Virgin. Catching it in his arms, heedless of the wail from its worshipper, he ran aft. "Come, wife," he cried. "I will lash thee and the child to this. It is worm-eaten, but it will serve."

She turned her great dark eyes upon him, saying a single word: "Thyself?!" But with wonderful magnanimity and tenderness. "I am a man and have no child to care for." "Ah!" she replied, her face animating with a desire to live. He lashed the image to her side. With the hope of life, she lost some of her heroic calm; her body trembled, but her eye remained steady.

The ship was so low in the water that using an oar as a lever, he could slide her into the waves. "Come," he said, "while yet there is time." She turned her wet Roman eyes upon him. "Poor youth! God forgive me! My child!" He launched her onto the surge, keeping her from battering against the ship with his oar.

A heavy hand fell on him, and a deep, sonorous voice sounded in his ear: "'Tis well. Now come with me." It was the gigantic friar. Gerard turned, and the friar took two strides, seizing the broken mast. Gerard did the same, obeying instinctively. Together, after a prodigious effort, they hoisted the remainder of the mast and carried it off. "Fling it in," said the friar, "and follow it." They threw it in, but one of the bewildered passengers had run after them, jumping first onto one end. Gerard seized the other, the friar the middle.

It was a terrible situation. The mast rose and plunged with each wave like a kicking horse, spray flogging their faces and blinding them. A long grating noise echoed ahead; the ship had struck. Soon, stationary, they were hurled against her with tremendous force. Their companion's head struck the broken rudder with a horrible crack, smashing like a coconut by a sledgehammer. He sank directly, leaving no trace but a red stain on the water and a white clot on the jagged wood, his death cry ringing in their ears as they drifted under the lee of the black hull. The friar uttered a short Latin prayer for his soul and took his place composedly.

They rolled along; one moment they seemed in a basin of watery hills, the next catching glimpses of the shore speckled with people throwing up their arms in wild Italian gestures to encourage them. The black boat drove bottom-upwards, and between it and them, the woman rose and fell. She had found a paddle and held her child tight with her left arm, paddling gallantly with her right.

After a long time, the friar said quietly, "I touched the ground." "Impossible, father," Gerard replied. "We are more than a hundred yards from shore. Leave not our faithful mast." "My son," said the friar, "you speak prudently. But know I have business for Holy Church and may not waste time floating when I can walk in her service. There I felt it with my toes; see the benefit of sandals, not shoes. Again; and sandy. Thy stature is less than mine: keep to the mast! I walk." He left the mast and, extending his powerful arms, rushed through the water. Gerard soon followed. At each overpowering wave, the monk stood like a tower, closing his mouth and throwing his head back to encounter it, disappearing entirely before emerging to plough lustily on.

Finally, they came close to shore, but the outward suction baffled all attempts to land. Natives sent stout fishermen into the sea, holding long spears in a triple chain, and so dragged them ashore. The friar shook himself, bestowed a short paternal benediction on the natives, and walked on to Rome, eyes bent on earth according to his rule, without pausing or casting a glance back at the sea that had nearly engulfed him but lacked power to harm him without his Master's leave.

While the friar stalked alone to Rome, I, not in the service of Holy Church, stop to note that the reader and I were within six inches of this giant once before, escaping him then. Now I fear we are in for him. Gerard grasped every hand on the beach. They brought him to an enormous fire and, with a delicacy rare in the north, left him to dry alone. Only then did he consent to don a fisherman's dress, leaving his own by the fire, and descend to the beach.

What he saw was brief. The captain stuck by the ship, convinced it was idle to resist Castor or Pollux, whichever had come for him in a ball of fire. Nevertheless, the sea broke up the ship, sweeping the poop, captain and all, clear of the rest, taking him safely ashore. Gerard had a principal hand in pulling him from the water. The disconsolate Hebrew landed on another fragment, offering a reward for his bag upon touching earth, which excited little sympathy but some amusement. Two more were saved on pieces of the wreck. The thirty egotists came ashore one by one, dead; one breathed still. The natives, with excellent intentions, took him to a hot fire. So he too retired from this shifting scene.

As Gerard stood by the sea, watching his late companions washed ashore with mixed horror and curiosity, a hand was laid lightly on his shoulder. He turned. It was the Roman matron, burning with womanly gratitude. She took his hand gently, raising it slowly to her lips to kiss it, so nobly that she seemed to confer an honor upon a deserving hand. Then, with a face beaming and moist eyes, she held her child up to kiss his preserver. Gerard kissed the child more than once; he was fond of children. But he said nothing. He was deeply moved, for she did not speak, communicating only with her eyes, glowing cheeks, and noble, antique gesture, so large and stately. Perhaps she was right. Gratitude is not a

Gerard secured a modest lodging on the west bank of the Tiber, embarking each day on a relentless quest for employment. He carried samples to every workshop rumored to commission such work, only to be met with icy disdain. "Our letters are somewhat thinner than this," one merchant scoffed. "How dark your ink is," another remarked. Yet the prevailing cry was dismissive: "What avail does this serve? Latin writ is scarce here now. Can you not write Greek?"

"Ay, but not nearly so well as Latin," Gerard admitted.

"Then you shall never earn your bread in Rome."

Undeterred, Gerard borrowed a magnificent Greek manuscript at a steep price, returning home with a hollow purse but an unbroken spirit. Within a fortnight, he had mastered the Greek character with remarkable speed. Eager to capitalize on this skill, he devoted his mornings to study and his afternoons to hunting clients. When he finally presented a Greek specimen superior to any they possessed, the traders merely informed him that both Greek and Latin were unsaleable; the city was flooded with works from across Europe. They advised him bluntly that he should have arrived the previous year.

Desperate, Gerard purchased a psaltery. His landlady, charmed by his appearance and demeanor, often offered him kind words. One day, she invited him to dine and, surprised by his despondency, asked what had dampened his spirits. He confessed his struggles. She offered her interpretation: "Those sly traders," she declared, "have writers in their pay, compensated nobly for their work while paying you poorly. No wonder they blow cold on you. I suspect you write too well. How do I know? Because you do not lock your door, like the churl Pietro, and women are naturally curious. Yes, you write too well for them."

Gerard requested clarification.

"Why," she explained, "your excellent work might blind those to whom they are selling inferior goods."

Gerard sighed. "Alas, dame, you read people on the wrong side, yet you are so kind and frank yourself."

"My dear little heart, these Romans are a subtle race. I am a Siennese, thanks to the Virgin."

"My mistake was leaving Augsburg," Gerard lamented.

"Augsburg?" she replied haughtily. "Is that a place to compete with Rome? I have never heard of it."

She then assured him that he would make his fortune despite the booksellers. "Seeing you as a stranger, they lie without sense or discretion. All the world knows that our great figures are bitten by the writing spider; they pour out their money like water, turning good land and houses into scribbled sheepskins to hide in chests. God help them, and has sent them safely through this fury, as He has through many others. Since the age of scribbling began, there has been less cutting and stabbing among rival factions, less vindictive eating of opponents' livers, minced and fried. Consider Cardinal Bassarion and the Pope himself—a pair who could keep a score like you writing night and day. I shall speak to Teresa; she hears the court gossip."

The following day, she reported seeing Teresa and learning of five additional signors afflicted by the writing spider. Gerard recorded their names, purchased parchment, and spent several days preparing specimens. He left one, bearing his name and address, at each of their doors, awaiting results in hopeful silence.

There were none.

Days turned into weeks, leaving him heartsick. Strangely, this was the same period when Margaret fought desperately against overwhelming odds to feed her male dependents in Rotterdam, only to be arrested for practicing medicine without a license rather than killing with it.

Gerard saw ruin staring him in the face.

He spent his afternoons collecting canzonets and mastering them, laying in playing cards for color, and striking off a meal each day. This final stroke of genius brought him fresh trouble. In these "camere locande," the landlady prepared all meals, though the lodgers purchased the provisions. His hostess quickly detected his deception. With a brusque opening that made him blush and look frightened, she pacified herself instantly, appealing to his common sense: "Adversity is not a thing to be overcome on an empty stomach."

"Patienza, my lad! Times will mend; meantime, I will feed you for the love of heaven."

"Nay, hostess," Gerard replied, "my purse is not yet quite void, and it would add to my trouble if true folk lost their due by me."

"Why, you are as mad as your neighbor Pietro, with his one bad picture."

"Why, how do you know 'tis a bad picture?"

"Because nobody will buy it. There is one who has no gift. He must don casque and glaive and carry his panel as a shield."

Gerard pricked up his ears. She elaborated: Pietro had arrived from Florence with money and an unfinished painting, renting an unfurnished room opposite Gerard's and furnishing it neatly. Upon completion, he received visitors and offers—liberal ones, in her opinion—but refused them so disdainfully that he made enemies. Since then, he had often taken the painting out to sell, only to return it defeated. Over the last month, she had watched his possessions vanish; now he wore but one suit and slept on a great chest. She had discovered this only by peering through the keyhole, for he locked his door vigilantly whenever he left. "Is he afraid we shall steal his chest or his picture, that no soul in all Rome is weak enough to buy?"

"Nay, sweet hostess; do you not see 'tis his poverty he seeks to screen from view?"

"And the more fool he! Are all our hearts as ill as his? A trial might suffice."

"How you speak of him. Why, his case is mine; and your countryman to boot."

"Oh, we Siennese love strangers. His case yours? Nay, 'tis just the contrary. You are the comeliest youth ever lodged in this house; hair like gold. He is a dark, sour-visaged loon. Besides, you know how to take a woman on her better side; not he. Nevertheless, I wish he would not starve to death in my house, earning me a bad name. Anyway, one starveling is enough in any house. You are far from home, and it is for me, the mistress here, to number your meals—for me and the Dutch wife, your mother, who is far away. We two women shall settle that matter. Mind your own business, being a man, and leave cooking and such to us, who are in the world for little else but roasting fowls, suckling men at starting, and sweeping grown-up cobwebs."

"Dear kind dame, in sooth you often remind me of my mother, who is far away."

"All the better; I'll put you more in mind of her before I have done with you." The honest soul beamed with pleasure.

Gerard, neither an egotist nor blinded by female partiality, saw his own grief reflected in poor, proud Pietro. The more he pondered it, the more he resolved to share his humble means with the unlucky artist; Pietro's sympathy would repay him. He attempted to intercept Pietro but failed.

One day, he heard a groan from the room. He knocked, receiving no answer, then knocked again. A surly voice commanded him to enter.

He obeyed timidly, stepping into a garret furnished with a chair, a picture facing the wall, an iron basin, an easel, and a long chest. Coiled upon the chest was a haggard young man with a wonderfully bright eye. Nothing resembled a coiled cobra ready to strike the first comer more than the sight.

"Good Signor Pietro," Gerard said, "forgive me that, weary of my own solitude, I intrude on yours. I am your nearest neighbor in this house, and methinks your brother in fortune. I am an artist too."

"You are a painter? Welcome, signer. Sit down on my bed."

Pietro jumped off and waved him into the vacant throne with magnificent courtesy.

Gerard bowed and smiled, yet hesitated. "I may not call myself a painter. I am a writer, a caligrapher. I copy Greek and Latin manuscripts when I can find patrons to pay for them."

"And you call that an artist?"

"Without offense to your superior merit, Signor Pietro."

"No offense, stranger, none. Only, it seems to me an artist is one who thinks and paints his thought. Now a caligrapher merely draws in black and white the thoughts of another."

"'Tis well distinguished, signor. But then, a writer can record the thoughts of the great ancients, matters of pure reason such as no man may paint: yes, and the thoughts of God, which angels could not paint. But let that pass. I am a painter as well; but a sorry one."

"The better thy luck. 'They will buy thy work in Rome.'"

"But seeking to commend myself to one of thy eminence, I thought it well rather to call myself a capable writer than a scurvy painter."

At that moment, footsteps echoed on the stairs. "Ah! 'tis the good dame," cried Gerard. "Hostess, I am here in conversation with Signor Pietro. I dare say he will let me have my humble dinner here."

The Italian bowed gravely.

The landlady brought in Gerard's dinner, smoking and savory. She set the dish down on the bed with a face divested of all expression and departed.

Gerard began to eat. But before he had consumed many mouthfuls, he stopped. "I am an ill-mannered churl, Signor Pietro. I never eat to my mind when eating alone. For our Lady's sake, put a spoon into this ragout with me; 'tis not unsavory, I promise you."

Pietro fixed his glittering eye on him.

"What, good youth, thou a stranger, and offerest me thy dinner?"

"Why, see, there is more than one can eat."

"Well, I accept," said Pietro, taking the dish with some appearance of calmness, then flinging the contents out the window.

He turned, trembling with mortification and ire. "Let that teach thee to offer alms to an artist thou knowest not, master writer."

Gerard's face flushed with anger, and it cost him a bitter struggle not to box this high-souled creature's ears. To go and destroy good food! His mother's milk curdled in his veins with horror at such impiety. Finally, pity for Pietro's petulance and egotism, mixed with a touch of respect for poverty-struck pride, prevailed.

However, he said coldly, "Likely what thou hast done might pass in a novel of thy countryman, Signor Boccaccio; but 'twas not honest."

"Make that good!" said the painter sullenly.

"I offered thee half my dinner; no more. But thou hast taken it all. Hadst a right to throw away thy share, but not mine. Pride is well, but justice is better."

Pietro stared, then reflected.

"'Tis well. I took thee for a fool, so transparent was thine artifice. Forgive me! And prithee leave me! Thou seest how 'tis with me. The world hath soured me. I hate mankind. I was not always so. Once more excuse that my discourtesy, and fare thee well."

Gerard sighed and moved for the door.

Suddenly, a thought struck him. "Signor Pietro," he said, "we Dutchmen are hard bargainers. We are the lads 'een eij scheeren,' that is, 'to shave an egg.' Therefore, I, for my lost dinner, do claim to feast mine eyes on your picture, whose face is toward the wall."

"Nay, nay," said the painter hastily. "Ask me not that; I have already misconducted myself enough towards thee. I would not shed thy blood."

"Saints forbid! My blood?"

"Stranger," said Pietro sullenly, "irritated by repeated insults to my picture, which is my child, my heart, I did in a moment of rage make a solemn vow to drive my dagger into the next one that should flout it, and the labor and love I have given to it."

"What, are all to be slain that will not praise this picture?" He looked at its back with curiosity.

"Nay, nay; if you would but look at it, and hold your parrot tongues. But you will be talking. So I have turned it to the wall forever. Would I were dead, and buried in it for my coffin!"

Gerard reflected.

"I accept the condition. Show me the picture! I can but hold my peace."

Pietro went and turned its face, placing it in the best light the room afforded, then coiled himself again on his chest, his eye and stiletto glittering.

The picture represented the Virgin and Christ flying through the air in a sort of cloud of shadowy cherubic faces; underneath was a landscape forty or fifty miles in extent, with a purple sky above.

Gerard stood and looked at it in silence. Then he stepped close, examined it, retired as far as possible, and looked again; but said not a word.

When he had been at this game half an hour, Pietro cried out querulously and somewhat inconsistently: "Well, have you not a word to say about it?"

Gerard started. "I cry your mercy; I forgot there were three of us here. Ay, I have much to say." And he drew his sword.

"Alas! Alas!" cried Pietro, jumping in terror from his lair. "What wouldst thou?"

"Marry, defend myself against thy bodkin, signor; and at due odds, being, as aforesaid, a Dutchman. Therefore, hold aloof, while I deliver judgment, or I will pin thee to the wall like a cockchafer."

"Oh! Is that all?" said Pietro, greatly relieved. "I feared you were going to stab my poor picture with your sword, already stabbed by so many foul tongues."

A week had passed since their last encounter, yet the atmosphere in the lodging remained thick with unspoken tension. Pietro and Gerard sat side by side, ostensibly working, but their spirits were worlds apart. Pietro attacked his canvas with frantic, erratic bursts of energy, producing miracles in moments before collapsing into silence to hurl curses at his own handiwork. He would resume his labor in a fit of rage only to abandon it again with a heavy sigh. Throughout this tempest of emotion, the shrewd Dutchman remained an island of calm, his hands moving steadily, a serene smile fixed upon his face.

In truth, Gerard had long ago tamed his friend. The two men were painting playing cards, a project designed to fill their coffers. Once finished, the indignant master took up his painting to begin his daily rounds in search of a buyer. Gerard implored him to take the cards as well, urging him to try selling them. Pietro recoiled as if bitten by a viper, but eventually, embracing Gerard in the warm, familiar manner of Italy, he agreed. First, however, he dried the freshly painted cards in the fierce, golden sun that bathed this fortunate land.

Left alone, Gerard sketched a few Greek letters and then repaired a tear in his hose. His landlady observed him thus occupied and queried, with a tone dripping with irony, whether there were no women in the house. "When you have finished that," she said, "come and speak with Teresa, my friend—the one I mentioned to you. She has a husband who is little good for anything, yet he boasts of knowing the great."

Gerard descended the stairs, and who should meet him but the Roman matron. "Ah, Madam," he greeted her, "is it you? The good dame told me nothing of this. And the little fair-haired boy? Is he well? Has the voyage in that strange vessel harmed him in any way?"

"He is well," the matron replied simply.

"Why, what are you two discussing?" the landlady demanded, staring alternately at them with wide eyes. "And why do you tremble so, Teresa mia?"

"He saved my child's life," Teresa answered, struggling to compose herself.

"What!" the landlady exclaimed. "My lodger? And he never told me a word of such a thing! Are you not ashamed to look me in the face?"

"Alas! Speak not harshly to him," the matron interceded. She then turned to her companion and poured forth a glowing account of Gerard's conduct. During this recital, Gerard stood blushing with the intensity of a maiden, scarcely recognizing his own actions as gratitude rendered them so beautiful.

"And to think you asked me to serve your lodger, of whom I knew nothing save that he possessed your good word, oh, Fiammina! That was sufficient for me. Dear youth, in serving you, I serve myself."

What followed was an eager exchange between the two women regarding the obstacles that stood between the patrons of art and an unknown artist in the Eternal City—a thick wall of fees, commissions, and legal chicanery. Teresa smiled with a sad amusement at Gerard's naivety in having left specimens of his skill at the doors of the powerful without proper introduction.

"What!" she cried. "Without promising the servants a share? Without even offering them a fee to allow the signori to view your merchandise! It would have been wiser to cast it into the Tiber."

"Well-a-day!" sighed Gerard. "Then how is an artist to find a patron? For artists are poor, not rich."

"By seeking a city nobler and less greedy than this," Teresa advised. "The Roman court does not wish for a sheep without wool."

She fell into thought and declared she would return the following day. The landlady congratulated Gerard, remarking that Teresa had something stirring in her mind.

Scarcely had Teresa departed when Pietro returned with his picture, looking black as thunder. Gerard exchanged a glance with the landlady and followed him upstairs to offer comfort.

"What! Have they allowed you to bring home your masterpiece?"

"As heretofore."

"More fools they, then."

"That is not necessarily a bad thing."

"Why, what is the matter?"

"They have bought the cards!" Pietro shouted, hammering the air furiously in every direction.

"All the better," Gerard said cheerfully.

"They flew at me for them! They were enraptured. They tried to conceal their longing, but failed utterly. I saw, I feigned, I pillaged; curse those simpletons."

He threw a dozen small silver coins onto the floor, leaped upon them, danced with basilisk-like eyes, and then kicked them assiduously, sending them spinning and scattering across the room. Gerard dropped to his knees, gathered the maltreated innocents directly, and transferred them tenderly into his purse.

"You should rather smile at their ignorance and put it to profit," he suggested.

"And so I will," Pietro replied, his indignation now concentrated. "The brutes! We shall paint a pack a day; we shall set the whole city gambling and ruining itself, while we live like princes upon their vices and stupidity."

"There was one of the queens, however, that I would have kept back," Pietro continued. "'Twas you who painted her, brother. She had lovely red-brown hair and sapphire eyes, and above all, a soul."

"Pietro," Gerard said softly, "I painted that one from my heart."

The quick-witted Italian nodded, his eyes twinkling. "You love her so well, yet you leave her."

"Pietro," Gerard replied gently, "it is precisely because I love her so dearly that I have wandered this weary road."

This poignant conversation was interrupted by the landlady's cry from below: "Come down, you are wanted." Gerard descended, and there stood Teresa once more.

"Come with me, Ser Gerard."

Gerard walked in silence beside Teresa, his mind wandering as artists often do, speculating on her intentions rather than daring to ask. Finally, she spoke of her own volition. A friend had informed her of a goldsmith's wife seeking a writer; her shop was nearby.

Soon they arrived at the goldsmith's dwelling.

"Madama," Teresa said, "Leonora tells me you require a writer. I have brought you a fine one; he saved my child from the cruel waves. Pray, look upon him with favor."

The goldsmith's wife, in a sense, complied. Her gaze lingered on Gerard's handsome face, reluctant to withdraw. Yet her reply was disappointing. "Nay, I have no use for a writer. Ah! Now I recall, it was my gossip, Claelia the sausage-maker, who sought one. She told me, and I passed it to Leonora."

Teresa offered a polite remark and withdrew.

Claelia lived further away. Upon reaching her home, she was absent. Teresa remained calm, stating, "I shall wait for her return," and sat so still, so dignified, that Gerard began to draw her sketch furtively until Claelia appeared.

"Madama, I hear from the excellent Olympia, the goldsmith's wife, that you need a writer," Claelia said, taking Gerard's hand and leading him forward. "I have brought you a beautiful one; he rescued my child from the treacherous sea. For our Lady's sake, look kindly upon him."

"My good dame, my fair Sir," Claelia replied, "I have no need for a writer; but now that you remind me, it was my friend Appia Claudia, a tailor living in the Via Lepida, who requested one recently."

Teresa retired quietly.

"Madama," Gerard interjected, "this seems likely to be a tedious pursuit for you."

Teresa opened her eyes. "What has ever been accomplished without a measure of patience?" she added gently. "We shall knock at every door in Rome, and you shall receive justice."

"But, madama, I believe we are being followed. I noticed a man trailing us, sometimes at a distance, sometimes close."

"I have seen it," Teresa said coldly, though her cheeks flushed faintly. "It is my poor Lodovico."

She paused, turned, and beckoned with her finger. A figure approached them, somewhat unwillingly.

Upon seeing him, Teresa gazed steadily into his face, and he looked sheepish.

"Lodovico mio," she said, "know this young Sir, of whom I have spoken so often. Know him and love him, for he is the one who saved your wife and child."

At these words, Lodovico, who had been bowing and grinning artificially, suddenly transformed into an expression of heartfelt gratitude and embraced Gerard warmly.

Yet, there was something in the man's initial demeanor, and the fact that he had followed his wife in secret, that made Gerard uneasy under this embrace. Nevertheless, he asked, "Shall we have your company, Sir Lodovico?"

"No, signor," Lodovico replied, "I do not go that way across the Tiber."

"Addio, then," Teresa said significantly.

"When shall you return home, Teresa mia?"

"When I have completed my errand, Lodovico."

They continued their journey in silence. Teresa now wore a sad, almost gloomy expression.

Briefly, Appia Claudia proved merciful. She did not send them across the river again, but merely a hundred yards down the street to Lucretia, who kept a glove shop. Lucretia also required a writer, though Appia Claudia could not fathom the purpose. Lucretia was a cheerful little lady who received them warmly, declaring she needed no writer as she kept all her accounts in her head. "It was for my confessor, Father Colonna; he is mad after them," she explained.

"I have heard of his excellence," Teresa remarked.

"Who has not?"

"But, good dame, he is a friar; he has taken a vow of poverty. I cannot allow the young man to write without payment. He saved my child at sea."

"Did he now?" Lucretia cast an approving glance at Gerard. "Well, rest assured; a Colonna never lacks funds. The good father need only speak the word, and the princes of his lineage will pour a thousand crowns into his lap. And what a confessor, dame! The finest in Rome. His mind is leagues and leagues away all the while; he never heeds what you say. Why, I confess my sins to him no more than I would tell them to that wall. Once, to test him, I confessed, along with the others, that I had killed my lodger's little girl and baked her in a pie. Well, when my voice ceased confessing, he startled out of his dream, gathered a gloom, and said, 'My erring sister, say three Paternosters and three Ave Marias kneeling, and eat no butter nor eggs next Wednesday, and pax vobiscum!' Then off he went with his hands behind him, looking as though I did not exist."

Teresa waited patiently, then calmly steered this discursive lady back to the point: "Would you be so kind as to accompany this good youth to the friar and speak for him?"

"Alack! How can I leave my shop? And what need? His door is always open to writers, painters, and scholars, and all such cattle. Why, once he refused to receive the Duke d'Urbino because a learned Greek was closeted with him; the friar's head and the Greek's were so close over a dusty parchment from Greece that you could have placed one cowl over both. His wench Onesta told me. She mostly comes here for a chat when she runs an errand."

"This is the man for thee, my friend," Teresa said.

"All you must do," Lucretia continued, "is go to his lodgings—my boy shall show you—and tell Onesta you come from me and are a writer, and she will take you up to him. If you place a piece of silver in the wench's hand, it will do you no harm; that stands to reason."

"I have silver," Teresa said warmly.

"But stay," Lucretia cautioned, "mind one thing. Whatever the young man claims he can do, he must be able to do, or let him shun the good friar like poison. He is a very wild beast against all bunglers. Why, 'twas but the other day, someone brought him an ill-carved crucifix. He said, 'Is this how you present "Salvator Mundi"? Who died for you in mortal agony, and you grudge him careful work? This slovenly trinket, a crucifix? But since it is a crucifix of some sort, and I am a holy man, I would dust your jacket with your crucifix,' he declared. Onesta heard every word through the keyhole; so mind."

"Have no fears, madama," Teresa said loftily. "I will answer for his ability; he saved my child."

Gerard was not subtle enough to appreciate this conclusion; indeed, he was far from sharing Teresa's confidence and begged for a respite. He would rather not go to the friar today; would tomorrow not suffice?

"Here is a coward for ye," Lucretia said.

"No, he is not a coward," Teresa snapped, "he is modest."

"I am afraid of this high-born, fastidious friar," Gerard admitted. "Consider he has seen the handiwork of all the writers in Italy, dear dame Teresa. If you would but let me prepare a better piece of work than I have done yet, and then tomorrow I will face him."

"I consent," Teresa said.

They walked home together.

Not far from his lodging was a shop selling vellum. A beautiful white skin hung in the window. Gerard looked at it wistfully but knew he could not afford it, so he hurried past. However, he soon decided where to obtain vellum. Parting with Teresa at his own door, he ran hastily upstairs, took the bond he had brought all the way from Sevenbergen, and laid it with a sigh on the table. He then prepared his chemicals to erase the old writing; but since this was his last chance to read it, he overcame his deadly repugnance to bad handwriting and proceeded to decipher the deed despite its detestable contractions. It appeared that Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was to advance money to Floris Brandt on a piece of land, to be repaid from the rent.

On this, Gerard felt it would be imprudent and improper to destroy the deed. On the contrary, he vowed to decipher every word at his leisure. He went downstairs, determined to buy a small piece of vellum with his share of the card-money.

At the bottom of the stairs, he found the landlady and Teresa talking. At the sight of him, the former cried, "Here he is. You are caught, donna mia. See what she has bought you?" and whipped out from under her apron the very skin of vellum Gerard had longed for.

"Why, dame! Why, donna Teresa!" He was speechless with pleasure and astonishment.

"Dear donna Teresa, there is not a skin in all Rome like it. However did you hit upon this one? 'Tis glamour."

"Alas, dear boy, did not thine eye rest on it with desire? And didst thou not sigh in turning away from it? And was it for Teresa to let thee want the thing after that?"

"What sagacity! What goodness, madama! Oh, dame, I never thought I should possess this. What did you pay for it?"

"I forget. Addio, Fiammina. Addio, Ser Gerard. Be happy, be prosperous, as you are good." And the Roman matron glided away while Gerard hesitated, thinking how to offer such a stately creature payment for her purchase.

The next afternoon, he went to Lucretia, and her boy took him to Fra Colonna's lodgings. He announced his business, fed Onesta, and she took him up to the friar. Gerard entered with a beating heart. The room was large, strewed and heaped with objects of art, antiquity, and learning, lying in rich profusion and confusion. Manuscripts, pictures, carvings in wood and ivory, musical instruments; and amidst this glorious chaos sat the friar, poring intently over an Arabian manuscript.

He looked up a little peevishly at the interruption. Onesta whispered in his ear.

"Very well," he said. "Let him be seated. Stay; young man, show me how you write?" He threw Gerard a piece of paper and pointed to an inkhorn.

"So please you, reverend father," Gerard said, "my hand trembles too much at this moment; but last night I wrote a vellum page of Greek, and the Latin version by its side, to show the various characters."

"Show it me?"

Gerard brought the work to him in fear and trembling, then stood heart-sick, awaiting his verdict.

When it came, it staggered him. For the verdict was a Dominican falling upon his neck.

The next day, an event occurred in Holland whose effect on Gerard's destiny no mortal at the time, nor even my intelligent reader now, could foresee.

Marched up to Eli's door a pageant brave to the eye of sense and to the vulgar judgment noble, but to the philosophic, pitiable more or less.

It looked like one animal, a centaur; but on severe analysis proved two. The human half was sadly bedizened with those two metals with which man clothes his carcass and lines his pouch, occasionally disposing of his soul; still, the horse was the vainer brute of the two. He was far worse beflounced, bebonneted, and bemantled than any fair lady reigning in crinoline. For the man, under the color of a warming-pan, retained Nature's outline. But it was *subaudi equum*! Scarce a pennyweight of honest horse-flesh to be seen. Our crinoline spares the noble parts of women and makes but the baser parts gigantic (why this preference?); but this poor animal from stem to stern was swamped in finery. His ears were hidden in great sheaths of white linen tipped with silver and blue. His body was swaddled in stiff, gorgeous cloths descending to the ground, except just in front, where they left him room to mince. His tail, though dear to memory, no doubt, was lost to sight, being tucked in heaven knows how. Only his eyes shone out like goggles through two holes pierced in the wall of haberdashery, and his little front hoofs peeped in and out like rats.

Yet did this compound, gorgeous and irrational, represent power; absolute power. It came straight from a tournament at the Duke's court, which, being on a progress, lay the previous night at a neighboring town—to execute the behests of royalty.

"What ho!" cried the upper half, and on Eli emerging, with his wife behind him, saluted them. "Peace be with you, good people. Rejoice! I am come for your dwarf."

Eli looked amazed and said nothing. But Catherine screamed over his shoulder, "You have mistaken your road, good man; here abides no dwarf."

"Nay, wife, he means our Giles, who is somewhat small of stature: why gainsay what gainsayed may not be?"

"Ay!" cried the pageant, "that is he, and discourseth like the big taber."

"His breast is sound for that matter," said Catherine sharply.

"And prompt with his fists though at long odds."

"Else how would the poor thing keep his head in such a world as this?"

"'Tis well said, dame. Art is as ready with thy weapon as he; Art is his mother, likely. So bring him forth, and that presently. See, they lead a stunted mule for him. The Duke hath need of him, sore need; we are

Happy is the man who possesses two anchor cables: Merit, and Women. Oh, that I, like Gerard, might possess a "chain of ladies" to haul myself up by! Then I should be a prose laureate, or a professor of the spasmodic, or something of the sort, in no time at all. Until then, let me sketch the portrait of Fra Colonna.

The true revivers of ancient learning and philosophy were two writers of fiction—Petrarch and Boccaccio. Their labours were not crowned with great, public, and immediate success; but they sowed the good seed, and it never perished, but quickened in the soil, awaiting sunshine. From their day, Italy was never without a native scholar or two, versed in Greek; and each learned Greek who landed there was received fraternally. The fourteenth century, ere its close, saw the birth of Poggio, Valla, and the elder Guarino; and early in the fifteenth, Florence under Cosmo de Medici was a nest of Platonists. These, headed by Gemistus Pletho, a born Greek, began about A.D. 1440 to write down Aristotle. For few minds are big enough to be just to great A without being unjust to capital B.

Theodore Gaza defended that great man with moderation; George of Trebizond with acerbity, and retorted on Plato. Then Cardinal Bessarion, another born Greek, resisted the said George, and his idol, in a tract "Adversus calumniatorem Platonis." Pugnacity, whether wise or not, is a form of vitality. Born without controversial bile in so zealous an epoch, Francesco Colonna, a young nobleman of Florence, lived for the arts. At twenty he turned Dominican friar. His object was quiet study. He retired from idle company, and faction fights, the humming and the stinging of the human hive, to St. Dominic and the Nine Muses.

An eager student of languages, pictures, statues, chronology, coins, and monumental inscriptions. These last loosened his faith in popular histories. He travelled many years in the East, and returned laden with spoils; master of several choice MSS., and versed in Greek and Latin, Hebrew and Syriac. He found his country had not stood still. Other lettered princes besides Cosmo had sprung up. Alfonso King of Naples, Nicolas d'Este, Lionel d'Este, etc. Above all, his old friend Thomas of Sarzana had been made Pope, and had lent a mighty impulse to letters; had accumulated 5000 MSS. in the library of the Vatican, and had set Poggio to translate Diodorus Siculus and Xenophon's Cyropaedia, Laurentius Valla to translate Herodotus and Thucydides, Theodore Gaza, Theophrastus; George of Trebizond, Eusebius, and certain treatises of Plato, etc. etc.

The monk found Plato and Aristotle under armistice, but Poggio and Valla at loggerheads over verbs and nouns, and on fire with odium philologicum. All this was heaven; and he settled down in his native land, his life a rosy dream. None so happy as the versatile, provided they have not their bread to make by it. And Fra Colonna was Versatility. He knew seven or eight languages, and a little mathematics; could write a bit, paint a bit, model a bit, sing a bit, strum a bit; and could relish superior excellence in all these branches. For this last trait he deserved to be as happy as he was. For, gauge the intellects of your acquaintances, and you will find but few whose minds are neither deaf, nor blind, nor dead to some great art or science—"And wisdom at one entrance quite shut out." And such of them as are conceited as well as stupid shall even parade instead of blushing for the holes in their intellects.

A zealot in art, the friar was a sceptic in religion. In every age there are a few men who hold the opinions of another age, past or future. Being a lump of simplicity, his scepticism was as naif as his enthusiasm. He affected to look on the religious ceremonies of his day as his models, the heathen philosophers regarded the worship of gods and departed heroes: mummeries good for the populace. But here his mind drew unconsciously a droll distinction. Whatever Christian ceremony his learning taught him was of purely pagan origin, that he respected, out of respect for antiquity; though had he, with his turn of mind, been a pagan and its contemporary, he would have scorned it from his philosophic heights.

Fra Colonna was charmed with his new artist, and having the run of half the palaces in Rome, sounded his praises so, that he was soon called upon to resign him. He told Gerard what great princes wanted him. "But I am so happy with you, father," objected Gerard. "Fiddlestick about being happy with me," said Fra Colonna; "you must not be happy; you must be a man of the world; the grand lesson I impress on the young is, be a man of the world. Now these Montesini can pay you three times as much as I can, and they shall too-by Jupiter." And the friar clapped a terrific price on Gerard's pen. It was acceded to without a murmur. Much higher prices were going for copying than authorship ever obtained for centuries under the printing press. Gerard had three hundred crowns for Aristotle's treatise on rhetoric.

The great are mighty sweet upon all their pets, while the fancy lasts; and in the rage for Greek MSS. the handsome writer soon became a pet, and nobles of both sexes caressed him like a lap dog. It would have turned a vain fellow's head; but the canny Dutchman saw the steel hand beneath the velvet glove, and did not presume. Nevertheless it was a proud day for him when he found himself seated with Fra Colonna at the table of his present employer, Cardinal Bessarion. They were about a mile from the top of that table; but never mind, there they were and Gerard had the advantage of seeing roast pheasants dished up with all their feathers as if they had just flown out of a coppice instead of off the spit: also chickens cooked in bottles, and tender as peaches. But the grand novelty was the napkins, surpassingly fine, and folded into cocked hats, and birds' wings, and fans, etc., instead of lying flat. This electrified Gerard; though my readers have seen the dazzling phenomenon without tumbling backwards chair and all.

After dinner the tables were split in pieces, and carried away, and lo, under each was another table spread with sweetmeats. The signoras and signorinas fell upon them and gormandized; but the signors eyed them with reasonable suspicion. "But, dear father," objected Gerard, "I see not the bifurcal daggers, with which men say his excellency armeth the left hand of a man." "Nay, 'tis the Cardinal Orsini which hath invented yon peevish instrument for his guests to fumble their meat withal. One, being in haste, did skewer his tongue to his palate with it, I hear; O tempora, O mores! The ancients, reclining godlike at their feasts, how had they spurned such pedantries."

As soon as the ladies had disported themselves among the sugar-plums, the tables were suddenly removed, and the guests sat in a row against the wall. Then came in, ducking and scraping, two ecclesiastics with lutes, and kneeled at the cardinal's feet and there sang the service of the day; then retired with a deep obeisance: In answer to which the cardinal fingered his skull cap as our late Iron Duke his hat: the company dispersed, and Gerard had dined with a cardinal and one that had thrice just missed being pope.

But greater honour was in store. One day the cardinal sent for him, and after praising the beauty of his work took him in his coach to the Vatican; and up a private stair to a luxurious little room, with a great oriel window. Here were inkstands, sloping frames for writing on, and all the instruments of art. The cardinal whispered a courtier, and presently the Pope's private secretary appeared with a glorious grimy old MS. of Plutarch's Lives. And soon Gerard was seated alone copying it, awe-struck, yet half delighted at the thought that his holiness would handle his work and read it.

The papal inkstands were all glorious externally; but within the ink was vile. But Gerard carried ever good ink, home-made, in a dirty little inkhorn: he prayed on his knees for a firm and skilful hand, and set to work. One side of his room was nearly occupied by a massive curtain divided in the centre; but its ample folds overlapped. After a while Gerard felt drawn to peep through that curtain. He resisted the impulse. It returned. It overpowered him. He left Plutarch; stole across the matted floor; took the folds of the curtain, and gently gathered them up with his fingers, and putting his nose through the chink ran it against a cold steel halbert. Two soldiers, armed cap-a-pie, were holding their glittering weapons crossed in a triangle. Gerard drew swiftly back; but in that instant he heard the soft murmur of voices, and saw a group of persons cringing before some hidden figure.

He never repeated his attempt to pry through the guarded curtain; but often eyed it. Every hour or so an ecclesiastic peeped in, eyed him, chilled him, and exit. All this was gloomy, and mechanical. But the next day a gentleman, richly armed, bounced in, and glared at him. "What is toward here?" said he. Gerard told him he was writing out Plutarch, with the help of the saints. The spark said he did not know the signor in question. Gerard explained the circumstances of time and space that had deprived the Signor Plutarch of the advantage of the spark's conversation.
