"Oh! one of those old dead Greeks they keep such a coil about." "Ay, signor, one of them, who, being dead, yet live." "I understand you not, young man," said the noble, with all the dignity of ignorance. "What did the old fellow write? Love stories?" and his eyes sparkled: "merry tales, like Boccaccio." "Nay, lives of heroes and sages." "Soldiers and popes?" "Soldiers and princes." "Wilt read me of them some day?" "And willingly, signor. But what would they say who employ me, were I to break off work?" "Oh, never heed that; know you not who I am? I am Jacques Bonaventura, nephew to his holiness the Pope, and captain of his guards. And I came here to look after my fellows. I trow they have turned them out of their room for you." Signor Bonaventura then hurried away. This lively companion, however, having acquired a habit of running into that little room, and finding Gerard good company, often looked in on him, and chattered ephemeralities while Gerard wrote the immortal lives.

One day he came a changed and moody man, and threw himself into chair, crying, "Ah, traitress! traitress!" Gerard inquired what was his ill? "Traitress! traitress!" was the reply. Whereupon Gerard wrote Plutarch. Then says Bonaventura, "I am melancholy; and for our Lady's sake read me a story out of Ser Plutarcho, to soothe my bile: in all that Greek is there nought about lovers betrayed?" Gerard read him the life of Alexander. He got excited, marched about the room, and embracing the reader, vowed to shun "soft delights," that bed of nettles, and follow glory.

Who so happy now as Gerard? His art was honoured, and fabulous prices paid for it; in a year or two he should return by sea to Holland, with good store of money, and set up with his beloved Margaret in Bruges, or Antwerp, or dear Augsburg, and end their days in peace, and love, and healthy, happy labour. His heart never strayed an instant from her. In his prosperity he did not forget poor Pietro. He took the Fra Colonna to see his picture. The friar inspected it severely and closely, fell on the artist's neck, and carried the picture to one of the Colonnas, who gave a noble price for it. Pietro descended to the first floor; and lived like a gentleman. But Gerard remained in his garret. To increase his expenses would have been to postpone his return to Margaret. Luxury had no charms for the single-hearted one, when opposed to love. Jacques Bonaventura made him acquainted with other gay young fellows. They loved him, and sought to entice him into vice, and other expenses. But he begged humbly to be excused. So he escaped that temptation. But a greater was behind.

Fra Colonna possessed the keys to the Pope's library, a privilege that allowed him to wander the city with Gerard whenever the friar's scholarly duties permitted. On these excursions, the artist's spirit soared; he viewed the Eternal City through rose-tinted spectacles, intoxicated by the grandeur of its churches, palaces, and crumbling ruins. Fra Colonna, however, granted the ruins their due reverence but poured cold water upon the rest of the city's splendor.

"This place Rome?" the friar would muse, gesturing to the modern thoroughfares. "It is merely the tomb of mighty Rome." He guided Gerard's gaze downward, pointing out how twenty or thirty feet of ancient triumphal arches lay buried beneath the cobblestones, and how the contemporary streets ran directly over the rooftops of ancient palaces and the severed tops of columns. Coupling the cramped boundaries of the modern city with the gigantic, sporadic vestiges of antiquity that poked through the soil, the friar offered a striking simile. "I tell thee, this village they call Rome is but as one of those swallow's nests ye shall see built on the eaves of a decayed abbey."

"Old Rome must indeed have been fair then," Gerard remarked, captivated.

"Judge for yourself, my son," the friar insisted. "You see the great sewer, a work of the Romans in their infancy, destined to outlast Vesuvius. You see fragments of the Temple of Peace. Imagine the Capitol with its twenty-five temples! Note this Monte Savello; what is it, if you please, but the ruins of the ancient Theatre of Marcellus? And as for Testaccio, one of the highest hills in modern Rome, it is but an ancient dust heap; the women of old Rome flung their broken pots and pans there, and lo—a mountain."

Gerard listened with respectful attention, but when the holy friar proceeded to imply, by analogy, that the moral superiority of the heathen Romans was proportionally grand, the artist resisted stoutly. "Has then the world lost anything by Christ's coming?" he asked, though he immediately blushed, sensing he was reproaching his benefactor.

"Saints forbid!" the friar exclaimed. "'Twere heresy to say so." Having made this direct concession, he gradually evaded the implication through subtle circumlocution until they reached the forbidden door via a spiral staircase.

In the midst of their journey, they arrived at a church where a knot of people gathered in the porch. Inside, a demon was being exorcised. Fra Colonna had a peculiar habit of uttering a curious, low moan when facts that Zeno or Epicurus would reject were presented to him. This moan conveyed to those who knew it not only strong dissent but also a profound pity for human credulity, ignorance, and error—especially when such blindness obscured the merits of Paganism.

The friar moaned and said, "Then come away."

"Nay, father, prithee! prithee! I ne'er saw a devil cast out."

The friar accompanied Gerard into the church, though he first gave a good shrug. Inside, they found the demoniac forced down on his knees before the altar, a scarf tied tightly around his neck by which the officiating priest held him like a dog on a chain. Few people were present, for rumor had spread that the last demon cast out in that church had fled no farther than into the next person in the congregation: *"as a cony ferreted out of one burrow runs to the next."*

When Gerard and the friar arrived, the priest seemed to deem the number of spectators sufficient and began his ritual. He faced the demoniac, breviary in hand, first attempting to identify the entity.

"Come out, Ashtaroth. Oho! it is not you then. Come out, Belial. Come out, Tatzi. Come out, Eza. No; he trembles not. Come out, Azymoth. Come out, Feriander. Come out, Foletho. Come out, Astyma. Come out, Nebul."

"Aha! what, have I found ye? 'Tis thou, thou reptile; at thine old tricks. Let us pray!"

"Oh, Lord, we pray thee to drive the foul fiend Nebul out of this thy creature: out of his hair, and his eyes, out of his nose, out of his mouth, out of his ears, out of his gums, out of his teeth, out of his shoulders, out of his arms, legs, loins, stomach, bowels, thighs, knees, calves, feet, ankles, finger-nails, toe-nails, and soul. Amen."

The priest rose from his knees and turned to the company with quiet geniality. "Gentles, we have here as obstinate a devil as you may see in a summer day." Facing the patient, he spoke with great rigor, alternating between addressing the man and the fiend. They answered in turn through the same mouth, now claiming hatred for the holy names the priest uttered, now complaining of internal misery.

It was the priest who first confused the victim and the culprit in the minds of the onlookers, cuffing the man soundly, kicking him, and spitting repeatedly in his face. He then took a candle, lit it, and held it down until it burned his fingers, dropping it quickly. Next, he produced the custodial and showed the patient the *Corpus Domini*. He burned another candle cautiously, then spoke civilly to the demoniac in his human guise, dismissed him, and received the compliments of the assembly.

"Good father," said Gerard, "how you have their names by heart. Our northern priests possess no such exquisite knowledge of the hellish squadrons."

"Ay, young man, here we know all their names, and eke their ways, the reptiles. This Nebul is a bitter hard one to hunt out."

The friar then recounted several of his experiences to the company in an affable manner, concluding with a feat from the previous day when he drove a great hulking fiend out of a woman by her mouth, leaving behind nails, pins, and a tuft of hair. The creature had cried out in anguish, *"Tis not thou that conquers me. See that stone on the window sill. Know that the angel Gabriel coming down to earth once lighted on that stone: 'tis that has done my business."*

The friar moaned. "And you believed him?"

"Certes! who but an infidel has discredited a revelation so precise."

"What, believe the father of lies? That is pushing credulity beyond the age."

"Oh, a liar does not always lie."

"Ay, doth he whenever he tells an improbable story to begin, and shows you a holy relic; arming you against the Satanic host. Fiends (if any) are not so simple. You should have answered him out of antiquity—*'Timeo Danaos et dona ferentes.'* Some blackguard chopped his wife's head off on that stone, young man; you take my word for it." And the friar hurried Gerard away.

"Alack, father, I fear you abashed the good priest."

"Ay, by Pollux," said the friar, chuckling. "I blistered him with a single touch of 'Socratic interrogation.' What modern can parry the weapons of antiquity."

One afternoon, after Gerard finished his day's work, a fine lackey demanded his attendance at the Palace Cesarini. He was ushered into a noble apartment where a girl sat working on a tapestry. She rose and left, promising to inform her mistress. Gerard waited a good hour in the vast room, beginning to fret. "These nobles think nothing of a poor fellow's time." Just as he decided to slip out, the door opened and a superb beauty entered, followed by two maids. It was the young princess of the house of Cesarini. She entered talking loudly and haughtily to her dependents, but at the sight of Gerard, her voice softened to a very feminine tone.

"Are you the writer, messer?"

"I am, Signora."

"'Tis well."

She seated herself; Gerard and the maids remained standing.

"What is your name, good youth?"

"Gerard, signora."

"Gerard? Body of Bacchus! Is that the name of a human creature?"

"It is a Dutch name, signora. I was born at Tergou, in Holland."

"A harsh name, girls, for so well-favoured a youth; what say you?"

The maids assented warmly.

"What did I send for him for?" the lady inquired with lofty languor. "Ah, I remember. Be seated, Ser Gerardo, and write me a letter to Ercole Orsini, my lover; at least he says so."

Gerard seated himself, took out paper and ink, and looked up for instructions. The princess, seated on a much higher chair, almost a throne, looked down at him with inquiring eyes.

"Well, Gerardo."

"I am ready, your excellence."

"Write, then."

"I but await the words."

"And who, think you, is to provide them?"

"Who but your grace, whose letter it is to be?"

"Gramercy! What, you writers, find you not the words? What avails your art without the words? I doubt you are an impostor, Gerardo."

"Nay, Signora, I am none. I might make shift to put your highness's speech into grammar, as well as writing. But I cannot interpret your silence. Therefore speak what is in your heart, and I will empaper it before your eyes."

"But there is nothing in my heart. And sometimes I think I have got no heart."

"What is in your mind, then?"

"But there is nothing in my mind; nor my head neither."

"Then why write at all?"

"Why, indeed? That is the first word of sense either you or I have spoken, Gerardo. Pestilence seize him! Why writeth he not first? Then I could say nay to this, and ay to that, withouten headache. Also, is it a lady's part to say the first word?"

"No, signora: the last."

"It is well spoken, Gerardo. Ha! ha! Shalt have a gold piece for thy wit. Give me my purse!"

She paid him for the article on a nail à la moyen âge. Money never yet chilled zeal. Gerard, after getting a gold piece so cheap, felt bound to pull her out of her difficulty if the wit of man might achieve it.

"Signorina," said he, "these things are only hard because folk attempt too much, are artificial and labour phrases. Do but figure to yourself the signor you love—"

"I love him not."

"Well, then, the signor you love not—seated at this table, and dictate to me just what you would say to him."

"Well, if he sat there, I should say, 'Go away.'"

Gerard, who was flourishing his pen by way of preparation, laid it down with a groan.

"And when he was gone," said Floretta, "your highness would say, 'Come back.'"

"Like enough, wench. Now silence, all, and let me think. He pestered me to write, and I promised; so mine honour is engaged. What lie shall I tell the Gerardo to tell the fool?" She turned her head away and fell into deep thought, her noble chin resting on her white hand, half clenched.

She was so lovely and statuesque, looking so inspired with celestial thoughts as she sat thus, impregnating herself with mendacity, that Gerard forgot all except art, and proceeded eagerly to transfer that exquisite profile to paper. He had very nearly finished when the fair statue turned brusquely round and looked at him.

"Nay, Signora," said he, a little peevishly; "for Heaven's sake change not your posture—'twas perfect. See, you are nearly finished."

All eyes were instantly on the work, and all tongues active.

"How like! and done in a minute: nay, methinks her highness's chin is not quite so."

"Oh, a touch will make that right."

"What a pity 'tis not coloured. I'm all for colours. Hang black and white! And her highness hath such a lovely skin. Take away her skin, and half her beauty is lost."

"Peace. Can you colour, Ser Gerardo?"

"Ay, signorina. I am a poor hand at oils; there shines my friend Pietro; but in this small way I can tint you to the life, if you have time to waste on such vanity."

"Call you this vanity? And for time, it hangs on me like lead. Send for your colours now—quick, this moment—for love of all the saints."

"Nay, signorina, I must prepare them. I could come at the same time."

"So be it. And you, Floretta, see that he be admitted at all hours. Alack! Leave my head! leave my head!"

"Forgive me, Signora; I thought to prepare it at home to receive the colours. But I will leave it. And now let us despatch the letter."

"What letter?"

"To the Signor Orsini."

"And shall I waste my time on such vanity as writing letters—and to that empty creature, to whom I am as indifferent as the moon? Nay, not indifferent, for I have just discovered my real sentiments. I hate him and despise him. Girls, I here forbid you once for all to mention that signor's name to me again; else I'll whip you till the blood comes. You know how I can lay on when I'm roused."

"We do. We do."

"Then provoke me not to it;" and her eye flashed daggers, before she turned to Gerard all instantaneous honey. "Addio, il Gerardo." And Gerard bowed himself out of this velvet tiger's den.

He came the next day and coloured her; the following day he was set to make

Princess Claelia commanded a full-length portrait of herself. Gerard advised her to entrust the commission to his friend, Pietro Vanucci.

She declined immediately. "'Twill be time to put a slight on the Gerardo," she said, "when his work discontents me." Gerard, aware that while he was an excellent draughtsman, his coloring was not so refined, begged her to pose as a Roman statue. He demonstrated how closely he could mimic marble on paper. She agreed at first, but hesitated when he explained she must don the tunic, toga, and sandals of antiquity.

"Why, I had as lief be presented in my smock," she replied with medieval frankness.

"Alack, signorina," Gerard protested, "you have surely never noted the ancient habit; so free, so ample, so simple, yet so noble. It is most becoming your highness, to whom Heaven hath granted Roman features, a shapely arm, and a hand of such grace that modern attire cannot suit it."

"What, can you flatter, like the rest, Gerardo?" she asked. "Well, give me time to think on't. Come on Saturday, and then I will say ay or nay."

The respite gained was spent in constructing the ancient garments and trying them on in her chamber to see if they compensated for being a thousand years out of date.

Gerard, hurrying to this interview, was suddenly arrested by a shop window. His keen eye discerned a copy of Lactantius lying open. "That is fairly writ, anyway," he thought.

He stared at it a moment longer.

It was not written. It was printed.

Gerard groaned. "I am sped; mine enemy is at the door. The press is in Rome."

He entered the shop, feigning nonchalance, and inquired how long the printing press had been in the city. The man claimed there was no such thing in Rome. "Oh, the Lactantius; that was printed on the top of the Apennines."

"What, did the printing-press fall down there out of the moon?"

"Nay, messer," the trader laughed, "it shot up there out of Germany. See the title-page!"

Gerard eagerly examined the book and read:

"Will ye buy, messer? See how fair and even be the letters. Few are left can write like that; and scarce a quarter of the price."

"I would fain have it," Gerard said sadly, "but my heart will not let me. Know that I am a caligrapher, and these disciples of Fust run after me round the world, taking the bread out of my mouth. But I wish them no ill. Heaven forbid!" He hurried from the shop.

"Dear Margaret," he whispered to himself, "we must lose no time; we must make our hay while the sun shines. One month more, and an avalanche of printer's type shall roll down from those Apennines and lay us waste."

He almost ran to the Princess Claelia.

He was ushered into an apartment new to him. It was not large, but luxurious; a fountain played in the center, and the floor was covered with panther skins, dressed with the hair, so that no footfall could be heard. The room was an antechamber to the princess's boudoir, for on one side there was no door, only an ample curtain of gorgeous tapestry.

Gerard was left alone until he became uneasy, doubting if he had been shown the wrong place. These doubts were soon dispelled.

A light step came swiftly behind the curtain; it parted, and there stood a figure the heathens might have worshipped. It was not quite Venus, nor quite Minerva, but between the two—nobler than Venus, more womanly than Jupiter's daughter. Toga, tunic, sandals; nothing was modern. As for beauty, that is of all times.

Gerard started up, and all the artist in him flushed with pleasure.

"Oh!" he cried innocently, gazing in rapture.

This added the last charm to his model: a light blush tinted her cheeks, her eyes brightened, and her mouth smiled with delicious complacency at this genuine tribute to her charms.

They looked at one another for some time. When she saw Gerard's eloquence was confined to ejaculating and gazing, she spoke. "Well, Gerardo, thou seest I have made myself an antique monster for thee."

"A monster? I doubt Fra Colonna would fall down and adore your highness, seeing you so habited."

"Nay, I care not to be adored by an old man. I would liefer be loved by a young one: of my own choosing."

Gerard took out his pencils, arranged his canvas, which he had covered with stout paper, and set to work. So absorbed was he that he had no mercy on his model. At last, after nearly an hour in one posture, "Gerardo," she said faintly, "I can stand so no more, even for thee."

"Sit down and rest awhile, Signora."

"I thank thee," she said, sinking into a chair, turning pale and sighing.

Gerard was alarmed and realized he had been inconsiderate. He took water from the fountain and was about to throw it in her face, but she put up a white hand deprecatingly: "Nay, hold it to my brow with thine hand: prithee, do not fling it at me!"

Gerard timidly and hesitantly applied his wet hand to her brow.

"Ah!" she sighed, "that is reviving. Again."

He applied it again. She thanked him and asked him to ring a little hand-bell on the table. He did so, and a maid came, who was sent to Floretta with orders to bring a large fan.

Floretta speedily arrived with the fan. She no sooner came near the princess than that lady's highbred nostrils suddenly expanded like a bloodhorse's. "Wretch!" she cried, rising with a sudden return to vigor, seizing Floretta with her left hand, twisting it in her hair, and with the right hand boxing her ears severely three times.

Floretta screamed and blubbered but obtained no mercy.

The antique toga left quite disengaged a bare arm, now seeming as powerful as it was beautiful; it rose and fell like the piston of a modern steam-engine, and heavy slaps resounded one after another on Floretta's shoulders. The last one drove her sobbing and screaming through the curtain, where she was heard crying bitterly for some time after.

"Saints of heaven!" cried Gerard, "what is amiss? what has she done?"

"She knows right well. 'Tis not the first time. The nasty toad! I'll learn her to come to me stinking of the musk-cat."

"Alas, Signora, 'twas a small fault, methinks."

"A small fault? Nay, 'twas a foul fault." She added with an amazing sudden descent to humility and sweetness, "Are you wroth with me for beating her, Gerardo?"

"Signora, it ill becomes me to school you; but methinks such as Heaven appoints to govern others should govern themselves."

"That is true, Gerardo. How wise you are, to be so young." She then called the other maid and gave her a little purse. "Take that to Floretta, and tell her 'the Gerardo' hath interceded for her; and so I must needs forgive her. There, Gerardo."

Gerard colored all over at the compliment; but not knowing how to turn a phrase equal to the occasion, he asked her if he should resume her picture.

"Not yet; beating that hussy hath somewhat breathed me. I'll sit awhile, and you shall talk to me. I know you can talk, an it pleases you, as rarely as you draw."

"That were easily done."

"Do it then, Gerardo."

Gerard was taken aback. "But, signora, I know not what to say. This is sudden."

"Say your real mind. Say you wish you were anywhere but here."

"Nay, signora, that would not be sooth. I wish one thing though."

"Ay, and what is that?" she said gently.

"I wish I could have drawn you as you were beating that poor lass. You were awful, yet lovely. Oh, what a subject for a Pythoness!"

"Alas! he thinks but of his art. And why keep such a coil about my beauty, Gerardo? You are far fairer than I am. You are more like Apollo than I to Venus. Also, you have lovely hair and lovely eyes—but you know not what to do with them."

"Ay, do I. To draw you, signora."

"Ah, yes; you can see my features with them; but you cannot see what any Roman gallant had seen long ago in your place. Yet sure you must have noted how welcome you are to me, Gerardo?"

"I can see your highness is always passing kind to me; a poor stranger like me."

"No, I am not, Gerardo. I have often been cold to you; rude sometimes; and you are so simple you see not the cause. Alas! I feared for my own heart. I feared to be your slave. I who have hitherto made slaves. Ah! Gerardo, I am unhappy. Ever since you came here I have lived upon your visits. The day you are to come I am bright. The other days I am listless, and wish them fled. You are not like the Roman gallants. You make me hate them. You are ten times braver to my eye; and you are wise and scholarly, and never flatter and lie. I scorn a man that lies. Gerardo, teach me thy magic; teach me to make thee as happy by my side as I am still by thine."

As she poured out these strange words, the princess's mellow voice sank almost to a whisper, trembling with half-suppressed passion, and her white hand stole timidly yet earnestly down Gerard's arm, resting like a soft bird upon his wrist, ready to fly away at a word.

Destitute of vanity and experience, wrapped up in his Margaret and his art, Gerard had not seen this revelation coming, though it had come by regular and visible gradations.

He blushed all over. His innocent admiration of the regal beauty that besieged him did not for a moment displace the absent Margaret's image. Yet it was regal beauty, wooing with a grace and tenderness he had never even figured in imagination. How to check her without wounding her?

He blushed and trembled.

The siren saw, and encouraged him.

"Poor Gerardo," she murmured, "fear not; none shall ever harm thee under my wing. Wilt not speak to me, Gerardo mio?"

"Signora!" muttered Gerard deprecatingly.

At this moment his eye, lowered in his confusion, fell on the shapely white arm and delicate hand that curled round his elbow like a tender vine, and it flashed across him how he had just seen that lovely limb employed on Floretta.

He trembled and blushed.

"Alas!" said the princess, "I scare him. Am I then so very terrible? Is it my Roman robe? I'll doff it, and habit me as when thou first camest to me. Mindest thou? 'Twas to write a letter to yon barren knight Ercole d'Orsini. Shall I tell thee? 'twas the sight of thee, and thy pretty ways, and thy wise words, made me hate him on the instant. I liked the fool well enough before; or wist I liked him. Tell me now how many times hast thou been here since then. Ah! thou knowest not; lovest me not, I doubt, as I love thee. Eighteen times, Gerardo. And each time dearer to me. The day thou comest not 'tis night, not day, to Claelia. Alas! I speak for both. Cruel boy, am I not worth a word? Hast every day a princess at thy feet? Nay, prithee, prithee, speak to me, Gerardo."

"Signora," faltered Gerard, "what can I say, that were not better left unsaid? Oh, evil day that ever I came here."

"Ah! say not so. 'Twas the brightest day ever shone on me or indeed on thee. I'll make thee confess so much ere long, ungrateful one."

"Your highness," began Gerard, in a low, pleading voice.

"Call me Claelia, Gerardo."

"Signora, I am too young and too little wise to know how I ought to speak to you, so as not to seem blind nor yet ungrateful. But this I know, I were both naught and ungrateful, and the worst foe e'er you had, did I take advantage of this mad fancy. Sure some ill spirit hath had leave to afflict you withal. For 'tis all unnatural that a princess adorned with every grace should abase her affections on a churl."

The princess withdrew her hand slowly from Gerard's wrist. Yet as it passed lightly over his arm it seemed to linger a moment at parting.

"You fear the daggers of my kinsmen," she said, half sadly, half contemptuously.

"No more than I fear the bodkins of your women," said Gerard haughtily. "But I fear God and the saints, and my own conscience."

"The truth, Gerardo, the truth! Hypocrisy sits awkwardly on thee. Princesses, while they are young, are not despised for love of God, but of some other woman. Tell me whom thou lovest; and if she is worthy thee I will forgive thee."

"No she in Italy, upon my soul."

"Ah! there is one somewhere then. Where? where?"

"In Holland,

Gerard woke not to hope, but to a fresh tide of despair. By the second day, fever had seized his mind, turning his thoughts into a chaotic storm. Beside his bed lay the tangle of his once-rich auburn hair, now discarded like a woman's, a stark symbol of his fallen state. The symptoms of his illness marched forward with terrifying speed. By the fifth day, the leeches, seeing no remedy, withdrew in defeat. That evening, as the sun dipped below the horizon, he succumbed to a profound slumber. Rumors spread that he would never wake, that this sleep was merely the prelude to death. Yet, an elderly woman, a seasoned nurse whose counsel held weight among the locals, offered a glimmer of defiance. "His youth may yet save him," she insisted, "if he can endure twelve hours of rest."

Moved by this hope, his landlady swept the room clear of all distractions and stood vigil beside his bed. She vowed a wax candle to the Virgin Mary for every hour he remained unconscious. Twelve hours passed. The woman knelt in gratitude, thanking the heavens. Twenty-four hours followed. Doubt began to creep into the hearts of the caretakers. At the thirty-hour mark, she summoned the physician.

"Thirty hours!" she asked anxiously. "Shall we rouse him?"

The doctor examined him closely, his brow furrowed. "His breathing is steady, his skin moist. I know learned men would wake him to inspect his tongue, to perform their rituals and learn nothing. But we, being women, possess the wisdom to let nature take its course. When has sleep ever harmed a shattered mind or a broken heart?"

Forty-eight hours of silence ensued. The news leaked out, drawing a crowd of curious onlookers, though they were kept at bay until only two figures were permitted entry: Fra Colonna and his towering companion, Fra Jerome. The friars took over from the women, sitting in solemn silence. Fra Colonna gazed at his young friend with tears welling in his eyes, while Fra Jerome, beads clicking softly in his hand, watched with the calm detachment of a man who had faced the sea's fury alongside Gerard.

Around the sixtieth hour of this unnatural stillness, the landlady nudged Fra Colonna. He looked down. Gerard's eyelids fluttered, and he opened his eyes with the gentleness of one merely dozing. He stared into the void, then drew himself up slightly in the bed. Reaching for his head, he felt the smooth, bare scalp where his hair had once been. He caught sight of Fra Colonna and a smile of relief touched his lips. But the smile died instantly. His face twisted into a mask of unspeakable agony, and a loud cry tore from his throat as he turned his face to the wall.

The landlady wept, remembering the hollow ache of waking alone after loss. Fra Jerome began to chant psalms from his breviary, a low, rhythmic drone against the silence. Gerard curled into the bedclothes, shrinking from the world. Fra Colonna approached, whispering with a trembling voice that all was not lost. He spoke of the divine Muses, immortal and faithful, urging Gerard to transfer his love to them. They would never betray him, unlike fragile creatures of flesh. Overcome by emotion, the simple priest hurried away.

Fra Jerome remained. "Young man," he said, his voice grave, "the Muses exist only in the minds of pagans and dreamers. It is the Church alone that offers peace to the heart on earth and salvation to the soul in the hereafter. Has the earth deceived you? Has passion torn your heart? Then the Church opens her arms. Consecrate your gifts to her! The Church is peace of mind."

He spoke these words solemnly at the threshold and vanished before Gerard could respond.

"The Church!" Gerard shrieked, rising with a fury that shook the room. He shook his fist at the empty air where the friar had stood. "Malediction on the Church! But for the Church, I should not lie broken here, while she lies cold, cold, cold in Holland. Oh, my Margaret! Oh, my darling! My darling! And I must flee from you for the few months you had left to live. Cruel! Cruel! The monsters allowed her to die. Death does not come without warning; the blind, selfish fools saw the signs or chose to ignore them, but I saw them. I, who loved her. Oh, had I been there, I would have saved her, I would have saved her! Idiot! Idiot! To leave her for a single moment."

He wept bitterly for a long time, his body wracked with grief. Then, suddenly, the sorrow burst into a tempest of rage. "The Church! For whose sake I was driven from her! My curse be upon the Church and the hypocrites who use its name to wound my broken heart! Accursed be the world! Ghysbrecht lives; Margaret dies. Thieves, murderers, harlots, live forever. Only angels die. Curse life! Curse death! And curse whoever forged them into such wretched shapes!"

The friar had not heard these mad, wicked words, only the raw yell of rage cast after him. It was fortunate he had not. Had he heard, he would have demanded Gerard be burned in the forum with the same casual indifference as roasting a kid.

The landlady, who had accompanied Fra Colonna downstairs, heard the raised voice and returned in alarm. She found Gerard dressing, his hands moving mechanically as tears streamed down his face.

"What good is lying here?" he muttered gloomily. "Can I find what I seek within these walls?"

"Saints preserve us! Is he distraught again? What do you seek?"

"Oblivion."

"Oblivion, my little heart? Oh, but you are too young to speak such words."

"Young or old, what else remains for me to live for?"

He donned his finest clothes. The good woman protested. "My pretty Gerard, know that it is Tuesday, not Sunday."

"Oh, Tuesday is it? I thought it had been Saturday."

"Nay, you have slept long. You never wear your brave clothes on working days. Consider."

"What I did when she lived, I did. Now I shall do whatever I once refused. The past is the past. There lies my hair, and with it, my way of life. I served one Master as well as I could. You see my reward. Now I will serve another, and give him a fair trial."

"Alas!" the woman sighed, her face draining of color. "What do these dark words mean? And what new master is this whose service you intend to try?"

With this terrible declaration on his lips, the wretched creature walked out. His cap and feather were set jauntily askew, a mocking contrast to his feeble limbs and a sinister face pale as ash, drawn down as if by the heavy hand of age.

A heavy shadow descended upon the mind of a noble soul. What had once been his guiding star, a pure and unrivalled love for Margaret, was now extinguished. He drifted aimlessly across a dark and hopeless sea. Yet, his torment was not merely despair; it was a searing exasperation at the futility of his own sacrifices. Every act of self-denial, every moment of fortitude, every peril endured, and every virtue displayed felt like a cruel joke, for they had served no purpose. The bitter truth gnawed at him: had he remained idle and selfish at home, he might have saved Margaret's life.

These twin poisons, rage and regret, churned within his young blood, driving him to madness. He threw himself violently into pleasure, seeking oblivion. In those days, pleasure was indistinguishable from vice. Wine, women, gambling—anything that could procure an hour of excitement or a moment of forgetting—he pursued with the desperate frenzy of a man rushing into enemy fire because he has grown weary of living.

The substantial savings he had gathered for Margaret now funded his debauchery. Soon, he became the leader of a circle of loose companions he had previously avoided. His heart rotted alongside his morals. He grew sullen toward his old landlady, rejecting her gentle advice and warnings, until he finally moved to a different part of the town to escape her remonstrances and the painful memories they evoked. As time passed, his hand grew unsteady; he could no longer write to satisfy his need for connection. His patience eroded as the habits of pleasure took root. He abandoned the art of writing and turned to painting portraits in color, though he neglected this craft whenever his idle companions called for him.

Thus, he dove into foul waters, searching for a worthless shell of oblivion. It was not necessary to detail the coarse scenes of vice in which this unfortunate young man now indulged. The essential truth remains: he had become a rake, a debauchee, and a drunkard, one of the most wicked and reckless youths in Rome. Those who hide the sins of the virtuous or soften the edges of wickedness rob the despairing sinner of any hope of redemption.

The man was not made for half-measures, nor was his vice partial. His former humble female friends whispered of his decline. His old landlady told Teresa that he was going to ruin and begged her to find his whereabouts. Teresa relayed the sad story to her husband, Lodovico, asking him to search for the young man's current abode. "If you remember his face, Lodovico mio?"

"Teresa, a man of my station never forgets a face, especially that of a benefactor. But you know I rarely venture abroad in daylight."

Teresa sighed. "And how long will this last, Lodovico?"

"Until some cavalier drives his sword through me. They will not allow a poor fellow like me to pursue any honest trade."

Pietro Vanucci, who bore prosperity far worse than adversity, had been ejected from their old lodging for keeping late hours. Meeting Gerard in the street, he greeted him warmly and soon secured quarters in the same house. He brought with him a lad named Andrea, who ground his colors and served as both pupil and model—a youth of striking beauty and sharp wit.

Pietro had not entirely forgotten the past and professed a warm friendship for Gerard. Gerard, however, in whom all warmth of feeling seemed dead, accepted the other's friendship with cold indifference. It was a strange alliance. Pietro was not only a libertine but also a half-misanthrope and an open infidel. Together, they formed a couple with little in common, a rare phenomenon indeed.

One day, having undermined his health, stripped himself of his beauty, and squandered most of his money, Vanucci organized a festive party to ascend the Tiber in a boat pulled by buffaloes. Lorenzo de' Medici had introduced these beasts to Florence three years prior, but they were novelties in Rome. Nothing satisfied the "beggar on horseback," Vanucci, except to be drawn upstream by the brutes.

Each libertine was required to bring a lady, who must be handsome, or face a fine. The one presenting the most beautiful companion would be crowned with laurel and hailed as a public benefactor, interpreting the question "Who is the good man?" in their own twisted way. They procured a splendid galley and twelve buffaloes. All the libertines and their female associates gradually assembled at the embarkation point, but Gerard was absent.

They waited for him, first patiently, then with growing impatience. Vanucci offered an excuse. "I heard him say he forgot to procure a fardingale. Comrades, the good lad is hunting for a beauty worthy to stand among these peerless ladies. Consider the difficulty, ladies, and be patient!"

Finally, Gerard appeared in the distance, holding a woman by the hand.

"She is long enough," one of the women critiqued from afar.

"Gemini! Look at her steps," said another. "Oh, it is wise to hurry into good company," Pietro muttered as an excuse.

But when the pair approached, satire choked in their throats. Gerard's companion was a peerless beauty; she outshone the entire boatload like the rising sun extinguishes the stars. She was tall but not towering, straight as a dart yet supple as a young panther. Her face was a perfect oval, her forehead pale, her cheeks a rich olive flushed with eloquent blood, and her glorious eyes framed by long, thick silken lashes seemingly designed to sweep away sensitive hearts. Her lips were saucy red, her teeth the whitest ivory.

The women were visibly depressed by this wretched sight; the men were in ecstasy. They received her with loud shouts and waving caps, and one enthusiast even knelt on the boat's gunwale, hailing her of divine origin. However, his mistress pulled his hair, and the goddess delivered a sharp kick; simultaneously, he lay supine. The peerless creature skipped over his body without deigning him a glance, taking her seat at the prow. Pietro Vanucci sat in a collapse, glaring at her, his mouth agape like a dying codfish.

The drover spoke to the buffaloes, tightened the ropes, and they moved upstream.

"What do you think of this new beef, mesdames?"

"We have never seen such vile, ill-favored monsters, with their nasty horns that make one afraid, and their foul nostrils cast skyward. Holes they are, not nostrils."

"Signorina, the cattle are a gift from Florence the Beautiful. Would you look a gift beef in the nose?"

"They are so dull," objected a lively lady. "I went up the Tiber twice as fast last time with only five mules and an ass."

"Nay, that can be remedied," cried a gallant. Jumping ashore, he drew his sword. Despite the drivers' protests, he went down among the dozen buffaloes, goading them.

They snorted and whipped their tails but made no faster progress. At this, the boatload laughed loudly and for a long time. Finally, he goaded a patriarch bull, who instantly turned on the sword. With a furious jerk of his prodigious neck, he sent the man flying over his head into the air. He described a bold parabola and fell sitting, unconsciously waving his glittering blade, into the yellow Tiber. The laughing ladies screamed and wrung their hands, except for Gerard's fair companion. She uttered something very much like an oath, seized the helm, and steered the boat out. The gallant surfaced sputtering, gripped the gunwale, and was drawn in, dripping wet.

He glared around him, confused. "I do not understand that," he said, somewhat peevishly, puzzled and thus appearing discontented. Finding, by some strange accident, that he was not slain—his doublet perforated rather than his body—they began to laugh even louder.

"What are you cackling at?" the spark remonstrated. "I wish to know how it is that one moment a gentleman is out there pricking African beef, and the next moment—"

Gerard's lady. "Disporting in his native stream."

"Tell him not, a soul of you," cried Vanucci. "Let him solve his own riddle."

"Confound you all. I might puzzle my brains until doomsday and never find it out. Also, where is my sword?"

Gerard's lady. "Ask the Tiber! Your best way, signor, will be to do it over again; and, in a word, keep pricking African beef until your mind receives light. So shall you comprehend the matter by degrees, as lawyers mount heaven and buffaloes the Tiber."

Here, a chevalier remarked that the last speaker transcended the sons of Adam in wit as much as she did the daughters of Eve in beauty.

At this, and indeed at all their compliments, Pietro Vanucci's conduct became peculiar. He had ceased staring and gaping bewildered; now he sat coiled like a snake, his mouth muffled, two bright eyes fixed on the lady, twinkling and scintillating most comically.

He did not appear to interest or amuse her in return. Her glorious eyes and lashes swept him calmly at times, but she scarcely distinguished him from the benches and things.

Soon, the unanimity of the party suffered a momentary check. Mortified by the attention the cavaliers paid to Gerard's companion, the ladies began to pick her to pieces in whispers and aloud.

The lovely girl then revealed that, while rich in beauty, she was poor in feminine tact. Instead of revenging herself through the men, she allowed herself to overhear and openly retaliate against her detractors.

"There is not one of you who wears Nature's colors," she said. "Look here," pointing rudely in someone's face. "This is the beauty sold in every shop. Here is cerussa, here is stibium, and here purpurissum. Oh, I know the articles, bless you; I use them every day—but not on my face, no thank you."

Here Vanucci's eyes twinkled nearly out of sight.

"Why, your lips are colored, and the very veins in your forehead: not a charm but would come off with a wet towel. And look at your great coarse black hair like a horse's tail, drugged and stained to look like tow. And then your bodies are as false as your heads and your cheeks, and your hearts I trow. Look at your padded bosoms, and your wooden-heeled chopines to raise your little stunted limbs and deceive the world. Skinny dwarfs you are, cushioned and stultified into great fat giants. Aha, mesdames, well is it said of you, grande-di legni: grosse-di straci: rosse-di bettito: bianche-di calcina."

This drew a rejoinder. "Away, vulgar toad, telling the men everything. Your coarse, ruddy cheeks are your own, and your little handful of African hair. But who is padded more? Why, you are shaped like a fire-shovel."

"You lie, malapert."

"Oh, the well-educated young person! Where did you pick her up, Ser Gerard?"

"Hold thy peace, Marcia," said Gerard, awakened from a gloomy reverie by the raised trebles. "Be not so insolent! The grave shall close over thy beauty as it hath over fairer than thee."

"They began," said Marcia petulantly.

"Then be thou the first to leave off."

"At thy request, my friend." She then whispered to Gerard, "It was only to make you laugh; you are distraught, you are sad. Judge whether I care for the quips of these little fools or the admiration of these big fools. Dear Signor Gerard, would I were what they take me for? You should not be so sad."

Gerard sighed deeply and shook his head. But touched by the earnest young tones, he caressed the jet-black locks, much as one strokes the head of an affectionate dog.

At this moment, a galley drifting slowly downstream got entangled for an instant in their ropes; for, the river turning suddenly, they had shot out into the stream, and this galley came between them and the bank. In it sat a lady of great beauty under a canopy, with gallants and dependents standing behind her.

Gerard looked up at the interruption. It was Princess Claelia.

He blushed and withdrew his hand from Marcia's head.

Marcia was filled with admiration. "Aha! ladies," she said, "here is a rival if ye will. Those cheeks were colored by Nature, like mine."

"Peace, child! Peace!" said Gerard. "Make not too free with the great."

"Why, she heard me not. Oh, Ser Gerard, what a lovely creature!"

Two of the females had been putting their heads together and casting glances at Marcia for some time. One of them now addressed her.

"Signorina, do you love almonds?"

The speaker held a lapful of them.

"Yes, I love them; when I can get them," said Marcia pettishly, eyeing the fruit with ill-concealed desire; "but yours is not the hand to give me any, I trow."

"You are much mistaken," said the other. "Here, catch!" And suddenly threw a double handful into Marcia's lap.

Marcia brought her knees together by an irresistible instinct.

"Aha! You are caught, my lad," cried she of the nuts. "'Tis a man; or a boy. A woman still parts her knees to catch the nuts the surer in her apron; but a man closes his for fear they should all fall between his hose. Confess, now, didst never wear a fardingale ere to-day?"

"Give me another handful, sweetheart, and I'll tell thee."

"There! I said he was too handsome for a woman."

"Ser Gerard, they have found me out," observed the Epicaene, calmly cracking an almond.

The libertines vowed it was impossible, and all glared at the goddess like a battery. But Vanucci struck in, reminding

The chamber was a cage of luxury, the floor strewn with the spotted and striped hides of beasts, a testament to wealth that felt less like comfort and more like a gilded trap. Within this room, a lady sat rigid, her arms stretched forward as if bracing against an invisible storm. Her face was a canvas of conflicting emotions, flushing pale and then crimson in rapid succession, her foot tapping a frantic, restless rhythm against the rug.

Suddenly, the heavy curtain parted, drawn back by a silent servant. The lady's brow flared with alarm.

"Altezza," the maid whispered, her voice trembling with awe-struck reverence, "the man is here."

With a sharp command, the lady signaled for entry. In a blur of motion, she snatched a small black mask from the table and pressed it over her face. Instantly, the flush drained from her cheeks, leaving her skin the color of marble. The stark contrast between the jet-black mask and her pallid features created a visage that was both strange and terrifying.

A man entered, bowing and scraping with the mechanical precision of a man made of the very crowds he served. He was unremarkable—short-haired, with a roundish head and plain, decent clothing. His features were neither handsome nor forbidding, save for one thing: a singularly restless eye that darted about the room.

He bowed profusely until he stood directly opposite the masked figure, waiting for her pleasure.

"They have told you for what you are wanted?" she asked, her voice cool despite the mask.

"Yes, Signora," he replied.

"Did those who spoke to you agree as to what you are to receive?"

"Yes, Signora. 'Tis the full price; and purchases the greater vendetta: unless of your benevolence you choose to content yourself with the lesser."

"I understand you not," she said, her tone sharpening.

"Ah; this is the Signora's first. The lesser vendetta, lady, is the death of the body only. We watch our man come out of a church; or take him in an innocent hour; and so deal with him. In the greater vendetta, we watch him, and catch him hot from some unrepented sin, and so slay his soul as well as his body. But this vendetta is not so run upon now as it was a few years ago."

"Man, silence his tongue, and let his treasonable heart beat no more. But his soul I have no feud with."

"So be it, signora. He who spoke to me knew not the man, nor his name, nor his abode. From whom shall I learn these?"

"From myself."

At this, the man, showing the first flicker of anxiety, entreated her to be cautious and particular.

"Fear me not," she said. "Listen. It is a young man, tall of stature, with auburn hair and dark blue eyes, and an honest face that would deceive a saint. He lives in the Via Claudia, at the corner house; the glover's. In that house there lodge but three males: he; and a painter short of stature and dark-visaged, and a young, slim boy. He that hath betrayed me is a stranger, fair, and taller than thou art."

The bravo listened with rapt attention. "It is enough," he said.

"Stay, Signora; haunteth he any secret place where I may deal with him?"

"My spy doth report me he hath of late frequented the banks of the Tiber after dusk; doubtless to meet his light o' love, who calls me her rival; even there slay him! and let my rival come and find him; the smooth, heartless, insolent traitor."

"Be calm, signora. He will betray no more ladies."

"I know not that. He weareth a sword, and can use it. He is young and resolute."

"Neither will avail him."

"Are ye so sure of your hand? What are your weapons?"

The bravo produced a steel gauntlet. "We strike with such force we need must guard our hand. This is our mallet." He then undid his doublet, revealing a coat of mail beneath, and finally laid his glittering stiletto on the table with a flourish.

The lady shuddered at first, but presently took it up in her white hand and tested its point against her finger.

"Beware, madam," warned the bravo.

"What? Is it poisoned?"

"Saints forbid! We steal no lives. We take them with steel point, not drugs. But 'tis newly ground, and I feared for the Signora's white skin."

"His skin is as white as mine," she said, a sudden, fleeting gleam of pity crossing her masked face. It vanished as quickly as it came. "But his heart is black as soot. Say, do I not well to remove a traitor that slanders me?"

"The signora will settle that with her confessor. I am but a tool in noble hands; like my stiletto."

The princess appeared not to hear the speaker. "Oh, how I could have loved him; to the death; as now I hate him. Fool! he will learn to trifle with princes; to spurn them and fawn on them, and prefer the scum of the town to them, and make them a by-word." She looked up, her eyes burning behind the mask. "Why loiter'st thou here? haste thee, revenge me."

"It is customary to pay half the price beforehand, Signora."

"Ah! I forgot; thy revenge is bought. Here is more than half," she said, pushing a heavy bag across the table to him. "When the blow is struck, come for the rest."

"You will soon see me again, signora."

And he retired, bowing and scraping once more.

The princess remained seated, burning with jealousy, mortified pride, and a deep dread of exposure—for until she knew Gerard, no public stain had fallen on her. She sat there, masked, with her arms straight out before her, the nails of her clenched hand digging into the wood of the table.

So sat the fabled Sphinx; so sits a tigress.

Yet, as the assassin faded from sight, a chill crept over her. Moody misgivings heaved within her chest, precursors of vain remorse. Gerard and Margaret were before their age. This was the true medieval world: proud, amorous, vindictive, generous, foolish, cunning, impulsive, unprincipled—and ignorant as dirt.

Power was the curse of such a creature. Forced to do her own crimes, the weakness of her nerves would have balanced the violence of her passions, and her bark would have been worse than her bite. But power gave a feeble, furious woman male instruments. And the effect was as terrible as the combination was unnatural.

In this instance, it had whetted an assassin's dagger for a poor, forlorn wretch just meditating suicide.

Two days after the turmoil that had shaken his spirit, Gerard wandered through the squalid arteries of Rome, seeking refuge from a sudden, violent thunderstorm. He sought shelter in a low hostelry, where he ordered wine and, as the rain lashed against the roof, drank himself into a stupor. With his head resting in his hands upon the table, he drifted into a heavy doze.

Soon, the room filled with the clamor of rough guests, their noise slowly pulling him back to consciousness. It was then that he became aware of two figures leaning close together, whispering in a hushed tone near his elbow. One was a pardoner, a man of the church's shadowy trade. The other, though dressed in clean but modest garments that suggested a decent tradesman, wore his hat slouched low over his brow, obscuring every feature save his beard. This was the mark of a man who shuns the gaze of honest folk and the reach of the law. They were negotiating a bargain in the black market of sin, sealing a celestial contract for a crime that had not yet been committed.

The man with the slouched hat grumbled about the rising cost of salvation. "If prices climb any higher," he complained, "we poor fellows shall be shut out of heaven altogether."

The pardoner denied the accusation flatly. "Indulgences have never been cheaper for good husbandmen."

"Who were they?" the buyer asked.

"Why, those who sin by the market, like reasonable creatures," the pardoner replied. "But if you are so perverse as to pick out a crime the Pope has set his face against, blame yourself, not me!"

To demonstrate that such sins were within the reach of all but the very dregs of society, the pardoner read aloud from a parchment scroll. It was a curious list, one too dark for print, yet familiar to any great library. Suffice it to say that the price for the murder of a layman was far less than many offenses my readers might consider trivial. The transaction proceeded with a little trifling concession on each side; the money changed hands, and the aspirant to heaven's favor was absolved in advance for the removal of one layman. The cost for eliminating a clerk, however, bore no such proportion. Not once did the word *assassination* leave either man's lips.

All this buzzed in Gerard's ears, yet he never lifted his head from the table, listening only with a dull, drunken stupor. Only when the parties rose and separated did he half-lift his head, eyeing the retreating figure of the purchaser with a scowl.

"If Margaret were alive," he muttered, his voice thick with grief and rage, "I would take thee by the throat and throttle thee, thou cowardly stabber. But she is dead; dead; dead. Let the whole world perish; it matters nothing to me. So let me die among the first."

When he finally returned home, a man in a slouched hat paced briskly to and fro on the opposite side of the street.

"Why, there is that cur again," Gerard thought.

But in his shattered state of mind, the sight made no impression upon him whatsoever.

Two nights following this event, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea sat in the dim chamber, waiting for Gerard's supper. The air grew heavy with anticipation, and Pietro's patience frayed into peevishness. The clock had struck nine, yet Gerard remained absent. In a final, desperate gamble, Pietro dispatched Andrea to the master's room, hoping perhaps the scholar had slipped in unnoticed. Andrea shrugged, accepting the errand, and departed.

He returned empty-handed regarding Gerard, but clutching a folded scrap of paper. Though Andrea lacked the scholarly training to decipher the script—a skill reserved for the learned of his age—he possessed a sharp eye and had long memorized the distinctive hand of Pietro Vanucci.

"That is for you, I trow," Andrea declared, swelling with a pride in his own deduction.

Pietro snatched the note, his eyes darting over the lines while he read aloud to Andrea, his tone laced with bitter satire. "'Dear Pietro, dear Andrea, life is too great a burden.' So it is, my lad, yet that offers no excuse for wandering abroad when supper is served. Food is no burden."

"'Wear my habits!' Said the poplar to the juniper bush."

"'And thou, Andrea, mine amethyst ring; and me in both your hearts a month or two.' Why, Andrea?"

"'For my body, ere this ye read, it will lie in the Tiber. Trouble not to look for it. 'Tis not worth the pains. Oh unhappy day that it was born! Oh happy night that rids me of it! Adieu! Adieu! The broken-hearted Gerard.'"

"Here is a sorry jest of a peevish rogue," Pietro muttered, though his pallid cheeks and trembling teeth betrayed the terror gripping his heart.

Andrea, however, could not contain his grief. He filled the house with wailing cries. "O, miserable day! O, calamity of calamities! Gerard, my friend, my sweet patron! Help! Help! He is killing himself! Oh, good people, help me save him!" His screams tore through the household, and he burst into the street, bareheaded, imploring every Christian soul to aid him in rescuing his friend.

Soon, a crowd gathered, drawn by the clamor. Yet, poor Andrea found their sluggishness immovable. "Go down to the river?" they asked, shaking their heads. "No. It is not our business." "What part of the river?" "A wild goose chase." It was ill fortune to venture near the water after sunset; too many ghosts haunted those banks throughout the night.

However, a lackey who had stood idly opposite the house stepped forward, offering to accompany Andrea. This small gesture rallied three or four of the younger men, and the little band set off toward the Tiber.

As they approached the riverbank, the lackey questioned Andrea. Sobbing, the boy recounted the letter and Gerard's increasingly moody demeanor. Unbeknownst to them, this lackey was a spy of Princess Claelia. Their Italian tongues flew rapidly until they neared the water's edge.

But the moment the cool, damp air of the river struck them, mingling with the scent of the stream on that calm spring night, silence fell upon the group like a shroud. The moon shone clear and unclouded above, casting a stark light on the scene. Their footsteps echoed loudly and ominously against the stillness; their tongues were hushed.

Hurrying around a corner, they encountered a man. He halted, irresolute, at the sight of their approaching forms. He was bareheaded, his dripping hair glistening under the moonlight, and with the next step, they saw his garments were soaked through with water.

"Here he is!" cried one of the youths, who did not recognize Gerard's face or figure.

The stranger turned instantly and fled. They pursued him with all their might, Andrea leading the charge, the princess's lackey close behind. Andrea gained on him briefly, but the fugitive twisted sharply into a narrow alley. Andrea shot past, unable to check his momentum, and the pursuers soon found themselves lost in a labyrinthine maze. It was futile to chase a quick-footed fugitive who knew every inch of the streets; they could follow him only by the sound of his retreating footsteps.

Returning to their companions, they found them standing frozen at the spot where the man had paused, utterly confounded. For Pietro had insisted the fugitive bore neither the features nor the stature of Gerard.

"Are ye verily sure?" the others demanded. "He had been in the river. Why, in the saints' names, did he flee at our approach?"

Then Vanucci spoke, "Friends, methinks this has nought to do with him we seek. What shall we do, Andrea?"

Here the lackey interjected. "Let us track him to the water's side to make sure. See, he hath come dripping all the way."

This counsel was approved, and with little difficulty, they retraced the man's path. Yet, soon they encountered a new enigma.

Scarcely fifty yards had they gone when the trail of water turned away from the river, leading instead to the gate of a large, gloomy building—a monastery. They stood irresolute before the dark pile, gazing at its imposing walls, sensing that it hid some horrible mystery.

Suddenly, Andrea let out a shout. "Here be the drops again!" he cried. "And this road leadeth to the river."

They resumed the chase. Soon it became clear that the moisture was guiding them home. The track grew wetter and wetter, leading them directly to the Tiber's edge. There, on the bank, a bucketful of water appeared to have been discharged from the stream.

At first, they shouted in triumph, believing they had made a discovery. But reflection quickly sobered them. It amounted to nothing. Certainly, a man had been in the water and emerged safely, but that man was not Gerard. One suggested they know a fisherman nearby who possessed nets and drags. They located the fisherman and paid him liberally to sink nets in the river below the spot and drag them above and below, promising gold if he found the body.

Then they ran vainly up and down the river, which flowed so calm and voiceless, holding a thousand strange secrets. Suddenly, Andrea, with a cry of hope, ran back toward the house.

He returned in less than half an hour.

"No," he groaned, wringing his hands in despair.

"What is the hour?" asked the lackey.

"Four hours past midnight."

"My pretty lad," the lackey said solemnly, "say a mass for thy friend's soul: for he is not among living men."

Morning broke, gray and cold. Worn out by fatigue and heart-sick with uncertainty, Andrea and Pietro returned home. The days rolled on, mute as the Tiber itself, offering no answer to Gerard's fate.

It would have been a marvel indeed if men, armed with such scanty clues, could have deciphered the silent script written upon the riverbank. For upon that very spot, an event had just transpired, one so singular in the annals of humanity that it may well stand alone until the final hour of time. Yet, this tale shall be recounted in but a few words, woven together from the voice of the narrator and the memory of a witness.

Gerard, having penned his brief farewell to Pietro and Andrea, descended to the river's edge as the shadows lengthened into night. His preparations were marked by a grim, unyielding resolve, a trait often found in those determined to extinguish their own light. He stuffed his pockets with every piece of silver and copper he owned, ensuring his weight would anchor him deep beneath the current, and hastened to a secluded bend of the stream he knew was rarely trodden.

Some, particularly women, cast anxious glances around to ensure a guardian stands nearby. But this desperate soul scanned the darkness solely to confirm the absence of any living breath. To his chagrin, however, he spotted a solitary figure leaning against the shadowed corner of an alley. Pretending to wander aimlessly, Gerard moved away, only to return moments later.

Sure enough, the same figure emerged from a side street, lingering with predatory patience. "Does he watch me? Does he know my purpose?" Gerard wondered, though he dismissed the thought as impossible. He strode briskly down the lane, circled through two streets, and returned. The man had vanished, yet when Gerard cast a wary glance around, there he stood mere yards behind, ostensibly adjusting his shoe.

Gerard realized he was being hunted. In the pale moonlight, he discerned a steel gauntlet gripped in the sentinel's hand. Then, the truth crashed upon him: he faced an assassin. Strangely, it never crossed his mind that his own life was the target; he remained unaware that enemies lurked in the world.

Turning to confront the bravo, Gerard approached with frantic desperation. "My good friend," he cried eagerly, "sell me thine arm! A single stroke! Behold, here is all I possess!" He thrust his meager coins into the assassin's grasp. "Oh, pray thee! Pray thee! Perform one act of mercy and liberate me from this hateful existence!" As he spoke, he undid his doublet, exposing his trembling bosom.

The man stared, unmoved.

"Why do you hesitate?" Gerard shrieked, his voice breaking. "Do you lack compassion? Is it so great a burden to raise your arm and deliver the blow? Is it because I am poor and cannot offer gold? Wretched fool, you can only strike a man from behind, refusing to meet his gaze. Very well, then! Turn your head and keep your silence!"

With a snarl of utter contempt, the assassin fled, leaving Gerard to throw himself into the dark water.

"Margaret!"

At the heavy splash of his body hitting the stream, the bravo seemed to rouse from a trance. He sprinted to the bank, issuing a strange cry before plunging in after the man who sought death. What followed shall be told by the assassin himself.

In the silent corridors of history, a woman bears her own burdens, often as heavy as any man's, yet the chronicler of the male experience rarely dares to map the labyrinth of female sorrow. We sketch the outline; it is the intuition of the reader, particularly the woman, that must breathe life into these cold lines, filling the void where description fails. Thus, I shall merely signal the path Margaret Brandt trod until that fateful day when she crossed the threshold of Eli's home as an adversary, discovered her beloved's letter, and emerged transformed into a friend.

Now, the trial most profound for a woman approached, while Gerard remained distant, lost to the winds of fate.

Margaret made scant use of Eli's sudden kindness. For this reserve, she possessed a plausible excuse at all times, though the truth lay deeper: within that house dwelt two men whose very presence triggered an instinctive shudder of dread in her soul. In their eyes, she had read wickedness and hatred; in their gestures, secret pacts of intelligence. She preferred the sanctuary of her own hearth. Catherine, her constant companion, visited daily, her heart swelling with anticipation for the coming birth.

Catherine belonged to that order of women whose sole purpose seems to be multiplication and the rearing of the multiplied. Once she ceased pelting every room with infants, she hovered over the remaining "fair scourges" of her brood, clucking with a fervor that suggested she multiplied by proxy. It was in this spirit she beseeched Eli to remain in Rotterdam while she returned to Tergou.

"The poor lass hath no soul about her who understands aught of anything," Catherine argued, her voice thick with maternal concern. "What good are a pair of soldiers? Such cattle ought to be cast out at such a time."

Needless to say, this was a great comfort to Margaret.

Poor soul, she was consumed by anxiety as the hour neared. She feared death; she feared Gerard's absence. Yet, the scales of fate balanced themselves. The poverty that had once nearly crushed her, and her father's helpless state, which had cost her such a desperate struggle, now stood as her shield. Adversity's iron hand had forged her against the lethargy that often overpowers the rich of her sex, compelling her to stand forever upon her feet, working. She maintained this vigilance until the end, guided by Catherine's counsel.

So it came to pass that on a fine evening, just as the sun dipped below the horizon, she lay weak as water, yet safe. A small face rested beside her, and the vast, open sky of maternity stretched before her.

"Why dost weep, sweetheart? All of a sudden?"

"He is not here to witness it."

"Ah, well, lass. He shall be here ere he is weaned. Meanwhile, God has been as good to thee as to any woman born. Consider, it might have been a girl; did not my very own Kate threaten me with one? And here we have secured the bonniest boy in Holland, a rare heavy one. The saints be praised for it."

"Ay, mother, I am but a sorry, ungrateful wretch to weep. If only Gerard were here to behold it. It is strange; I bore him well enough to be away from me in my sorrow, but oh, it seems so hard that he should not share my joy. Prithee, prithee, come to me, Gerard! Dear, dear Gerard!" She stretched out her feeble arms toward the empty air.

Catherine bustled about, deliberately avoiding Margaret's gaze, for she could not restrain her own tears at hearing her own absent child thus earnestly invoked.

Soon, turning around, she found Margaret regarding her with a singular, piercing expression. "Did you hear nothing?"

"No, my lamb. What?"

"I cried out for Gerard, but now..."

"Ay, ay, surely I heard that."

"Well, he answered me."

"Tush, girl: do not say such things."

"Mother, as certain as I lie here with his boy by my side, his voice returned to me. 'Margaret!' So it was. Yet, I thought it was not his happy voice. Perhaps that was the distance. All voices grow sad and faint when carried afar. Why art thou not happy, sweetheart? And I, so happy this night? Mother, I seem never to have felt a pain or known a care." Her sweet eyes turned, glowing with a strange intensity, fixed upon the little face in silence.

That very night, Gerard threw himself into the Tiber. And in that very hour, she heard him speak her name; he cried aloud in the jaws of death and the grip of despair.

"Margaret!"

Let those who can account for it explain. I cannot.

In the quiet seclusion of a Dominican guest chamber, a solitary figure lay prostrate, his strength ebbing away after violent convulsions of sickness that had finally subsided. He was as frail and broken as Margaret had been in Holland that same night. A roaring fire crackled on the hearth, casting dancing shadows against the walls where the patient's garments hung to dry. Beside the bed sat a massive friar, his presence commanding yet serene, reading aloud from his breviary. The stranger watched him with half-lidded eyes, listening intently at times, then closing his face to the moans of pain. The monk knelt, forehead touching the stone floor in fervent prayer, before rising to bid him adieu. "Day breaks," the friar announced softly. "I must prepare for matins."

"Good Father Jerome," the stranger gasped, "before you depart, tell me—how did I come to be here?"

"By the hand of Heaven," the friar replied, his voice steady. "You cast aside God's gift; He has restored it to you. Reflect on this! You have tasted the gall of the world; now taste the peace of the Church. *Pax vobiscum*."

With those words, he departed. Gerard lay back, his mind reeling in a haze of wonder and exhaustion, until sleep claimed him.

When consciousness returned, a new guardian sat by his side. This was a layman, his eyes small and darting with restless anxiety, a stark contrast to the calm majesty of Friar Jerome.

"How do you feel, messer?" the man asked earnestly.

"Extremely weak," Gerard murmured. "Have I seen you before?"

"None the worse for my gauntlet?" the man inquired, his tone laced with concern. "I nearly struck you down, or both of us would have sunk to the bottom of the Tiber."

Gerard stared, confusion giving way to recognition. "What? Was it you who saved me? How?"

"At your service, signor," the man said. "I was on an errand by the riverbanks when you approached me, begging for a dagger stroke. Yet, even as you spoke, I recognized you—the nobleman who saved my wife and child upon the sea."

"It is Teresa's husband," Gerard whispered, horror dawning. "And an assassin?"

"Indeed. After you threw yourself into the Tiber, bidding me stay back, I hesitated. Men are my enemies; I could let them all drown or hang on a single rope. But when you sank, crying out 'Margaret!' my heart screamed 'Teresa!' How could I return home and face her if I let you die, especially since your death was the very act that saved hers? So I entered the water. Lucky for us both, I swim like a duck. You, intent on destruction, tried to seize me to end us together. But I swam around you and, with my apologies, struck the back of your neck with my steel-gloved fist. You lost consciousness. Against the strong current, I dragged your body to shore, insensible and filled with water. I carried you home, thinking Teresa would nurse you and bless me. But a holy friar, the largest I have ever seen, intercepted us. He examined you closely. 'I know this face,' he said. 'It is Gerard, a fair youth from Holland.' I confirmed it. Then he declared, 'He has friends among our brethren. Leave him with us! Charity is our office.' He noted the convent possessed better means to tend you than I did. True enough. So I negotiated to visit daily, and here you are."

As he spoke, the man cast a look upon Gerard that was strangely affectionate, tinged with a deep, twisted interest.

Gerard recoiled internally, feeling as though a serpent coiled within the room. He closed his eyes.

"Ah, you wish to sleep," the man said eagerly. "I shall withdraw." He retreated on tiptoe, promising his daily return.

Gerard remained with his eyes shut, awake in a state of profound contemplation. Saved from death by an assassin. Was this not the finger of Heaven? Of that Heaven he had once insulted, cursed, and defied. A shudder ran through him at the memory of his blasphemies. He attempted to pray. He found he could utter the words, but his spirit refused to join them.

"I am doomed eternally," he cried out in despair. "Doomed, doomed."

Suddenly, the organ of the convent church erupted in rich, solemn harmony. The voices of the choir rose to meet it, chanting a full service. Among them floated a voice that seemed to tower above the rest—a sweet boy's voice, pure and angelic. Gerard closed his eyes and listened. In those harmonious tones, the days of his own boyhood flooded back, unburdened by earthly dross or the foul, fierce passions that had once rent his soul. Peace. Sweet, balmy peace.

"Yes," he sighed, tears welling in his eyes. "The Church is peace of mind. Until I left her bosom, I knew neither sorrow nor sin."

The weary creature wept openly. Just as his grief peaked, the sweet, reverend face of one he had not expected to see again beamed upon him. It was Father Anselm. The good father had arrived at the convent only the night before. Gerard recognized him instantly and cried out, "Oh, Father Anselm! You healed my wounded body in Juliers; now cure my hurt soul in Rome!"

"Alas," Anselm replied gently, "you cannot."

He sat by the bedside, placing a soothing hand upon Gerard's head to calm his agitation. Having learned the tale of Gerard's arrival, Anselm spoke to him with kindness and solemnity, making him feel the weight of his crime and urging him toward repentance and gratitude for the Divine Power that had thwarted his will to save his soul.

"Come, my son," Anselm said. "First, purge your bosom of its burden."

"Oh, father," Gerard confessed, "in Juliers I could; but now, an impious monster that I am, I dare not confess to you."

"Why not, my son? Do you think I have not sinned against Heaven in my time, and deeply? Oh, how deeply! Come, poor laden soul, pour forth your grief, pour forth your faults, hold back nothing! Do not lie oppressed and crushed by hidden sins."

Soon, Gerard was at Father Anselm's knees, confessing every sin with sighs and groans of penitence.

"Your sins are great," Anselm acknowledged. "Your temptation was also great, terribly great. I must consult our good prior."

Kissing Gerard's brow, the good father departed to determine the appropriate penance. And lo! Gerard could pray now. He prayed with all the fervor of his heart.

The phase through which this remarkable mind now passed may be summed in a single word: Penitence. He turned with terror and aversion from the world, begging passionately to remain in the convent. To him, the cloister was like a wounded bird returning to its gentle nest. He spent his novitiate in prayer, mortification, pious reading, and meditation.

Meanwhile, the Princess Claelia's spy returned home to report that Gerard was certainly dead, though the manner of his death remained unknown. She seemed literally stunned. When she finally found breath to speak, it was to lament her lot, cursed with such ready instruments of evil. "So soon," she sighed. "See how swiftly these monsters commit ill deeds. They come to us in our hot blood, first tempting us with their venal daggers, then enacting the mortal deeds we never would have conceived but for them."

Within hours, her pity for Gerard and her hatred for his murderer rose to a fever pitch, burning with the same intensity as her own foolish rage. "Poor soul! I cannot call thee back to life. But he shall never live that traitorously slew thee."

She immediately placed armed men in ambush, keeping them on guard all day, ready to fall upon Lodovico in a certain antechamber the moment he came for his money, hacking him to pieces.

"Strike at his head," she commanded. "For he wears a privy coat of mail; and if he goes hence alive, your own heads shall answer for it."

Thus she sat weeping over her victim, pulling the strings of machines designed to shed the blood of a second man in retribution for having served as her instrument to kill the first.

Gerard, the novice, had imposed upon himself a peculiar penance: to receive Lodovico with kindness, though he viewed the man internally as a slimy serpent. Yet, in the economy of self-denial, this burden proved too light to sustain itself. Like many rational acts of sacrifice, it quickly ceased to feel like a penance at all. Initially, the assassin's gaze upon the single life he had spared was a mixture of ludicrous pride and pathetic complacency. But once a good door is opened in a hardened heart, a stream of virtue flows through the aperture. Finding the act of saving life so sweet, Lodovico gradually grew averse to taking it; from that aversion came remorse, and from remorse, something resembling true penitence.

Teresa aided this transformation, threatening for the first time to depart unless he consented to live honestly. The good fathers of the convent supported her resolve, and Lodovico and Teresa were dispatched by sea to Leghorn, where Teresa possessed friends. There, the former assassin settled down, trading his blade for the heavy burden of a porter's trade.

At first, he found the labor miserably dull, complaining often of the monotony. Yet, perhaps this drudgery was far superior to the brief, violent excitement of being hewn in pieces by the Princess Claelia's myrmidons. His exile had spared him that fate. The princess, thwarted in her revenge, retreated into a profound melancholy. She dismissed her old confessor and summoned a new one renowned for his piety, entrusting to him what she termed her "griefs." This new confessor was none other than Fra Jerome. She could scarcely have fallen into better hands.

Fra Jerome listened to her grimly, then seized her by the shoulders and shook the delusions from her mind with the roughness one might reserve for a kitchen maid. For, on the path of duty, this hard monk feared the anger of princes no more than he feared the sea. In a few words, delivered with thunder and lightning, he revealed her as the criminal of criminals.

"You are the devil," he declared, "who with your money tempted one man to slay his fellow, and then, blinded by self-love, instead of blaming and punishing yourself, thirst for the blood of others—men less guilty than you."

At first, she resisted, claiming she was unused to being reprimanded by her confessors. But Fra Jerome overpowered her.
