He threatened her with the Church's curse, both in this life and the next; he tore the scales from her eyes and thundered until she sank down, groveling in remorse and terror at the feet of the gigantic Boanerges.

"Oh, holy father, have pity on a poor, weak woman," she cried, "and help me save my guilty soul. I was benighted for lack of ghostly counsel like yours, good father. I wake as from a dream."

"Doff thy jewels," Fra Jerome commanded sternly.

"I will. I will."

"Cast off thy silk and velvet; and clad in humbler garb than thy meanest servant, hasten instantly to Loretto."

"I will," the princess whispered faintly.

"No shoes; but a bare sandal."

"No, father."

"Wash the feet of pilgrims, both those going and those coming. To such as are holy friars, tell thy sin, and abide their admonition."

"Oh, holy father, let me wear my mask."

"Humph!"

"Oh, mercy! Think again! My features are known throughout Italy."

"Ay. Beauty is a curse to most of you. Well, thou mayst mask thine eyes; no more."

With this concession, she seized his hand, intending to kiss it, but he snatched it away rudely.

"What would you do? That hand handled the Eucharist but an hour ago; is it fit for one such as you to touch it?"

"Ah, no. But oh, do not go without giving your penitent daughter your blessing."

"There is time enough to ask it when you return from Loretto."

Thus, that marvelous occurrence by the banks of the Tiber left its mark upon all who acted in it, just as prodigies are said to do. The assassin, softened by the act of saving the life he was paid to take, turned from the stiletto to the knot of porters. The princess went barefoot to Loretto, weeping her crime and washing the feet of the base-born.

As for Gerard, carried from the Tiber into that convent as a suicide, he now passed for a young saint within its walls. Loving yet experienced eyes watched him. Upon a probation shorter than usual, he was admitted to priest's orders. Soon thereafter, he took the monastic vows and became a friar of St. Dominic. Dying to the world, the monk parted with the very name by which he had lived, thereby breaking the last link of association with earthly feelings. Here Gerard ended, and Brother Clement began.

"As is the fleeting race of leaves, so too is that of men." In this very year, within the humble confines of a tailor's shop in Rotterdam, a great soul budded unnoticed, while a mighty figure fell to the earth with resounding fanfare.

At Bruges, Philip, Duke of Burgundy and Earl of Holland, lay dying. In the natural order, the poor fall ill and recover as fate decrees; but for the wealthy, in an age where one physician could slay three, disaster often followed. The Duke's ailment, unnamed in those days but known today as diphtheria, was a wasting sickness that claimed the aged. Thus, Dr. Remedy turned to the ancient art of bloodletting.

The Duke grew ice-cold—a marvel indeed.

Undeterred, the doctor summoned the arcane secrets of science. "Ho! This is grave," he cried. "Flay an ape immediately and affix it to the Duke's breast!"

State officers scrambled in every direction, their faces pale, hunting for an ape to counteract the bloodthirsty folly of humanity. But perdition awaited them; the Duke was without apes. Buffaloes, lizards, Turks, and leopards lay ready, yet none were the requisite beast.

"Why, there used to be an ape about," one officer stammered. "I saw him standing just there."

Indeed, there had been one; but a mastiff had torn the lively creature apart for stealing its supper, fulfilling the grim human adage: "Be of your time."

In this desperate hour, the seneschal cast his despairing gaze around the room, and hope suddenly ignited in his eyes.

"Here is this," he whispered urgently. "Surely this will suffice: it is altogether apelike, doublet and hose aside."

"Nay!" the chancellor snapped, his voice peevish. "The Princess Marie would have us hanged. She dotes upon this creature."

It was then that our friend Giles appeared, strutting with unconscious pride in cloth of gold.

Driven by impatience, Dr. Remedy commanded, "Then flay a dog."

"A dog is the next best thing to an ape," the doctor insisted, "provided it is a dog of a single color."

So they skinned a liver-colored dog and pinned its still-beating heart to their sovereign's chest. The Duke died.

Philip the Good, thus dispatched by the crude methods of his era, left behind thirty-one children. Somehow, one was deemed legitimate and ascended to rule in his stead. The benevolent duke had provided for nineteen of the others; the remainder were left to shift for themselves.

Flemish chronicles claimed the deceased prince descended from the kings of Troy through Thierry of Aquitaine and the ancient rulers of Franconia. Yet, this lineage offered no true distinction. Not a single prince of his day could be found who did not trace his blood back to Troy. In the medieval mind, "Priam" was merely a synonym for "Adam."

The Duke's body was carried to Burgundy and interred in a noble mausoleum of black marble at Dijon. Holland rang with mourning for his passing, little dreaming that within its borders, that same year, something as famous was being born. That judgment has since been reversed. Men now gaze with reverence at the tailor's house where the great birth of the fifteenth century occurred. As the song goes, no one knows or cares where the good duke died.

And why?

Dukes like Philip the Good come and go, leaving mankind neither a halfpenny wiser, nor better, nor different from how they found it.

But when, once in three hundred years, a child such as Margaret's son is born to the world, behold! A human torch kindled by fire from heaven; and "Let there be Light" thunders from pole to pole.

The Dominicans, once the towering giants of European piety, were now retreating into the shadows, their influence waning before the surging tide of their Franciscan rivals. Even in England, a land steeped in faith where the Black Friars had once ruled supreme, the shift was palpable. Consequently, the order's wise leaders grew desperate to recruit and dispatch sons of exceptional talent to reclaim the world for their cause.

Clement possessed exactly such gifts. His mastery of languages—Latin, Italian, French, and both high and low Dutch—was renowned. He was destined for the pulpit in England, a mission sanctioned by Rome. Yet, Jerome, who held the superior's ear, obstructed this path.

"Clement," Jerome warned, "still carries the milk of the world in his veins. His feelings and weaknesses are fresh. Let not his new zeal or his humility tempt us to discard our ancient wisdom. Test him first, temper him, lest we one day lean on a reed for a staff."

"It is prudent advice," the Prior agreed. "Take him in hand yourself."

Thus, Jerome, adhering to the order's ancient prudence, began his trial of Clement.

First, he led the novice to a field where young men played their games, a haunt of Clement's former friends. Soon, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea passed by, casting a casual glance at the two friars. They failed to recognize their dead friend in the shaven monk. Clement gave a slight start, lowered his eyes, and recited a *Pater Noster*.

"Would you not speak with them, brother?" Jerome probed.

"No, brother. Yet it was good for me to see them. They remind me of sins I can never repent enough."

"It is well," Jerome replied, filing a cold report in Clement's favor.

Next, Jerome subjected Clement to the grim theater of death. He took him to dying beds, then into dungeons where darkness was absolute and the stench pestilential. There, men resembling wild beasts lay coiled in rags and filth, consumed by despair. The trials hardened Clement's body, but his soul gathered all its strength to comfort the wretched. In this crucible, Clement shone. Jerome reported that Clement's spirit was willing, though his flesh was weak.

"Good!" declared Anselm. "His flesh is weak, but his spirit is willing."

Yet, a greater trial awaited.

One morning, a principal street in Rome was choked with a crowd witnessing an execution. It was no common crime; the victim was no vulgar criminal. The Governor of Rome had been found slaughtered in his bed at dawn, his hand severed at the wrist, his throat cut, his temples bruised. The murder was traced to his servant, and the retribution was to be exacted immediately.

While Italian executions were rarely cruel, this demanded bloody precision. The criminal was dragged to the murdered man's house and pinned to the wall for half an hour. Then, his left hand was hacked off, mirroring his victim's. A raw chicken was slit open and bound to the bleeding stump—a macabre addition of some doctor's fancy. Mutilated and bandaged, the man was hurried to the scaffold. There, a young friar prayed with him, holding a crucifix close to his eyes.

Suddenly, the executioner shoved the friar aside and struck the culprit with a heavy mallet, then cut his throat from ear to ear. A cry of horror erupted from the crowd. The young friar swooned. A gigantic monk stepped forward and carried him off like a child.

Brother Clement returned to the convent discouraged. He confessed to the Prior with tears.

"Courage, son Clement," the Prior said. "A Dominican is not made in a day. You shall face another trial. And I forbid you to go fasting."

Clement bowed in obedience. He need not wait long. A robber, a monster of wanton cruelty, was brought to the scaffold. Clement spent his last night in prison with the man, accompanied him to the block, and prayed for him with such earnestness that the hardened ruffian wept and embraced him. Clement, though his flesh quivered with repugnance, embraced him too, holding the crucifix before the dying man's eyes.

As the man was garroted, Clement lost sight of the crowd but prayed loudly as the dark spirit departed. The moment the man died, the hangman raised his hatchet and quartered the body on the spot. The crowd, who had watched the living man die with indifference, uttered piteous cries at each stroke of the axe upon the corpse that could feel nothing. Clement shuddered but stood firm, like a rock that vibrates but cannot be thrown down.

Suddenly, Jerome's voice rang in his ear. "Brother Clement, mount that cart and preach to the people! Strike with all your force while the iron is hot, and souls may be saved."

Clement's color fled and returned; he breathed hard. Yet he obeyed. With an ill-assured step, he climbed the cart and faced his first crowd. The sea of upturned faces seemed to parch his throat; his heart thumped, his voice trembled.

But soon, the gravity of the occasion and the sight of the eager faces ignited the pale monk. He recounted the robber's history, fresh from his own lips in the prison cell, showing the crowd the ladder of folly and crime. As he thundered against shedders of blood and moved them to charity, his tremors vanished. He felt strung tight like a lute, gifted with an unsuspected force. He mastered the listening crowd, feeling their very pulse, playing sacred melodies on them as on a psaltery. Sobs and groans attested his power.

Jerome stared, astonished, like one lighting a rocket. When Clement caught his look of surprise and saw no approval, he broke off abruptly and joined Jerome.

"It was my first endeavor," he apologized. "Your behest came like a thunderbolt. Was I? Did I? Oh, correct me, aid me with your experience, Brother Jerome."

"Humph!" Jerome said doubtfully. After long reflection, he added sullenly, "Give the glory to God, Brother Clement. My opinion is you are an orator born."

He reported this to headquarters half-reluctantly, for he was an honest friar, albeit a disagreeable one.

Later, a case of sacrilege arose involving Julio Antonelli. Three witnesses placed him at the scene of the theft; others provided an alibi. Neither testimony could be shaken. Antonelli was granted the trial by water. The clergy, favoring him, recommended the hot water ordeal, which involved dipping an arm into boiling water to retrieve a pebble. Antonelli lacked the nerve and chose the cold trial instead.

Antonelli took the sacrament, was stripped naked on the banks of the Tiber, and tied hand and foot to prevent him from sinking by struggling. He was lowered gently into the stream, floating with only his hair above water. A simultaneous roar from the banks proclaimed him guilty. Moments later, the ropes, which happened to be new, became waterlogged, and he sank. Another roar declared his innocence. Left at the bottom for the appointed time, he was drawn up gurgling and gasping, screaming for mercy. After the prayers, he was dismissed, cleared of the charge.

On the bank, Clement prayed earnestly. When it ended, he thanked God in a loud, slightly quavering voice.

"Brother Jerome," he asked later, "ought not the man be compensated?"

"For what?"

"For the pain, the dread, the suffocation. Poor soul, he lives, but has tasted all the bitterness of death. Yet he did no ill."

"He is rewarded enough in being cleared of his fault."

"But being innocent, he drank Death's cup, though not to the dregs. His accusers, less innocent, suffered nought."

Jerome replied sternly, "It is not in this world men are truly punished, Brother Clement. Unhappy are they who sin yet suffer not. Happy are they who endure ills earth can inflict; it is counted to them above, a hundred-fold."

Clement bowed submissively. "May your good words not fall to the ground, but take root in my heart, Brother Jerome."

The severest trial, however, was unpremeditated. Jerome, in an indulgent mood, took Clement to Fra Colonna. There lay *The Dream of Polifilo*, freshly copied. The author, proud of his work, pointed out a masterstroke.

"For ages," Colonna declared, "fools have lavished poetic praise on mortal women, mere creatures of earth... Sirens at windows, Magpies at doors, Angels in the street, Devils in the house. Then come I and ransack minstrels' lines... and lay the whole bundle of spice at the feet of the only females worthy of amorous incense: the Nine Muses."

"By this goodly stratagem," Jerome interjected, turning the pages, "you, a friar of St. Dominic, have produced an obscene book." He dashed the manuscript onto the table.

"Obscene? Thou discourteous monk!" Colonna cried, snatching the book away, locking it, and trembling with mortification. "My Gerard, pshaw! Brother What's-his-name had not found *Polifilo* obscene. *Puris omnia pura*."

"Such as read your *Polifilo*—Heaven grant they may be few—will find him what I find him."

Colonna swallowed the bitter pill. Being a high-born gentleman and scholar, he avoided a vulgar quarrel. Instead, he turned the conversation to a beautiful chrysolite lent by Cardinal Colonna. While Clement handled the stone, he enlarged on its moral virtues, a trait of his age as a worshipper of jewels.

Jerome, however, expostulated. "One dead stone cannot confer valour, chastity, safety from poison, or temperance."

"The experience of ages proves they do," Colonna insisted. "As for temperance, look at this Gerard. He comes from the north where men drink like fishes, yet he is abstemious. Why? He carries an amethyst, the clearest I ever saw. Where is yours, unbeliever? Show it!"

"And 'twas that amethyst that made the boy temperate?" Jerome asked ironically.

"Certainly. What is the derivation of *amethyst*? *A* negative, and *methua* to tipple. Names are but signs of things. A stone is not called *amethustos* for two thousand years out of mere sport."

Colonna proceeded to illustrate the moral properties of rubies, sapphires, emeralds, and opals with anecdotes from grave historians.

"These are old wives' fables," Jerome scoffed. "Was ever such credulity?"

Credulity is a reproach skeptics often incur, yet it mortifies them none the less. The believer in stones writhed under the insult and dropped the subject. Mistaking his silence, Jerome exhorted him to abandon his pagan lore and study the lives of the saints. "Blot out these heathen superstitions from thy mind, brother, as Christianity hath blotted them from the earth."

In this strain, Jerome repeated loose theological statements. Then, the smarting *Polifilo* revenged itself. Colonna flew out, hurling a mountain of crude, miscellaneous lore upon Jerome.

"The heathen blotted out? Why, they hold four-fifths of the world. What have we Christians invented without their aid? Painting? Sculpture? These are heathen arts. What modern mind can conceive god-like forms as did the Athenian sculptors, the Libyan Licas, Scopas, Timotheus, Leochares, Briaxis? Chares, Lysippus, and the immortal three of Rhodes who wrought Laocoon from a single block? What prince has the genius to turn mountains into statues, as done at Bagistan? What town the soul to plant a colossus of brass in the sea?

"Is it architecture we invented? Why, here too we are children. Can we match the Parthenon with its Doric columns? Or the theaters of Greece and Rome? Or the prodigious temples of Egypt, with avenues a mile long of sphinxes? Even now their polished columns lie o'ergrown with acanthus, sparkling still, flouting the slovenly art of modern workmen. Is it sewers, aqueducts, viaducts? We have lost the art of making a road. Is it sepulchres? No Christian nation has ever erected a tomb that does not set a scholar laughing. Think of the Mausoleum, the Pyramids, the monstrous tombs of the Indus and Ganges.

"What then have we invented? Geometry? Logic? We are pupils of Euclid and Aristotle. Written characters? We no more invented them than Cadmus. Poetry? Homer, Virgil, Horace—none approached. Tragedy or comedy? Poets, actors, theaters fell to dust at our touch. Have we revived them? Would you compare our miserable mysteries with a Greek play performed inside a marble miracle, the audience a seated city, the poet a Sophocles?

"What then? Monotheism? The learned Greeks and Romans held it. *Zeus estin ouranos...* Their vulgar were polytheists; and what are ours? We have not invented the invocation of the saints. Our *sancti* answer their *Daemones* and *Divi*. The pagan vulgar prayed to deified mortals to intercede; the ruder minds worshipped them. So do the mob of Christians today. Holy Writ knows no polytheism. We have not invented a variety of it. The pagan vulgar worshipped deified mortals; our vulgar canonizes them. Each has a favorite to whom they pray ten times for once to God. Call that invention? Invention is confined to the East.

"Our vulgar make images and adore them, which is absurd. Adoration is due from creature to Creator. Here man is the creator; so the statues ought to worship him. But even this abuse is ancient. The pagan vulgar made images, knelt before them, adorned them, offered incense, lit tapers,

A woman named Catherine is a formidable force in a stranger's home. Her influence is immediate and absolute. Hardly has she crossed the threshold before the pots gleam, the hearth sweeps itself clean, and the windows seem to widen, flooding the room with light. Yet, this domestic alchemy is driven by a restless spirit—a "Busy Body" with no sense of discrimination. She seeks to correct everything and everyone, believing that order is always virtue. While many things improve under her scrutiny, not everything can bear such correction; save for the grave, nothing stands still enough to remain untouched by her rearranging hands.

Catherine moved every chair and table in Margaret's house, perhaps improving the layout, but her ambition soon turned to the living furniture itself. As Margaret's confinement drew near, Catherine cunningly enlisted the help of Denys and Martin. When the two simple men earnestly asked how they might serve, she directed them to take a walk, hinting that the longer the stroll, the better. This was not a generous offer. The dynamic of the strong being commanded by the weak would have enraged a she-bear with three cubs, let alone two weary travelers. Consequently, they slipped out at dawn and returned like thieves at dusk. If caught inside, they moved with the caution of cats walking on glass, their faces etched with awe and their spirits crushed by submission.

Yet, their presence remained a nuisance. Their very existence felt ill-timed, jarring against Catherine's nerves. Did an instinct whisper that a pair of depopulators had no place in a house destined to multiply? The breastplate offered no shield against a woman's tongue, and Catherine drove them away with relentless verbal pricks. By the time Margaret descended the stairs, the kitchen was cleared of its male occupants. Martin, old and stiff, had retreated no further than the street, carrying off his prize: a stool, upon which he sat suspended in the air. Margaret, seeing him bathed in sunlight, assumed he was merely resting, unaware he had become a fixture of the sun itself. She called for Denys, expecting his cheerful return.

Catherine, wiping a bowl with exaggerated vigor, lied smoothly. "Denys is gone to his friends in Burgundy," she declared. "High time, too; he hasn't been near them in three years."

"What? Gone without bidding me farewell?" Margaret asked, her eyes lifting like tender violets.

Catherine flushed. The accusation pricked her conscience, but she dismissed it with a casual toss of her head. "Oh, you were asleep then. I would not have woken you."

"Poor Denys," Margaret sighed, and visible dew gathered on her violet eyes.

Catherine, catching the sentiment from the corner of her eye, turned her attention to the remaining man. Suddenly, she showered Martin with hospitality and tenderness. He was old and stiff in more ways than one.

"No, thank you, dame," he replied, accustomed to the outdoors. "I dislike changing and changing. I meddle with nobody here, and nobody meddles with me."

"Oh, you nasty, cross old wretch!" Catherine screamed, shifting instantly from syrup to vinegar. She flounced back into the house.

Later, in quiet reflection, she felt a pang of regret. To soothe her conscience, she vowed that Margaret would never miss her "plagues of soldiers." Still uneasy, she dispelled the lingering doubt with a simple, sovereign act: she took the child and washed him.

From head to foot, she scrubbed the infant in tepid water, and the grievances of heroes dissolved like dust in an ocean of soap. During this celestial ritual, Margaret could not remain silent. She hovered around the woman performing the task, eager for even an apparent hand in the process. She dipped her finger into the water, ostensibly to pave the way for her son, though she knew Catherine would not allow her to judge the temperature. Kneeling opposite the little Gerard, Margaret prattled with amazing fluency, careful not to use adult pronunciations that a cherub might find ridiculous.

"I wish you could wash out THAT," she said, fixing her gaze on the boy's hand.

"What?"

"What? Have you not noticed? On his little finger."

Granny looked, and there, indeed, was a small brown mole.

"Eh, but this is wonderful!" she cried. "Nature, my lass, is strong and meddling to boot. Have you noticed such a mark on anyone else? Tell the truth, girl!"

"On him? Nay, mother, not I."

"Well, then he has; and on the very spot. You never noticed that much. But, dear heart, I forgot; you haven't known him from child to man as I have. I have had him hundreds of times on my knees, just as this, and washed him from top to toe in luke-warm water." She swelled with conscious superiority, and Margaret looked up meekly, as a woman facing a competitor beyond reach.

Catherine looked down from her dizzy height of experience and moralized. Unlike other busybodies, she occasionally reflected, though not deeply.

"It is strange," she said, "how things come round and about. Life is but a whirligig. At least, for us poor women, our lives follow one pattern. Wasn't I, in my youth, trying to wash out my Gerard's mole? 'Oh, fie! Here's a foul blot,' I would say, scrubbing until the poor wight cried, until I thought it time to stop. And now you say to me, 'Mother, do try and wash out my Gerard's finger.' Think on't!"

"Wash it out?" Margaret cried. "I wouldn't for all the world. It is the sweetest bit of his little darling body. I'll kiss it morning and night until he who owns it first returns to us three. Oh, bless you, my jewel of gold and silver, for being marked like your own daddy, to comfort me."

She kissed the little mole, but could not stop there. Soon, she had the boy sprawling on her lap, kissing his back over and over again, seeming to worry him like a wolf worrying a lamb. Catherine watched, smiling. She had seen many such savage onslaughts in her day.

This little sketch captures the tenor of Margaret's life for several months. A few small incidents occurred during this time, but they are reserved for another string of beads. While her boy's father endured fearful tempests of the soul, ending in the dead calm of the monastery, Margaret's life could be summed in one great, blissful word: Maternity.

You, who know the weight of that word, expand my sketch. See the young mother nursing, washing, dressing, and undressing; crowing and gambolling with her first-born. Then, swifter than lightning, dart your eye to Italy. See the cold cloister and the monks passing like ghosts, eyes downcast, hands meekly crossed over bosoms dead to earthly feeling.

One of these cowled ghosts is the man whose return, full of love, youth, and joy, the radiant young mother awaits.

In the valley of Grindelwald, the traveler sees on one side the perpendicular Alps—rock, ice, and everlasting snow, towering above the clouds and piercing the sky. On the other hand lie little, everyday slopes, green as emeralds, studded with cows and pretty cottages, teeming with life. Those lofty neighbors stand leafless, lifeless, inhuman, yet sublime. Elsewhere, the sweet commonplaces of nature pass unnoticed; but facing the grim Alps, they soothe and gently strike the mind by contrast with their tremendous opposites.

Such, in their way, are the two halves of this story, rightly viewed. On the Italian side: rugged adventure, strong passion, blasphemy, vice, penitence, pure ice, holy snow, soaring directly at heaven. On the Dutch side: all on a humble scale and womanish, yet ever green. And as a pathway parts the ice towers of Grindelwald, aspiring to the sky, from its little sunny braes, so here is but a page between "the Cloister and the Hearth."

The new Pope favored the Dominican order, and a message arrived from the Vatican summoning a capable friar to teach at the University of Basel. Clement was the very monk required for this task; well-versed in languages and having attended the lectures of Guarini the Younger in his worldly days, he was perfectly suited. His planned visit to England was therefore postponed, though not abandoned. Instead, he was dispatched to Basel, with the instruction that, since he would not be needed there for three months, he should preach along the way.

He departed through the northern gate with his eyes lowered, the entire figure wrapped in pious contemplation.

Hopeful, happy love; bereavement, despair, impiety, vice, suicide, remorse, religious despondency, penitence, death to the world, resignation.

And all within twelve short months.

Now the traveler was on foot again. Yet everything had changed; there were no perilous adventures now. Even thieves and robbers bowed to the ground before him, and instead of robbing him, forced stolen money upon him and begged his prayers. Consequently, this journey furnished few picturesque incidents. However, I have readers in mind who care little for melodrama and expect a quiet peep at what passes inside a man. To such students, undramatic things are often vocal, denoting the progress of a mind.

The first Sunday of Clement's journey was marked by this. He prayed for the soul of Margaret. He had never done so before. Not that her eternal welfare was not dearer to him than anything on earth; it was his humility. The terrible impieties that burst from him upon hearing the news of her death horrified his well-disposed readers, but not as on reflection they horrified him who had uttered them. For a long time during his novitiate, he was oppressed with religious despair. He thought he must have committed that sin against the Holy Spirit which dooms the soul forever. By degrees, that dark cloud cleared away, aided by Anselmo; but deep self-abasement remained. He felt his own salvation insecure, and moreover, thought it would be mocking Heaven should he, the deeply stained, pray for a soul so innocent, comparatively, as Margaret's. So he used to coax good Anselm and another kindly monk to pray for her. They did not refuse, nor did they do it by halves. In general, the good old monks—and there were good, bad, and indifferent in every convent—had a pure and tender affection for their younger brethren, which, in truth, was not of this world.

Clement, having preached on Sunday morning in a small Italian town and being mightily carried onward, was greatly encouraged. That day, a balmy sense of God's forgiveness and love descended upon him. He prayed for the welfare of Margaret's soul. From that hour, this became his daily habit, the one purified tie that by memory connected his heart with earth.

For his family were to him as if they had never been. The Church would not share with earth. Nor could even the Church cure the great love without annihilating the smaller ones.

During most of this journey, Clement rarely felt any spring of life within him, but when he was in the pulpit. The other exceptions were when he happened to relieve some fellow-creature.

A young man was tarantula-bitten, or perhaps, like many more, merely fancied it. Fancy or reality, he had been for two days without sleep, and in most extraordinary convulsions, leaping, twisting, and beating the walls. The village musicians had only excited him worse with their music. Exhaustion and death followed the disease when it gained such a head. Clement passed by and learned what was the matter. He sent for a psaltery and tried the patient with soothing melodies; but if the other tunes maddened him, Clement's seemed to crush him. He groaned and moaned under them and groveled on the floor. At last, the friar observed that at intervals his lips kept going. He applied his ear and found the patient was whispering a tune; and a very singular one, that had no existence. He learned this tune and played it. The patient's face brightened amazingly. He marched about the room on the light fantastic toe, enjoying it; and when Clement's fingers ached nearly off with playing it, he had the satisfaction of seeing the young man sink complacently to sleep to this lullaby, the strange creation of his own mind; for it seems he was no musician and never composed a tune before or after. This sleep saved his life. And Clement, after teaching the tune to another, in case it should be wanted again, went forward with his heart a little warmer.

On another occasion, he found a mob haling a decently dressed man along, who struggled and vociferated, but in a strange language. This person had walked into their town erect and sprightly, waving a mulberry branch over his head. Thereupon the natives first gazed stupidly, not believing their eyes, then pounced on him and dragged him before the podestà. Clement went with them; but on the way, he drew quietly near the prisoner and spoke to him in Italian; no answer. In French, German, Dutch; no assets. Then the man tried Clement in tolerable Latin, but with a sharpish accent. He said he was an Englishman and oppressed with the heat of Italy, had taken a bough off the nearest tree to save his head. "In my country, anybody is welcome to what grows on the highway. Confound the fools; I am ready to pay for it. But here is all Italy up in arms about a twig and a handful of leaves."

The pig-headed podestà would have sent the dogged islander to prison; but Clement mediated, and with some difficulty made the prisoner comprehend that silkworms, and by consequence mulberry leaves, were sacred, being under the wing of the Sovereign and his source of income. He urged on the podestà that ignorance of his mulberry laws was natural in a distant country, where the very tree perhaps was unknown. The opinionative islander turned the still vibrating scale by pulling out a long purse and repeating his original theory that the whole question was mercantile. "Quid damni?" said he, "Dic, et cito solvam." The podestà snuffed the gold: fined him a ducat for the duke, about the value of the whole tree, and pouched the coin.

The Englishman shook off his ire the moment he was liberated and laughed heartily at the whole thing, but was very grateful to Clement.

"You are too good for this hole of a country, father," said he. "Come to England! That is the only place in the world. I was an uneasy fool to leave it and wander among mulberries and their idiots. I am a Kentish squire, and educated at Cambridge University. My name is Rolfe, my place Betshanger. The man and the house are both at your service. Come over and stay till Domesday. We sit down forty to dinner every day at Betshanger. One more or one less at the board will not be seen. You shall end your days with me and my heirs if you will. Come now! What an Englishman says he means." And he gave him a great hearty grip of the hand to confirm it.

"I will visit thee some day, my son," said Clement; "but not to weary thy hospitality."

The Englishman then begged Clement to shrive him. "I know not what will become of my soul," said he, "I live like a heathen since I left England."

Clement consented gladly, and soon the islander was on his knees to him by the roadside, confessing the last month's sins.

Finding him so pious a son of the Church, Clement let him know he was really coming to England. He then asked him whether it was true that the country was overrun with Lollards and Wickliffites.

The other colored up a little. "There be black sheep in every land," said he. Then, after some reflection, he said gravely, "Holy father, hear the truth about these heretics. None are better disposed towards Holy Church than we English. But we are ourselves, and by ourselves. We love our own ways, and above all, our own tongue. The Norman could conquer our bill-hooks, but not our tongues; and hard they tried it for many a long year by law and proclamation. Our good foreign priests utter God to plain English folk in Latin, or in some French or Italian lingo, like the bleating of a sheep. Then comes the fox Wickliff and his crew, and reads him out of his own book in plain English, that all men's hearts warm to. Who can withstand this? God forgive me, I believe the English would turn deaf ears to St. Peter himself, spoke he not to them in the tongue their mothers sowed in their ears and their hearts along with mothers' kisses." He added hastily, "I say not this for myself; I am Cambridge bred; and good words come not amiss to me in Latin; but for the people in general. *Clavis ad corda Anglorum est lingua materna*."

"My son," said Clement, "blessed be the hour I met thee; for thy words are sober and wise. But alas! how shall I learn your English tongue? No book have I."

"I would give you my book of hours, father. 'Tis in English and Latin, cheek by jowl. But then, what would become of my poor soul, wanting my 'hours' in a strange land? Stay, you are a holy man, and I am an honest one; let us make a bargain; you to pray for me every day for two months, and I to give you my book of hours. Here it is. What say you to that?" And his eyes sparkled, and he was all on fire with mercantility.

Clement smiled gently at this trait; and quietly detached a manuscript from his girdle and showed him that it was in Latin and Italian.

"See, my son," said he, "Heaven hath foreseen our several needs and given us the means to satisfy them: let us change books; and, my dear son, I will give thee my poor prayers and welcome, not sell them thee. I love not religious bargains."

The islander was delighted. "So shall I learn the Italian tongue without risk to my eternal weal. Near is my purse, but nearer is my soul."

He forced money on Clement. In vain the friar told him it was contrary to his vow to carry more of that than was barely necessary.

"Lay it out for the good of the Church and of my soul," said the islander. "I ask you not to keep it, but take it you must and shall." And he grasped Clement's hand warmly again; and Clement kissed him on the brow and blessed him, and they went each his way.

About a mile from where they parted, Clement found two tired wayfarers lying in the deep shade of a great chestnut-tree, one of a thick grove the road skirted. Near the men was a little cart, and in it a printing-press, rude and clumsy as a vine-press. A jaded mule was harnessed to the cart.

And so Clement stood face to face with his old enemy.

And as he eyed it, and the honest, blue-eyed faces of the wearied craftsmen, he looked back as on a dream at the bitterness he had once felt towards this machine. He looked kindly down on them and said softly—

"Sweynheim!"

The men started to their feet.

"Pannartz!"

They scuttled into the wood and were seen no more.

Clement was amazed and stood puzzling himself.

Presently a face peeped from behind a tree.

Clement addressed it, "What fear ye?"

A quavering voice replied—

"Say, rather, by what magic you, a stranger, can call us by our names! I never clapt eyes on you till now."

"Oh, superstition! I know ye, as all good workmen are known—by your works. Come hither and I will tell ye."

They advanced gingerly from different sides, each regulating his advance by the other's.

"My children," said Clement, "I saw a Lactantius in Rome, printed by Sweynheim and Pannartz, disciples of Fust."

"D'ye hear that, Pannartz? our work has gotten to Rome already."

"By your blue eyes and flaxen hair I wist ye were Germans; and the printing-press spoke for itself. Who then should ye be but Fust's disciples, Pannartz and Sweynheim?"

The honest Germans were now astonished that they had suspected magic in so simple a matter.

"The good father hath his wits about him, that is all," said Pannartz.

"Ay," said Sweynheim, "and with those wits would he could tell us how to get this tired beast to the next town."

"Yea," said Sweynheim, "and where to find money to pay for his meat and ours when we get there."

"I will try," said Clement. "Free the mule of the cart and of all harness but the bare halter."

This was done, and the animal immediately lay down and rolled on his back in the dust like a kitten. Whilst he was thus employed, Clement assured them he would rise up a new mule.

"His Creator hath taught him this art to refresh himself, which the nobler horse knoweth not. Now, with regard to money, know that a worthy Englishman hath entrusted me with a certain sum to bestow in charity. To whom can I better give a stranger's money than to strangers? Take it, then, and be kind to some Englishman or other stranger in his need; and may all nations learn to love one another one day."

The tears stood in the honest workmen's eyes. They took the money with heartfelt thanks.

"It is your nation we are bound to thank and bless, good father, if we but knew it."

"My nation is the Church."

Clement was then for bidding them

Clement exhaled, a long, weary sound that seemed to carry the weight of his own uncertainty. He wondered if he had been rash in engaging with a spirit so fiercely passionate. Yet, even in his youth, he had absorbed the hard lessons of ecclesiastical prudence. He knew the value of the pause—the sacred silence required when faced with difficulty, a time to withhold both action and speech until the fog of emotion lifted slightly.

So, he held his tongue and offered a silent prayer for wisdom. His only physical movement was the gentle withdrawal of his foot.

But the penitent reacted with a sudden, desperate violence. She threw her arms around the limb, letting out a piteous cry, clutching it convulsively as tears streamed down her face. In that moment, the crushing agony of shame mingled with her penitence, flushing her throat a vivid, alarming red as she trembled beneath him.

"My daughter," Clement said, his voice softening despite the turmoil, "take courage. Do not torment yourself further over this Gerard, who is no longer of consequence. As for me, I am Brother Clement, whom Heaven has sent to you today to offer comfort and aid in saving your soul. Since you have made me your confessor, I now claim your obedience."

"Oh, yes," she sobbed, her voice breaking.

"Leave this pilgrimage immediately and return to Rome," he instructed. "Penitence sought abroad holds little value. Where we dwell lie the true temptations we must defeat or perish; we must not flee to escape them, seeking others that appear more showy but are far less lethal. It is easy to wash the feet of strangers while masked; it is hard to remain merely meek and charitable with those who surround us."

"I'll never, never lay a finger on her again."

"Nay," Clement corrected gently. "I speak not only of servants, but of dependents, kin, and friends. This is your penance: at the last hour of the night and the first after matins, recall your sin and God's boundless goodness. Let this memory make you humble and gentle toward the faults of those around you. The world courts the rich; seek instead the poor—not the beggar, for they are often neither honest nor truly destitute, but rather those who blush to ask for help yet need it desperately. To give to them is to lend to Heaven. And finally, marry a good son of the Church."

"Me? I will never marry."

"You will marry within the year," Clement declared firmly. "I entreat and command you to wed one who fears God. You are but clay; mated poorly, you shall become nothing. But wed a worthy husband, and by God's grace, you may live as a pious princess and die a saint."

"I?" she whispered, disbelief coloring her tone.

"Thou," he replied, pointing a steady finger.

He then asked her to rise and begin the good work he had assigned her. She rose to her knees, removed her mask, and cast an eloquent, searching look upon him before lowering her eyes in meek submission.

"I will obey you as I would an angel," she said, her voice trembling. "How happy I am, yet how unhappy; for oh, my heart tells me I shall never look upon you again. I cannot leave until I have dried your feet."

"It is not necessary," Clement said. "I have excused you from this bootless penance."

"'Tis no penance to me," she insisted, her voice rising. "Ah! You do not forgive me if you will not let me dry your poor feet."

"So be it then," Clement said, resigning himself to her fervor. *Levius quid foemina,* he thought to himself—a woman is a lighter burden, easily swayed. Yet, these fragile creatures, who gravitate toward the small as celestial bodies do toward the great, possess flashes of angelic intelligence that surprise even the wise.

After the princess had dried the friar's feet, she looked up at him, tears glistening in her beautiful eyes. With a singular tenderness, she murmured, "I will have masses said for her soul. May I?"

A faint blush colored the monk's cheek, and his cold blue eyes moistened. It was a sudden, unexpected grace from one he had just rated so lowly.

"It is a gracious thought," he admitted. "Do as thou wilt; often such acts fall back upon the doer like blessed dew. I am your confessor, not hers; it is your soul I must strive to save, or woe betide my own. My daughter, my dear daughter, I see good and ill angels battling for your soul this very moment. Fight on your own side. Do you remember all I have bade you?"

"Remember!" she affirmed. "Sweet saint, every syllable of yours is graved in my heart."

"But one word more, then. Pray much to Christ, and little to his saints."

"I will."

"And that is the best counsel I have to offer. So, let us part on this. You to the place that suits you best—your father's house—and I to my holy mother's work."

"Adieu," she faltered. "Adieu, thou that I have loved too well, hated too ill, known and revered too late; forgiving angel, adieu—for ever."

The monk caught her words, though they faltered in a sigh.

"For ever?" he cried aloud, his voice suddenly ardent. "Christians live 'for ever' and love 'for ever,' but they never part 'for ever.' They part as the earth and sun separate, only to meet more brightly in a little while. You and I part here for life. But what is our life? It is but one line in the great story of the Church, whose son and daughter we are; one handful in the sand of time, one drop in the ocean of 'For ever.' Adieu—for the little moment called 'a life!' We part in trouble, but we shall meet in peace; we part as creatures of clay, but we shall meet as immortal spirits; we part in a world of sin and sorrow, but we shall meet where all is purity and divine love, where no ill passions exist, but only Christ and His saints clad in white. There, in the turning of an hourglass, in the breaking of a bubble, in the passing of a cloud, she, thou, and I shall meet again. We shall sit at the feet of angels, archangels, apostles, and saints, beaming with joy unspeakable in the light of the shadow of God upon His throne, FOR EVER—AND EVER—AND EVER."

Thus they parted. The monk stood erect, his eyes turned heavenward, glowing with the sacred fire of zeal. The princess retreated slowly, turning more than once to cast a lingering glance of awe and tender regret upon that inspired figure.

She returned home subdued and purified. Clement, in due course, reached Basel and resumed his duties, teaching in the university and preaching throughout the town and its neighborhood. He led a life that could be summarized in two words: deep study and mortification. Those who knew him had already glimpsed the depths of his soul. In Basel, he advanced in holy zeal and knowledge, and the brethren of his order began to see in him a true descendant of the saints and martyrs.

When little Gerard was barely three months old, a messenger arrived, breathless and hot from Tergou, bearing news for Catherine.

"Go back," she commanded sharply, "and tell them I cannot come, and I will not. They have already taken Kate." With that, the messenger departed, and Catherine resumed her sentence. "There, child, I must go. They are in a state of complete confusion; this is the third time they have asked. Tomorrow, my man would come himself and drag me home by the ear, with a flea in his ear." She then recounted her experiences with infants, instructing Margaret on how to handle every conceivable emergency, and pressed money upon her. Margaret declined with gratitude, but Catherine insisted, her tone turning angry. Margaret offered excuses so logically sound that Catherine dismissed them with cold contempt; to her mind, they lacked the necessary feminine spirit.

"Come, out with your heart," Catherine said, "as we part. Perhaps we shall never see one another's face again."

"Oh! Mother, do not say that."

"Alas, girl, I have seen it so often. It comes to my mind now at every parting. When I was your age, I never had such thoughts. Nay, we all believed we would live forever then. So, get it out."

"Well, then, Mother—I would rather not have told you—Cornelis must say to me, 'So you have come to share with us, eh, Mistress?' Those were his words. I told him I would be very sorry."

"Beshrew his ill tongue! What does it signify? He will never know."

"Most likely he would sooner or later. But whether or not, I will take no begrudging bounty from any family; unless I saw my child starving, and—Heaven only knows what I might do. Nay, Mother, give me but your love. I prize that above silver, and they do not grudge me that, by all I can find—for not a single stiver of money will I take from your house."

"You are a foolish lass. Were it me, I'd take it just to spite him."

"No, you would not. You and I are apples from the same tree."

Catherine yielded with good grace. When the actual moment of parting arrived, embraces and tears burst forth on both sides.

Once she was gone, the child cried profusely. All attempts to pacify him failing, Margaret suspected a pin and searched between his clothes and his skin, finding a gold angel uncomfortably lodged against his backbone.

"There, now, Gerard," she said to the babe. "I thought Granny gave in rather suddenly."

She took the coin, wrapped it in a piece of linen, and laid it at the bottom of her box, bidding the infant observe that she could be as resolute as Granny herself.

Catherine later told Eli of Margaret's foolish pride and how she had baffled it. Eli replied that Margaret was right and Catherine was wrong. Catherine tossed her head in disagreement; Eli pondered the matter in silence.

Margaret was not without domestic anxieties. She still had two men to feed and could not work as hard as she had done. She had enough to do to keep the house, care for the child, and cook for them all. Yet she had a little money saved, and she used to tell her child that his father would be home to help them before it was spent. With these bright hopes and that treasury of bliss, her son, she spent some happy months.

Time wore on; no Gerard came. Stranger still, there was no news of him.

Then her mind grew disquieted. Contrary to her practical nature, she was often lost in sad reverie, sighing in silence. While her heart was troubled, her money melted away. It came to pass that one day she found the cupboard empty and looked into her dependents' faces. At the sight of them, her bosom filled with pity. She appealed to the baby whether she could let Grandfather and poor old Martin go hungry, then went to retrieve Catherine's angel. As she unfolded the linen, a tear of gentle mortification fell upon it. She sent Martin out to exchange it. While he was gone, a Frenchman arrived with one of the dealers in illuminated works, who had previously offered her a very poor price. He told her he was employed by his sovereign to collect masterpieces for her Book of Hours. She showed him her two finest pieces, and he was charmed by one of them—the flowers, raspberries, and creeping things shaded by Margaret Van Eyck. He offered an unheard-of price.

"Nay, flout not my need, good stranger," she said. "There are three mouths in this house, and none to fill them but me."

Curious arithmetic! Number one was left out.

"I would outdo thee not, fair mistress. My princess charged me strictly: 'Seek the best craftsmen.' But I make no hard bargains; make them content with me, and me with them."

In the next minute, Margaret was on her knees, kissing little Gerard in the cradle and showering four gold pieces upon him again and again, relating the whole occurrence to him in very broken Dutch.

"And oh, what a good princess! Wasn't she? We will pray for her, won't we, my lambkin, when we are old enough?"

Martin returned, furious. "They will not change it. I trow they think I stole it."

"I am beholden to thee," Margaret said hastily, almost snatching the coin from Martin, wrapping it up again, and restoring it to its hiding place.

Before these unexpected funds were spent, she returned to her ironing and starching. In the midst of this work, Martin sickened and died after an illness of nine days.

Nearly all her money went to bury him decently.

He was gone, leaving an empty chair by her fireside. For he had preferred the hearth to the sun as soon as the Busy Body had left. Margaret would not allow anyone to sit in this chair now. Yet whenever she let her eye dwell too long on it vacant, it was sure to cost her a tear.

Now there was no one to carry her linen home. To do it herself, she must leave little Gerard in the charge of a neighbor, but she dared not trust such a treasure to mortal hands; besides, she could not bear him out of her sight for hours on end. So she set inquiries on foot for a boy to carry her basket on Saturdays and Mondays.

A plump, fresh-colored youth named Luke Peterson, who looked fifteen but was actually eighteen, came in. Blushing and twiddling his bonnet, he asked if a man would not serve her turn as well as a boy.

Before he spoke, she was saying to herself, "This boy will just do."

But she took the cue and said, "Nay; but a man may seek more than I can well pay."

"Not I," said Luke warmly. "Why, Mistress Margaret, I am your neighbor, and I do very well at coopering. I can carry your basket for you before or after my day's work, and welcome. You have no need to pay me anything. 'Tisn't as if we were strangers, ye know."

"Why, Master Luke, I know your face, for that matter; but I cannot call to mind that ever a word passed between us."

"Oh yes, you did, Mistress Margaret. What, have you forgotten? One day you were trying to carry your baby and your pitcher full of water. And I said, 'Give me the baby to carry.' 'Nay,' says you, 'I'll give you the pitcher, and keep the bairn myself.' And I carried the pitcher home, and you took it from me at this door, and you said to me, 'I am muckle obliged to you, young man,' with such a sweet voice—not like the folk in this street speak to a body."

"I do mind now, Master Luke; and methinks it was the least I could say."

"Well, Mistress Margaret, if you will say as much every time I carry your basket, I care not how often I bear it, nor how far."

"Nay, nay," said Margaret, coloring faintly. "I would not put upon good nature. You are young, Master Luke, and kindly. Say I give you your supper on Saturday night, when you bring the linen home, and your dawn-meal on Monday; would that make us anyways even?"

"As you please; only say not I sought a couple of diets for such a trifle as yon."

With chubby-faced Luke's timely assistance and the health and strength Heaven gave this poor young woman to balance her many ills, the house went pretty smoothly for a while. But her heart became more and more troubled by Gerard's long, and now most mysterious, silence.

And then that mental torturer, Suspense, began to tear her heavy heart with his hot pincers, until she cried often and vehemently, "Oh, that I could know the worst."

While she was in this state, one day she heard a heavy step mounting the stairs. She started and trembled. "That is no step that I know. Ill tidings?"

The door opened, and an unexpected visitor, Eli, came in, looking grave and kind.

Margaret eyed him in silence, with increasing agitation.

"Girl," he said, "the skipper is come back."

"One word," gasped Margaret. "Is he alive?"

"Surely I hope so. No one has seen him dead."

"Then they must have seen him alive."

"No, girl; neither dead nor alive hath he been seen these many months in Rome. My daughter Kate thinks he has gone to some other city. She bade me tell you her thought."

"Ay, like enough," said Margaret gloomily. "Like enough. My poor babe!"

The old man, in a faintish voice, asked her for a morsel to eat; he had come fasting.

The poor thing pitied him with the surface of her agitated mind and cooked a meal for him, trembling, scarce knowing what she was doing.

Ere he went, he laid his hand upon her head and said, "Be he alive, or be he dead, I look on thee as my daughter. Can I do nought for thee this day? Bethink thee now?"

"Ay, old man. Pray for him; and for me!"

Eli sighed and went sadly and heavily down the stairs.

She listened half stupidly to his retiring footsteps until they ceased. Then she sank moaning down by the cradle and drew little Gerard tight to her bosom. "Oh, my poor fatherless boy; my fatherless boy!"

The evening meal at Tergou was interrupted by the frantic entry of Luke Peterson, a man coated in the dust of the road. "Good people," he gasped, "Mistress Catherine is required instantly at Rotterdam."

"My name is Catherine, young man," the woman corrected sharply, though her heart leapt. "Call me Kate, or Margaret."

"Ay, dame," Luke replied, breathless. "She said to me, 'Good Luke, hasten to Tergou and summon Eli the hosier's wife, Catherine, for God's love.' I did not wait for dawn."

"Saints preserve us!" cried the hostess, her mind racing with wild theories. "He has returned? No, she would surely have announced such news. What calamity could compel this?"

"Perhaps the youth can enlighten us," Kate ventured timidly.

"I can," Luke declared. "Her infant is dying. She was consumed by worry for it."

Catherine sprang to her feet. "What is the ailment?"

"Nay, I know not the specifics, but it has wasted away steadily."

A fleeting, furtive exchange of satisfaction passed between Cornelis and Sybrandt, unnoticed by Catherine, whose gaze was fixed on her husband. "Now, Eli," she commanded fiercely, "if you utter a single word against this, we shall quarrel after all these years."

"Who contradicts you, foolish woman?" Eli retorted cheerfully. "Quarrel with your own shadow while I fetch Peter's mule for you."

"Bless thee, my good man! Didst thou ever fail me in a pinch?" she exclaimed, already moving. "Eat your dinner, whoever can, while I prepare."

She drove Luke away in the cart, interrogating him with a mixture of severity and seduction until he confessed everything he knew. Upon arriving, Margaret met them at the threshold, pale and trembling, throwing her arms around Catherine's neck in a desperate embrace.

"Come," Catherine said, scanning the woman's face. "He lives, thank God."

She surveyed the failing child and the hollow-eyed mother. "Fortunate you summoned me," she observed clinically. "The child is poisoned."

"Poisoned! By whom?"

"By you. You have fretted."

"Nay, indeed, mother. How can I avoid fretting?"

"Do not feign ignorance. A nursing mother must turn her mind from grief to the comfort in her lap. Does she not know that the child wastes if the mother vexes herself? This stems from your reading and writing—idle crafts suited to men, which deprive women of practical wisdom. The child must be weaned."

"Oh, cruel woman!" Margaret cried vehemently. "I regret sending for you. Would you steal from me the only solace I possess? While nursing Gerard, I forget I am the unhappiest creature under the sun."

"That you do not," Catherine replied sharply. "Or he would not be as he is."

"Mother!" Margaret pleaded.

"'Tis hard," Catherine softened, yet remained firm. "Consider: would it not be harder to look down upon his lovely face gazing up at you from a small coffin?"

"Oh, Jesu!"

"And how could you face your other burdens with a heart full and a lap empty?"

"Oh, mother, I consent to anything. Only save my boy."

"That is a good lass. Trust me; I see clearer than you."

Yet another consent was required—the babe's—and he proved more stubborn than his mother. Margaret attempted to feign regret at his behavior, claiming he loved her too well, but Catherine was a match for both. Having noticed a healthy young woman nearby with an infant, Catherine approached her, explaining the crisis. The stranger agreed with a smile, placing her own child in the cradle and entering Margaret's home. She curtsied, and Catherine handed the pining infant to her. The stranger examined the baby with pity, cooed over it, and began to nurse it as if it were her own.

Margaret, paralyzed by the stranger's assurance, cast a rueful glance at Catherine before bursting into tears. The visitor looked up. "What is the matter? Wife, you told me the mother was unwilling."

"She is not; she is merely a fool. Never heed her. And you, Margaret, I am ashamed of you."

"You are a cruel, hard-hearted woman," Margaret sobbed.

"Those who undertake to guide the weak must be stern. And forgive me; you are not my flesh and blood, but your boy is."

Allowing her words to settle, Catherine added, "Come now. She sacrifices her own to save yours, and you can think of nothing better than weeping in the woman's face. Out, fie, for shame!"

"Nay, wife," the nurse replied gently. "Thank Heaven, I have enough for myself and hers to spare. Pray, do not blame her; perhaps life's troubles have soured her milk."

"And her heart as well," Catherine remarked without mercy.

Margaret stared at Catherine, her eyes dropping to the floor. Turning to the nurse, she sobbed, "I know I ought to be very grateful to you," then turned her head away, unable to bear the sight of another woman nursing her Gerard, who showed no distinction between the new mother and the one who had abandoned him.

The nurse responded, "You are very welcome, my poor woman. And so are you, Mistress Catherine, my townsfolk, though you seem unaware."

"What, are you from Tergou? All the better. I cannot recall your face."

"Oh, you know me not. My husband and I are humble folk compared to you. Yet true Eli and his wife are known and respected throughout the town. So, I am at your call, dame; at yours, wife; and at yours, my pretty poppet, night or day."

"There is a woman of the right old sort," Catherine murmured as the door closed behind her.

"I HATE her. I HATE her. I HATE her," Margaret whispered with fervent intensity.

Catherine merely laughed at the outburst. "That is right," she said. "Better to speak it openly than think it slyly. It is very natural, after all. Come, here is your bundle of comfort. Take it and hate it, if you can," placing the child in Margaret's lap.

"No, no," Margaret protested, turning her head halfway away. She could not bring herself to turn fully. "He is not my child now; he is hers. I do not know why she left him here, but it was kind of her not to take him to her own house, cradle and all. Oh! oh! oh!"

"Ah! Well, one comfort remains: he is not dead. This gives me light: some other woman has taken him from me. Like father, like son," Margaret wailed.

Catherine felt pity for her and allowed her to cry in peace. Later, when assistance was needed, Catherine would take Gerard out for fresh air. Margaret never objected, expressing no incredulity, yet she was always in tears upon their return.

This tacit agreement lasted briefly. Margaret became entirely eager to wean little Gerard. Once done, the child recovered his health and vigor, only to face a new trial immediately upon teething. Here, Catherine's experience proved invaluable. Amidst her grief regarding the father, Margaret found moments of bliss watching her son's tiny teeth emerge. "Teeth, mother? I call them not teeth, but pearls of pearls." Each pearl that appeared on his red gums seemed to Margaret the greatest achievement of Nature. Her companion shared the illusion. If told that standing corn was equally admirable, Margaret might have transformed into a reproachful gazelle and Catherine would have thrown them both out; thus, every new tooth was announced with a shriek of triumph by whichever woman discovered it first.

Catherine conversed with Joan and learned she was the wife of Jorian Ketel of Tergou, formerly a servant to Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, who had fallen out of favor and returned to Rotterdam. His friends had secured him the position of sexton at the parish, and combined with carpentry, he managed quite well.

In return, Catherine told Joan the identity of the child she had nursed, recounting the tale of Margaret and Gerard and the deep anxiety caused by the boy's silence. "Ay," Joan said, "the world is full of trouble." One day she advised Catherine, "I believe my man knows more about your Gerard than anyone here; but he has grown closer to his duties lately. Drop in some day just before sunset and set him talking. For our Lady's sake, do not say I sent you. The only hiding he ever gave me was for babbling his business, and I do not wish for another. Gramercy! I married a man for the comfort of the thing, not to be hidden."

Catherine visited. Jorian was eager to explain how he had befriended her son and perhaps saved his life, but this was no news to Catherine. The moment she began cross-examining him about why her lost boy neither came nor wrote, he cast a grim look at his wife, who received it with a calm, stolid candor and innocent unconsciousness. His answers grew short and sullen.

"What should he know more than another?" he deflected. He added, after a pause, "Do you think the burgomaster keeps such as me in his secrets?"

"Oh, then the burgomaster knows something?" Catherine asked sharply.

"Likely. Who else should?"

"I'll ask him."

"I would."

"And tell him you say he knows."

"That is right, dame. Go make him mine enemy. That is what a poor fellow always gets if he speaks to you women."

From that moment, Jorian withdrew, becoming impenetrable as a hedgehog, almost as prickly. His conduct caused both women agonies of mind, alarm, and irritated curiosity. Ghysbrecht was, for some reason, Gerard's mortal enemy; he had stopped his marriage, imprisoned him, and hunted him. Here was his former servant, who, when off guard, had hinted that this enemy held the key to Gerard's silence. Despite sifting every word and look from Jorian, everything remained dark and mysterious. Catherine then urged Margaret to go herself. "You are young and fair. You may extract more from him than I could."

The conjecture proved reasonable. Margaret went with her child, tapping timidly at Jorian's door just before sunset. "Come in," a sturdy voice called. She entered, finding Jorian by the fireside. At her sight, he rose, snorted, and burst out of the house. "Is that for me, wife?" Margaret asked, turning very red.

"You must excuse him," Joan replied somewhat coldly. "He lays it to your door that he is a poor man instead of a rich one. It concerns a piece of parchment. One was missing, and he received nothing from the burgomaster regarding it."

"Alas! Gerard took it."

"Likely. But my man says you should not have let him; you were pledged to keep them all safe. And truly, I do not blame my Jorian for being angry. 'Tis hard for a poor man to be so near fortune and lose it through those he has befriended. However, I tell him another story. Says I, 'Folk out of trouble like you and me should not be too hard on folk in trouble; and she has plenty.' Going already? What is all your hurry, mistress?"

"Oh, it is not for me to drive the goodman out of his own house."

"Well, let me kiss the bairn before you go. He is not in fault anyway, poor innocent."

Upon receiving this cruel rebuff, Margaret made a resolution she did not even confide to Catherine.

After six weeks, the good woman returned home. On the child's birthday, which occurred shortly thereafter, Margaret performed no work. She donned her Sunday clothes, took her boy in her arms, and went to the church to pray long and fervently for Gerard's safe return.

That same day and hour, Father Clement celebrated a mass and prayed for Margaret's departed soul in the minster church at Basle.

A lie is not like a blow with a curtal axe. One can anticipate the shatter of bone or the gash of flesh, but a falsehood has no bounds; it ramifies beyond human calculation, often costing lives or laying waste to families. In this story, one heartless falsehood wrought tremendous consequences, yet the tellers reaped little. The brothers, who invented the tale merely to reduce the number of claimants to their father's estate, saw little Gerard take their brother's place in their mother's heart. Worse still, Eli openly proclaimed that, since Gerard was lost and likely dead, he had provided by will for little Gerard and for Margaret, his poor son's widow. At this, a silent warning passed between the black sheep. Cornelis had the words on his lips—that Gerard was most likely alive—but seeing his mother's gaze, he checked himself in time.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the other partner in that deception, was now a failing man. He sensed the period approaching when all his wealth would drop from his body, leaving his misdeeds to cling to his soul. Too intelligent to deceive himself entirely, he had never been free from gusts of remorse. In taking Gerard's letter to Margaret, he had compounded his sin. "I cannot give up land and money," his giant Avarice had declared, while his dwarf Conscience whispered, "I will cause her no unnecessary pain." So, after tampering with the seal and finding no syllable regarding the deed, he took it to her with his own hand, making a merit of it to himself—a set-off on a scale not uncommon where the self-accuser is the judge.

The birth of Margaret's child surprised and shocked him, casting his treachery in a new, ghastly light. If his forged letter were to take effect, he would cause the dishonor of a woman who was the daughter of one friend, the granddaughter of another, and from whom he was unjustly withholding her land. These thoughts preyed on him during that period of life when bodily strength decays and the memory of old friends revives, filling him with gloomy horrors. Yet he dared not confess. The cure was an honest man who would have made him disgorge, but with him, Avarice was an ingrained habit, and Penitence only a fleeting sentiment.

It was on such a day, returning from the town hall to his own house, that he found a woman waiting in the vestibule, a child in her arms. She was veiled, and concluding she had something to be ashamed of, he addressed her magisterially. On this, she let down her veil and looked him full in the face. It was Margaret Brandt. Her sudden appearance and manner startled him, and he could not conceal his confusion.

"Where is my Gerard?" she cried, her bosom heaving. "Is he alive?"

"For aught I know," he stammered. "I hope so, for your sake. Prithee come into this room. The servants!"

"Not a step," said Margaret, taking him by the shoulder and holding him with the energy of an excited woman. "You know the secret of that which is breaking my heart. Why does not my Gerard come, nor send a line for these many months? Answer me, or all the town is like to hear me, let alone thy servants. My misery is too great to be sported with."

In vain he persisted that he knew nothing of Gerard. She told him that those who had sent her to him had told her another tale.

"You do know why he neither comes nor sends," she said firmly.

At this, Ghysbrecht turned paler and paler, though he summoned all his dignity. "Would you believe those two knaves against a man of worship?"

"What two knaves?" she asked keenly.

He stammered, "Said ye not—? There I am a poor old broken man, whose memory is shaken. And you come here, and confuse me so, I know not what I say."

"Ay, sir, your memory is shaken, or sure you would not be my enemy. My father saved you from the plague when none other would come anigh you; and was ever your friend. My grandfather Floris helped you in your early poverty and loved you, man and boy. Three generations of us you have seen; and here is the fourth. This is your old friend Peter's grandchild, and your old friend Floris his great-grandchild. Look down on his innocent face, and think of theirs!"

"Woman, you torture me," sighed Ghysbrecht, sinking upon a bench. But she saw her advantage, kneeling before him and placing the boy on his knees. "This fatherless babe is poor Margaret Brandt's, that never did you ill, and comes of a race that loved you. Nay, look at his face. 'Twill melt thee more than any word of mine. Saints of heaven, what can a poor desolate girl and her babe have done to wipe out all memory of thine own young days, when thou wert guiltless as he is, that now looks up in thy face and implores thee to give him back his father?"

With her arms under the child, she held him up higher and higher, smiling under the old man's eyes. He cast a wild look of anguish on the child, then on the kneeling mother, and started up shrieking, "Avaunt, ye pair of adders."

The stung soul gave the old limbs a momentary vigour, and he walked rapidly, wringing his hands and clutching at his white hair. "Forget those days? I forget all else. Oh, woman, woman, sleeping or waking I see but the faces of the dead, I hear but the voices of the dead, and I shall soon be among the dead. There, there, what is done is done. I am in hell. I am in hell."

Unnatural force ended in prostration. He staggered, and but for Margaret would have fallen. With one disengaged arm, she supported him as well as she could and cried for help. A couple of servants came running and carried him away in a state bordering on syncope. The last Margaret saw of him was his old furrowed face, white and helpless as his hair that hung down over the servant's elbow.

"Heaven forgive me," she said. "I doubt I have killed the poor old man."

Then this attempt to penetrate the torturing mystery left it as dark, or darker, than before. For when she came to ponder every word, her suspicion was confirmed that Ghysbrecht did know something about Gerard. "And who were the two knaves he thought had done a good deed, and told me? Oh, my Gerard, my poor deserted babe, you and I are wading in deep waters."

The visit to Tergou took more money than she could well afford, and a customer ran away in her debt. She was once more compelled to unfold Catherine's angel. But strange to say, as she came downstairs with it in her hand, she found some loose silver on the table with a written line: *For Gerard his wife.*

She fell with a cry of surprise on the writing, and soon it rose into a cry of joy. "He is alive. He sends me this by some friendly hand." She kissed the writing again and again and put it in her bosom.

Time rolled on, and no news of Gerard. Yet about every two months, a small sum in silver found its way into the house. Sometimes it lay on the table; once it was flung in through the bedroom window in a purse; once it was at the bottom of Luke's basket. He had stopped at the public-house to talk to a friend. The giver or his agent was never detected. Catherine disowned it; Margaret Van Eyck swore she had no hand in it; so did Eli. And Margaret, whenever it came, used to say to little Gerard, "Oh, my poor deserted child, you and I are wading in deep waters."

She applied at least half this modest, but useful supply, to dressing little Gerard beyond his station in life. "If it does come from Gerard, he shall see his boy neat." All the mothers in the street began to sneer, especially such as had brats out at elbows.

Months rolled on, and a dead sickness of heart succeeded to these keener torments. She returned to her first thought: "Gerard must be dead. She should never see her boy's father again, nor her marriage lines." This last grief, which had been somewhat allayed by Eli and Catherine recognizing her betrothal, now revived in full force; others would not look so favorably on her story. And often she moaned over her boy's illegitimacy. "Is it not enough for us to be bereaved? Must we be dishonoured too? Oh, that we had ne'er been born."

A change took place in Peter Brandt. His mind, clouded for nearly two years, seemed now to be clearing; he had intervals of intelligence, and then he and Margaret used to talk of Gerard until he wandered again. But one day, returning after an absence of some hours, Margaret found him conversing with Catherine in a way he had never done since his paralytic stroke. "Eh, girl, why must you be out?" she said. "But indeed I have told him all; and we have been a-crying together over thy troubles."

Margaret stood silent, looking joyfully from one to the other. Peter smiled on her and said, "Come, let me bless thee." She kneeled at his feet, and he blessed her most eloquently. He told her she had been all her life the lovingest, truest, and most obedient daughter Heaven ever sent to a poor old widowed man. "May thy son be to thee what thou hast been to me!" After this, he dozed.

Then the females whispered together, and Catherine said, "All our talk even now was of Gerard. It lies heavy on his mind. His poor head must often have listened to us when it seemed quite dark. Margaret, he is a very understanding man; he thought of many things: 'He may be in prison,' says he, 'or forced to go fighting for some king, or sent to Constantinople to copy books there, or gone into the Church after all.' He had a bent that way."

"Ay, mother," whispered Margaret in reply, "he doth but deceive himself as we do."

Ere she could finish the sentence, a strange interruption occurred. A loud voice cried out, "I SEE HIM, I SEE HIM!" And the old man with dilating eyes seemed to be looking right through the wall of the house. "IN A BOAT; ON A GREAT RIVER; COMING THIS WAY. Sore disfigured; but I knew him. Gone! gone! all dark."

And he sank back, asking feebly where was Margaret. "Dear father, I am by thy side," she cried. "Oh, mother! mother, what is this?"

"I cannot see thee, and but a moment agone I saw all round the world," he replied. "Ay, ay. Well, I am ready. Is this thy hand? Bless thee, my child, bless thee! Weep not! The tree is ripe."

The old physician read the signs aright. These calm words were his last. The next moment he drooped his head and gently, placidly, drifted away from earth, like an infant sinking to rest. The torch had flashed up before going out.

Margaret, who had wept so bitterly for the frailty of old Martin, could not endure the finality of his passing with the stoic resignation that often accompanies such losses. Catherine's attempts to soothe her with platitudes—that it was a blessed release, that the tree was ripe, that now there were fewer mouths to feed—fell flat. Her most grievous error came when she suggested that the burden of care had lightened.

"Such cares are the only joys I possess," Margaret replied, her voice trembling. "They fill this desolate heart, leaving it void and wasted. Look at this empty chair; my bosom aches to see it. Poor old man, how could I love him by halves, when I used to sit and gaze upon him, thinking, 'Without me, you would starve'? He, once so wise and learned, has become helpless as my own infant, and I loved him as if he were my child rather than my father. Oh, empty chair! Oh, empty heart! Well-a-day, well-a-day!"

Her tears flowed freely, unbidden and deep. Catherine fell silent, lowering her head in shame. Later, she confessed, "I speak from my head, not my heart; you are right to ignore me. If I were in your place, I would mourn the old man exactly as you do."

Moved by this rare moment of genuine empathy, Margaret embraced her. It brought her some comfort, unlike the hollow words before. Catherine's heart ached for her, and she spoke with sudden urgency. "My poor girl, you were not born to live alone. I must regard you as my own daughter. Do not waste your youth on my son Gerard. He is either dead or a traitor; it cuts me to say it, but the truth stares us in the face. Your father is gone, and I cannot watch over you forever. Here is an honest lad who has loved you for many days. I would take him and Comfort together. Heaven sends us creatures both to torment and to comfort us; without them, we are nothing."

Seeing Margaret's perplexed expression, Catherine pressed on, "Surely you are not so blind as not to see it?"

"See what?"

"Who else but Luke Peterson?"

"What? Our Luke? The boy who carries my basket?"

"Nay, he is nearly nineteen, a fine, healthy lad. I have made inquiries, and they all say he is a capable worker who never touches a drop. That is rare in a Rotterdam lad, who are mostly half-man, half-sponge."

For the first time in days, Margaret smiled. "Luke loves dried puddings dearly," she admitted. "I make them to his taste; that is why he comes courting here." Suddenly, she turned crimson. "But if I thought he came after your son's wife—or the one who ought to be—I would turn him out at once."

"Nay, nay! For Heaven's sake, do not make mischief. Poor lad! Why, girl, fancy cannot be bridled. Bless you, I wormed it out of him nearly a year ago."

"Oh, Mother, and you allowed it?"
