"Well, I thought of you. I told myself, 'If he is foolish enough to be her slave for nothing, all the better for her. A lone woman is lost without a man to fetch and carry her little matters.' But now my mind has changed; I think the best use you can put him to is to marry him."

"So then, his own mother opposes him and wishes to wed me to the first comer?" Margaret cried out. "And Gerard, thou hast but me. I will not believe thee dead until I see thy tomb, nor false until I see thee with another lover in thine hand. Foolish boy, I shall never be civil to him again."

Afflicted by Catherine's meddling protection, Luke Peterson received a cold reception in the household where he had previously found kindness. Soon, finding himself ignored and then sharply rebuked, the gentle giant began to whimper. He approached Margaret directly, asking if he had done something to offend her.

"Nothing," she replied softly. "I am to blame. I am cursed. If you will take my counsel, keep out of my way for a while."

"It is all because of me, Mistress Catherine," Luke said.

"You, Luke? What have I done to set her against me?"

"Nay, I meant all for the best. I told her I saw you looking at her through a wedding ring, but she would not hear of it."

"There was no need to tell her that, wife; she knows I have been courting her for a year."

"Not I," Margaret said firmly. "Or I should never have opened the street door to you."

"Why, I come here every Saturday night. That is how the lads in Rotterdam court. If we sup with a lass on Saturdays, that is the wooing."

"Oh, that is Rotterdam, is it? Then next time you come, let it be Thursday or Friday. For my part, I thought you came after my puddings, boy."

"I like your puddings well enough. You make them better than my mother does. But I like you still better than the puddings," Luke said tenderly.

"Then you have seen the last of them," Margaret declared, her tone gentle yet resolute. "How dare you speak so to another man's wife, especially one whose husband is far away?" She concluded, "You need not trouble yourself to come here any more, Luke. I can carry my basket myself."

"Oh, very well," Luke said. After sitting silently and stupefied for a moment, he rose and addressed Catherine sadly, "Dame, I suppose I have lost my job," before walking out.

The following Saturday, Catherine found him seated on the doorstep, weeping openly. He explained that he had grown accustomed to coming there, and every other place seemed strange. Catherine went inside and told Margaret. Margaret sighed, saying, "Poor Luke; he might have had a chance if he knew his place and treated her like a married wife."

When this was communicated to Luke, he hesitated. "Pshaw!" Catherine scoffed. "Promises are like pie-crusts. Promise her the whole world sooner than sit outside like a fool. If you simply humour her in everything, and then, if poor Gerard does not return to claim her, you will surely have her—in time. A lone woman is always worn out, thou foolish boy."

Brother Clement had spent over a year teaching and preaching in Basel when Jerome appeared before him, dust-streaked and wearing a gaze of triumphant anticipation.

"Give the glory to God, Brother Clement," Jerome declared. "Thou mayest now journey to England with me."

"I am ready, Brother Jerome," Clement replied, his voice steady. "I have awaited thee for many months. In the quiet hours between lessons and prayer, I have studied the English tongue with close attention."

"It was a wise decision," Jerome acknowledged. He explained that he had delayed their departure only until he secured special faculties from the Pope: the power to collect funds for the Church's needs in England and to hear confessions within all secular monasteries. "Now, gird up thy loins. Let us depart and strike a decisive blow for the Church, and against the Franciscans."

Thus, the two friars descended the Rhine toward England. In the great cities, they preached together; in the smaller towns, they often split, taking opposite banks of the river to meet later at a designated point. Both were skilled orators, yet their styles diverged sharply.

Jerome's delivery was noble and imposing, though occasionally constrained in its religious scope and prone to a certain monotony. Clement, however, possessed a torrential variety. His speech flowed tenderly, curling around the listener's reason and heart, yet it could rise with the soaring thought. At his peak, Clement transcended Jerome entirely, becoming a figure of prophetic fire: vehement as a storm, excited as a rushing torrent, inflamed as lightning, thundering and flashing, sweeping all before him with the rapid waves of eloquence.

I might offer literal examples of such passages, but five reasons stand against it: the difficulty of translation, the brevity of our time, the fact that I have already done so elsewhere, the superior imitation of others, and the truth that while similarity is a virtue in peas, it is a vice in books.

Nevertheless, to avoid evasion, it must be noted that Clement secretly endeavored to learn the current events and the specific vices of every town he was to address. Jerome, rigid and unbending, scorned such preparation, refusing to tailor his message to the particular sins of any people.

At a great city several leagues from the Rhine, they shared the same pulpit. Jerome preached against vanity in dress, a favorite theme of his. He was eloquent and satirical, and the crowd listened with complacency, for vanity was not a vice they commonly practiced.

Clement, conversely, attacked drunkenness—a besetting sin in those regions. The clergy themselves were often infected by it, and popular prejudice shielded the habit. Clement struck merciless blows, drawing upon Holy Writ and worldly experience. He declared it a crime, the nursing mother of theft and murder. He reminded them of a recent parricide committed by a man in liquor, and of a band of drunkards who had roasted one of their own comrades alive in a neighboring village.

"Your last prince," Clement proclaimed, "is reported to have died of apoplexy, yet you know he died of drink. One of your aldermen perished miserably last month, dead drunk and suffocated in a puddle. You strip your children's backs bare to fill your bellies with that which makes you the worst of beasts—silly as calves, yet fierce as boars. This drives your families to want and your souls to hell. I tell you, your town, yes, your very nation, would sink to the bottom of mankind if your women drank as you do. How long will they remain temperate, resisting nature and the example of their husbands and fathers? Vice never stands still. Amend yourselves, or watch them descend to your level. Already in Bohemia, they drink alongside the men. How does a drunken woman appear? Would you love to see your wives and mothers drunk?"

A shout of horror erupted from the medieval audience, unused to sitting silently during a moving sermon.

"Aha, that strikes home!" cried the friar. "What madness! Do you think it shocks the all-pure God less to see a man, His noblest work, turned into a drunken beast, than it shocks you, creatures of sin and unreason, to see a woman become a thing no better nor worse than yourselves?"

He concluded with two vivid portraits: the chaotic house and family of a drunkard, and the orderly home of a sober man. Both were so true and dramatic in detail that the wives fell into exclamations of "Oh!" and "Ah!" and cried, "Eh, but that is a true word."

The discourse caused an uproar. Hearers formed knots; the men were indignant. Consequently, the women flattered them and openly sided against the preacher. A married man had a right to a drop; he needed it to sustain the family. As for the women, they cared not to exchange their husbands for milksops.

Double-faced indeed! That very evening, a group of men surrounded Brother Clement near a hundred strong, filling his wallet with the finest coins, offering him the roses plucked from their own heads, kissing his frock, and blessing him for taking up the cause to mend their sots.

Jerome deemed this sermon too earthly.

"Drunkenness is not heresy, Clement," he remarked. "It is folly for a whole sermon to be preached against it."

As they traveled on, Jerome found to his surprise that Clement's sermons sank deeper into the hearts of his hearers than his own. They made people listen, think, weep, and sometimes amend their ways. "He has the art of sinking to their peg," Jerome thought. "Yet he can soar high enough at times."

Overall, it puzzled Jerome, who harbored a secret sense of superiority over his tenderer brother. After about two hundred miles, it began to displease him as well as puzzle him. Yet he tried to suppress this sentiment as petty and unworthy. "Souls differ like locks," he reasoned. "Preachers must differ like keys, or the Church shall open fewer doors for God to pass through. And certainly, this novice holds the key to these northern souls, being himself a northern man."

So they moved slowly down the Rhine, sometimes drifting a few miles downstream, but generally walking the banks, preaching, teaching, and confessing sinners in towns and villages, until they reached Düsseldorf.

There lay the little quay where Gerard and Denys had once taken boat upstream. The friars landed there. There were the familiar streets, and there stood "The Silver Lion." Nothing had changed except the friar who walked through it barefoot, his heart calm and cold, his hands crossed over his breast, his eyes bent meekly to the ground—a true son of Dominic and the Holy Church.

"Eli," Catherine commanded, her voice cutting through the quiet of the room. "Answer me one question like a man, and I shall ask no more today. What is wormwood?"

Eli stared at her, momentarily paralyzed by the sudden demand on his wits. He recovered quickly, offering a practical definition. "It is a herb that tastes of main bitterness."

"That is a fair answer, my man, but not the one I seek."

"Then answer it yourself."

"And shall," Eli groaned, the weight of her words settling heavily upon him. "Wormwood is... to have two men in the house doing naught but waiting for your shoes and mine." The shaft struck home, piercing the pretense of idleness.

"Methinks waiting for their best friend's coffin, that and nothing to do, are enough to make them worse than Nature intended. Why not set them up somewhere, to give them a chance?"

Eli hesitated, admitting he was willing but fearful that they would drink and gamble their very shelves away before they could earn a crust.

"Nay," Catherine replied sharply. "Do you take me for a simpleton? Of course I intend to watch them at the start, driving them with a loose rein, as the saying goes."

"Where did you think of going? Not here; to divide our own custom."

"Not likely. I say Rotterdam against the world. Then I could start them."

Oh, self-deception! The true motive behind this bold declaration was simply to draw near to little Gerard.

Following many discussions and eager promises of amendment from Cornelis and Sybrandt, Catherine traveled to Rotterdam to hunt for a shop, taking Kate with her for a change. Soon they found a suitable location in a good street, though it was sadly out of order. Yet, they secured it at a lower price and immediately set to work, brushing it up, fitting proper shelves for the business, and rendering the dwelling habitable.

Meanwhile, Luke Peterson was constantly asking Margaret what he could do for her. Her answer, delivered in a sad tone, was invariably, "Nothing, Luke, nothing."

"What, you who are so clever, can you think of nothing for me to do for you?"

"Nothing, Luke, nothing."

But finally, she varied her reply: "If you could make something to help my sweet sister Kate about."

The slave of love consented joyfully. Soon, he crafted a little cart for Kate, cushioned it, and yoked himself into it. At eventide, he drew her out of the town and along the pleasant boulevard, with Margaret and Catherine walking beside them. It appeared a happier party than it truly was.

Kate, for one, enjoyed the outing keenly. Little Gerard was placed in her lap, and she doted on him; it seemed like a cherub carried by a little angel, or a rosebud resting in the cup of a lily.

The vulgar jeered, asking Luke how a thistle tasted and mocking the fact that his mistress could not afford a horse with four legs.

Luke did not mind these taunts; Kate minded them for him.

"You have made the cart for me, good Luke," she said. "'Twas much. I did ill to let thee draw me too; we can afford to pay some poor soul for that. I love my rides, and to carry little Gerard, but I'd liefer ride no more than thou be mocked for it."

"Much I care for their tongues," Luke retorted. "If I did care, I'd knock their heads together. I shall draw you until my mistress says give over."

"Luke, if you obey Kate, you will oblige me."

"Then I will obey Kate."

An honorable exception to the popular humor was Jorian Ketel's wife. "That is strength well laid out, to draw the weak," Joan observed. "And her prayers will be your guerdon; she is not long for this world; she smileth in pain."

These were the words of Joan, spoken with a mixture of pity and resignation.

Single-minded Luke answered that he did not want the poor lass's prayers; he did it to please his mistress, Margaret.

After that, Luke often pressed Margaret to give him something to do, without success. But one day, seemingly tired with his importuning, she turned on him with a look and an accent she would later try in vain to convey.

"Find me my boy's father."

"Mistress, they all whisper he is dead."

"Not so," Margaret replied, her voice trembling. "They feed me with hopes."

"Ay, to your face, but behind your back, every tongue agrees he is gone."

At this cruel revelation, Margaret's tears began to flow. Beside her, Luke whimpered, seeking comfort. Though he possessed the sturdy frame of a man, his heart beat with the fragile rhythm of a girl.

"Prithee, weep not so sore, sweet mistress," he pleaded gently. "I would bring him back to life rather than see thee weed so deeply."

Margaret confessed that her sorrow stemmed from their double-tongued deceit. Composing herself, she placed a solemn hand upon his shoulder. "Luke, he is not dead. Dying men possess a strange sight. Listen to me: when my poor father was on his deathbed, and I, a simple fool, rejoiced thinking he would recover, he spoke to my mother and me. He sat in this very chair where you stand, and my mother was here, while I worked on a sleeve nearby. He said, 'I see him!' He saw him clearly—not like a failing man, but aflame. 'Sore disfigured—on a great river—coming this way.'"

"Ah, Luke," she cried, her desperation rising, "if you were a woman, possessing the depth of feeling you claim, you would pity me and find him for me. Think on it! He is the father of my child!"

"Alack, I would if I knew how," Luke sighed. "But how can I?"

"Nay, of course you cannot. I am mad to think it. But oh, if anyone truly cared for me, they would; that is all I know."

Luke fell silent, pondering. "The old folk say dying men see more than living wights. Let me think: my mind cannot gallop like yours. On a great river... Well, the Maas is a great river." He paused, weighing the possibilities. "Coming this way? Then if it were the Maas, he would have arrived by now. It is not the Maas. The Rhine is greater, far longer. I think it must be the Rhine."

"And so do I, Luke," Margaret insisted. "Denys bade him come down the Rhine. Yet, even if it is, he may turn off before reaching his birthplace. He does not pine for me as I for him; that is clear. Luke, do you not think he has deserted me?" She sought contradiction, but he only replied, "It looks very like it; what a fool he must be!"

"What do we know?" Margaret implored.

"Let me think again," Luke said slowly. "I cannot gallop."

His meditation yielded a plan. He knew a station sixty miles up the Rhine where all public boats docked. He would go there and intercept the truant. Though he did not know Gerard by sight, he would mingle with the passengers on every arriving boat and ask if a Gerard was present. "And, mistress," he added, "if you would give me a letter to him; for, being strangers, he may not believe a word I say."

"Good, kind, thoughtful Luke," she said, blushing as she realized how little she had valued him. "I will. But give me until supper-time to write it."

At supper, she handed him a long letter, tied with silk in the fashion of the day and sealed over the knot. Luke weighed it in his hand, a shadow of discontent crossing his face. "Say your father was not dreaming," he said gravely, "and say I have the luck to fall in with this man. Say that if he turns out to be better stuff than I think, and comes home to you then and there... what becomes of me?"

Margaret flushed to her brow. "Oh, Luke, Heaven will reward thee. I shall fall on my knees and bless thee; I shall love thee all my days, sweet Luke, as a mother loves her son. I am so old by thee; trouble ages the heart. Thou shalt not go; 'tis not fair of me. Love makes us all selfish."

"Humph!" Luke grunted. "And if," he resumed, "that scapegrace reads thy letter, hears me tell him how thou pinest for him, and yet, being a traitor or a mere idiot, refuses to turn to thee... what then? Must I die a bachelor, and thou fare lonely to thy grave, neither maid, wife, nor widow?"

Margaret panted with fear and emotion at this terrible, sensible warning. Finally, she faltered, "If, which our Lady be merciful to me and forbid... Oh!"

"Well, mistress?"

"If he should read my letter, hear thy words—and, sweet Luke, be just and tell him what a lovely babe he hath, fatherless, fatherless. Oh, Luke, can he be so cruel?"

"I trow not but if?"

"Then he will give thee up my marriage lines," she whispered, "and I shall be an honest woman, and a wretched one, and my boy will not be a bastard. Of course, then we could both go into any honest man's house that would be troubled with us. And even for thy goodness this day, I will—I will—ne'er be so ungrateful as to go past thy door to another man's."

"Ay, but will you come in at mine? Answer me that!"

"Oh, ask me not! Some day, perhaps, when my wounds cease bleeding. Alas, I'll try. If I don't fling myself and my child into the Maas. Do not go, Luke! Do not think of going! 'Tis all madness from first to last."

But Luke was as slow to forego an idea as to form one. His reply revealed how quickly love was forging a man within him. "Well," he said, "madness is something, anyway. I am tired of doing nothing for thee, and I am no great talker. To-morrow, at peep of day, I start. But hold, I have no money. My mother takes care of all mine; I ne'er see it again."

Then Margaret took out Catherine's gold angel, which had escaped her grasp so often, and gave it to Luke. He set out on his mad errand.

It did not seem so mad to him as it did to us. In a superstitious age, Luke acted on the dying man's dream, vision, or illusion much as we would act on respectable information.

But Catherine was downright angry when she heard of it. "To send the poor lad on such a wild-goose chase! But you are like many more girls; mark my words; by the time you have worn that Luke fairly out, and made him as sick of you as a dog, you will turn as fond on him as a cow on a calf, and 'Too late' will be the cry."

Meanwhile, the two friars reached Holland from the south just twelve hours after Luke started up the Rhine. Thus, whether wild-goose chase or not, the parties were rapidly nearing each other. Jerome, unable to preach in low Dutch, pushed on toward the coast, anxious to reach England as soon as possible. Having the stream with them, the friars would in fact have missed Luke by passing him in full stream below his station, but for the incident I am about to relate.

About twenty miles above the station Luke was making for, Clement landed to preach in a large village. Towards the end of his sermon, he noticed a grey nun weeping. He spoke to her kindly, asking what her grief was.

"Nay," she said, "'tis not for myself these tears flow; 'tis for my lost friend. Thy words reminded me of what she was, and what she is, poor wretch. But you are a Dominican, and I am a Franciscan nun."

"It matters little, my sister, if we are both Christians, and if I can aid thee in aught."

The nun looked in his face. "These are strange words, but methinks they are good; and thy lips are oh, most eloquent. I will tell thee our grief."

She revealed that a young nun, the darling of the convent and her bosom friend, had been lured away from her vows. After various gradations of sin, she was now living in a small inn as a chambermaid, in reality as a decoy, known to be selling her favors to wealthier customers. "Anywhere else we might, by kindly violence, force her away from perdition," she added. "But this innkeeper is the servant of the fierce baron on the height there, and hath his ear still. He would burn our convent to the ground were we to take her by force."

"Moreover, souls will not be saved by brute force," Clement said.

While they talked, Jerome came up, and Clement persuaded him to lie at the convent that night. In the morning, when Clement told him he had had a long talk with the abbess and promised to try to win back her nun, Jerome objected, saying it was not their business and a waste of time. Clement, however, was no longer a mere pupil. He stood firm, and finally they agreed that Jerome should go forward to secure their passage in the next ship for England, while Clement was allowed time to make his well-meant but idle experiment.

About ten o'clock that day, a figure in a horseman's cloak, great boots, and a large flapping felt hat stood like a statue near the auberge where the apostate nun, Mary, was. The friar thus disguised was truly wretched. Ardent natures undertake wonders, but are dashed when they come hand-to-hand with sickening difficulties. Yet, as their hearts are steel, though their nerves are anything but iron, they turn not back, but panting and dispirited, struggle on to the last.

Clement hesitated long at the door, praying for help and wisdom, before entering the inn and sitting down faint of heart, his body in cold perspiration. Inside, however, he was another man. He called lustily for a cup of wine, which the landlord brought him. Clement paid with money the convent had supplied and made a show of drinking.

"Landlord," he said, "I hear there is a fair chambermaid in thine house."

"Ay, stranger, the buxomest in Holland. But she gives not her company to all comers, only to good customers."

Friar Clement dangled a massive gold chain in the landlord's sight. The landlord laughed and shouted, "Here, Janet, here is a lover for thee who would bind thee in chains of gold; and a tall lad into the bargain, I promise thee."

"Then I am in double luck," said a female voice. "Send him hither."

Clement rose, shuddered, and passed into the room where Janet was seated, playing with a piece of work, laying it down every minute to sing a mutilated fragment of a song. In her mode of life, she lacked the patience to carry anything out.

After a few words of greeting, the disguised visitor asked if they could not be more private somewhere.

"Why not?" she said, rising and smiling, tripping before him. He followed, groaning inwardly and sorely perplexed.

"There," she said. "Have no fear! Nobody ever comes here but such as pay for the privilege."

Clement looked round the room and prayed silently for wisdom. Then he went softly and closed the window-shutters carefully.

"What on earth is that for?" Janet asked, in some uneasiness.

"Sweetheart," the visitor whispered with a mysterious air, "it is that God may not see us."

"Madman," Janet retorted. "Think you a wooden shutter can keep out His eye?"

"Nay, I know not. Perchance He has too much on hand to notice us. But I would not the saints and angels should see us. Would you?"

"My poor soul, hope not to escape their sight! The only way is not to think of them; for if you do, it poisons your cup. For two pins I'd run and leave thee. Art pleasant company in sooth."

"After all, girl, so that men see us not, what signify God and the saints seeing us? Feel this chain! 'Tis virgin gold. I shall cut two of these heavy links off for thee."

"Ah! now thy discourse is to the point," she said, handling the chain greedily. "Why, 'tis as massy as the chain round the virgin's neck at the conv—" She did not finish the word.

"Whisht! whisht! whisht! 'Tis it. And thou shalt have thy share. But betray me not."

"Monster!" cried Janet, drawing back with repugnance. "What, rob the blessed Virgin of her chain and give it to an—"

"You are none," cried Clement exultingly, "or you had not recked for that—Mary!"

"Ah! ah! ah!"

"Thy patron saint, whose chain this is, sends me to greet thee."

She ran screaming to the window and began to undo the shutters. Her fingers trembled, and Clement had time to disabuse himself of his boots and hat before the light streamed in upon him. He then let his cloak quietly fall and stood before her, a Dominican friar, calm and majestic as a statue, holding his crucifix towering over her with a loving, sad, and solemn look that somehow relieved her of the physical part of fear but crushed her with religious terror and remorse. She crouched and cowered against the wall.

"Mary," he said gently, "one word! Are you happy?"

"As happy as I shall be in hell."

"And they are not happy at the convent; they weep for you."

"For me?"

"Day and night; above all, Sister Ursula."

"Poor Ursula!" The strayed nun began to weep at the thought of her friend.

"The angels weep still more. Wilt not dry all their tears in earth and heaven and save thyself?"

"Ay! would I could; but it is too late."

"Satan avaunt," cried the monk stern

Waiting for a letter that carries weight rarely leaves the mind unchanged. Margaret, having poured her weary energy and trembling faith into her response to her dying father's vision—or perhaps his delusion—found herself suddenly adrift. Once the ink was dry and Luke departed, a sharp pang of guilt struck her conscience regarding the man she had sent away. Catherine arrived shortly after, her words sharp as flint, scolding her for the folly of false hope. The price Margaret paid for her momentary elevation of spirit was a plunge into a despondency far deeper than before.

In this state of hollow grief, she was found by a steadfast friend she had recently made, Joan Ketel. Joan entered the room radiant with a sudden inspiration. "Margaret, I know the remedy for your sorrow," she declared. "There is a hermit in Gouda, a man of wondrous holiness. When the mood takes him, he can foresee the future."

"I have heard of him," Margaret replied, her voice devoid of hope. Yet, with some difficulty, Joan persuaded her to walk as far as Gouda to consult the mystic. They carried a basket of butter and eggs, seeking the hermit's cave.

Their friendship had blossomed only six weeks prior, born of desperation. Jorian Ketel had fallen violently ill with a bowel disease that began with agonizing pain. When the pain subsided, leaving him weak, a terrifying symptom emerged: neither liquid nor solid could remain in his stomach for a single minute. The physician had declared, "He must die if this continues; therefore, boil a chicken with a golden angel in the water and let him drink it!" Alas, the gilded broth shared the fate of the humblest fare. Next, the cure steeped the thumb of St. Sergius in beef broth, with the same disastrous result. Desperate, Joan wept to Margaret to borrow linen for a shroud. "Let me see him," Margaret commanded. She entered, felt his pulse, and gasped, "Ah! They have not touched the root. Open the window! You are stifling him. Change all his linen."

"Alack, woman, what for? Why foul more linen for a dying man?" the medieval wife protested.

"Do as thou art bid," Margaret said dully, retreating from the room.

Joan, compelled by her friend's authority, obeyed. Margaret returned with her apron heavy with a flowering herb. She brewed a decoction and brought it to the bedside. Before offering it to the patient, she took a spoonful herself, smacking her lips with feigned delight. "That is fair," Jorian murmured, making a feeble attempt at humor. "Why, 'tis sweet, and now 'tis bitter." She engaged him in conversation immediately after he drank it. This bitter-sweet mixture stayed with him. Observing its effect, she built upon it like a gambler building a hand: she mixed a trace of schiedam in the third spoonful and a beaten egg yolk in the seventh. With the patience characteristic of her sex, she coaxed his body back from Death's grasp. Finally, Nature, patted gently rather than kicked under the bed, set Jorian Ketel upon his legs again. But the doctress made them both swear a solemn oath of silence. "They would imprison me, taking me away from my child," she had whispered.

The simple herb that saved Jorian was called sweet feverfew. She had gathered it from his own garden, her eagle eye having spotted it growing through the window pane.

Thus, Margaret and Joan arrived at the hermit's cave, placing their offering of butter and eggs upon the small stone platform. Margaret pressed her lips to the aperture designed for such prayers. "Holy hermit," she intoned, "we bring thee the best butter and eggs. And I, a poor deserted girl, a wife yet no wife, and mother of the sweetest babe, come to pray thee tell me whether he lives or dies, whether he remains true to his vows or has faltered."

A faint, rasping voice drifted from the darkness. "Trouble me not with the trifles of earth, but send me a holy friar; I am dying."

"Alas!" cried Margaret. "Is it truly so, poor soul? Then let us enter to aid thee."

"Saints forbid! Thine is a woman's voice. Send me a holy friar."

They turned and retraced their steps exactly as they had come. Joan could not suppress her indignation. "Are women imps of darkness, then, that we must not approach a dying bed?" she asked.

But Margaret was too deeply crushed to offer a reply. Joan offered rough consolation, which was ignored until she spoke of Jorian. "And Jorian will speak out soon; he is just on the boil. He is very grateful to thee, believe it."

"Seeing is believing," Margaret answered, her tone quiet and bitter.

"Not that he does not think you might have saved him with something more extraordinary than yon herb," Joan continued. "'A man of my stature cured with feverfew,' he says. 'Why, if there is a sorry herb,' he adds. 'I was thinking of pulling all mine up,' he said. I told him remedies are no better for being far-fetched; you and the feverfew cured him when the grand medicines failed. So I said, 'You may go down on your four bones to feverfew.' But indeed, he is grateful at bottom; you are all his thought and all his talk. But he sees Gerard's people coming around, good friends, and he said only last night—"

"Well?"

"He made me vow not to tell ye."

"Prithee, tell me."

"Well, he said: 'If I reveal what little I know, it won't bring him back, and it will set them all by the ears. I wish I had more headpiece,' he said; 'I am sore perplexed. But least said is soonest mended.' That is his favorite phrase; he returns to it from a mile away."

Margaret shook her head. "Ay, we are wading in deep waters, my poor babe and me."

It was Saturday night, and there was no sign of Luke.

"Poor Luke!" Margaret murmured. "It was very kind of him to go on such an errand."

"He is one out of a hundred," Catherine replied warmly.

"Mother, do you think he would be kind to little Gerard?"

"I am sure he would. So be kinder to him when he returns! Will you promise?"

"Ay."

***

Brother Clement, guided by the nuns, navigated a bend in the river with determined strides, reaching a station miles closer to the coast than where Luke lay in wait for Gerard Eliassoen. The following morning, he started early and arrived in Rotterdam by noon. He made straight for the port, eager not to keep Jerome waiting.

He observed several monks of his order on the quay and approached them, but Jerome was not among them. He asked one if Jerome had arrived. "Surely, brother," was the reply.

"Prithee, where is he?"

"Where? Why, there!" the monk pointed, indicating a ship cutting through the waves in full sail. Clement noticed that all the monks were gazing seaward.

"What, gone without me! Oh, Jerome! Jerome!" he cried, his voice filled with anguish. Several friars turned to stare.

"You must be Brother Clement," one said eventually. They kissed him and greeted him with brotherly warmth, handing him a letter Jerome had entrusted to them. It was a hasty scrawl. The writer informed him coldly that a ship was about to sail for England and that he loathed to lose time. He told Clement he might follow if he wished, but it would be far better to stay behind and preach to his own countrymen. "Give the glory to God, brother; you have a wonderful power over Dutch hearts; but you are no match for those haughty islanders: you are too tender."

"Know thou that on the way I met one who asked for thee under the name thou didst bear in the world. Be on thy guard! Let not the world catch thee again by any silken net. Remember, Solitude, Fasting, and Prayer are the sword, spear, and shield of the soul. Farewell."

Clement was deeply shocked and mortified by this contemptuous desertion and the icy tone of the message. He promised the good monks he would sleep at the convent and preach wherever the prior appointed, for Jerome had once raised him to the skies as a preacher. Now, wounded to the quick, he withdrew abruptly, needing solitude. He questioned himself: Was there some incurable fault in him, repulsive to such a true son of Dominic? Or was Jerome himself devoid of that Christian Love which St. Paul had placed above Faith itself? Shipwrecked together, saved on the same fragment of wreckage, his pupil, his penitent, his son in the Church, and now for four hundred miles his fellow-traveler in Christ; yet to be shaken off like dirt at the first opportunity, with harsh and cold disdain. "Why are worldly hearts no colder nor less untrustworthy than this," he muttered. "The only one who ever truly loved me lies in a grave nearby. Fly me, fly to England, man born without a heart; I will go and pray over a grave at Sevenbergen."

Three hours later, he passed Peter's cottage. A troop of noisy children played about the door, and the house had been repaired, a new outhouse added. He turned his head hastily away, unwilling to disturb a picture his memory treasured, and walked toward the churchyard.

He searched among the tombstones for Margaret's. He could not find it. He could not believe they had grudged her a marker, so he searched the churchyard again and again.

"Oh poverty! Stern poverty! Poor soul, thou wert like me; no one was left that loved thee when Gerard was gone."

He entered the church, kissed the steps, and prayed long and earnestly for the soul of her whose resting place he could not locate.

Upon exiting, he saw a very old man looking over the little churchyard gate. He approached and asked if he lived in the place.

"Four score and twelve years, man and boy. And I come here every day of late, holy father, to take a peep. This is where I look to bide ere long."

"My son, can you tell me where Margaret lies?"

"Margaret? There are many Margarets here."

"Margaret Brandt. She was the daughter of a learned physician."

"As if I didn't know that," the old man said pettishly. "But she does not lie here. Bless you, they left this place a long while ago. Gone in a moment, and the house empty. What, is she dead? Margaret a Peter dead? Now only think on't. Like enough; like enough. Great towns disagree terribly with country folk."

"What great towns, my son?"

"Well, 'twas Rotterdam they went to from here, so I heard tell; or was it Amsterdam? Nay, I trow 'twas Rotterdam? And gone there to die!"

Clement sighed.

"'Twas not in her face now that I saw it," the old man continued. "And I can mostly tell. Alack, there was a blooming young flower to be cut off so soon, and all old weed like me left standing still. Well, well, she was a May rose yon; dear heart, what a winsome smile she had, and—"

"God bless thee, my son," Clement said, "farewell!" and he hurried away.

He reached the convent at sunset, watching and praying in the chapel for Jerome and Margaret until long past midnight, until his soul had recovered its cold, distant calm.

Sunday dawned bright and crisp over the Hoog Straet, casting a golden hue upon Catherine's newly prepared shop. The air buzzed with anticipation; the sign, depicting a white lamb nursing a gilded ewe, fluttered cheerfully above the door where the names "Cornelis and Sybrandt" were freshly painted. Eli had arrived early, laden with fresh stock from his store, eager to ensure his brothers-in-law had a prosperous opening. Inside, the atmosphere was thick with the scent of possibility. Cornelis and Sybrandt strolled through the garden, their spirits buoyant, openly declaring their weariness of their agrarian past. They spoke of commerce with the fervor of men discovering a new religion, vowing to cling to their trade with the tenacity of wax. Through an open window, Catherine's mother overheard this declaration, her sharp ears catching every word. She called out to Eli, announcing that the evening supper would be a grand inauguration, a feast to welcome Margaret and her son, complete with counsel from the elders and wine to toast the successful transition from the barren fields of agriculture to the fertile plains of commerce.

Margaret descended the stairs, her usual lethargy shaken off by the prospect of the celebration. Her face flushed with the warmth of exertion and hope. Soon, Joan entered carrying a crisp salad from Jorian's garden. "He cut it for you, Margaret," Joan remarked, her tone laced with playful jealousy. "You are his favorite; I shall be forced to compete. I told him you were feasting today. But oh, lass, did you hear the sermon in the new church? Preaching! I have never witnessed such a thing."

"I wish I had been there," Margaret sighed, her voice soft. "I feel as though I have been dried up, lacking the dew of heaven."

"Why, he preaches again this afternoon," Joan replied. "But perhaps your duties call you here."

"Not she," Catherine interjected firmly. "Go on, if you are minded."

"Indeed," Margaret hesitated. "Yet, I fear I might dampen the spirits at table if I miss the warmth of a good sermon."

"Then you must be brisk," Joan urged, pointing toward the street where the congregation was gathering. "Look! There goes the holy friar." The woman bounded out of the house, crossing the road to intercept the cleric. She returned moments later, breathless but triumphant. "I was determined to see him close up. He said to me, 'My daughter, I shall go to him before sunset, God willing.' His voice was sweet as honey. But oh, my ladies, look at those thin cheeks on a young man, and those great eyes—so much like yours, Margaret."

"I have a strong desire to hear him," Margaret confessed. "But my cap is not quite clean, and all the women will be wearing their pristine white veils."

"There," Catherine said, reaching for a basket. "Take my handkerchief. You cannot keep the child; I need him for my poor Kate. She is having one of her difficult days."

Margaret accepted the handkerchief and ascended the stairs to find Kate. The room was quiet, save for the soft breathing of the invalid. "How art thou, sweetheart?" Margaret asked gently, noting the girl's pale countenance without needing to ask. "Thou smilest so. Look, I have brought thee one thou lovest."

"Two, by my way of counting," Kate replied, her smile radiant despite her suffering. A sudden spasm of pain seized her, a convulsion that would have startled a bull, yet she remained composed.

"In your lap?" Margaret gestured to the bundle in Kate's arms. "No, he is too heavy for thee in such pain."

"I love him too dearly to feel his weight," Kate whispered.

Seizing the moment, Margaret attended to her own appearance, smoothing her cap and adjusting her veil. "I am bound for the church," she announced, "to hear a beautiful preacher." Kate sighed deeply. "And yet, a moment ago, I was eager to go. That is my state for the past month; up and down, up and down, like the waves of the Zuyder Zee. I would rather stay beside thee; say the word!"

"Nay," Kate pleaded softly. "Pray, go. Bring me back every word. Alas, that I cannot go myself." Tears welled in the patient's eyes, a silent plea that settled heavily in Margaret's heart. This sight resolved her. She kissed Kate tenderly, glanced down at the sleeping boy, and let out a small, resigned sigh. "I think I must not," she murmured. "I could never kiss him lightly; and my father was always against waking a child by day or night. When it is thy pleasure to wake, speak to Aunt Kate the two new words thou hast learned." With a final, loving look over her shoulder, she closed the door silently behind her.

Downstairs, the kitchen resumed its rhythm. "Joan, lend me a hand and peel these," Catherine commanded.

"With pleasure, mistress," Joan replied, her movements swift and efficient. The cooking proceeded with silent vigor.

"Now, Joan," Catherine continued, chopping vegetables with rhythmic precision, "those who help me cook and serve the meat shall also help me eat it. That is a rule."

"There are worse laws in Holland than that," Joan laughed. "Your will is my pleasure, mistress. Besides, my Luke has his supper in the air; he is digging today by good luck."

Margaret descended the stairs, fresh-faced and glowing. "Eh, woman, that is an ugly trade," Catherine observed, snapping her fingers toward Rotterdam. "There she has just washed her face and given her hair a turn, and now who is like her? Give us a buss, hussy! Mind, Eli will not wait supper for the duke. Loiter not after your church is over."

Joan and Catherine followed Margaret to the door, standing there and watching her walk down the street until she disappeared around a corner. Among homely housewives, such a departure was nearly an event in itself. Catherine turned to Joan, her expression thoughtful. "There, Joan, it is almost as if I had just sent off my own daughter to church and stood watching her go, something I have done countless times. Joan, lass, she will not hear a word against our Gerard; and since he is alive, he has treated her cruelly. That is why my bowels yearn for the poor wench. I am older and wiser than she; and so, I shall wed her to that simple Luke, and there will be an end to it. What is one grandchild?"

The sermon had barely commenced when Margaret stepped into the vast, unfinished cathedral of St. Laurens. It was a colossus of stone, halted in its ascent, a testament to the slow pace of divine architecture. The side aisles bore roofs, yet the central nave and the chancel remained open to the sky. Pillars stood proud and arches were carved with care, some already washed in white plaster, but the great windows were mere jagged wounds in the masonry, save for a single pane of glass that caught the light.

Yet, in this midsummer afternoon, the imperfections transformed into splendor. The sun, breaking through the irregular gaps, cast fractured beams of gold that danced across the rough surfaces, painting the dark side aisles with rivers of glory and setting the white pillars ablaze. The central aisle became a checkerboard of light and shadow, the darkness cool and soothing, the light shimmering like amber diamonds infused with celestial fire. Above, the blue vault of the sky pressed close against the high roof, while the white hoods of the congregation formed a serene sea against the vivid azure.

Though the great central aisle was packed, a profound silence held sway. The preacher's words, delivered in a mellow, earnest voice, bound the crowd in mute reverence. Margaret stood transfixed by the sheer beauty and the collective devotion, a "great calm" settling over her. Seeking refuge from the glare, she slipped behind a pillar in the north aisle. There, amidst the loveliness of the place and the music of the voice, a sweet languor washed over her, smoothing the waves of her heart until she leaned against the stone, eyes half-closed, lost in a dreamy reverie.

It felt right to be there.

Soon, she noticed a lady vacating a prime spot opposite her to escape the scorching sun pouring from a high window. Margaret moved swiftly and silently, securing the seat. Now positioned beside a pillar in the south aisle, less than fifty feet from the pulpit, she was close enough to hear every syllable, standing just behind and to the side of the speaker.

Her concentration, however, was briefly shattered by the bobbing shadow of a man's head and shoulders, moving with such peculiar, almost comical rhythm that she struggled to suppress a smile. Yet, it was no cause for mirth. It was the sexton, diligently digging. She confirmed this by glancing through the open window to see the source of the shadow.

As her gaze lingered on Jorian Ketel working below, a specific tone of the preacher's voice struck her ear with such distinct force that it seemed to vibrate through her very bones. Her hand flew to her bosom, startled by the sudden thrill. Turning sharply, she sought the source, but only saw the back of the man and his tonsure. She sighed; the visual image of the monk contradicted the powerful resonance of his voice.

Still, she leaned forward, eyes cast down, waiting for that accent to return. It did not. Yet, the voice itself grew strangely potent, rising and falling as the preacher warmed to his theme, awakening faint echoes of a thousand happy memories. She refused to look up, unwilling to dispel the melancholy pleasure the sound evoked.

Then, in the midst of an eloquent passage, the preacher stopped abruptly.

Margaret almost sighed; the soothing melody had ceased. Was the sermon over? She glanced around, but the congregation remained still. Several faces turned toward her. She spun sharply, but the space behind her was empty. Startled expressions nearby fixed on the preacher. Following their gaze, she saw him in the pulpit: a face like a staring corpse. The friar's naturally large eyes, widened by the hollows of his cheeks, were dilated to a supernatural size, glaring directly at her from a bloodless visage.

She cringed, turning fearfully, convinced some terrible specter stood behind her. There was nothing; she was merely an outsider among the listeners.

Suddenly, the church erupted into commotion. Figures surged upward, craning necks; hundreds of agitated faces swung between the friar and Margaret. The rustling of caps created a deafening noise, followed by shrieks from nervous women and the buzzing murmur of men. Seeing so many eyes leveled at her, Margaret shrank terrified behind the pillar, casting one frantic, hurried glance at the preacher.

In that fleeting moment, she caught an expression on his stricken face that made her shiver. She turned faint and sat down on a heap of chips left by the workmen, burying her face in her hands. The sermon resumed, but she heard only the sound, not the sense. Her mind spun in a whirl, fixated on a single image: the prodigy-stricken face bearing a look stamped upon it. The memory of that gaze made her quiver from head to toe.

That look was *Recognition*.

After wavering, the sermon concluded with a strain of feverish eloquence that nearly drowned out the memory of the preacher's strange pause and ghastly stare. Margaret hurriedly merged with the departing crowd and exited the church.

Outside, they dispersed toward their homes, but she turned at the threshold and walked into the churchyard, heading straight for Peter's grave. Though poor, she had secured him a slab and a headstone. Sitting upon the stone, she kissed it, then pulled her apron over her head to hide her hair from any prying eyes.

"Father," she whispered, "you have often heard me say I am wading in deep waters; but now I begin to think God alone knows the bottom of them. I will follow that friar round the world, but I will keep him at arm's length. He must tell me why he looked toward me like a dead man wakened, with no one else behind me. Oh, Father, you often praised me here; speak a word for me there. For I am wading in deep waters."

Her father's tomb offered a commanding view of the church door. From there, with her face concealed, she waited, waylaying the holy preacher.

The cool church, checkered with shafts of sunlight and crowned with a canopy of heavenly purple, soothed Father Clement as it had soothed Margaret long ago. It carried his mind directly to the Creator of all good things. His gaze swept over the great aisle, crammed with his country folk—a thousand snowy caps filigreed with gold. Many leagues he had traveled, yet he had seen nothing comparable, save perhaps the vast, silent snowfields. In the morning, he had thundered; but this sweet afternoon seemed entirely out of tune with threats. His heart yearned over that multitude; he felt compelled to tell them of God's love, for poor souls heard almost as little of it from the pulpit in those days as the heathen did. He spoke the glad tidings of salvation, and the people hung upon his gentle, earnest tongue.

Clement was not one of those preachers who gyrated in the pulpit like a weathercock on a steeple; he moved the hearts of others far more than his own body swayed. Yet, he did not neglect those in need. Warm with this theme, determined that none of the multitude should miss the joyful news of Christ's love, he turned toward the south aisle.

There, in a stream of sunshine pouring through the window, stood the radiant face of Margaret Brandt. He gazed at it without immediate emotion; it simply numbed him, soul and body alike. But soon, the words died in his throat, and he trembled as he stared.

There, with her auburn hair bathed in beams and glittering like the halo of a saint, her face glowing doubly with its own beauty and the light that framed it—stood his dead love. She leaned lightly against a white column, listening with tender, downcast lashes. He had seen her listen to him so a hundred times. There was no change in her; this was the blooming Margaret he had left, only a shade riper and more lovely.

He started at her with monstrous eyes and bloodless cheeks. The people faded from his sight. He heard, as in a dream, a rustling and rising throughout the church, yet he could not tear his prodigy-stricken eyes from that face—all life, bloom, beauty, and that wondrous auburn hair glistening gloriously in the sun. He gazed, thinking she must vanish. She remained.

Suddenly, she looked at him, fully.

Her own violet eyes!

At this, he was beside himself, his lips parting to shriek her name, when she turned her head swiftly and soon vanished. Yet, not without one final glance. Though rapid as lightning, it encountered his, leaving her couching and quivering with her mind in a whirl, and him panting and gripping the pulpit convulsively. For this glance, though not recognition, was the startled, inquiring, nameless look that precedes it. He made a mighty effort, muttering something no one could understand, then feebly resumed his discourse. He stammered and babbled for a while until, forcing himself to regard her as a vision from another world since she was gone, he rose into a state of unnatural excitement. He concluded his sermon in a style of eloquence that electrified the simple, bordering on rhapsody.

The sermon ended. He sat down on the pulpit stool, terribly shaken. Presently, an idea very characteristic of the time took possession of him. He had sought her grave at Sevenbergen in vain. Now she had been permitted to appear to him, showing him that she was buried here—probably hard by that very pillar where her spirit had shown itself. Once adopted, this idea settled on his mind with the certainty of a fact. He felt he had only to speak to the sexton, whom he had seen working during the sermon to his great disgust, to learn the spot where she lay.

The church was now quite empty. He descended from the pulpit and stepped through an aperture in the south wall onto the grass, approaching the sexton. He knew him instantly. But Jorian never suspected the poor lad, whose life he had saved, in this holy friar. The loss of his shapely beard had wonderfully altered the outline of his face, changing him even more than his tonsure, his short hair sprinkled with premature grey, and his cheeks thinned and paled by fasts and vigils.

"My son," said Friar Clement softly, "if you keep any memory of those whom you lay in the earth, prithee tell me: is any Christian buried inside the church, near one of the pillars?"

"Nay, father," said Jorian, "here in the churchyard lie buried all that are buried. Why?"

"No matter. Pray tell me then where lieth Margaret Brandt."

"Margaret Brandt?" Jorian stared stupidly at the speaker. "She died about three years ago and was buried here."

"Oh, that is another matter," said Jorian. "That was before my time; the vicar could tell you, likely, if so be she was a gentlewoman, or at least rich enough to pay him his fee."

"Alas, my son, she was poor—and paid a heavy penalty for it—but born of decent folk. Her father, Peter, was a learned physician; she came hither from Sevenbergen—to die."

When Clement had uttered these words, his head sank upon his breast, and he seemed to have no power nor wish to question Jorian further. He appeared lost in the past.

Jorian put down his spade, standing upright in the grave with his arms akimbo, and said sulkily, "Are you making a fool of me, holy sir, or has some wag been making a fool of you!" Having relieved his mind thus, he proceeded to dig again with a certain vigor that showed his somewhat irritable temper was ruffled.

Clement gazed at him with a puzzled but gently reproachful eye, for the tone was rude and the words unintelligible. Good-natured, though crusty, Jorian had not thrown up three spadefuls ere he became ashamed of himself. "Why, what a base churl am I to speak thus to thee, holy father; and thou standing there, looking at me like a lamb. Aha! I have it; 'tis Peter Brandt's grave you would fain see, not Margaret's. He does lie here, hard by the west door. There; I'll show you." And he laid down his spade, putting on his doublet and jerkin to go with the friar.

He did not know there was anybody sitting on Peter's tomb. Still less did he know that she was watching for this holy friar. Pietro Vanucci and Andrea did not recognize him without his beard. The fact is, that the beard which has never known a razor grows in a very picturesque and characteristic form, becoming a feature of the face; so that its removal may, in some cases, be an effectual disguise.

As Jorian donned his doublet and jerkin, preparing to depart for Peter's tomb, his tongue remained far from idle. "They used to call him a magician out in Sevenbergen," he remarked, his voice carrying the rough cadence of the market square. "And they say he gave them a taste of his trade before leaving. He told them he saw Margaret's boy descending the Rhine in fine clothes, laden with gold, yet his face scarred by a foreign blade, missing limbs that once were whole. But, dear heart, nothing came of it. Margaret still pines for her son; and Peter lies as still as his neighbors, though she has placed a stone slab over him to keep him where he rests, as you shall soon see."

He pressed both hands against the grave's edge, rising to leave, when the friar laid a trembling hand upon his shoulder. "How long since Peter Brandt passed?" the monk whispered, his voice strange and strained.

"About two months. Why ask?"

"And his daughter buried him, you say?"

"Nay, I performed the burial rites, but she paid the fee and commissioned the stone."

"Then... but he had only one daughter, Margaret?"

"No more, at least, that he acknowledged."

"Then you believe Margaret is—alive?"

"Believe? Why, I should be dead otherwise. Riddle me that."

"Alas, how can I understand? You love her!"

"No more than reason dictates, being a married man and father to four sturdy knaves like myself. Nay, the truth is, she saved my life scarcely six weeks ago. Had she been dead, she could not have kept me alive. Bless your heart, I could not keep food on my stomach; nor could any doctor. My Joan said, 'Tis time to buy thee a shroud.' I replied, 'I dare say, so it is; but try borrowing one first.' Then in came my lady, this Margaret—who, by your reckoning, died three years ago—opened the windows, shifted me from my bed, and cured me in the twinkling of an eye. With what? There pinches the shoe; with the scurviest herb, plucked from my own garden, sweet feverfew. An herb, quotha, 'tis a weed; leastways, it was a weed until it cured me. Now, whenever I pass my hunch, I doff my bonnet and cry, 'Fly service to thee.' Why, Father, you look wondrous pale, then red, then white? What is the matter? What, in Heaven's name, troubles you?"

"The surprise—the joy—the wonder—the fear," gasped Clement.

"Why, what does it concern thee? Art thou of kin to Margaret Brandt?"

"Nay; but I knew one who loved her well, so well her death nearly killed him, body and soul. And yet thou sayest she lives. And I believe thee."

Jorian stared, and after a considerable silence, spoke very gravely. "Father, you have asked me many questions, and I have answered them truly; now, for our Lady's sake, answer me but two. Did you, in very sooth, know one who loved this poor lass? Where?"

Clement stood on the brink of revealing himself, but remembering Jerome's letter, he shrank from bearing the name he had worn in the world. "I knew him in Italy," he said.

"If you knew him, you can tell me his name," Jorian urged cautiously.

"His name was Gerard Eliassoen."

"Oh, but this is strange. Stay, what made thee say Margaret Brandt was dead?"

"I was with Gerard when a letter arrived from Margaret Van Eyck. It told him the one he loved was dead and buried. Let me sit down, for my strength fails me. Foul play! Foul play!"

"Father," said Jorian, "I thank Heaven for sending thee to me. Ay, sit ye down; ye look like a ghost; ye fast too much to be strong. My mind misgives me; methinks I hold the clue to this riddle, and if I do, there be two knaves in this town whose heads I would fain batter to pieces as I do this mould," and he clenched his teeth, raising his long spade above his head and bringing it down furiously upon the earth several times. "Foul play? You never spoke a truer word in your life. If you know where Gerard is now, lose no time and show him the trap they have laid. Mine is but a dull head, but sometimes the slow hound puzzles out the scent—go on. And I do think you and I have grasped two ends of one stick, and a main foul one."

Jorian then, after those useless preliminaries men of his class always deal in, came to the point of the story. He had been employed by the burgomaster of Tergou to repair the floor of an upper room. When it was nearly done, curiosity seized him as he fetched his tools, hearing loud words below. He lay prone and heard the burgomaster speak of a letter Cornelis and Sybrandt intended to deliver to replace one Hans Memling was taking to Gerard. "Their will was good, but their stomach was small," the old man had said, showing them a drawer full of silver, promising each thief a handful if they succeeded.

"Well, Father," continued Jorian, "I thought little of it then, save for the bargain itself, which kept me awake mostly all night. Think on't! Next morning at peep of day, who should I see but my masters Cornelis and Sybrandt emerging from their house, each with a black eye. 'Oho,' says I, 'what mark has yon Hans put upon ye? Well now, I hope that is all you have got for your pains.' Didn't they make for the burgomaster's house? I hid in my place."

At this revelation, the monk's nostril dilated, his restless eye showing the suspense he endured.

"Well, Father," Jorian pressed on, "the burgomaster brought them into that same room. He held a letter in his hand; I am no scholar, however, I have as many eyes in my head as the Pope, and I saw the drawer opened. Those two knaves thrust in each a hand and drew it out full. Saints in glory, how they tried to hold more, and more, and more of that stuff! Sybrandt had daubed his hand in something sticky, I think 'twas glue, and he managed to carry one or two pieces away sticking to the back of his hand. He! He! He! 'Tis a sin to laugh. So you see luck was on the wrong side as usual; they had done the trick, but how they did it, that, methinks, will never be known till doomsday. They left their immortal jewels in yon drawer. Well, they got a handful of silver for them; the devil had the worst of that bargain. There, Father, that is off my mind. Often I longed to tell it to someone, but I durst not to the women; or Margaret would not have had a friend left in the world. For those two black-hearted villains are the favorites. 'Tis always so. Have not the old folk just taken a brave new shop in this very town, in the Hoog Straet? There you may see their sign, a gilt sheep and a lambkin; a brace of wolves sucking their dam would be nigher the mark. And there the whole family feasts this day; oh, 'tis a fine world. What, not a word, holy father? You sit there like stone, having not even a curse to bestow on them, the stony-hearted miscreants. Was it not enough the poor lad was all alone in a strange land? Must his own flesh and blood go and lie away the one blessing his enemies left him? And then think of her pining and pining all these years, sitting at the window looking down the street for Gerard! So constant, so tender, and true: my wife says she is sure no woman ever loved a man truer than she loves the lad those villains have parted from her. The day never passes but she weeps salt tears for him. And when I think that, but for those two greedy lying knaves, yon winsome lad, whose life I saved, might be by her side this day the happiest he in Holland; and the sweet lass, that saved my life, might be sitting with her cheek upon her sweetheart's shoulder, the happiest she in Holland in place of the saddest; oh, I thirst for their blood, the nasty, sneaking, lying, cogging, cowardly, heartless, bowelless—how now?"

The monk started wildly up, livid with fury and despair, rushing headlong from the place with both hands clenched and raised high. So terrible was this inarticulate burst of fury that Jorian's puny ire died out at sight of it, and he stood looking dismayed after the human tempest he had launched.

While thus absorbed, he felt his arm grasped by a small, tremulous hand.

It was Margaret Brandt.

He started; her presence there seemed so strange. She had waited long on Peter's tombstone, but the friar did not come. So she entered the church to see if he was still there. She could not find him.

Presently, going up the south aisle, the gigantic shadow of a friar came rapidly along the floor and past a pillar, seeming to pass through her. She was near screaming, but in a moment remembered Jorian's shadow had come in so from the churchyard; she tried to clamber out the nearest way. She did so, with some difficulty; by that time Clement was just disappearing down the street, yet so expressive is the body as well as the face, she could see he was greatly agitated. Jorian and she looked at one another, and at the wild figure of the distant friar.

"Well?" she asked Jorian, trembling.

"Well," he replied, "you startled me. How come you here of all people?"

"Is this a time for idle chat? What said he to you? He has been speaking to you; deny it not."

"Girl, as I stand here, he asked me whereabout you were buried in this churchyard."

"Ah!"

"I told him, nowhere, thank Heaven: you were alive and saving other folk from the churchyard."

"Well?"

"Well, the long and the short is, he knew thy Gerard in Italy; and a letter came saying you were dead; and it broke thy poor lad's heart. Let me see; who was the letter written by? Oh, by the demoiselle van Eyck. That was his way of it. But I up and told him nay; 'twas neither demoiselle nor dame that penned yon lie, but Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, and those foul knaves, Cornelis and Sybrandt; these changed the true letter for one of their own. I told him as how I saw the whole villainy done through a chink; and now, if I have not been and told you!"

"Oh, cruel! cruel! But he lives. The fear of fears is gone. Thank God!"

"Ay, lass; and as for thine enemies, I have given them a dig. For yon friar is friendly to Gerard, and he is gone to Eli's house, methinks. For I told him where to find Gerard's enemies and thine, and wow but he will give them their lesson. If ever a man was mad with rage, 'tis yon. He turned black and white, and parted like a stone from a sling. Girl, there was thunder in his eye and silence on his lips. Made me cold a did."

"Oh, Jorian, what have you done?" cried Margaret. "Quick! quick! help me thither, for the power is gone all out of my body. You know him not as I do. Oh, if you had seen the blow he gave Ghysbrecht; and heard the frightful crash! Come, save him from worse mischief. The water is deep enough; but not bloody yet, come!"

Her accents were so full of agony that Jorian sprang out of the grave and came with her, huddling on his jerkin as he went.

But as they hurried along, he asked her what on earth she meant. "I talk of this friar, and you answer me of Gerard."

"Man, see you not, this is Gerard!"

"This, Gerard? What mean ye?"

"I mean, yon friar is my boy's father. I have waited for him long, Jorian. Well, he is come to me at last. And thank God for it. Oh, my poor child! Quicker, Jorian, quicker!"

"Why, thou art mad as he. Stay! By St. Bavon, yon was Gerard's face; 'twas nought like it; yet somehow—'twas it. Come on! come on! let me see the end of this."

"The end? How many of us will live to see that?"

They hurried along in breathless silence, till they reached Hoog Straet.

Then Jorian tried to reassure her. "You are making your own trouble," he said. "Who says he has gone thither? More likely to the convent to weep and pray, poor soul. Oh, cursed, cursed villains!"

"Did not you tell him where those villains bide?"

"Ay, that I did."

"Then quicker, oh, Jorian, quicker. I see the house. Thank God and all the saints, I shall be in time to calm him. I know what I'll say to him; Heaven forgive me! Poor Catherine; 'tis of her I think: she has been a mother to me."

The shop was a corner house with two doors; one in the main street for customers, and a house-door round the corner.

Margaret and Jorian were now within twenty yards of the shop when they heard a roar inside, like that of some wild animal, and the friar burst out, white and raging, tearing down the street.

Margaret screamed and sank fainting on Jorian's arm.

Jorian

Supper was served, the table groaning under the weight of savory meats and steaming dishes, yet the air felt heavy with an absence. Eli's family sat gathered, save for one. To Catherine's astonishment, Eli declared he would wait for her return.

"Why, I told her you would not wait for the Duke," someone remarked.

"She is no Duke," Eli replied, his voice firm despite the tension. "She is a poor, good lass who has waited not minutes, but years, for a graceless son of mine. Set the food down; we shall eat when she arrives, or not at all."

The aroma of the roasting meat was irresistible. Relenting, Eli nodded. "She will come if we begin. They always do." He gestured for Mistress Joan to take her seat. "You are not here as a servant, I trust, but as a guest." Just then, the sound of hurried footsteps echoed from the hallway. "Come, sit," Eli urged, pulling out his chair. "Fall to."

The covers were drawn back, and the knives were raised in anticipation.

Instead of the expected figure of Margaret, the door burst open to reveal a Dominican friar, his face a mask of livid rage. In a single, terrifying motion, he crossed the room, throwing his tall, gaunt frame over the narrow table. With both hands hovering above the shrinking heads of Cornelis and Sybrandt, like eagles poised to strike a trapped quarry, he unleashed a torrent of curses. It was a litany of ancient venom, so dense and blighting that words seemed insufficient to capture its horror.

"Cursed be the lips," he shrieked, his voice cracking with fury, "that spoke the lie that Margaret was dead! May they rot before the grave and kiss white-hot iron in hell thereafter! Doubly cursed be the hands that altered those letters, may they be severed by the hangman's blade to handle eternal fire! Thrice accursed be the cruel hearts that conceived this damned lie to tear true love asunder! May they sicken and wither on earth, joyless and hopeless, reduced to dust before their time and burned in everlasting flames!"

His wrath extended to the very meal before them; he cursed the meat in their throats and every atom of their bodies from hair to sole. Turning from the pair, who cowered beneath the table, trembling in fear, he tore a letter from his bosom and hurled it onto the table before his father.

"Read this, thou hard old man who imprisoned thy son! Read and behold the monsters thou hast spawned! The memory of my wrongs and hers shall haunt you forever! I shall meet you again at the Judgment Day; on this earth, thou shalt never see me once more."

In an instant, as he had arrived, he vanished, leaving the household stiff, cold, and pale as statues around the untouched feast.

This was the scene that greeted Margaret and Jorian. Figures of men and women, petrified in horror, surrounded the smoking board, a tableau reminiscent of Eastern poets' tales of despair.

Margaret scanned the room, her breath catching in her throat. "Oh, joy! All are here; no blood has been shed. Oh, you cruel, cruel men! I thank God he has not slain you."

At the sight of her, Catherine let out an eloquent scream before turning her head away in shame. But Eli, having just glanced at the false letter and grasping the terrible truth, saw the other victim enter with her own sorrows etched into her pale, sweet face. A transport of rage seized him. He sprang to his feet, shouting, "Stand clear! Let me reach the traitors! I will hang for them!"

Before anyone could react, he whipped out his short sword and lunged forward.

"Fly!" Margaret screamed. "Fly!"

They slipped howling under the table, scrambling out the other side. But before they could reach the door, the furious old man rounded the table to intercept them. Catherine merely screamed and wrung her hands; in moments of crisis, the notables are often useless. Blood would surely have flowed, but Margaret and Jorian seized the elderly man's arms, holding him fast with all their might while the couple escaped. They released him only as they fled, and he went on, vainly raging after them into the street.

They ran like hares for a furlong until safety was assured.

Inside, the old man hacked down the board bearing their names, carried it indoors, and threw it into the fireplace. Catherine sat rocking herself, her apron pulled over her head in despair. Joan had rushed to her husband. Margaret clung to Catherine's neck, pale and panting, yet striving to offer comfort.

"It cannot be done," she whispered. "Oh, my poor children! Oh, miserable mother! 'Tis a mercy Kate was ill upstairs. There, I have lived to thank God for that!" She broke into fresh sobs. "It would have killed her. He had better have stayed in Italy, coming home only to curse his own flesh and blood and set us all by the ears."

"Oh, hold your tongue, woman," Eli snapped angrily. "You still stand on the side of the wrongdoer. You are cheaply served; your weakness made these rogues what they are. I was prepared to correct them in their youth with sharp remedies for sore ills, but you sided with their faults and undermined me, baffling wise severity. And you, Margaret, leave comforting her who ought to comfort you. What is her hurt compared to yours? But she never possessed a grain of justice under her skin, nor will she. So come to me, for I am thy father from this hour."

It was a command. Margaret kissed Catherine goodbye and tottered toward him. He placed her on a chair beside him, and she laid her feeble head against his honest breast, though not a tear fell; the sorrow was too deep for such a display.

"Poor lamb," he murmured. After a pause, in a broken voice, he addressed Jorian and Joan. "Come, good folks, we are in a little trouble, as you see, but that is no reason you should starve. For our Lady's sake, eat. Do not add to my grief the reputation of a churl. What the dickens!" He forced a ghastly attempt at stout-heartedness. "The more knaves I have the luck to get rid of, the greater my need of true men and women to help me clear the dish and cheer my eye with honest faces where else there are gaps. Eat, I entreat you."

Catherine, sobbing, backed his request. Poor, simple, antique, hospitable souls! Jorian, whose appetite, especially since his illness, was keen, was ready to accept this invitation. But Joan whispered a word in his ear, and he instantly recoiled. "Nay, I will touch no meat that Holy Church hath cursed."

"In sooth, I forgot," Eli apologized. "My son, who was reared at my table, hath cursed my victuals. That seems strange. Well, what God wills, man must bow to."

The supper was flung out into the yard.

Jorian took his wife home, and a heavy sadness reigned in Eli's house that night.

Meanwhile, where was Clement?

He lay full-length upon the floor of the convent church, his lips pressed against the lowest step of the altar. He existed in an indescribable state of terror, misery, penitence, and self-abasement, through which struggled faint gleams of joy that Margaret was alive.

Night fell, finding him there weeping and praying. Morning would have found him there too; but suddenly, he remembered that absorbed in his own wrongs and Margaret's, he had committed another sin besides intemperate rage. He had neglected a dying man.

He rose instantly, groaning at his accumulated wickedness, and set out to repair the omission. The weather had turned; it rained hard. As he cleared the town, he heard wolves bay; they were upon his heels. Yet Clement was himself again, or nearly so. He thought little of danger or discomfort, driven by a shameful omission of religious duty he must repair. He moved stoutly forward through rain and darkness.

As he walked, he often beat his breast, crying out, "Mea Culpa! Mea Culpa!"

The turmoil that swept through Clement's sensitive mind, his tender conscience, and his loving heart within mere hours of such a sudden and tremendous shock defies the power of words to describe. Imagine yourself in his place, bearing the weight of such a revelation. If I were to write a volume on this internal storm, this very scene would be its conclusion. Instead, I shall recount only his next two overt acts, which reveal the state of his spirit once the first fierce tempest had subsided.

After spending the night with the dying hermit, offering and receiving holy consolations, Clement did not head for Rotterdam. His destination was Tergou. He went there to confront his fatal enemy, the burgomaster, armed with a parchment whose history was itself a romance, intending to force the man to disgorge the stolen goods and restore them to Margaret.

He arrived heated and dusty, stopping at a fountain where he began to eat his black bread and drink the cool water. Midway through this frugal meal, a female servant ran up, begging him to come and hear the confession of her dying master. Clement returned the bread to his wallet and followed her without a word.

She led him to the Stadthouse. He drew back with a slight shudder as she entered, but he quickly recovered his composure and followed her inside, up the stairs. There, propped up by pillows, lay his deadly enemy, Ghysbrecht, looking already like a corpse.

Clement eyed him from the doorway for a moment, recalling the tower, the wood, and the letter. Then, his voice trembling slightly, he spoke in a low tone, "Pax vobiscum!"

The sick man welcomed him with eager gratitude, limited only by his weakness. "Thank Heaven, thou art come in time to absolve me from my sins, father, and pray for my soul, thou and thy brethren."

"My son," Clement replied, "before absolution comes confession. In this act, there must be no reservation, if you value your soul's welfare. Consider carefully wherein you have most offended God and the Church, while I offer a prayer for wisdom to guide you."

Clement knelt and prayed. When he rose, he addressed Ghysbrecht with apparent calm, "My son, confess thy sins."

"Ah, father," the sick man sighed, "they are many and great."

"Great, then, be thy penitence, my son; so shalt thou find God's mercy great."

Ghysbrecht clasped his hands and began to confess with every appearance of contrition. He admitted to eating meat during mid-Lent, frequently missing Mass on Sundays and saints' days, and trifling with other religious observances, listing them with scrupulous fidelity.

When he finished, the friar said quietly, "'Tis well, my son. These are faults. Now, to thy crimes. Thou hadst done better to begin with them."

"Why, father, what crimes lie to my account if these be none?" Ghysbrecht asked.

"Am I confessing to thee, or thou to me?" Clement asked somewhat severely.

"Forgive me, father! Surely, I confess to you. But I know not what you call crimes."

"The seven deadly sins, art thou clear of them?"

"Heaven forefend I should be guilty of them. I know them not by name."

"Many do them all who cannot name them. Begin with that one which leads to lying, theft, and murder."

"I am quit of that one, any way. How do you call it?"

"AVARICE, my son."

"Avarice? Oh, as to that, I have been a saving man all my days; but I have kept a good table and have not altogether forgotten the poor. But alas, I am a great sinner. Mayhap the next will catch me. What is the next?"

"We have not yet done with this one. Bethink thee, the Church is not to be trifled with."

"Alas! Am I in a condition to trifle with her now? Avarice? Avarice?" He looked puzzled and innocent.

"Hast thou ever robbed the fatherless?" the friar inquired.

"Me? Robbed the fatherless?" Ghysbrecht gasped. "Not that I mind."

"Once more, my son, I am forced to tell thee thou art trifling with the Church. Miserable man! Another evasion, and I leave thee, and fiends will straightway gather round thy bed to tear thee down to the bottomless pit."

"Oh, leave me not! Leave me not!" shrieked the terrified old man. "The Church knows all. I must have robbed the fatherless. I will confess. Who shall I begin with? My memory for names is shaken."

The defense was skilful, but in this case, it failed.

"Hast thou forgotten Floris Brandt?" Clement asked stonily.

The sick man reared himself in bed in a pitiable state of terror. "How knew you that?"

"The Church knows many things," Clement said coldly. "And by many ways that are dark to thee. Miserable impenitent, you called her to your side, hoping to deceive her. You said, 'I will not confess to the cure, but to some friar who knows not my misdeeds. So will I cheat the Church on my deathbed and die as I have lived.' But God, kinder to thee than thou art to thyself, sent to thee one whom thou couldst not deceive.
