"Well, let me see. At first I was much dazzled by the sun-light, and could not go abroad, but that is passed; and good Reicht Heynes—humph!"

"What of her?"

"This to thine ear only, for she is a diamond. Her voice goes through me like a knife, and all voices seem loud but thine, which is so mellow sweet. Stay, now I'll fit ye with tidings; I spake yesterday with an old man that conceits he is ill-tempered, and sweats to pass for such with others, but oh! so threadbare, and the best good heart beneath."

"Why, 'tis a parish of angels," said Margaret ironically.

"Then why dost thou keep out on't?" retorted Gerard. "Well, he was telling me there was no parish in Holland where the devil hath such power as at Gouda; and among his instances, says he, 'We had a hermit, the holiest in Holland; but being Gouda, the devil came for him this week, and took him, bag and baggage; not a ha'porth of him left but a goodish piece of his skin, just for all the world like a hedgehog's, and a piece o' old iron furbished up.'"

Margaret smiled.

"Ay, but," continued Gerard, "the strange thing is, the cave has verily fallen in; and had I been so perverse as to resist thee, it had assuredly buried me dead there where I had buried myself alive. Therefore in this I see the finger of Providence, condemning my late, approving my present, way of life. What sayest thou?"

"Nay, can I pierce the like mysteries? I am but a woman."

"Somewhat more, methinks. This very tale proves thee my guardian angel, and all else avouches it, so come to Gouda manse."

"Well, go you on, I'll follow."

"Nay, in the cart with me."

"Not so."

"Why?"

"Can I tell why and wherefore, being a woman? All I know is I seem—to feel—to wish—to come alone."

"So be it then. I leave thee the cart, being, as thou sayest, a woman, and I'll go a-foot, being a man again, with the joyful tidings of thy coming."

When Margaret reached the manse, the first thing she saw was the two Gerards together: the son performing his capriccios on the plot, and the father slouching on a chair, in his great hat, with pencil and paper, trying very patiently to sketch him.

After a warm welcome, he showed her his attempts. "But in vain I strive to fix him," he said, "for he is incarnate quicksilver. Yet do but note his changes, infinite, but none ungracious; all is supple and easy; and how he melteth from one posture to another." He added presently, "Woe to illuminators! Looking on thee, sir baby, I see what awkward, lopsided, ungainly toads I and my fellows painted missals with, and called them cherubs and seraphs." Finally, he threw the paper away in despair, and Margaret conveyed it secretly into her bosom.

At night, when they sat round the peat fire, he bade them observe how beautiful the brass candlesticks and other glittering metals were in the glow from the hearth. Catherine's eyes sparkled at this observation. "And oh, the sheets I lie in here," he said, "often my conscience pricketh me, and saith, 'Who art thou to lie in lint like web of snow?' Dives was ne'er so flaxed as I. And to think that there are folk in the world that have all the beautiful things which I have here yet not content. Let them pass six months in a hermit's cell, seeing no face of man, then will they find how lovely and pleasant this wicked world is, and eke that men and women are God's fairest creatures." Margaret was always fair, but never to his eye so bright as now. Margaret shook her head incredulously. Gerard continued, "My mother was ever good and kind, but I noted not her exceeding comeliness till now."

"Nor I neither," said Catherine; "a score years ago I might pass in a crowd, but not now."

Gerard declared to her that each age had its beauty. "See this mild grey eye," he said, "that hath looked motherly love upon so many of us; all that love hath left its shadow, and that shadow is a beauty which defieeth Time. See this delicate lip, these pure white teeth. See this well-shaped brow, where comeliness just passeth into reverence. Art beautiful in my eyes, mother dear."

"And that is enough for me, my darling. 'Tis time you were in bed, child. Ye have to preach the morn."

And Reicht Heynes and Catherine interchanged a look which said, "We two have an amiable maniac to superintend; calls everything beautiful."

The next day was Sunday, and they heard him preach in his own church. It was crammed with persons who came curious but remained devout. Never was his wonderful gift displayed more powerfully; he was himself deeply moved by the first sight of all his people, and his bowels yearned over this flock he had so long neglected. In a single sermon, which lasted two hours and seemed to last but twenty minutes, he declared the whole scripture: he terrified the impenitent and thoughtless, confirmed the wavering, consoled the bereaved and the afflicted, uplifted the heart of the poor, and when he ended, left the multitude standing rapt, unwilling to believe the divine music of his voice and soul had ceased. Need I say that two poor women in a corner sat entranced, with streaming eyes?

"Wherever gat he it all?" whispered Catherine, with her apron to her eyes. "By our Lady, not from me."

As soon as they were by themselves, Margaret threw her arms round Catherine's neck and kissed her. "Mother, mother, I am not quite a happy woman, but oh, I am a proud one." And she vowed on her knees never by word or deed to let her love come between this young saint and Heaven.

Reader, did you ever stand by the seashore after a storm, when the wind happens to have gone down suddenly? The waves cannot cease with their cause; indeed, they seem at first to the ear to lash the sounding shore more fiercely than while the wind blew. Still, we are conscious that inevitable calm has begun, and is now but rocking them to sleep. So it was with those true and tempest-tossed lovers from that eventful night when they went hand in hand beneath the stars from Gouda hermitage to Gouda manse. At times a loud wave would every now and then come roaring, but it was only memory's echo of the tempest that had swept their lives; the storm itself was over, and the boiling waters began from that moment

In the silent, hallowed chamber of death, a priest finds his truest vocation. Gerard performed the final rites for the departed with the mechanical precision he would have applied to his humblest parishioner, his mind still fogged by the shock of loss. Only after the rituals were concluded did the full weight of the blow strike him, leaving him stupefied. Then, in a sudden surge of religious fervor, he knelt to thank Heaven for her Christian end.

His singular thought became to remove her from the gaze of strangers and lay her to rest within his own churchyard. That very evening, a covered cart drawn by a single horse rolled toward Gouda. Inside lay a coffin, and beside it, a broken-hearted man slumped with his arms and chin resting upon the cold wood.

The mourner's brief reserve of strength had vanished in the necessities of preparation. Now, he lay crushed, clinging desperately to the lead coffin that held her. The driver, a silent figure who walked by the horse's head, offered no words of comfort. Even when the horse needed rest, the driver spoke only in whispers, respecting the mute agony of his passenger. When they halted for the night, the innkeeper and his guests, well-meaning in their concern, urged the mourner to seek rest and food. Gerard repulsed them, snarling like a faithful dog denied its master, refusing to let go of the lead all through the long darkness. Finally, they draped a heavy cloak over him and left him in peace.

At noon, the sorrowful cart arrived at the manse. A dozen parishioners had gathered, their small troubles forgotten in anticipation of the parson. Seeing him so utterly stricken, they raised a loud wail of grief that pierced the air. The sound roused Gerard from his lethargy of woe; he saw their faces, tried to speak—"Oh, my children! my children!"—but choked back the words in his anguish.

Yet, the next day, despite all remonstrances, he buried her himself. He read the service with a voice that trembled only occasionally, and many tears fell upon the fresh earth. When the service ended, he stood like a statue in the churchyard, unmoving, while the people departed out of respect.

He remained in a trance-like state until the sexton, a man of simple wit, began to fill the grave without warning. At the sound of earth falling upon her, Gerard uttered a piercing scream. The sexton paused. Gerard staggered, clutching his breast, and the sexton supported him, calling for aid. Jorian Ketel, who had lingered nearby to mourn his benefactress, ran into the churchyard. Together, the two men supported Gerard back to the manse.

"Ah, Jorian! good Jorian!" he cried, his voice breaking. "Something snapped within me; I felt it, and I heard it. Here, Jorian, here," he gasped, pressing his hand firmly against his chest.

Two weeks later, a pale, bowed figure made his way to the Dominican convent in the suburbs of Gouda, seeking an audience with Brother Ambrose. Ambrose governed the house as deputy, for the prior had recently passed away and his successor had not yet arrived. This visitor was Gerard, come to end his life as he had begun it. He had entered as a novice on probation, yet the truth was he was a failing man, aware of his own decline. He sought only to die in peace, surrounded by the gentle kindness of Ambrose and the other monks whose doors and hearts had always been open to him. His former home felt too heavy, too saturated with memories of her.

Ambrose, recognizing both the value of his friend and the depth of his sorrow, harbored a kindly hope that he might be cured and restored to his parish duties. To this end, he placed Gerard in a comfortable cell above the gateway and strictly forbade him from fasting or practicing any austerities. But within a few days, the new prior arrived, proving to be a man of stern, almost brutal discipline. Initially consumed with rooting out abuses and tightening the rules, his demeanor shifted abruptly when he learned that the vicar of Gouda had entered the convent as a novice. "It is well," the prior declared sharply, "but let him first resign his vicarage, or depart. I will have no fat parsons in my house." The prior then summoned Gerard, who obeyed and went before him. The moment their eyes met, they both recoiled in shock.

"Clement!"

"Jerome!"

Jerome remained as stern and gloomy as his nature allowed, yet a thaw had begun in his heart toward Gerard. Throughout his time in England, he had missed his brother more than he realized, wondering constantly of his whereabouts. News from Gouda had only elevated his opinion of the younger monk, particularly the report that Gerard had stood firm against the Pope and the Minorites regarding "the infernal heresy of the immaculate conception." However, when a novice recounted the cause of Gerard's illness with tears streaming down his face, Jerome's disdain returned instantly. "Dying for a woman?" he muttered.

Driven to prevent such a scandal, Jerome visited Clement twice daily in his cell, deploying all his former influence and eloquence to urge the dying man to abandon this unspiritual despondency. He pleaded that the Church could not afford to lose Gerard's piety and gift of speech at such a critical juncture.

Gerard listened, acknowledging the logic, admiring Jerome's fortitude, and confessing his own frailty. Yet, he continued to fade toward the land of the faithful. One day, Jerome admitted he had heard Gerard's story and listened with pride. "But now," Jerome argued, "you ruin it all, Clement. This is the triumph of earthly passion. It would have been better to yield and repent while she lived, rather than resist her while she drew breath and succumb to her now, body and soul alike."

"Dear Jerome," Clement replied, his voice so sweet it stripped the rebuke of its sting, "I believe you misunderstand me. There is no passion here, only a profound affection for which I shall not blush in this place, nor in the next. Consider, Jerome: does the poor dog who dies of grief upon his master's grave commit a sin of passion? Neither do I. Passion would have saved my life but cost me my soul. She was my guardian angel; she upheld me in my duties and charity. Her face encouraged me in the pulpit; her lips soothed me under ingratitude. She became woven into every good thing in my life, and after leaning on her so long, I could not stand alone. Believe me, I am no rebel against Heaven. It is God's will to release me. When they threw the earth upon her poor coffin, something snapped within my chest that cannot be mended. I heard it, and I felt it. From that hour, Jerome, no food I ate had any flavor. With my eyes bandaged, I could no longer distinguish bread from flesh by taste."

"Holy saints!" Jerome gasped.

"And from that same moment, my deep depression vanished, and I smiled again. I often smile—why? I read it thus: He who holds the issues of life and death gave me that minute the great summons. Some cord of life snapped within me. He is merciful. I would have lived unhappy, but He said, 'No; enough is done, enough is suffered. Poor, feeble, loving servant, your shortcomings are forgiven, your sorrows have reached their end; come to your rest.' I come, Lord, I come!"

Jerome groaned. "The Church has always had her holy but feeble servants," he lamented. "Now I would give ten years of my life to save yours. But I see it cannot be. Die in peace."

Thus, within a few days, Gerard lay dying in a state of such holy happiness that more than one aged saint seemed present to gather his final words. In the evening, he had seen Giles, begging him not to let poor Jack starve, to ensure the trustees of little Gerard fulfilled their duties, to kiss his parents for him, and to send Denys to his friends in Burgundy, noting that the boy would feel strange without his comrade. Afterward, he had a private audience with Jerome alone. What transpired between them remains unclear, but it must have been extraordinary, for Jerome left the door with his hands crossed over his breast, his head bowed low, sighing heavily as he walked away.

The two monks who watched him until matins reported that throughout the night, Gerard broke into pious ejaculations, praises, and thanksgivings. Once, they said he wandered, believing he saw her walking in green meadows with other spirits clad in white, beckoning him; and they all smiled and beckoned back. Both monks claimed—though perhaps it was mere fancy—that just before dawn, three light taps struck the wall, one after another, very slowly. The dying man heard them and said, "I come, love, I come."

It is certain that Gerard uttered these words and prepared for his departure. He summoned all the monks keeping vigil. They gathered around him like gentle spirits, speaking holy words. Some prayed silently with faces touching the ground; others tenderly supported his head. When one mentioned his life of self-denial and charity, Gerard stopped him. Addressing them all, he declared, "My dear brethren, take note: he who dies so happy here holds not these new-fangled doctrines of man's merit. Oh, what a miserable hour it would have been for me if I did! Nay, I hold, with the Apostles and their pupils in the Church, the ancient fathers, that we are justified not by our own wisdom, or piety, or the works we have done in holiness of heart, but by faith."

Silence fell, and the monks exchanged significant glances.

"Please you sweep the floor," the dying Christian said, his voice restored with supernatural clarity and force.

They instantly obeyed, moved by awe and curiosity.

"Make me a great cross with wood ashes."

They strewed the ashes upon the floor in the shape of a great Cross.

"Now lay me down on it, for so will I die."

They gently lifted him from his bed and laid him upon the cross of wood ashes.

"Shall we spread out thine arms, dear brother?"

"Now God forbid! Am I worthy of that?"

He lay silent, eyes raised in ecstasy. Presently, speaking half to them, half to himself, he said with subdued but concentrated rapture, "Oh, I feel it buoyant. It lifts me floating in the sky whence my merits had sunk me like lead."

Day broke, displaying his face cast upward in silent rapture, his hands joined together, like Margaret's. Just about the hour she died, he spoke his last word in this world.

"Jesu!"

And with that word, he fell asleep.

They laid him out for his final resting place. Under his linen, they found a horse-hair shirt.

"Ah!" cried the young monks, "behold a saint!"

Beneath the haircloth, they discovered a long, thick tress of auburn hair.

They started, horrified, and a babel of voices arose, some condemning, some excusing.

In the midst of the uproar, Jerome entered. Hearing the dispute, he turned to an ardent young monk named Basil, who was crying out the loudest about scandal. "Basil," Jerome said, "is she alive or dead that owned this hair?"

"How may I know, father?"

"Then for all you know, it may be the relic of a saint?"

"Certes it may be," Basil replied skeptically.

"You have then broken our rule, which says, 'Put ill construction on no act done by a brother which can be construed innocently.' Who are you to judge such a man as this was? Go to your cell and stir not out for a week by way of penance."

Jerome then took the lock of hair. When the coffin was to be closed, he cleared the cell and placed the tress upon the dead man's bosom. "There, Clement," he said to the dead face. Setting himself a penance for the act, he nailed the coffin shut himself.

The next day, Gerard was buried in the Gouda churchyard. The monks followed him in procession from the convent. Jerome, evidently carrying out the deceased's wishes, read the service. The grave was deep, and at the bottom lay a lead coffin. Poor Gerard's body, light as a feather due to his wasting, was lowered and placed beside it.

After the service, Jerome addressed the crowd of parishioners who had come for a last look at their beloved friend. When he spoke of the departed's virtues, loud wailing and weeping burst forth, and tears fell upon the coffin like rain.

The monks returned home. Jerome gathered them in the refectory and spoke thus: "We have this day laid a saint in the earth. The convent will keep his trentals, but will feast, not fast; for our good brother is freed from the burden of the flesh; his labours are over, and he has entered into his joyful rest. I alone shall fast and do penance; for to my shame I say it, I was unjust to him, and knew not his worth till it was too late. And you, young monks, be not curious to inquire whether the lock he bore on his bosom was a token of pure affection or the relic of a saint; but remember the heart he wore beneath: most of all, fix your eyes upon his life and conversation, and follow them as ye may: for he was a holy man."

Thus, after life's fitful fever, these true lovers were at peace. The grave, kinder to them than the Church, united them forever. Now, a man of another age and nation, touched by their fate, has labored to build their tombstone and rescue them from long and unmerited oblivion.

He asks for your sympathy, but not your pity.

No, put this story to a wholesome use.

Fiction must often give false views of life and death. Here, as it happens, curbed by history, it gives you true ones. Let the barrier that kept these true lovers apart prepare you for this: here on earth, there will nearly always be some obstacle to your perfect happiness. To their early death, apply your Reason and your Faith, by way of exercise and preparation. For if you cannot bear to be told that these died young—who, had they lived a hundred years, would still be dead—how shall you bear to see the gentle, the loving, and the true glide from your own bosom to the grave, and fly from your house to heaven?

Yet this is in store for you. In every age, the Master of life and death, who is kinder as well as wiser than we are, has transplanted to heaven, young, earth's sweetest flowers.

I ask your sympathy, then, for their rare constancy and pure affection, and their cruel separation by a vile heresy within the bosom of the Church; but not your pity for their early but happy end.

"Beati sunt qui in Domino moriuntur."

The curtain finally falls, yet I remain upon the stage, compelled by a custom I detest but lack the courage to defy. I linger here to gather the scattered fragments of humanity I have left behind. Let it be understood that these minor figures hold no claim upon my affection; they have served their purpose merely to convince you that Gerard traversed the path from Holland to Rome among living souls, and not through a parade of lifeless dolls.

Eli and Catherine lived to a great age, their longevity stretching until both faded into dim memories for Gerard and Margaret. Giles too was long-lived; he journeyed to the court of Bavaria and remained alive there at ninety, though he had somehow withered into nothing more than leather and bone, his voice reduced to a hollow trumpet tone.

Cornelis, freed from all rivals and long forgiven by his mother—who clung to him with increasing desperation as her brood dispersed—waited and waited for his parents' passing. Yet Catherine's shrewd prophecy proved true: before she and her mate could wear themselves out, this worthy man rusted away. At sixty-five, he lay dying of old age in his mother's arms, she herself a hale woman of eighty-six. He had lain unconscious for a time, but returned to consciousness in the very moment of his death. Seeing her near him, he spoke of transforming the shop and premises once they were his. "Yes, my darling," the poor old woman soothed him gently. Within another minute, he was clay, and that clay was followed to the grave by every foot that had ever waited for his shoes.

Denys, broken-hearted by his comrade's death, returned to Burgundy. There, a small pension from the court sustained him until an unexpected inheritance from a distant relative brought him a considerable sum. For many years, he was known in his native land as a crusty old soldier, capable of recounting tales of war when he chose, and a bitter railer against women.

Jerome, disgusted by the northern laxity, retired to Italy. Through high connections, he became a mitred abbot at the age of seventy. He tightened the screw of discipline; his monks revered him and hated him in equal measure. He ruled with an iron rod for ten years. Then, one night, he died alone, for he had never found the way to a single heart. The Vulgate lay upon his pillow, the crucifix in his hand, and upon his lips rested something resembling a smile—a sight never witnessed while he lived. Thus, at that awful hour, he was not quite alone. *Requiescat in pace.* The Master he served has many servants, each with many minds; now and then, a faithful one proves to be a surly one, just as it is in these mortal mansions.

The yellow-haired lad, Gerard Gerardson, belongs not to fiction but to history. She has recorded his birth in terms far grander than mine. Above the tailor's house in the Brede Kirk Straet, she has inscribed: *"Haec est parva domus natus qua magnus Erasmus,"* and she has penned half a dozen lives of him. Yet, there remains something for her yet to do. She has not fully comprehended the *Magnum Erasmus*, nor can any other pigmy comprehend a giant, or a partisan understand a judge.

First scholar and divine of his epoch, he was also the heaven-born dramatist of his century. Some of the finest scenes in this new chronicle originate from his medieval pen, illuminating the pages where they appear. For the words of a genius so lofty as his are not born to die. Their immediate work upon mankind fulfilled, they may seem to lie torpid; yet with each fresh shower of intelligence that Time pours upon their students, they prove their immortal race. They revive, they spring from the dust of great libraries; they bud, they flower, they bear fruit and seed, passing from generation to generation, from age to age.
